## Aesthetica — Aesthetics

Written by

ALEXANDER GOTTLIEB BAUMGARTEN

Professor of Philosophy.

Frankfurt on the Oder,

at the expense of Johann Christian Kleyb, 1750.

## Praefatio. — Preface.

When in the year 1742 I was asked to set forth, in a new course of lectures, certain counsels for directing the lower faculties in the cognition of truth, I dictated, after the customary manner, the principal definitions and demonstrations to the pens of my hearers, and reserved the rest to be illustrated in freer discourse. What I had gathered together so pleased both certain other most learned men, into whose hands the written notes had come, and especially the most celebrated Georg Friedrich Meier, most worthy professor at Halle, that he not only commented on it in new lectures at the Fridericiana, but also set his mind to writing a book on the same subject. Two parts of that most useful work I have already seen, and in them I discovered with singular pleasure my own thoughts, not so much most dexterously turned into the vernacular tongue as enlarged and adorned with many excellent things. While I silently congratulate my sweetest friend, the learned who will read it, and myself on this, in the past year most generous and most noble fellow-students came to me once more to request a course of lectures on aesthetics. Resolved to satisfy so honourable a desire, and mindful how much time I once had to spend in wearying the right hands of those taking down my dictation, when I dictated the things of which I made mention above, and yet being asked for my own, I resolved to commit them to the press. Never, however, have my own things, if such they be, so pleased me that I would exempt them from second cares. Hence it was my intention to make even those things which I had conceived eight years ago better, if I could, by new additions and emendations. What it was possible to finish writing during this winter, let it now become public property in a small, yet sufficient, volume. To the execution of the rest I shall consecrate the first leisure I can obtain. Then perhaps it will be permitted to say more by way of preface. Now there is one thing that a grateful mind bids me testify publicly: that, as the writings of Werenfels and Vossius, among others, so also the thesaurus of the most celebrated Gesner, which I have truly found exceedingly rich not only in language but also in matters conducing to true beauty, brought me much assistance when I meditated anew what I had once written. Whatever fortune may await this and the rest of my cares in discharging the office which I acknowledge as received from holy providence, it will certainly be enough for me, before the good men who will judge these things, to have brought forward something from which meditations not only more beautiful but also better than mine may be able to cultivate minds and to profit morals not a little. Written at Frankfurt on the Oder, 26 March 1750.

## Prolegomena. — Prolegomena.

[p. 1] § 1. AESTHETICS (the theory of the liberal arts, the lower gnoseology, the art of thinking beautifully, the art of the analogue of reason) is the science of sensate cognition.

§ 2. The natural degree of the lower cognitive faculties, increased by use alone, without disciplinary cultivation, can be called NATURAL AESTHETICS, and can be distinguished, as natural logic usually is, into the innate, an innate beautiful wit, and the acquired, and this again into the teaching and the using.

§ 3. Of the artificial aesthetics that is added to the natural, § 1, the use among others will be the greater 1) [p. 2] to prepare good material for the sciences, which are to be known chiefly by the intellect, 2) to accommodate what is known scientifically to the grasp of anyone, 3) to carry the improvement of cognition even beyond the boundaries of what we can know distinctly, 4) to supply good principles to all the gentler studies and the liberal arts, 5) in common life, other things being equal, to excel in the doing of all things.

§ 4. Hence the special uses: 1) philological, 2) hermeneutic, 3) exegetical, 4) rhetorical, 5) homiletic, 6) poetic, 7) musical, etc.

§ 5. It could be objected to our science, § 1, 1) that it extends too widely to be exhausted in one little book, one course of lectures. Reply: by conceding it. But something is better than nothing. 2) That it is the same as rhetoric and poetics. Reply: a) it extends more widely; b) it comprises what these have in common with other arts and among themselves, so that, once these have been surveyed here in their fitting place, each art may cultivate its own ground more happily without useless tautologies. 3) That it is the same as criticism. Reply: a) there is also a logical criticism; b) a certain species of criticism is a part of aesthetics; c) for this, a certain prior notion of the rest of aesthetics is almost necessary, unless one wishes, in judging [p. 3] things beautifully thought, said and written, to dispute about mere tastes.

§ 6. It could be objected to our science 4) that sensate things, phantasms, fables, the perturbations of the affects, etc., are unworthy of philosophers and lie beneath their horizon. Reply: a) the philosopher is a man among men, and does not rightly think so great a part of human cognition foreign to himself; b) the general theory of things beautifully thought is being confounded with the particular practice and execution.

§ 7. Objection 5) confusion is the mother of error. Reply: a) but it is the condition sine qua non of discovering truth, where nature makes no leap from obscurity into distinctness. From night through dawn comes noon. b) Therefore confusion must be attended to, lest errors arise from it, as many and as great as they are among the careless; c) confusion is not commended, but cognition is improved, insofar as something of confusion is necessarily mixed into it.

§ 8. Objection 6) Distinct cognition is superior. Reply: a) For a finite spirit, only in weightier matters; b) the positing of the one is not the exclusion of the other; c) therefore, following rules distinctly known, we go straight at what is to be known beautifully [p. 4] first, from which a distinctness all the more perfect may sometimes arise, § 3, 7.

§ 9. Objection 7) Through the cultivation of the analogue of reason, it is to be feared that the territory of reason and solidity may suffer some loss. Reply: a) this argument is among those that prove too much, since the same danger exists whenever a composite perfection is sought; it incites to caution, it does not counsel the neglect of a true perfection. b) An uncultivated and more corrupt analogue of reason is no less harmful to reason and to a stricter solidity.

§ 10. Objection 8) Aesthetics is an art, not a science. Reply: a) These are not opposed habits. How many things that were once only arts are now at the same time sciences? b) that our art can be demonstrated, experience will prove, and it is evident a priori, since psychology, etc., supply certain principles; that it deserves to be raised into a science, the uses mentioned, among others, in § 3, 4, teach.

§ 11. Objection 9) Aestheticians are born, not made, like poets. Reply: Hor. A. Poet. v. 408. Cic. de Or. l. II. c. 60. Bilfinger in dilucidd. § 268. Breitinger von Gleichnissen p. 6. The born aesthetician is helped by a more complete theory, one more [p. 5] commendable by the authority of reason, more exact, less confused, more certain, less faltering, § 3.

§ 12. Objection 10) The lower faculties, the flesh, are rather to be subdued than to be excited and strengthened. Reply: a) Command over the lower faculties is demanded, not tyranny. b) To this, insofar as it can be obtained naturally, aesthetics will lead as if by the hand. c) The lower faculties are not to be excited and strengthened by aestheticians insofar as they are corrupt, but to be directed by them, lest they be further corrupted by perverse exercises, or lest, under the lazy pretext of avoiding abuse, the use of a talent divinely granted be done away with.

§ 13. Our aesthetics, § 1, like logic, its elder sister, is I) THEORETICAL, teaching, general, P. I, giving precepts 1) about things and what is to be thought, HEURISTICS, C. I, 2) about lucid order, METHODOLOGY, C. II, 3) about the signs of things beautifully thought and disposed, SEMIOTICS, C. III; II) PRACTICAL, using, special, P. II. On either side,

*for him whose matter has been chosen according to his powers,* / *neither eloquence will desert him, nor lucid order.* Hor. E.

Let *the matter* be your first care, *lucid order* the second, / and *the signs* the third care in the last place.

[p. 6]

## Pars I. Aesthetica Theoretica. — Part I. Theoretical Aesthetics.

### Caput I. Heuristica. — Chapter I. Heuristics.

#### Sectio I. Pulcritudo Cognitionis. — Section I. The Beauty of Cognition.

§ 14. The end of aesthetics is the perfection of sensate cognition, as such, § 1. But this is beauty, Metaphysic. § 521, 662. And its imperfection, as such, is to be avoided, § 1. But this is deformity, Metaphysic. § 521, 662.

§ 15. Perfections of sensate cognition so recondite that they either remain wholly obscure to us, or we cannot intuit them except by understanding, the aesthetician, as such, does not care for. § 14.

[p. 7] § 16. Imperfections of sensate cognition so recondite that they either remain wholly obscure to us, or can be detected only by intellectual judgement, the aesthetician, as such, does not care for, § 14.

§ 17. SENSATE COGNITION is, by a denomination taken from the predominant feature, the complex of representations subsisting below distinctness. If we now wished to survey by understanding, all at once, either the beauty and elegance alone, or the deformity alone, § 15, 16, of this cognition as it exists, as the spectator of learned taste sometimes contemplates them, the distinctness necessary to science, § 1, would crumble as if overwhelmed by the mass of charms or blemishes, generic through their various classes, specific, or numerical. Hence let us first survey BEAUTY, insofar as it is common to almost all beautiful sensate cognition, the UNIVERSAL and catholic beauty, together with its opposite, § 14.

§ 18. The universal beauty of sensate cognition will be, § 14, 1) the agreement of the thoughts, insofar as we still abstract from their order and signs, among themselves towards one thing, which is a phenomenon, § 14, Metaphysic. § 662: [p. 8] THE BEAUTY OF THINGS AND THOUGHTS, to be distinguished from the beauty of cognition, of which it is the first and primary part, § 13, and from the beauty of objects and matter, with which, on account of the received signification of “thing”, it is often but wrongly confounded. Ugly things can be thought beautifully, as such, and more beautiful things uglily.

§ 19. The universal beauty of sensate cognition, § 14, since there is no perfection without order, Metaphys. § 95, is 2) the agreement of the order in which we meditate the things beautifully thought, both internal and with the things, as a phenomenon, § 14: THE BEAUTY OF ORDER and disposition.

§ 20. The universal beauty of sensate cognition is, § 14, since we do not perceive what is signified without signs, Met. § 619, 3) the agreement of the signs, internal, and with the order, and with the things, as a phenomenon: THE BEAUTY OF SIGNIFICATION, such as diction and elocution, when the sign is speech or discourse, and at the same time action, when the discourse is delivered by the living voice. You have the three catholic graces of cognition. § 18, 19.

§ 21. Just as many can there be, and are to be avoided, the deformities, vices, blemishes of sensate cognition, either in the thoughts and things, § 18, [p. 9] or in the conjunction of several things thought, § 19, or in the signification, § 20, M. § 121, in the order in which we have enumerated them, § 13.

§ 22. Richness, magnitude, truth, clarity, certainty, and the life of cognition, insofar as they agree in one perception, and among themselves, e.g. richness and magnitude towards clarity, truth and clarity towards certainty, all the rest towards life, insofar as the various other elements of cognition, § 18–20, agree towards these same, give the perfection of all cognition, M. § 669, 94, and as phenomena the beauty of sensate cognition, § 14, the universal beauty, § 17, especially of things and thoughts, § 18, in which there is delight in

Abundance, nobility, the sure light of a truth that moves.

§ 23. Narrowness, meanness, falsity, M. § 551, imperspicuous obscurity, doubtful fluctuation, M. § 531, inertness, M. § 669, are imperfections of all cognition, M. § 94; as phenomena they deform sensate cognition, § 14, in general, § 17, the principal vices of things and thoughts, § 21.

§ 24. The beauty of sensate cognition, § 14, and the elegance of things itself, § 18, are composite perfections, § 18–20, 22, and universal ones too, § 17, M. § 96. This is evident also from the fact [p. 10] that no simple perfection is a phenomenon for us. M. § 444. Hence they admit very many exceptions, not to be counted among the vices, even if they become phenomena, provided that they do not remove the greatest agreement that has place; hence let them be, so far as they can, the fewest and the least. M. § 445.

§ 25. These things being posited, beauty is posited, if we may say ELEGANCE; EXCEPTIONS such as we have described in § 24, when e.g. a weaker rule of beauty yields to a stronger, a less fruitful to a more fruitful, a nearer to a more remote one to which it is subordinated, etc., M. 446, will be NOT INELEGANT. Hence in establishing the rules of beauty in cognition, attention is rightly paid at the same time to their strength, M. 180.

§ 26. A perception, insofar as it is a ground, is an ARGUMENT. There are therefore arguments that enrich, ennoble, prove, illustrate, persuade, move, § 22, of which aesthetics demands not only force and efficacy, M. § 515, but also elegance, § 25. The part of cognition in which a peculiar elegance is detected is a FIGURE (schema). There are therefore figures 1) of things and thoughts, § 18, FIGURES OF THOUGHT, 2) of order, § 19, 3) of signification, whence the figures of diction, § 20. [p. 11] Of the figures of thought there are as many kinds as of arguments.

§ 27. Since the beauty of cognition, § 14, is an effect of the one thinking beautifully, neither greater nor nobler than his living powers, M. § 331, 332, let us before all else delineate some genesis and idea of the one who is to think beautifully, THE CHARACTER OF THE HAPPY AESTHETICIAN, an enumeration of those things which in the soul are naturally the nearer causes of beautiful cognition. But for the reasons adduced in § 17, let us now stop at the general and, as it were, CATHOLIC character, such as beautiful thoughts of every kind require, not descending to any SPECIAL one, the complement of the general character for actually producing a given, definite beautiful cognition of a determinate species.

#### Sectio II. Aesthetica Naturalis. — Section II. Natural Aesthetics.

§ 28. For the general character of the happy aesthetician, presupposing the more general things, § 27, there is required I. INNATE NATURAL AESTHETICS, § 2, (φύσις [nature], natura, εὐφυΐα [good natural endowment], ἀρχέτυπα στοιχεῖα γενέσεως [the archetypal elements of one’s birth],) the natural disposition [p. 12] of the whole soul for thinking beautifully, with which it is born.

§ 29. To nature, of which § 28, belongs 1) AN INNATE CHARMING AND ELEGANT WIT, an innate wit in the wider sense, whose lower faculties are more easily excited and conspire in a proportion apt for the elegance of cognition.

§ 30. To charming wit, of which § 29, belong A) the lower cognitive faculties and their natural dispositions: a) that of sensing acutely, M. § 540, not only so that the soul may obtain the first material for thinking beautifully by the external senses, but also so that by inner sense and the most intimate consciousness, M. § 535, it may be able to experience the changes and effects of its remaining faculties, in order to direct them. So that the faculty of sensing may one day conspire with the rest, let it be so great in a charming wit that it will not always and everywhere, by whatever sensations of its own, suppress whatever heterogeneous thoughts there may be. § 29.

§ 31. b) The natural disposition for imagining, § 30, by which a charming wit is εὐφαντασίωτον [of lively imagination], since 1) past things are often to be thought beautifully, 2) present things often pass away [p. 13] before the beautiful thought of them is completed, 3) future things are known not from present things alone, but also from past ones. So that the imagination may one day conspire with the other faculties, let it be so great in a charming wit that it does not always and everywhere obscure with its phantasms all the other perceptions, each naturally weaker than each single imagination. § 29. If the faculty of feigning is referred to phantasy, as often among the ancients, the greater necessity of it in a charming wit is twofold.

§ 32. c) The natural disposition for perspicacity, § 30, M. § 573, by which the things supplied through sense and phantasy, etc., § 30, 31, are polished, as it were, by acumen and wit. Through these faculties both the beauty of cognition, insofar as it demands proportions as phenomena and does not admit disproportions as phenomena, and the beautiful proportion itself of wit in the wider sense are to be produced. § 29, M. 572. Hence, since acumen not rarely lies hidden at the same time under the name of wit, all beautiful cognition is sometimes ascribed to wit. Yet so that perspicacity too may one day conspire properly with the other faculties of the mind, let it be so great that it is borne only upon material sufficiently prepared for it, § 29.

§ 33. d) The natural disposition for recognising, and memory, M. § 579. Mnemosyne [p. 14] was called the mother of the Muses by the ancients, who referred the reproduction of the imagination also to memory, § 31. Nor yet can one who is, for example, to narrate beautifully dispense with the faculty of recognising itself; indeed, one who feigns must be mindful, lest what goes before should uglily contradict what follows.

§ 34. e) The poetic disposition, M. § 589, which is required to such a degree that it has procured for the more excellent class of practical aestheticians the name of poets. Nor will the psychologist wonder, when he weighs how great a portion of beautiful meditation is to be formed by combining and abstracting phantasms. Yet so that it may conspire properly with the other faculties, let it be so great that it does not withdraw the world it has, as it were, created from the polishings of the others, e.g. of perspicacity, § 31. § 29.

§ 35. f) The disposition for a taste not common, but rather delicate, M. § 608, which, together with perspicacity, § 31, is the lower judge of things sensed, phantasms, fictions, etc., M. § 607; whenever particular things are judged through the intellect, M. § 641, that is of no concern to beauty. § 15.

§ 36. g) The disposition for foreseeing, M. § 595, and presaging, M. § 610. Observing this [p. 15] in the more beautiful wits, the ancients ascribed it, extraordinary to a degree not found in many, like some prodigy and miracle, to the gods. Whence poets were in turn seers. Nor yet is this, which lies open to all, to be required in passing only for I know not what aesthetic oracles; since it is required for all the life of cognition, the primary beauty. § 22, M. § 665. So that this faculty and divinatory disposition may finally conspire with the others, § 29, M. § 616, let it be so great that in its own place and time it yields neither to a heterogeneous sensation, much less to a heterogeneous imagination, § 30, 31.

§ 37. h) The disposition for signifying its perceptions, M. § 619, now more, now less necessary, according as one attends to the character of one who merely thinks beautifully, within the soul, or of one who also expresses what has been beautifully thought. Yet in the former it cannot be wholly lacking, § 20. So that it may one day agree with the rest, let it not be so great as to suppress the intuition necessary for beauty: § 35, M. § 620.

§ 38. To charming wit, of which § 29, belong B.) the higher cognitive faculties, M. § 624, insofar as a) intellect and reason, through the soul’s command over itself, not rarely contribute much to exciting the lower faculties, [p. 16] M. § 730; b) the agreement of these and the proportion apt for beauty is often obtained only through the use of intellect and reason, § 29; c) the natural consequence for a spirit of great vividness in the analogue of reason is the BEAUTY of the intellect, M. § 637, and OF REASON, of the extensively distinct insight into a nexus.

§ 39. A charming wit is naturally so disposed that it may at some time, abstracting not only from its own past state, whatever memory brings back, but from the external sensations themselves, attend to some feigned state as future, intuit the same perspicaciously as good or evil, and be able to set it before the eyes with fitting signs, under the command of intellect and reason, § 30–38.

§ 40. Whether in jest or by a grave error, Democritus excludes the sane from Helicon, § 39, M. 594, and a good part, M. § 639, of those who hope to obtain the worth and the name of a man of charm is rather foolish,

*if it never entrusts a head incurable by three Anticyras* / *to the barber Licinus.* Hor.

§ 41. Greater lower faculties, and naturally such, are required in one who is to think [p. 17] beautifully, § 29. But these can not only exist together with naturally great higher faculties, M. § 649, but are even required for them, as a condition sine qua non. M. § 637. Hence it is a prejudiced opinion that beauty of wit is by nature repugnant to the stricter gifts of understanding and reasoning, insofar as these are received as innate from nature.

§ 42. There can be a beautiful wit which has badly neglected the use of intellect and reason; there can be a philosophical and mathematical wit not sufficiently furnished with the ornaments of the analogue of reason. If there can be a wit even moderately charming which is nevertheless by its very nature inept for the more solid sciences, there cannot however be a wit born for conceiving those sciences which is, as it is born, unfit for all charm of cognition. M. § 649, 247.

§ 43. Rather, the higher and universal wits through all ages, Orpheus and the founders of poetic philosophy, Socrates, called εἴρων [the dissembler], Plato, Aristotle, Grotius, Descartes, Leibniz, teach a posteriori that the disposition for thinking beautifully and solidly can agree well and dwell in one seat, not too narrow, even with the stricter discipline of philosophers and mathematicians.

[p. 18] § 44. For the born aesthetician, § 28, there is required 2) a temper that will more readily follow worthy and moving cognition, and that proportion of the appetitive faculties by which it is more easily borne towards beautiful cognition, i.e. AN INNATE AESTHETIC TEMPERAMENT, M. § 732.

§ 45. Since every man is borne towards every kind of desirable things, insofar as it is known to him, etc., M. § 665, let us place some of them, roughly, as befits the aesthetician, § 15, in the order of dignity: money, wealth, labour, comparative leisure, external delights, liberty, honour, friendship, vigour and firm health of body, the shades of the virtues, beautiful cognition with its corollary, amiable virtue, higher cognition with its corollary, venerable virtue. It will therefore be permitted to attribute to aesthetic temperaments some INNATE MAGNITUDE OF HEART, an instinct chiefly towards great things, especially among those who attend to how easy the transition is from these to the greatest. § 38, 41.

§ 46. According to the trite doctrine of the temperaments the melancholic is usually commended by those who do not sufficiently distinguish the more prolix beautiful meditations [p. 19] from the shorter ones, which are to be finished quickly enough. For the latter the sanguine, as he is called, for the former the melancholic will be more apt. But since they prefer to be choleric,

*To those whom glory has carried to the stage in her windy chariot,* / *may the same glory give strength as they attack a great work.*

#### Sectio III. Exercitatio Aesthetica. — Section III. Aesthetic Exercise.

§ 47. For the character of the happy aesthetician there is required II.) Ἄσκησις [practice] and AESTHETIC EXERCISE, the more frequent repetition of actions homogeneous in this respect, so that there may be some agreement of the wit and temper described in § 28–46 upon a given theme, i.e., lest anyone think of themes given out by Orbiliuses, upon one thing to be thought, upon one matter, § 18, so that the habit of thinking beautifully may gradually be acquired, M. § 577.

§ 48. Nature, of which S. II, cannot subsist at the same degree even for a shorter time, M. § 550; hence unless its dispositions or habits are increased by continual exercises, § 47, it decreases somewhat, however great it be supposed, and grows torpid, M. § 650. Yet I do not commend merely exercises of the faculties of [p. 20] which S. II, but aesthetic ones, § 47. There are EXERCISES that corrupt a sufficiently beautiful nature and DISFIGURE it, phenomena to be avoided, § 16, among active wits that are always doing something, and not more happily than by the commendable substitution of better ones, M. § 698.

§ 49. I now demand some agreement in the aesthetic exercises themselves, and indeed in all of them, § 47. Otherwise they are not effects of a beautiful nature, and hence do not increase its force, § 47, M. § 139. But only some agreement. The stake-drill does not demand soldiers as great as battle does. I concede that some exercises, at the same time somewhat corrupting a sufficiently beautiful nature, are aesthetic, § 16. I concede also some that are somewhat disfiguring, § 48, provided they agree more than they disagree, which we may call aesthetic from the predominant feature, § 47. I concede finally those whose deformity is greater than their beauty, provided they are accompanied by that consciousness, § 35, of the preponderant ugliness, through which it will not be so hereafter, if it is bad now, M. § 666.

§ 50. Not of the wit alone, but of the wit and the temper, of which S. II, § 49, do I demand some agreement in the aesthetic exercises, § 47. Let the wit be cultivated by dead and inert exercises, Ethic. § 403, the temper wholly neglected, or altogether corrupted and depressed, e.g. into [p. 21] a dominant passion and prevailing lust for hypocrisy, for athletic ferocity, for the fellowship of spendthrifts, for ambition, licence, bacchanals, idleness, sloth, economic cares, or money altogether, § 46: the meanness of a thin heart, showing through everywhere, will disfigure whatever may seem charmingly thought, § 48.

§ 51. Let the temper be kept upright, as will be seen, or raised up, if by any other means it can be done, M. § 732, while the wit, of which S. II, is left rough, Ethic. § 403: there will perhaps be born from this what in § 45 I called the shades of the virtues; but partly the roughness of the wit, showing through everywhere, will disfigure the motions which you might call good, of a good heart, as they are wont to say, § 48; partly the mind, at least averse to beautiful cognition, or not sufficiently desiring it, will let the wit, under evil auspices, grow torpid, not unwillingly, § 48, to the point from which it can never again be raised up to know anything beautifully. § 27.

§ 52. Aesthetic exercises, § 47, will be 1) αὐτοσχεδιάσματα [improvisations], undertaken without the direction of a learned art in which the one exercising is proficient. Here belongs that rough Saturnian measure in which the ancient husbandman of the Casci, brave and lifting up his spirit at festival time, § 50,

[p. 22] *poured out rustic reproaches in alternating verses.* Hor.

Here belong all the specimens of beautiful cognition of the human race before the learned arts were invented; here the first sparks of any more beautiful nature, anticipating all art, when, e.g., as Ovid relates of himself,

*Whatever he tried to say was verse.*

§ 53. In aesthetic matters too let us take especial care not to take a rough and unlearned wit for these. Ethic. § 405. Surely the wit of Homer, and of Pindar, etc., was surely not rough,

*A wit, not uncultivated, not uglily shaggy,* Hor.

yet they were archetypes rather than ectypes of the learned arts. The unlearned man can be of a wit, even an aesthetic one, § 29, quite polished, § 52, Ethic. § 403, just as the learned man can be of a wit, as regards beauty, rather rough, § 42.

§ 54. As Leibniz called music an arithmetical exercise of a soul that does not know it is counting, so too, through the expectation of similar cases and the first imitation, as it were innate, that arises from it, the infant, still almost unaware that it thinks, much less that it thinks beautifully, is already [p. 23] being exercised, by good fortune if anyone to be educated falls into the hand of a craftsman who shapes the tender and stammering mouth of the boy. § 37.

*He turns the ear even now away from obscene talk,* / *Soon too he forms the heart with friendly precepts,* § 50, 45. / *He reports deeds rightly done,* § 31, 32, 35, *the rising years,* § 36, *with well-known* § 30. / *examples he instructs,* § 32, etc. Hor. L. II. ep. I. v. 126.

§ 55. Wit that is beautiful by nature is further exercised, and now exercises itself more openly, § 54, even if it does not know what it is doing, while the boy chats, while he plays, especially where he is the inventor of the games, or the little director among his comrades, and intent upon them in earnest he already sweats, and bears much, does much, while he sees, hears, reads what he beautifully understands, provided only that all such things are directed according to § 49–51, so that the exercises are aesthetic ones, § 47.

§ 56. Even as adults we are not seldom deceived, when in reading or hearing things beautifully said, or written, etc., we understand them beautifully, and behold their beauty and as it were taste it, § 35, so that we silently acclaim the author within ourselves: *beautiful! good! right,* and yet do not [p. 24] sufficiently notice that we ourselves, by imitating together with him, are thinking beautifully at the same time. Hence aesthetic exercise is greater than it generally seems, § 54, the exemplars of the most beautiful authors being

*turned over by the hand at night, turned over by day.* § 54. / *To the Greeks* (the Gauls) *wit, to the Greeks* (the Gauls) *the Muse gave speech with rounded mouth,* / *greedy for nothing but praise.* Hor.

§ 57. It is self-evident that greater exercises now both give greater powers and attest them: αὐτοσχεδιάσματα εὑριστικά [inventive improvisations], which the mind pours forth by its own efforts αὐτομάτως [spontaneously], when it is now able to swim without a cork, whether taught, § 54–56, or born to it, § 53.

§ 58. Aesthetic exercises will be 2) more correct and more certain, if to the innate, S. II, and acquired natural aesthetics, the mistress nature, § 2, there is added learned art, § 47–57, without which wits that are beautiful indeed, yet not divine, often make trial of the way to the elegances of cognition,

*As is a journey under an uncertain moon, beneath a grudging light,* / *in the woods, when Jupiter has buried the sky in shade* / *and black night has taken the colour from things.* Virg.

[p. 25] § 59. Each kind of exercise, § 52, 58, as often as the one to be exercised efficiently resolves upon beautiful cognition, brings not wit alone, but also the aesthetic disposition and temperament into a habit and confirms it by custom, § 45, intensifying the innate magnitude of the heart. § 46. M. 247.

§ 60. AESTHETIC DYNAMICS, or the critique of a given man’s powers for attaining a given beauty of a given cognition, cannot measure the innate powers of nature except from their effects, from exercises, § 27, where the following is rightly concluded: So great is the αὐτοσχεδίασμα [improvisation], so great the specimen of the given man; therefore so great also is his innate nature, which, with the preceding exercises added, has been able to reach so far. M. § 57. Yet the following, though more often inferred, does not hold equally well: For so great an αὐτοσχεδίασμα [improvisation], or specimen, the powers of the given man, as they now are, did not suffice; therefore he is destitute of the nature to be born with him for meditations of this kind, M. § 60.

§ 61. The aesthetic dynamist will often have need of AESTHETIC ATTEMPTS (trials), that is, of exercises resolved upon, among other things, in order to find out by experience whether the powers of a given man, and [p. 26] how great ones, suffice for a given beautiful cognition, M. § 697. Then, if the attempts proceed well, the affirmative experience that the powers sufficed holds rightly enough, § 60; if they proceed less well, a defect of nature is not always the cause, much less does the inference hold from the defect of some special aesthetic character perhaps required for the given trial to the defect of the general one, or perhaps of other special ones. § 27. Cicero’s poetic trials, the trials of Ovid and Horace at epic, did not succeed well.

#### Sectio IIII. Disciplina Aesthetica. — Section IIII. Aesthetic Discipline.

§ 62. For the general character of the happy aesthetician, § 27, there is required III.) Μάθησις [learning] and AESTHETIC DISCIPLINE, a theory, more perfect than is usually obtained by nature alone and by its mere use, of the things that more closely influence the matter and the form of beautiful cognition, now to be brought into use by stricter exercises, lest the habit wander from ignorance or uncertainty of the things to be thought, or of the rules and their reasons, or think licentiously, or, thinking that all will see its faults, [p. 27] of which it is nevertheless itself unaware, be deterred from the very use of beautiful meditation, § 47, 48.

§ 63. To aesthetic discipline belongs 1) all BEAUTIFUL ERUDITION, i.e. erudition in so far as it exhibits a cognition, better than the unlearned one, of the objects about which one must at some time think beautifully, a cognition by which, once imbued, wit beautiful by nature and aroused by daily exercises, and the aesthetic heart, in so far as it is moved, in so far as it is affected and, as Persius has it, seethed within, can more happily conspire to think beautifully upon a given theme, § 62, 47.

§ 64. The more important parts of beautiful erudition are the disciplines that exhibit God, the universe, man, in respect especially of his moral state, histories, not even excluding mythical ones, and antiquities, and the genius of signs, § 63.

§ 65. In the doctrine of such disciplines the aesthetician cares only for that perfection which becomes a phenomenon in things beautifully meditated, § 14, 15, partly negatively, while it enables him to avoid disfiguring παροράματα [oversights], partly positively, while often by a single phrase or a sufficiently brief sign it compels the learned reader or spectator [p. 28] to expect anything great from an author of such doctrine, even though he conceals much of his learning by an exception that is not inelegant, § 48, 25.

§ 66. We do not refer here to any childish instructions, § 54, nor to the hasty acquaintance with the things treated in the disciplines, gained by use, by desultory reading and by miscellaneous hearings, of which S. III, but to a methodical and manly skill in them, however perfect, since the latter accomplishes more, yet only to the extent that it accomplishes more of the things mentioned in § 65.

§ 67. Nevertheless we do not demand that the aesthetician be a polyhistor or a pansophist, since the general character demands only erudition in general; in that species of beautiful cognition in which someone intends to excel, the special character will determine more carefully the parts of erudition that bear more closely upon it, in which it is unseemly for him to be a stranger who has taken upon himself the cultivation of this species of beautiful cognition, § 27.

§ 68. To aesthetic discipline belongs 2) a theory of the form of beautiful cognition, of the mode and manner of obtaining it by lawful ways, more perfect than is usually obtained by nature alone and [p. 29] by its mere use, now to be brought into use by more accurate and stricter exercises. § 62. Now a complex of rules disposed in order is usually called an ART. Hence in the general character of the good aesthetician there arises the requisite of an AESTHETIC ART.

§ 69. In the special characters of the happy aesthetician, e.g. of the orator, the poet, the musician, etc., the desires of this requisite have long since been satisfied by the art of rhetoric, of poetics, of music, etc. Whatever is usually discussed about their pleasantness, utility and necessity can, by raising the notions a little into more general ones, be applied to the aesthetic art, whose predicates of this kind extend as widely as the praises of all the rest taken together, § 68.

§ 70. Now it will be permissible to assume that an art is the more excellent 1) the more widely applicable the rules it embraces, i.e. rules whose application is useful, indeed necessary, on more occasions, and the more complete it itself is, being nevertheless a brief compendium of sufficient rules, 2) the stronger and weightier the rules it exhibits, i.e. rules which one can never neglect without greater loss, 3) the more exact and the more accurate its exposition of them, 4) the more perspicuous, 5) the more certain and the more derived from true principles, the souls of rules, [p. 30] 6) the more they entice one to direct one’s actions, and practice itself, according to their prescriptions, § 22.

§ 71. But the laws of the aesthetic art are diffused through all the liberal arts, as a kind of cynosure of the special ones, and have a still wider sphere, wherever it is better to cognise anything beautifully than basely, where scientific cognition of it is not needed. Hence more than any of the special arts, § 69, it deserves to be reduced to the form of an art, as something that will at last exhibit a more complete system of beauty in cognising, and the separate ἀποσπασμάτια [fragments] of the arts to be derived from it. Nothing complete can be hoped for in the special arts because of their infinite variety, unless, rising to the sources of both beauty and cognition, to the nature of each, we examine the first divisions of each, divided, on account of the principle of the excluded third, between two contradictories which exhaust the matter; and this will be done by reducing the aesthetic art to the form of a science, § 70.

§ 72. A higher rule is always stronger than all those subordinate to it. Hence the laws of the aesthetic art are stronger than all the rules of the special arts to be derived from them, and an exception, not inelegant, is to be made from the latter when they collide. Now if these alone are known, it will turn out otherwise, [p. 31] or even if the former are seen scarcely, and hardly even scarcely, as if at the bottom or from afar, while the latter, in all their ornament and apparatus of examples, dazzle the eyes, not without rather grave loss. Hence the complex of aesthetic laws, in preference to their more special corollaries, deserves to be reduced to the form of an art which at the same time sets their strength before the eyes with sufficient evidence, once it has been reduced to the form of a science. § 70.

§ 73. A false rule is always worse than none. Now laws abstracted from only one example or another, and passed off as universal without further reason, what else are they than a sufficiently gaping inference from the particular to the universal? How often, therefore, if not altogether false, do they nevertheless err in quantity? Now a complete induction can never be had. Hence there is need of a perspicacious insight a priori into the truth of the weightier rules, which experience may then confirm and illustrate, just as it was perhaps the first aid in discovering it. Hence the special arts, if one means to separate true rules from spurious, need a further principle from which they can cognise their special rules, and this, the aesthetic art, lest it too have to be established solely through the same ill-founded expectation of similar cases, needs to be reduced to the form of a science. § 70.

[p. 32] § 74. Since the intellect and reason must by moral necessity be the directors of all things beautifully thought, § 39, but this cannot happen if the rules of thinking beautifully are not distinctly perceived, M. § 624, 640, it does not suffice that these be perspicuously propounded and illustrated by many examples, especially since such a confused cognition of them can already be obtained without discipline through acquired natural aesthetics, § 62. Lest therefore the whole discipline of the aesthetic art pass into some mere coordination and disposition of rules formed by the analogue of reason, which is to direct the analogue of reason alone, § 68, it also strives to conceive the rules distinctly and with intellectual perspicuity, usefully raised at the same time to the form of a science. § 70.

§ 75. What Leibniz once said of metaphysics, before the restoration of it which he began: *I see that many who delight in mathematical doctrines shrink from metaphysics, because they notice light in the former, darkness in the latter. The chief cause of this I judge to be that the general notions, and those which are believed to be best known to us, have been made ambiguous and obscure by human negligence and inconstancy of thinking, and that the definitions commonly offered are not even nominal, so little do they explain. Acta er. Lips. A. 1694,* the same I would affirm, for a similar cause, of the disciplines of the [p. 33] liberal arts. Of the disciplines, I say, and of the heaps of rules. For although the learned and unlearned everywhere, if they have sense, are compelled to admire the execution of the most beautiful works even against their will, and very many also follow and praise them, yet most patrons even of the stricter science, agreeing with the common crowd, despise the doctrines of this kind which would direct those specious miracles,

*Because they believe wit more fortunate than* / *wretched art.*

But what if, from careful definitions, and ones truly explaining something beautiful and charming, through an aptly coherent concatenation of axioms and corollaries, the problems were resolved, certainly the first ones, of cognising not only well, but also prettily? What if not only the art, having entered the general character of the beautiful wit, were set up, but also clothed in the cloak of science? More certainly and more safely, by its rule, not the Lesbian one, we should both execute and judge the things that demand the ornament of thought, § 70.

§ 76. Nor do I think I shall be a vain augur, if I foretell, what I write having already learned by experience from some examples, that by the same way the good liberal studies will commend themselves to not a few wits, and those not small, and to loftier minds, so that [p. 34] they appear, as they are, worthy not only of boys but also of men, and of wise ones, and move them to the point that either they themselves dare something new and outstanding in aesthetic exercises or at least judge more moderately, more fairly, more honourably of the arts that lead thither, and of their applications, as judges well enough instructed and more competent. § 70.

§ 77. Again I warn. I am not the one who, by the aesthetic science, as if it were complete in every respect, would feign for myself or others a wit charming in general, or at all a specifically praiseworthy orator, poet, musician, etc. I have already demanded, before a theory of this kind, nature, wit, disposition, exercises, a culture of wit now scarcely to be obtained sufficiently without some erudition, a skill in the rules of thinking beautifully, which I have only proved to be truly excellent if it is a science at least in its first and principal part. Now I demand anew those more correct and more certain exercises, of which § 58, in which no day passes without a line, without which I declare that the dead and speculative rules, as they call them, useful indeed, but which you do not use, will never profit there where they most ought to. Nor shall I thereafter demand so many more. He who is to form the special character of the happy aesthetician will rightly demand still more. § 27.

[p. 35]

#### Sectio V. Impetus Aestheticus. — Section V. Aesthetic Impetus.

§ 78. For the general character of the happy aesthetician, § 27, there is required IV. AESTHETIC IMPETUS (the beautiful arousal and inflammation of the mind, ὁρμή [impulse], ecstasy, frenzy, ἐνθουσιασμός [enthusiasm], πνεῦμα θεοῦ [the spirit of God]), whose genesis is this: a more excitable nature, S. II, already in its very αὐτοσχεδιάσμασι [improvisations], S. III, further aided by doctrines sharpening the wit and nourishing a great soul, S. IIII, according to the posture of the body and the preceding state of the soul, M. § 512, 596, with favouring circumstances, M. § 323, now strains its lower faculties, habits and powers, until now dead, M. § 220, towards the very act of thinking beautifully, to such a degree that they live greater, in the consensus of the phenomenon, than those which many others could exhibit on the same theme, or the man himself, when not so aroused at another time, and so give effects equal to these living powers of his, greater than his ordinary powers, and standing to them roughly as the square to the root. The state of the soul whose genesis I have described is that very ὁρμή [impulse], of which I earlier gave the various denominations according to the diversity of its degrees.

[p. 36] § 79. In the lesser degrees of this fervour, the effect greater than the ordinary force of the mind, as such, is not evident to all; yet if the ὁρμή [impulse] has truly been present, it will come about for one who tries, while he is cold,

*that anyone may hope* / *the same for himself, may sweat much and labour in vain* / *having dared the same.* Hor.

Its character, for one who is a just estimator of himself, which one must sometimes be seized by, will be, 1) if e.g. his own writings, while he writes and immediately after their conception, please him less than after some time spent in heterogeneous occupations: 2) the greater speed itself, which is also the character of living forces in physics.

§ 80. It is evident to psychologists that in such an impetus the whole soul indeed strains its powers, but most of all the lower faculties, so that the whole ground, as it were, of the soul, M. § 511, rises somewhat higher, and breathes something greater, and readily supplies things which we had forgotten, which we had not experienced, which we seemed unable to foresee for ourselves, much less for others. Since this ground of the soul is still unknown to many, even philosophers, its extraordinary effect, which we described in § 78, has been ascribed to the gods as its authors, according to that saying of Lucretius:

*Ignorance of causes compels them to assign things* / [p. 37] *to the sway of the gods and to concede them the kingdom, so that* / *the things whose causes they can in no way see* / *they think are brought about by divine power.*

§ 81. Let us survey some opportunities for arousal of this kind, according to the posture of the body, either to be sought out or at least seized when offered, in which it is *now or never.* I refer hither 1) the motion and agitation of the body, especially of a somewhat melancholic body, e.g. a rather swift ride on horseback. § 46. Hence perhaps Pegasus opening Hippocrene, hence so many poems written on journeys. *It is wonderful how the mind is aroused by the agitation and motion of the body.* Plin. L. I. ep. 6.

§ 82. 2) An occasion of the impetus to think charmingly is the state of the soul in which foresight and presentiment are naturally easier, M. § 602, powers especially necessary for the things to be thought elegantly, ordinarily most feeble, § 39, M. § 597, and a sound mind in a sound body, E. § 254, is inclined to imitation by the charming wit of another, § 56, 44. One aroused in this manner is Φοιβόληπτος [seized by Phoebus]. So, around the critical vicissitudes of this life, when a great change is at hand, that will have very great moment;

*the time will come* / *to demand the fates. A god, behold, a god!* § 80.

[p. 38] § 83. 3) Histories (Clio), even fictitious ones, especially heroic (Calliope), whether of sadder (Melpomene) or of happier outcome (Thalia), music (Terpsichore), dances (Erato), pictures (Polyhymnia) and any specimens of the liberal arts, while they provoke to emulation, the very nature of the things placed outside us, while it either plays amiably or sweeps us into amazement with the greater spectacles of the world, e.g. of the sun (Urania), § 82. One aroused in this manner is μουσόληπτος καὶ μουσοπάτακτος [seized and struck by the Muses]. Hence the Muses, no less than the Graces, are companions, and are not well torn apart. When one flourished, e.g. around the times of Alexander the Great, of Augustus, of Louis XIIII, the others flourished too.

§ 84. 4) Comparative leisure, E. § 267, M. § 638, when the mind, released from cares, lays down the burden of labours and business and, wandering at random through pleasant walks, § 83, § 81, lies serenely open in retirement to divine inspirations, § 80. This perhaps is to pasture on Helicon, to dream on Parnassus. *O sweet and honourable leisure, and more beautiful than almost any business! O sea! O shore! true and secret μουσεῖον [seat of the Muses]! how many things you discover! how many things you dictate!* Plin. L. I. ep. 9.

§ 85. 5) If, for one who is about to think beautifully, there is required a state [p. 39] such as I described in § 39, especially one constituted amid sensations sufficiently heterogeneous to the theme, then for a drinker of water, according to the posture of the body, § 78, a moderate draught of a more generous liquor will be opportune, by which, as the sensations, e.g. troublesome ones, somewhat relent, the necessary ones, e.g. cheerful imaginations and foresights, may become the clearer. M. § 554. Natural historians relate that there are enough springs with this virtue. Suppose there was such a power in Aganippe.

§ 86. 6) To such a spring they will prefer wine, § 85, judging with Horace

*that no poems can please for long or live* / *which are written by drinkers of water.* / *That liquor taught voices to bend in song,* § 83, / *and moved untutored limbs to fixed measures,* § 81. / *Bacchus too gave the farmer’s heart, worn out by great labour,* / *to be loosed from sadness.* § 84, 52. / *Bacchus also brings rest to afflicted mortals,* / *though their legs ring, struck with the hard fetter.* § 85. Tib.

Hence Bacchus too is an author of aesthetic arousal and inflammation, § 80, although he is unwittily deceived who, about to imitate in good and genuine faith, as if it were real, the feigned drunkenness of Horace, od. L. II. 19, III. 25, so long pours libations to Apollo and the Muses, and sacrifices to Bacchus, until, now at the very point of uttering oracles,

[p. 40] *he is laid low, and lifeless, trembling, falls prostrate on the ground — — — — .* Virg.

§ 87. 7) A new occasion exists for chaste aestheticians, who, sitting opposite, again and again, look upon and hear I know not whom sweetly laughing. Which wretchedly snatches all their senses from them. For as soon as they behold their Lesbia, nothing is left to them,

*but the tongue grows numb, a thin flame* / *runs down beneath the limbs, with their own sound* / *the ears ring, the eyes are covered* / *with twofold night.* Cat.

But when they have walked up and down the alley of the absent one, § 81, saluting the closed door and the empty windows, suddenly they remove themselves from the city to the mountains and groves, § 84, and there from afar they see their miracle of nature sweetly laughing, hear her sweetly speaking, imagine, write, sing, play, paint, the wood being the interpreter of these cares and the stars their confidants, § 83, things they would not even have dared to hope for, which they foresee with the highest pleasure, most unwillingly, § 82. Certainly Venus and Cupid are held to be the authors of the arousal of which we speak, § 78, rather on account of these than on account of the criers of brothels,

*when they rattle off filthy and shameful things,* § 54, 59.

[p. 41] § 88. Occasions perhaps truer than the gods, § 80, will be, 8.) if

*bold poverty, since he was humbled, his wings clipped, and destitute* / *of his father’s hearth and farm, has driven* / *someone to make verses,*

as it did Horace, who goes on to relate of himself in jest what very many quite seriously think most true:

*Having enough that nothing is lacking,* / *what hemlocks will ever suffice to purge me,* / *if I did not think it better to sleep than to write verses?*

§ 89. 9) Indignation

*Rage armed Archilochus with his own iambus.* Hor.

Horace forebodes the same occasion for himself, Serm. L. II. 1. v. 40–60. Juvenal breathes it right from the start:

*Shall I always be a hearer only, and never retort,* / *vexed so often by the Theseid of hoarse Codrus?* etc. / *It is difficult not to write satire. For who is so patient* / *of the unjust city, so iron-hearted, as to hold himself in?* etc. / *Why should I tell with how great a wrath my dry liver burns?* etc. / *Shall I not believe these things worthy of the Venusine lamp?* / [p. 42] *Shall I not pursue these things?* / *If nature refuses, indignation makes the verse,* / *such as it can, such as I make.* Sat. I.

So he goes on in the rest. From the same source the Philippics of Demosthenes and Cicero, the latter’s Verrines and Catilinarians, Ovid’s Ibis, etc.

§ 90. Closely akin to indignation is 10) derision, M. § 684, yet friendlier to Horace than indignation, less bitter, and having more salt than gall. Hence the αὐτοσχεδιάσματα [improvisations] of which § 52, and of the soldiers crowding about the triumphal chariots. Hence the old

*comedy, not without much* / *praise, but its liberty fell into vice and a violence* / *worthy to be governed by law.* Hor.

Hence Aristophanes, hence almost all of Lucian, and many of Martial’s epigrams.

§ 91. 11.) All commotions of the mind which are not so great that the intuition overwhelms almost all symbolic cognition, when we speak not only of things to be thought beautifully but also of things to be expressed, 12) all the sadder accidents of fortune, unless they break the mind, and the happier ones, unless they altogether blunt and distract it. § 82. Yet we owe more elegances to the adverse fates of authors than to the very smiling ones.

[p. 43] § 92. 13. Let youth close the ranks in a catalogue easily enriched, or rather that period of this life in which the soul has not yet passed its ἀκμήν [prime] so far that even the imaginations have notably declined. For just as the lower cognitive faculties are the first to become phenomena in it, so too they naturally diminish in the same order in which they grew. Whence, when the two which are to supply the more important matter for thinking beautifully, § 30, 31, are already languishing, the chief opportunity for thinking elegantly is judged to have passed. Whence Horace not only teaches,

*Be wise and unharness in good time the ageing horse, lest* / *he stumble at the last, a laughing-stock, and strain his flanks,*

but also, having been sufficiently looked at and already presented with the wooden sword, does not want to be shut up in his old school,

*laying aside both verses and other playthings.*

§ 93. It is self-evident 1) that these occasions are not to be used against the stronger laws of the blessed life, 2) that they are occasions only, not solitary causes, with which a clumsy or slow wit might be surrounded on every side without the effect that was sought, § 78.

§ 94. Since in a greater beautiful meditation the beauty of the whole is more difficult [p. 44] than the beauty of any part, and the beauty of things and of order than the beauty of signs, § 18–20, the aesthetic impetus of the mind, while the work is glowing, and its occasions, § 78–92, are to be used above all for forming the former.

§ 95. While the ἐνθουσιασμῷ [enthusiasm] lasts, while the whole soul is bent on setting out the whole and the principal parts beautifully, § 78, even a sufficiently happy wit laudably withdraws itself from hewing out the lesser elegances of the lesser parts, M. § 529, lest it become like the craftsman who

*will both express the nails* / *and imitate the soft hair in bronze,* / *unhappy in the sum of his work, because he will not know* / *how to set out the whole. If I cared to compose anything, I should* / *no more wish to be this man than to live with a crooked nose,* / *to be gazed at for my black eyes and black hair.* Hor.

[p. 45]

#### Sectio VI. Correctio Aesthetica. — Section VI. Aesthetic Correction.

§ 96. Some things to be thought beautifully must be brought forth in such a way that they admit no later cares, and must be, as it were, extemporaneous, e.g. conversations and letters that are truly familiar. In these, where the chief thing shines and the principal matters are truly beautiful, § 94, since corrections have had no place, I should be the more unfair a judge if by a few

*I were offended by blemishes, which either carelessness let fall, / Or human nature too little guarded against.* § 95.

§ 97. Some things beautifully thought we are not obliged to bring forth before we have won for them all the graces we could, since they admit later cares. In these, since every partial beauty, other things being equal, increases the beauty of the whole, M. § 185, the character of the happy aesthetician is crowned by V.) the STUDY OF CORRECTION (the labour and delay of the file), or the habit of increasing, by sustained attention to a work that has been beautifully formed, as far as one can, the lesser perfections, even those of its [p. 46] minute parts, and of removing imperfections, the smallish phenomena, without detriment to the whole, § 27. Every beautiful thought will be like a picture.

*This one,* § 96, *loves the dark; that one,* § 97, *will wish to be seen in the light,* / *Which does not fear the keen acumen of the judge;* / *This one,* § 96, *pleased once; that one,* § 97, *will please ten times repeated.* Hor.

§ 98. Since correction is easier than the beautiful elaboration of the whole, § 94, 97, the author,

*Unless, unskilled, he thinks a blot shameful and fears the erasure,* Hor.

or is offended by the labour and delay of the file, M. § 527: when the greater impetus of the mind has now passed, § 78,

*As soon as the frenzy has ceased, and the raging lips have grown quiet,* Virg.

it will be possible to carry it through, now that the intellect and reason shine forth more distinctly, and the intellectual judgement, § 38, measures each single thing also by the rules of artificial aesthetics, S. IV.

§ 99. Artificial aesthetics will direct not only the conscience, so to speak, antecedent, S. IV, and concomitant, S. V, of what has been thought beautifully, or somewhat uglily, but also the consequent one, § 98, which is still not useless as long as

[p. 47] *It will be permitted to delete, with the parchments kept at home,* / *What you have not published,* § 98.

§ 100. If someone has nevertheless neglected to apply the file where he could well have done so, § 97, the more acute take this the more unfairly, because they see that he who could do the greater thing also sufficed for the lesser, § 98, but did not reckon his spectators worth so much; and they deem it their right to repay contempt of themselves with contempt of the author, and thus, things that seemed trifles,

*will lead to serious* / *Ills, once laughed at and received with ill omen.*

§ 101. Neglect of the correction of which we speak Horace judges to be the single cause why the Romans yield to the Greeks in writing, A. P. v. 291, reproving it in Lucilius in particular, Serm. I, 10. How necessary the final file is, let Apelles teach, behind his picture; Virgil, bequeathing the Aeneid to the flames by his testament; Ovid, who excuses his neglect of it only by the ills of exile, *de Ponto* I. *ep.* V. 17, remembers that at Rome he more than once made erasures, II. *ep.* IV, and praises it in the orator himself if he *polishes* his work *with fine care,* IV. *ep.* VI; Cicero concurring, whose *orator* must not handle even *small things carelessly,* and *the polished manner of speaking* is commended by his example and precepts.

§ 102. [p. 48] If someone meanwhile has felt with Naso:

*Yet I do not emend; this labour is greater than writing,* / *And the sick mind endures to suffer nothing hard.* / *Shall I, forsooth, begin to use the file more bitingly,* / *And call each single word before judgement?*

he confounds what brings more tedium with it and what demands more strength. The former, I grant, squares with emendation, not the latter, § 98. But whatever tedium there may be must be swallowed out of the reverence which is

*Most due* to those who are to approve your work, § 100.

§ 103. There are things to which we grew accustomed as boys, and willingly also as men, namely that another, forsooth, should correct our work, and we complain that such a one is wanting to us:

*To each is assigned his own error,* / *But we do not see what is in the wallet on our back.*

I grant that the recitation of the ancients, unless this too be corrupted by flatteries, is a most salutary counsel, and that he rightly complains who lacks a good and prudent man who, asked to judge our work while it is perhaps still being kept back to the ninth year,

*May become an Aristarchus.*

Yet from a censor of that kind no more is to be demanded than that he either say: *Correct, pray, this and this,* or that

[p. 49] *he smear a black mark on the unpolished lines* / *With his pen laid crosswise.*

That he himself should suggest better things is not a fair request for one man to make of another. Each has his own way of thinking, in some things peculiar to him. If we are taken as roughly equal in strength, I and the one who recites to me, it would certainly sometimes be easier for me to finish the whole work in my own way of thinking, § 78, than, with a more relaxed mind, § 98, so to interweave another’s thoughts with mine that they are better than those poured out by the author himself in his impetus, and yet conduce to and cohere aptly with the rest, which have not proceeded from me and my way of thinking.

*If you prefer to defend a fault rather than to change it,* / *We shall spend no further word or vain effort,* / *But that without rival you alone may love yourself and your own.* Hor.

#### Sectio VII. Cautiones Quaedam. — Section VII. Certain Cautions.

§ 104. If the NATURAL MANNER OF THINKING be defined as one proportioned to the natural powers of the soul that is to think, and to the nature of the objects and of those for whose use or delight the thought undertaken is to be presumed [p. 50] to be, or more briefly as one that imitates these natures, then the natural manner of thinking is so necessary to one who is to think beautifully, § 14, that while the nature of the soul that is to think beautifully, and of many objects, is as yet too little known, the whole art of charming thought would seem to be comprehended in this single rule: *Imitate nature.*

§ 105. Hence let him who is to think beautifully beware, 1) lest he affect the praise of a charming wit when he is destitute, if not altogether, yet signally, of that nature which we described in S. II, CONTRARY TO NATURE, an ass at the lyre; 2) lest, perhaps possessing in general as much of nature as is enough, he nevertheless take upon himself to think things that require a special character and a certain peculiar bent, so to speak, of nature, before he has seen whether nature has bestowed these gifts upon his own special character too, since otherwise he will think BESIDE NATURE, a Suffenus,

*A charming man, both witty and urbane,*

whose verses, if you read them,

*Then that pretty fellow, that urbane* / *Suffenus seems one goatherd, or a ditch-digger* / *Once more; so much does he differ and change.* Cat.

Each MANNER OF THINKING is UNDERTAKEN AGAINST THE WILL OF MINERVA:

*You will say or do nothing against the will of Minerva.* Hor. Cic. Off. L. I, 110.

§ 106. [p. 51] Let him who follows the natural manner of thinking most rightly, § 104, beware lest, embracing a cloud for Juno, he embrace a *fat* and *gross Minerva*,

*A rustic, a sage against the rule,*

setting down his own not so much juvenile as puerile productions, if any trace of a nature not to be despised, S. II, shines through in them, as already finished in every number, S. III, or even, content with a rude wit for his whole life, in place of the natural, which is to be praised, follow the RUDE MANNER OF THINKING, to whose nature, often middling enough, nothing of doctrine, nothing of art has been added. S. IV.

§ 107. The rude manner of thinking becomes a sufficiently deformed phenomenon in two ways, § 14: 1) in the UNSKILLED (unlearned, unerudite) MANNER OF THINKING, when either there is a graver offence against that degree of beautiful erudition which we require in general of everyone who dares to know more than the common people, or it appears in particular that someone has wished to bring forth his thoughts, as if they were charming, about things whose nature he could have known more thoroughly even with middling learning, § 104, and yet is ignorant about them of whatever

Not every *buyer of roasted chickpea* and *nut* may know. § 66, 67.

§ 108. [p. 52] The rude manner of thinking is, 2) the INERT MANNER OF THINKING, betraying again either, in general, ignorance of the well-founded rules of thinking elegantly, or, in particular, of that peculiar art of which you nevertheless profess to give a specimen, § 106, 68, 69, or else neglect of them by exceptions in which there is nothing of elegance, § 25, 72.

§ 109. Of this twofold fault, § 107, 108, the twofold

*Flight from blame leads into vice, when it lacks the art*

that truly and beautifully supplements nature, S. IV. The first is the SHADOWY and scholastic MANNER OF THINKING, when, in those things which require a beautiful way of thinking, and therefore must imitate and follow nature, and also a second nature, the custom of the more elegant kind of life among the better-mannered, § 104, either so much of the more recondite erudition is crammed in that beauty ceases to be, § 15, or things that altogether counterfeit the mere appearance of erudition, and that only among boys and some grammarians, are heaped together.

§ 110. The second is the AFFECTED and forced MANNER OF THINKING, which badly supplements nature by art, when *arts*, I know not which, [p. 53] *by an excessive affectation of subtlety break and cut to pieces*, as Quintilian teaches, *whatever is* of a *nobler* kind in the elegances of meditations. This plague, which kills the natural manner of thinking, § 105, shows itself 1) when the natural and uncosmeticised comeliness alone would have sufficed, and yet the pigments of some art or other are called in as a superfluous, and here harmful, aid; 2) where someone has been unhappy enough to fall into the bad rules of a deceiving art; 3) where the rules of a good art are applied where they have a place, but ineptly, § 105. E. § 336.

§ 111. Others, rightly following the natural manner of thinking, § 104, confuse with nature its ordinary state and its powers, advanced indeed by some doctrine, S. IV, yet dead and slack, from which, since they are alive by no impetus and no inflammation, arises the LANGUID (cold, somnolent, yawning) MANNER OF THINKING, contrary to S. V.

§ 112. *While fools avoid vices, they run into their contraries*, and, wishing to guard against a jejune languor in thinking, they fall into parenthyrsus and the UNBRIDLED MANNER OF THINKING, which, having shaken off not only the yoke of intellect and reason but neglectful also of purer taste, without any [p. 54] reason for the phenomenon that would roughly suffice, displays so great a frenzy that it seems to imitate nature alone, but the nature of an unsound head, § 104. To the derision of such a madman Horace dedicated the epilogue of his art:

*Surely he rages, and like a bear,* / *If he has been strong enough to break the bars set across his cage,* / *He puts to flight the unlearned and the learned alike.*

§ 113. Just as lazy philosophers and physicians sometimes choose nature, like a god from the machine, as the asylum of their idleness, there are also those who call natural the MANNER OF THINKING which, if not altogether rude, unskilled and inert, § 106–108, is yet unkempt and UNCOMPOSED, when not only in the very impetus of the mind, of which S. V, do you hasten not slowly enough, and adopt whatever occurs first as the best, but afterwards too you either never go back over what you poured out quickly enough with a mind to chastise it, or not without the fond indulgence of parents, by which they scarcely touch whatever vice ought to be rooted out, fearing lest they destroy the nature of their own. This bad manner of thinking is to be guarded against, according to S. VI, by the POLISHED MANNER OF THINKING.

§ 114. Yet no more is the FILE commended that [p. 55] WEARS AWAY rather than polishes, an excess in correcting, which either, on the offspring of wit that are demanded sudden and extemporaneous, spends so much study and diligence that, smelling of the lamp, they do not have the beautiful verisimilitude of improvisations, § 96; or, prodigal of the impetus given for greater things, delays or dissipates it on trifles, contrary to § 95; or so goes back over one’s own work, when *every movement of the mind*, *like a wind*, *has deserted* the thinker, *and all the ardour has settled*, that the things first poured out not without some force *grow flaccid*, and, some being rejected and others, as more ornate, added, all the incitement and, as it were, *flame* of the things to be thought beautifully *is extinguished* at the same time, § 111. A file of this kind prefers a lesser elegance to a greater by an inelegant exception, § 25, because

*in one who pursues smooth things the sinews* / *And the spirit fail.* Hor.

#### Sectio VIII. Ubertas Aesthetica. — Section VIII. Aesthetic Richness.

§ 115. If anyone who is partly already instructed by the things enumerated in S. II–VI, and partly not averse to them, a future aesthetician happy enough, § 27, should ask for further counsel about thinking beautifully in general, [p. 56] § 17, and especially about the beauty of things, § 18,

*I shall serve in the place of a whetstone, which can make* / *Iron sharp, though itself without a share in cutting.* / *The task and the duty, writing nothing myself, I shall teach:* / 1) *Whence resources are to be procured.* Hor.

The first care, namely, in the things to be thought, should be for RICHNESS (abundance, plenty, multitude, riches, resources), M. § 575, but AESTHETIC richness, by which a given subject, a certain person about to think, can think many things beautifully about a given object, a certain matter for thought, § 22.

§ 116. Thus Pliny begins the praises of Isaeus: *Supreme is his facility, his richness, his abundance.* L. II. *ep.* 3. Let us divide it first into ABSOLUTE richness, necessary for all beautiful thinking, § 115, 22, and RELATIVE richness, that degree of the absolute which happens to be necessary for certain things to be thought beautifully, e.g. for things less known, or very useful, which must nevertheless be inculcated upon a less attentive person, and which we may call *Platonic breadth*, following the same Pliny, when he calls all of Euphrates’ *discourse copious*, § 115, and yet affirms of the same man that he *frequently reproduces that Platonic breadth*, L. I. *ep.* X.

§ 117. [p. 57] Even if he who is to meditate beautifully should sometimes not wish to pursue comparative breadth, § 116, nevertheless a truly overflowing abundance of matter displays it elegantly enough, as if on a heaped dish, as from afar, lest the spectator think that the matter has been exhausted by things afterwards to be shown more closely, § 115. Thus Virgil:

*Not if I had a hundred tongues, and a hundred mouths,* / *A voice of iron,* etc.

Thus Cicero: *No one has so great a stream of genius, no one so great a force and so great an* abundance *of speaking or writing, that could, I will not say adorn, but recount,* etc.

§ 118. AESTHETIC RICHNESS, further, is either OBJECTIVE (of things, material), insofar as in the objects and the things to be thought themselves lies the chief reason why they can be richly painted by the powers of human wit, or SUBJECTIVE (of wit and person), the physical possibility and the power of a certain man, even in a certain respect and hypothetically such, to represent a certain thing richly. There are objects that offer their riches, as it were, of their own accord. Scarcely do they come to mind, when

*there appears blessed* Abundance *with her full* / *horn.* Hor.

Then, if only you have as much capacity as is enough, not to others only, but also

[p. 58] *to you abundance* / *Will flow to the full from her kindly* / *Horn, rich in the honours of the countryside.* Hor.

#### Sectio VIIII. Ubertas Materiae. — Section VIIII. Richness of Matter.

§ 119. The HORIZON (sphere) OF HUMAN COGNITION is the finite number of matters, out of the infinite universe of things, which can become clear to a middling human wit in the broader sense; those of these which can be conceived more perfectly by a middlingly philosophical wit constitute the LOGICAL HORIZON (territory and sphere) of reason and intellect; those which can shine beautifully for a middlingly aesthetic wit constitute the AESTHETIC HORIZON (territory and sphere of the beautiful of the analogue of reason). If there are things which

*You despair can shine when handled, leave them.* § 115.

§ 120. PLACED BENEATH THE AESTHETIC HORIZON are those things which a middlingly aesthetic wit cannot draw forth from comparative and relative obscurity [p. 59] into beautiful light. If he who is to think beautifully take such things as his themes, either this will be done in earnest, and there will be born a POVERTY OF MATTER and a *thinness of meditation*, contrary to § 115, 119, or he will slip into irrelevancies, or he will jest dangerously. Hence let there be a theme of this kind,

A theme *undiscovered, and better placed thus,* / *While the earth hides it, to be the stronger in scorning it.* Hor.

Let the *wondrous encomia of wondrous things* be compared.

§ 121. *Songs may even draw down the moon from heaven*, yet I would not advise him who is to think beautifully to choose for himself as matter THINGS PLACED ABOVE THE AESTHETIC HORIZON, nor do I here understand only, or chiefly, those which altogether transcend the horizon of human cognition, § 119, but above all those which a middlingly aesthetic wit cannot draw down from the superior, distinct and adequate cognition of the sciences into that beautiful light which, rosy, may please, and not dazzle the eyes of the analogue of reason and of the lower faculties of cognition. If you try nonetheless, you will either fall into the inconveniences mentioned in § 120, or expose to jests what you think most grave, or *be entangled in those narrow and concise disputations* which Antonius in Cicero, de Or. L. II, 61, casts in the teeth of the Greek *philosophers* in general, [p. 60] not without the solemn formula: *I understand no word at all.* Against which § 22.

§ 122. Shall I, then, reject and reprove the concise manner of thinking, to which I have sacrificed so many ambitious ornaments of my philosophical writings, and which I myself now follow, though a little more loosely? Would that the truth already discovered by Zeno the Stoic, which Cicero’s Orator repeats, 113, 114, and Quintilian II, 21, might at least in our times become more evident: that there are two manners of thinking, the one *continuous and broader, which is that of rhetoric*, the other *concise and more contracted, which is that of dialectic*. The former I hold proper to the aesthetic horizon, the latter to the logical, § 119. The comparison of *the hand pressed into a fist* and *opened out* I do not make my own. It is enough that the concise manner of thinking, which is especially necessary in scientific matters, above all acroamatic ones, as Aristotle, surely an aesthetician, theoretical as well as practical, already saw, can be a vice in an alien place and outside the circles of reason, and that if in those things which require copious discourse some *raise themselves by frequent and small efforts* into the logical horizon, they are *only uneven and, as it were, rugged, and neither attain the admiration of eminences, nor keep the grace of level places.* VIII, 5. *One must not use the rational* virtue and faculty *so minutely and concisely in* aesthetic matters [p. 61] *as in* the logical *disputations* of the sciences, XII, 2.

§ 123. Many matters can be common to the aesthetic and the logical horizon, § 119; hence, because something is treated in the sciences, the same is not therefore excluded altogether from the sphere of beauty, but only insofar as it would be conceived point by point and minutely, with philosophical and mathematical accuracy, § 121. But insofar as the same matter can be thought either itself by the beautiful intellect, M. § 637, while its marks are thought sensately and vividly, or itself too, now at least, only vividly and charmingly, it is received within the aesthetic horizon.

§ 124. *What is good, apart from the honourable? Are the senses true? What is the magnitude of the sun? Questions* of this kind Cicero *thinks that all easily understand to be far removed from the person of the orator. For in those matters,* he says, *on which we understand the highest geniuses of the philosophers to have been spent with the greatest labour, to assign these, like some small things, to the orator seems great madness.* He rightly saw that there are certain things in the sciences above the aesthetic horizon, § 121, which the orator, like everyone who is to meditate beautifully, would be wrong to choose as his themes, § 122. But the questions [p. 62] mentioned by way of example, if not the orator, at least the practical aesthetician could, I think, recount elegantly enough, provided he followed a different way of thinking about them from the ethical, logical, physical and mathematical demonstrator, § 123.

§ 125. It is not, indeed, about all things that the abundance for thinking beautifully is given, § 119–121, and *the full and perfect orator, who can speak about all things variously and copiously,* Cic. de Or. I, 59, together with the Stoic sage,

*I bid go and weep among the chairs of the pupils:*
