yet neither does the faculty of thinking charmingly lie only in civil controversies or other questions of lighter moment, but in the very 1) dogmas of the disciplines and sciences too, theological, physical, practical, historical, etc., regarded materially, when you so think them, and signify them, *that you seem to have wished to be familiar to those who are not the most erudite,* ib. II, 61; 2) in illustrations to be interspersed in the manner of scholia, § 123, cf. Cic. de Or. I, 61.

§ 126. The first theologians were singers of theogonies, to whom

*The Muse gave* to recount *on the strings the gods, and the sons of gods.* Hor.

[p. 63] *Long-haired Iopas* / *Sounds on his gilded lyre what mightiest Atlas taught.* / *He sings the wandering moon, and the labours of the sun,* / *Whence the race of men and the beasts, whence rain and fire,* etc. / *The Tyrians redouble their applause, and the Trojans follow.* Virg. Aen. I. 745.

*The Socratic pages will be able to show you the matter.* / *He who has learned what he owes his country, and what his friends,* / *With what love a parent, with what a brother and a guest is to be loved,* / *What is the duty of a senator, what of a judge, what* / *The part of a general sent to war,*

will have whence he may restore the name and honour of a once charming wisdom, if he knows how to accommodate the gravest matters to the common and popular sense, § 123, and thus

*to soothe tigers and raging lions,* / *To move stones by the sound of the lyre, and by gentle entreaty,* / *To separate public from private, sacred from profane,* / *To forbid promiscuous union, to give laws to the married,* / *To build towns, to carve laws on wood.* Hor.

*But history, the witness of the times, the light of truth, the life of memory, the mistress of life, the messenger of antiquity,* [p. 64] how elegantly it can be *commended to immortality,* § 125, let the single manner of thinking of Livy teach, *diffuse and drawn out, and flowing on with a certain even-tenored smoothness,* cf. Cic. de or. L. II, 36, 64.

§ 127. Placed within the aesthetic horizon are, besides the things it has in common with the logical horizon, § 121–126, those things about which a middlingly aesthetic wit is given to think more, and more beautifully, which nevertheless either the powers of men do not allow to be seen through with scientific and more exact cares, § 120, or which do not seem worth the effort to one busy with his own affairs, § 119. For example, let it be permitted

*Hither to bring the victorious boxer, and the horse first in the race,* / *And the cares of young men, and wine that makes free.*

§ 128. The dynamic aesthetician who is to explore whether a certain matter will be rich enough will be taught either by the trials of others, § 60, 61, or by his own, while he tests his powers, as it were, on the theme when it first comes to mind, either by nature alone, S. II, exercised somewhat in thinking beautifully, S. III, or aided by art, S. IV. If he feels himself borne by a greater impetus of mind towards a certain theme, and nevertheless experiences I know not what poverty of matter and a jejune thinness in the very forming of the [p. 65] work, § 120, or if all things, dry indeed and genuine, present themselves to his mind minutely and rather concisely, let him not hesitate to abandon it, since, whether it lies in the things or in himself, there is now certainly a weightier reason why one must despair of his faculty of carrying it out elegantly, S. V and § 119.

§ 129. The ART to be commended in these trials is twofold: 1) the ANALOGICAL, by which from one thing beautifully known, through the principle of reduction, the same (the similar, the equal, the congruent) is partially gathered in another. On this depends all manly imitation, and whatever there is anywhere of parodies. By this Virgil followed Theocritus in the eclogues, Homer in the Aeneid, Terence Menander, etc. And I do not deny that I have found it most useful. Lest you fear too much the irregularly appointed censors of plagiarism, behold the edict of a judge surely competent:

*Public matter will become private property,* Hor.

Yet do not neglect the same judge’s definitions:

*You will not take care to render word for word.* / *Nor will you, as an imitator, leap down into a narrow place* / *Whence shame forbids you to draw back your foot, or the law of the work.*

[p. 66]

#### Sectio X. Topica. — Section X. Topics.

§ 130. 2) TOPICS or TOPOLOGY, which is defined as the art or discipline of finding arguments, § 26, Cic. top. 2. Cicero, saluting it as the *art of finding*, l. c. 6, and Aristotle too, handing it down in many words, have established it, as it were, by their authority. Meanwhile, if the truth is to be told, it is not so much heuristic as the art of recalling to memory the predicates of a certain subject according to a certain order of the notions associated with the subject.

§ 131. The doctrine of topics can be divided into the LOGICAL, which is to supply arguments chiefly to intellectual cognition, § 130, and the AESTHETIC, which is to promote sensate cognition, § 17. The logical will again be either ANALYTIC, an aid for lighting upon questions which you then strive to demonstrate and to render completely certain from the completely certain, or DIALECTIC, an aid for procuring for oneself some stuff and abundance of notions and preconceptions about a given probable matter, whence one may gather a preponderance of the reasons for deciding over the reasons [p. 67] for doubting. This Aristotle took upon himself to cultivate, yet in such a way that many things bearing on it can conveniently be applied not only to analytic but also to aesthetic topics, § 130.

§ 132. A notion to be joined to the subject you take up for meditation, in order to make trial whether it can become an argument for you—either in the wider sense, one that enriches, or ennobles, or illustrates, or moves, § 26, or in the stricter sense, one that proves, or persuades, § 131—a part of that series whose order you have committed to memory, is a TOPIC, or seat of an argument. Such topics are recommended either as UNIVERSAL, to be fitted to all questions, to all themes, to be combined successively with any matter whatever on which you wish to meditate; or as PARTICULAR, proper and peculiar only to certain themes of a certain kind, and differing according to the disciplines, e.g., to which a given theme might be referred, § 131.

§ 133. A formula of universal topics of this kind, bent a little towards the natural manner of thinking, is the verse known even to schoolboys:

*Who? with what aids? what? how? / why? where, when?*

[p. 68] Another, once held in higher esteem and treated by the logicians too, § 132, is presented by the ten categories, whether Aristotle was the first to coordinate them or only made more celebrated what others had already constructed. A new one, that was to be of service even to the sciences, Lull promised with great fanfare; reading even just what S. R. Brucker’s hist. philos. crit. T. IV. P. I. relates about it on pp. 16–21, you will scarcely be able to restrain yourself from applying to that futile art the saying of Martial:

*It is shameful to make hard work of trifles, / And foolish is the labour spent on follies.*

Another was followed by Ramus, an imitator of Cicero even there, where the person of the orator does not suffice, § 119. From this the causal method has prevailed through all the ranks of the learned.

§ 134. By this reckoning, § 132, you could run through, in any subject whatever, I. the highest genus, II. the intermediate ones, III. the lowest, IV. the species, V. the individuals; in any subject whatever, I. the essence and the essentials, II. the proper and common attributes, III. the modes, IV. the respects and relations. Nor is there need of a new art, if anyone will take delight in aids of this kind, or has thought that they would be of service to him. Commit to memory some outline of the ontological notions, comprising their principal definitions, and you will have catholic topics, § 132.

[p. 69] § 135. Particular topics will be supplied by any discipline sufficiently completely known, if you fix deep in your memory the weightier points, comprised in a brief summary, which are to be attended to through it and in its objects, and against their series you can successively hold up the themes that belong more nearly to it, so as to explore their riches somewhat and thereafter to cast up your own accounts. Beautiful erudition will serve here, § 66. Singular themes and ideas have at least such marks that must chiefly be attended to, and predicates, with which you may not without profit shake out the pigeon-holes of some discipline most akin to them; or else the numerical difference of such ideas, known for the most part, will take the place of commonplaces. Where this too is lacking, do not take an idea of this kind as your theme without necessity, § 115.

§ 136. For my part, ever since I left behind the exercises of boyhood, whenever I have ventured in meditation to compose anything with a view to the requirements of elegance rather than to the exactness of the logicians and the rigour of demonstration, § 119, I do not remember ever having felt the want of, or employed, or found by experience so singularly [p. 70] useful, any topics—whether you prefer the universal ones, § 132, or certain particular ones, § 135—for procuring for myself at last some abundance and richness of matter to be worked out. If nevertheless I may be allowed to interpose my judgement about them, it seems to me that here too a distinction must be drawn between practice-bouts, ventures in copious thinking undertaken chiefly for the sake of exercise, and the real fight, works to be published, or at least exhibited to many, as something finished and complete, the work of a wit already moderately exercised in other matters, S. III.

§ 137. In exercises, such as I have described in § 136, I unhesitatingly choose, for a good many themes set within the aesthetic horizon, § 119, a more frequent and diligently sustained reflection according to any topics whatever, especially the catholic ones, § 132, since it is better that the faculty of copious thinking be exercised in any manner whatever, other things being equal, than that it should either not be acquired in greater measure at all, or, scarcely cultivated a little, grow numb and as it were dry up, § 48. The more perfect they are, the more convenient for the uses of exercise and for the memory, the more certainly will some topics among the universal ones be recommended in preference to others, § 133, 134; nor yet would I dissuade anyone from following even Lull’s own art a few times, provided it be done not without some awareness of its signal blemishes, § 133, 49.

§ 138. The exercises of which § 136 speaks, but those now more correct and more sure, of which § 58 speaks, and much [p. 71] more the meditations no longer instituted chiefly for the sake of exercise, § 136, think it rightly said to them:

*You will not linger about the cheap and open round, / What does not serve the design and fit it aptly*

you will cut away, even if it has come to mind most forcibly, § 18. In these, therefore, one mindful of the part imposed upon him, easily foreseeing that from the catholic topics, § 132, which fit all things, scarcely and not even scarcely anything can be hoped for except predicates for his theme that are most universal and most common, that will exhibit roundabout ways fetched from too far and too high and more trite than is right, or that will procure for the meditation only a perfection, § 15, set beyond the aesthetic horizon, § 121, will care less to scrape together with the aid of these topics a hyle that will be of little or no service to charming thought, § 132.

§ 139. Truth bids us judge more mildly of particular topics, § 132. For although their follower too may fall into the fault of which § 138 speaks, or into an affected and forced manner of thinking, § 110, yet these rocks can be beautifully avoided by one who will nonetheless carry off a pretty abundance from some discipline, compared with his theme as with a votive tablet, § 135. I shall now [p. 72] not dwell on foreign examples. The mnemonic verse, § 22, will, I think, usefully take the place of aesthetic topics, § 131, for those who have procured for themselves, either through this theory on which I am meditating or from elsewhere, a tolerably ample acquaintance with the single words it exhibits, and who, with a wit moderately happy, according to S. II–V, take upon themselves to shake out, by prejudicial questions as it were, some theme not to be rejected at the very first sight, § 120, 121, to see whether it will supply them largely and liberally with matter apt for elegant thinking, § 118, 128.

§ 140. Let us add another formula of aesthetic topics, § 131. The more numerous and the more perfect the lower faculties of the soul, the more, the greater, the more coherent the things they will be able to contribute, under the rule of the intellect and of reason, to thinking a given theme beautifully, the more apt will that theme be for beautiful meditation, § 22, M. § 185. Hence he who has the mind which we described in § 128, 139, once he has become a psychologist, which for many reasons I judge to be eminently necessary, should run through his theme, to be examined prejudicially, so to speak, against some sufficiently complete catalogue of the sensate faculties of the soul, with roughly these questions: Have I often sensed the given theme, have I sensed many things in it? things worthy enough? verisimilar? [p. 73] in such a light that I can present them vividly enough? etc., § 139. Can I still sense it? May it be imagined? many things in it? etc. Do I know many things similar, equal, congruent, proportionate to it? The rest psychology will suggest, e.g. M. P. III. S. II–XVII, and even its own synopsis of aesthetic matters, § 131, which, like the synopsis of ontological matters, will take the place of catholic topics, § 134, and is most easily enriched from the treatment itself, if one should please to call in here what seems

*a gift not granted for these uses*, § 119, 123.

§ 141. If, while you are occupied with preliminary collections of this kind, instituted according to any topical art whatever, § 130–140, that impetus and incitement of the mind of which S. V speaks seizes you, then, judging that the skirmishes have already profited you enough and more than enough, cast aside whatever there is of topics anywhere, § 132; let the works of the topics hang interrupted, and you, hastening to the outcome and, as if snatched, into the midst of things no otherwise than if they were known, attack the whole, lay down the head, shape the principal parts of the future work,

since *the winds call the sails, / Spread the whole bosom,*

lest, while you ingloriously weave your topical web to the end,

*nor hear the Zephyrs breathing favourably,*

[p. 74] you lose a most important matter by delaying, and let slip the occasion, in place of preparatory labours, of excellently finishing the weightiest part of the work itself, § 95; had you diligently used that opportunity,

*Jupiter, propitious, would at once / Have lighted upon both feet.* Cat.

#### Sectio XI. Argumenta Locupletantia. — Section XI. Enriching Arguments.

§ 142. I have already defined argument in the wider sense, and introduced mention of enriching arguments, at the same time showing that the same classes can conveniently be assigned to the figures of thought as to arguments, § 26. Now we may easily gather that the greater the force of an argument, the greater and better it will itself be; but if it contributes, not a little elegantly, not only to the richness of another thing, e.g. of the primary theme, but at the same time to its weight and gravity, etc., then a greater force and strength must be attributed to it. According to this scale of intension, therefore, that argument and figure will be most beautiful which at once make the cognition of another thing, e.g. of the primary theme, both richer and weightier and truer and clearer and more certain and more ardent, § 22.

[p. 75] § 143. Although the efficacy of arguments and figures can be manifold at once, they can nevertheless be referred to one of the classes mentioned, the denomination being taken either from their predominant force, or from that force whose end we are now chiefly attending to. Hence arguments and figures are for me now enriching ones when either their only force, which I should least approve, or their principal force, which I should approve more, or at least the force now to be attended to, is to contribute something to the abundance and riches of the whole work that is to be thought prettily, although we shall afterwards find the same arguments no less favourable to the remaining graces of thoughts, § 22, which I should approve most of all, § 142.

§ 144. Any topics whatever, at least in the first signification, § 143, will give enriching arguments to a moderately well-furnished wit, § 130–140. Those I hinted at in § 139, 140, are merely such only in their first places and in the divisions of places; in the remaining ones, however, they will set before the eyes of the one meditating, besides abundance, another grace at the same time, or several altogether, according to their order, § 143. Hence the two last kinds of topics will at once join to the simultaneous act of invention, or rather of recollection, that successive act of judgement, selecting from the confused hyle and forest whether and to what end something can be employed, which the others demand, § 130.

[p. 76] § 145. Among the figures of signification, e.g. of diction, there are some that are themselves related to this end, in the ordinary way indeed, § 26, whose force and elegance, however, if anyone uses them as a man should, show themselves in the things and thoughts themselves, on account of which, when rightly placed, they earn a place among the figures of thought. I shall adduce examples of the figures of diction, as they are called, since their names are better known, and indeed some of them which you might call enriching. Pleonasm seems to have expressed something idle, already sufficiently said by the accompanying words; nor is it a figure, but a fault, unless what was deemed idle be found, by an acute judge at least, quite busy towards a fuller cognition of the thing signified, § 143.

§ 146. Total synonymy is not a figure; partial synonymy is, if through the differences of the concepts adhering to each synonym the thing signified is presented at least more copiously than could have been done through one of the partial synonyms. The reckoning is the same for exergasia in PHRASES, or several words already indeed somewhat constructed among themselves, yet not constituting a period. Synthesis, or rather synesis, and *compositio*, when it joins words against syntax, as it seems, and hence compels one to think, besides those expressly set down, their partial synonyms, which syntax would favour—if it is a figure, [p. 77] it is a synonymy that is a figure, a cryptic one. Ellipsis itself belongs here, if the reader or hearer detects the gap in the words and,

*thinking better what lies hidden,*

himself supplies to himself far more things than could have been expressed in three words, § 145.

§ 147. Hypallage, surely, will not be a figure unless the sense of the words, in which the tongue seemed to outrun the mind, together with that sense which is either added as it were by corrections, or left to the reader and hearer as it were to be corrected, presents the thing at least more fully and roundly than could have been done without the affected hypallage, § 145, 143. Homoeoteleuton, a yoke intolerable to some of our native poets, there are others who say contributes much to the richness, abundance and riches of thoughts, so that it is not far from being counted among the topics, § 130. Periphrasis, in elegantly attaining other ends, can laudably have this end too, so that, if you foresee that by one word only one concept would be aroused about the thing, you arouse about the same thing, in place of one, as many partial concepts as there are words you substitute for the one word by periphrasis, § 145.

§ 148. Of the figures which are also commonly counted among [p. 78] the figures of thought, § 145, I shall name only one, as an example, among the enriching ones: praeteritio, which, if you know well how to use it, or even its crypsis, synathroismus, you attain not only this, that you can say many things, and yet briefly, § 143, but at the same time that the spectator, observing what lavish furnishings you pass over dry-shod as if neglecting them, says silently as it were to himself, § 117 (which I too would wish said of any truly beautiful thing which nature has granted us):

*The matter abounds in such great abundance / That the workman fails the work, not the work the workman,* Phaedr.

#### Sectio XII. Ubertas Ingenii. — Section XII. Richness of Wit.

§ 149. Let the thing which I might perhaps take for my theme be most rich, even aesthetically, § 115; yet I do not fix my eyes on it alone, but prudently also upon myself

*I look back, and survey what abundance there is around me,* Virg.

If the mind inclines to think something elegantly, the question is also raised about subjective richness, § 118. Just as [p. 79] in optics, in aesthetics too everyone has his own horizon, and something may be within the aesthetic horizon of many which nevertheless is not within mine, but either transcends it, or is pressed down beneath it, § 119–121, or at least is outside it. Just as in geography the apparent horizon is now wider, now narrower, so my aesthetic horizon can be contracted, can be dilated. There are wits for which sometimes

*The expanse of heaven is distant no more than three ells.*

§ 150. It is among the first rules of charming thinking, § 149, *to take up a matter equal* to one’s *strength,*

*And to turn over long what the shoulders refuse to bear, / What they are able to bear.*

Hence to the requisites of a person who will think beautifully belong not only the things recalled in S. II–VI, but also, in particular, that his strength too be sufficient for thinking copiously etc., § 22, about the given matter, and that on the given occasion, at the given time and place.

§ 151. Now the special character of aestheticians of a certain given kind must also be examined, § 27, lest you put on a part which you were neither born nor made to sustain, § 150. I. Does your genius, [p. 80] though otherwise well enough born, favour you also for prettily painting the given matter of a certain species? e.g. S. II, § 105.

*Dare / To tell the deeds of unconquered Caesar, to bear away many / Rewards for your labours. I would, best of fathers, but my strength / Fails. For not everyone describes the ranks bristling / With javelins, nor the Gauls perishing with broken spear, / Or the wounds of the Parthian slipping from his horse.* Hor. Serm. l. 1. 10.

§ 152. II. Have you long since been prepared by exercises, and by a long hand, for the special manner of pretty thinking to which the given theme belongs, and do you still have at hand the dexterity once obtained through them, in as great a measure as will suffice? S. III. I have found poets, even born ones, less apt for Latin poetry, who were going to lay the first rudiments of the art of writing a poem in this language only at the universities. *By speaking, men commonly bring it about that they speak. But truly this too is said,* that, if the exercise of *speaking more readily and more accurately, with time taken for thinking,* is interrupted, *men most easily achieve speaking perversely by speaking perversely,* though formerly not badly versed in speaking. Cic. de Or. l. 150.

[p. 81] *Often, as a boy, I remember / Laying the long suns to rest with singing. / Now so many songs are forgotten by me. His very voice too / Now flees Moeris himself.* Virg.

§ 153. III. Are you moderately instructed in those parts of beautiful erudition to which the theme you have chosen or are to choose more nearly belongs, do you know the special art to whose rules your peculiar work is to be measured and composed? § 67, 69, S. IV. It is rightly said that anyone speaks most shamefully of the things he does not know. But if it is not true, *what Socrates used to say, that all men are eloquent enough in what they know, this,* assuredly, *is truer, that no one can be fluent in what he does not know.* Cic. de Or. I. 63. *For what is so mad as the empty sound of words, even the best and most ornate, with no thought underlying, nor knowledge?* l. c. 51.

§ 154. *If teaching advances the inborn force, / And right culture strengthens the breast,* § 153,

then an unskilled manner of thinking does not smear a blot upon mistress nature, § 151, *and upon diligence,* § 152, *by which one virtue all the remaining virtues are contained,* Cic. de Or. II. 150, § 107, nor does an inert manner of thinking press the same

[p. 82] *With the shameful charge of an art unknown,* § 108. */ Not every judge sees unmusical poems. / Shall I therefore wander? shall I write licentiously? or shall I think / That all will see my faults? Safe and cautious within / The hope of pardon, I have at last avoided fault, / I have not earned praise.*

§ 155. IV. Are you now *placed in that part of human affairs* in which you can at all expect that impetus and incitement of the mind without which nothing excellent is done; do you definitely feel that inflammation of the soul, or is it granted you to hope for it, of such a kind and so great, intensively, extensively and protensively, as your given theme, or the theme you are to take up, will have demanded? S. V.

*It is the judge’s office to inquire into the times of things, as into the things themselves. / With the time inquired into, you will be safe. / Poems demand the retirement of the writer and leisure,* § 84. */ Me the sea, me the winds, me the fierce winter tosses.* Ou. trist. l. 1.

*Arms in weighty measure and violent wars I was preparing / To publish, with matter suiting the metre. / The lower verse was equal. Cupid is said to have laughed,* § 87, */ [p. 83] And to have stolen away one foot.* Ou. Am. l. 1.

*Phoebus, when I wished to tell of battles / And conquered cities, rebuked me with his lyre,* § 82, */ Lest over the Tyrrhenian sea / I should spread my small sails.* Hor. od. IV. 15.

§ 156. V. Will you now have leisure and room enough, not only for weaving to the end the web which you perhaps begin with great impetus of mind, lest this fall upon you:

*An amphora began / To be formed; as the wheel runs, why does a pitcher come out?* Hor.

but also for the file, so commendable? S. VI. Which, however, I would wish said only of those offspring of wit which someone thinks of exposing to the public light, and yet not as extemporaneous ones, § 96, because

Then *of work too hasty and lacking in care*

you could rightly be charged, as Lucilius was by Horace,

*Garrulous and too lazy to bear the labour of writing, / Of writing rightly; for as to writing much, I care nothing.* etc. Hor. Serm. l. 4, 12.

§ 157. In general, § 152–156 have been set out only so that you may see what you must chiefly [p. 84] think about, § 151, 118; not, however, that you should thence *indulge the causes of idleness,* and, parading now this, now that defect, always have *something on account of which* you may *forgive* yourself for venturing nothing at all, Quint. X. 3. Choosing for yourself themes set beneath the aesthetic horizon, whether the universal one, § 120, or your own, you would wrongly raise waves in a ladle, § 150. Pursuing themes set above the aesthetic horizon, whether the universal one, § 121, or your own, you would wrongly become an imitator of Icarus, § 150, who

*by Daedalus’ art on waxen / Wings strives, about to give / His name to the glassy sea.*

Beware of both blots in such a way that you neither *cheat your studies by endless delay, which, when it exceeds measure, is to be feared lest it take the name of sloth and idleness or even of timidity.* Plin. II. ep. 10.

#### Sectio XIII. Absoluta Breuitas. — Section XIII. Absolute Brevity.

§ 158. If riches, both of things, S. VIIII, and of the person, S. XII, flow in abundance, do not now applaud yourself, as in Terence Pamphilus does himself:

[p. 85] *Who is more fortunate than I, and indeed fuller of charm?*

From great heaps of this kind, and from the copious forest of things to be thought, 1) those things, so many and so great, are to be taken for your uses from which something complete, in relation to the end you have set yourself, can be composed and brought about. I do not demand the completeness which the logician would require in definitions, or in philosophico-mathematical problems, but that in which nothing is shamefully lacking, nothing becomes a phenomenon of insufficiency with respect to the end, nothing of what belongs to it is missed, unless perhaps through the sole force of the intellect, § 16. The MANNER OF THINKING that has attained this will be FULL AND PERFECT. Every well-filled manner of thinking will indeed be heaped up and copious too; yet a thought about some thing can be crammed and heaped up and be neither full nor perfect, which last is the highest point of aesthetic richness, § 115.

§ 159. Let a THOUGHT be vast, let it be diffuse: unless at the same time it seems complete in all its parts and entire, if it allows a fair judge of aesthetic matters, one acquainted with the things, to miss something, if it omits something which, with beauty preserved, cannot be absent, let it be deemed in some measure MAIMED, and begun rather than perfect. Here belongs the GAPING AND ABRUPT MANNER [p. 86] OF THINKING, teeming with aesthetically illegitimate leaps, § 158, Quint. IV. 2.

§ 160. 2) All those things which can be altogether absent while the degree of beauty is preserved are to be left aside, or even, if first seized upon as they came to hand, are to be thrown out again, because here superfluous distracting things do harm, M. § 638. This is laudable ABSOLUTE BREVITY. For not only

*Let it be brief, what you shamefully dare,* Iuu.

but also, if anything is done sweetly. Nor yet does this brevity have any relation to the time to be spent in drinking in brief thoughts of this kind that are truly beautiful,

*Often the day was briefer than my words.* Ou.

Who, however, would altogether deny Ovid laudable brevity? It will rather have a relation to the multitude and fewness of the things to be thought about the given theme in the given relation, yet fully and perfectly, § 158. On superfluities, perhaps, is sometimes lost

*brief, but irreparable time.*

But for those who exhibit things well placed in this laudable brevity

*Often the hours have seemed swift in long discourses.*

§ 161. The full description of brief narration in Cicero, de Inu. I. 28, will illustrate the definition of § 160. A thought *will be* absolutely and laudably [p. 87] *brief, if the beginning is taken from where it is necessary, and not fetched from the farthest point, and if, where it will suffice to have stated the sum of a matter, its parts are not stated. For often it is enough to say that a thing was done, not to recount how it was done; and if one does not proceed in narrating* (meditating) *further than there is need to know, and if there is no digression into any other matter, and if it is so said that sometimes from what has been said, what has not been said is understood, and if not only what hinders, but also what neither hinders nor helps, is passed over, and if each thing is said once, and if one does not begin again from that point at which one has just left off.* cf. Quint. IV. 2.

§ 162. For not *in narration* only, but everywhere, for one who is to think prettily *this middle way is certainly above all to be held: as much as is needed,* § 158, *as much as is enough,* § 160. *Pleasure deceives, and what delights seems less long. As a pleasant and soft road, even if it is of greater extent, tires less than a hard and dry short cut.* Quint. IIII. 2. It is pleasing, in l. II of Virgil’s Aen., in some 800 verses,

*briefly to hear the last agony of Troy.* V. II.

§ 163. Hence for those who are to think beautifully too the manner of thinking to be wished for is that which we philosophers would call [p. 88] adequate and attaining its end exactly, M. § 882, in which there is nothing incomplete, § 159, nothing superabundant, § 160, so far as the analogue of reason can attain the one and avoid the other, § 15, 16. It will turn out otherwise,

⁠1) *as if, when abundance beyond the right measure delights some, / The fierce Aufidus should bear them off, torn away together with the bank.* Hor.

to any of whom, when

*Scattered swimmers appear in the vast gulf,*

you may cry out that saying:

*You are no wiser, since / You are fuller,*

but on another’s tomb you may inscribe the epitaph:

*Briefer, I passed a more secure age.*

*Its very mass does harm.* Claud.

⁠2) Or so that you run into the faults noted in § 121, 122, 159, or so that *unadorned brevity becomes unlearned,* § 108, Quint. IV. 2.

§ 164. Absolute brevity, § 160, demands that to each part of a beautiful meditation only so much charm be given as the beauty of the whole admits, § 19: AESTHETIC PARSIMONY AND SOBRIETY, E. § 289, 260. *Parsimony is the science of avoiding superfluous expenses, or the art of using one’s estate* (the abundance and riches of which S. VIII–XII speak) *moderately. Yet we call the most parsimonious man one of a petty and contracted spirit,* § 120, 149, *since there is an infinite difference between measure* (which the thrifty man [p. 89] keeps) *and straitness,* § 23, M. § 551, Sen. de Ben. II. 34. Whether you prefer to be *the most sparing of the elegant,* like *Crassus,* or *the most elegant of the sparing,* like *Scaevola,* Cic. de claris or. 148, you will satisfy the learned palate as often as, in a full and perfect manner of thinking, § 158, you present to it

*With a sparing hand, what is enough.* § 160, 162.

§ 165. Just as absolute brevity in general will guard against what we philosophers now call the too prolix manner of thinking, § 160: so will the parsimony of elegances above all,

*lest the stalk sink beneath the heavy ears,* § 164. / *graze down the luxuriance of the crops in the tender blade,* § 158. / *For if the luxuriance of leaves runs riot in shade,* / *in vain shall the threshing-floor grind the fat-strawed stalks.* Virg. Georg. l. 191.

THE LUXURIANT MANNER OF THINKING, or καλλωπισμός [over-adornment], is that which either, through the whole thread of the meditation, crowds more charms and figures, § 26, into almost every particle than the modest beauty of the whole can bear, or at least sometimes fashions some one part, and then another, more elaborately than the beautiful harmony of the whole admits. Against the first luxuriance, for one who would think charmingly—the luxuriance whose *every word and deed is sprinkled, as it were, with sesame and* [p. 90] *poppy-seed,* Petr.—let that saying of the sage hold good: πλέον ἥμισυ παντός [the half is more than the whole]; against both:

*I hate, boy, Persian pomp.*

§ 166. The brevity that befits every manner of thinking beautifully, § 160, that full and well-stocked brevity, § 158, not maimed, not gaping, § 159, yet sparing and sober, § 164, not luxuriant or blotchy, § 165—let it be permitted to call it by the one name ROUNDED BREVITY. Therefore, however great your forest may be, S. VIII. XII, fill its gaps and chasms § 158, 159, yet at the same time

*checking what runs to luxuriance,* § 165.

and rounding everything harmoniously to the rules of praiseworthy brevity and parsimony, § 160–164, abandon not the aesthetic abundance, § 115, but

*the fastidious abundance.*

#### Sectio XIIII. Breuitas Relatiua. — Section XIIII. Relative Brevity.

§ 167. Just as aesthetic richness was either absolute or relative, § 116, so too must RELATIVE BREVITY be distinguished from the praiseworthy and [p. 91] rounded absolute brevity, of which S. XIII: it is that greater degree of absolute brevity, § 160, which happens to be elegantly necessary and peculiar to certain things to be thought. This brevity also bears some relation to the time to be spent in taking in thoughts that are brief in this sense. § 160. Nor can it be said of a meditation that demands relative brevity what Pliny holds of Cicero’s orations, *that the best is the longest,* to which he adds, less accurately: *every book is the better, the longer it is.* l. ep. 20—a passage which contains the whole aesthetic controversy that pertains here, and which in other respects is to be removed by the distinction of absolute and relative brevity, with Pliny winning.

§ 168. For many disputes have arisen from this, in defining the manners of beautiful thinking that demand relative brevity, § 167, when for some, in such a manner, 1) absolute brevity, not sufficiently distinguished from the relative, was rightly judged to be especially necessary, § 160, 166, 2) a good many special cases contained under such a manner occurred which demanded at the same time a brevity that was indeed relative, 3) brevity, even relative brevity, was found to be more pleasing to most people in general. For then to others, 1) who distinguished absolute from relative brevity no more clearly than the former, it seemed that [p. 92] brevity was not to be attributed in general to such a kind of themes, because 2) under a manner of this sort were contained cases where *it is a betrayal to touch cursorily and briefly* (relatively) *on things that ought to be driven home, impressed, repeated,* 3) *brevity* (relative) in such a kind of themes does not always and indiscriminately *please,* except *the idle, whose pleasures and sloth it would be ridiculous to regard as if they were judgement. For if you take these into counsel, it is not only better to speak briefly* (relatively) *but not to speak at all.*

§ 169. Since the natural method, which they call mathematical, has not yet here permitted principles sufficient for deciding controversies of this sort, § 168, to be set before the present treatment, let us look at a few only by way of example. I. *In pleading causes, does nothing please so much as brevity?* This is Pliny’s question, l. c., to which in our times let us add a similar question about sacred orations delivered from the pulpit. Reply: Absolute brevity, § 160, but rounded, § 166, is one of the first gifts of both, yet not the primary one; it demands relative brevity in some also throughout, in others as to certain parts, § 167. But not in all, much less in every part of all, can this relative brevity be borne, so far is it from being praised, so far from being able [p. 93] to be esteemed as most delightful by a severe, though not peevish, judge. § 168.

§ 170. II.) Must a narration be brief? Already *Aristotle dissented from Isocrates, mocking the precept of brevity, as if it were necessary that an exposition be long or brief, and not permitted to go by the middle way. The schools of Theodorus* also deny it, *because it is not always useful to expound briefly, nor always lucidly.* Quint. IIII. 2. Cicero sides with Isocrates,

*and the case is still before the judge.*

This is certain, that absolute brevity in a good many narrations demands a greater relative brevity than in some other parts of a speech, § 160, 167. For of relative brevity there are again degrees, § 167. Rightly Quintilian, l. c., while urging brevity of narration upon the orator, nevertheless says: *That Sallustian brevity is to be avoided, although in him it holds the place of a virtue, as is the abrupt manner of speech,* § 159, *which may perhaps less escape the leisurely reader, but flies past the hearer, and does not wait until it be repeated.* etc.

§ 171. On the occasion of brief narration Cicero touches on a witty and quite common error, when he says: *Many are deceived by the imitation of brevity,* (relative) *so that, while they think themselves brief, they are* [p. 94] *extremely long, because they take pains to say many things briefly, not to say altogether few things, and no more than is necessary.* de Inu. I. 28. He there offers an example, such as is also in Quint. l. c. In my way of conceiving it, I would call the pursuit of relative brevity with neglect of the absolute THE LONGEST BREVITY, AS IT WERE, which disfigures not orations alone. (the longest laconism, as it were.)

§ 172. That Cicero too saw what was true in the opinion of those who do not altogether approve relative brevity in narrating, is shown by l. de Or. III. 202, where he advises sometimes to employ *a dwelling on one matter which moves greatly, and a vivid explanation, and a placing of things almost before the eyes, as if they were being done, which both in setting forth a matter,* § 170, *is of the greatest avail, and for illustrating what is set forth, and for amplifying, so that to those who hear, the thing we magnify may seem to be as great as speech can make it,* § 116, but sometimes *precision* (relative brevity) *and a signification that conveys more to the understanding than you have said, and distinctly concise brevity and attenuation,* etc., referring both to figures of thought, § 26.

§ 173. Nor do I care about certain Greeklings’ [p. 95] distinction between a *circumcised exposition,* i.e. σύντομον [concise], *and a brief one,* when *the former, free of superfluities,* i.e. truly and absolutely brief, § 160, while the latter is defined as *that which can lack something of the necessary,* since the latter, unless it be pleasing to quarrel about words, is a fault, § 158, 159, wholly unworthy of the name of brevity, which is a virtue, § 160, 167. Quint. l. c. Otherwise I gladly concede a distinction between the concise manner of distinct reasoning and σύντομον on the one side, and the rounded brevity of aestheticians, § 166, and relative brevity too, § 167, on the other. § 121, 122.

§ 174. III. There is now a greater consensus among critics that relative brevity is required in maxims.

*Whatever you teach, be brief, that docile minds may swiftly grasp / what is said, and faithfully retain it; / everything superfluous flows away from the full breast.* Hor. A. P. 335.

*There is need of brevity, that the thought may run on, and not / entangle itself in words that burden weary ears.* Serm. I. 10.

*Brevity, not only in a senator but also in an orator, is a great merit in a maxim.* Cic. de leg. III. 40. Indeed *admirers* were lacking, *many centuries before* Cicero, *both to Philistus of Syracuse and to Thucydides himself, because Theopompus eclipses their concise maxims,* § 121, [p. 96] 122, *which are sometimes also not clear enough, both through brevity and through excessive acumen.* Cic. de claris or. 66. Nor yet were the admirers few that grateful posterity, and the fate that preserves their writings, have won for Thucydides, and for Tacitus himself, while it

*carries other writings down to the street that sells frankincense and perfumes, / and pepper, and whatever is wrapped in foolish papers.*

Meanwhile Cicero’s observation will finally teach that there is a limit to relative brevity even in maxims, § 170.

§ 175. IV. *Brevity is keener and swifter in urbanity.* Quint. VI. 4, where indeed, in what follows, adducing Marsus’ definition of urbanity: *Urbanity is a certain virtue compressed into a brief saying, most fit for resisting or provoking, as each matter and person requires,* he adds: *If you strip from it the reservation of brevity, it will have embraced all the virtues of speech. But why Marsus wished it to be brief, I do not know. — — — — Yet I shall not withhold the judgement of Marsus, a most learned man.* Horace confirms Marsus’ judgement, and at the same time seems to hint at the reason, when he demands a discourse

*that sometimes, urbane, spares its forces and / deliberately thins them out.* § 164, 172. Hor. Serm. I. 10.

§ 176. [p. 97] Briefly: Prepare resources, S. VIII; whence they can be expected, S. VIIII; whence you have taken them I should care little, S. X; only do you prepare them, S. XI, if you please, S. XII; once prepared, display them with rounded brevity, S. XIII; and let your meditation, not unmindful of the charms to be commemorated hereafter, § 22,

*suit my wish, being long,* § 116, *and brief,* S. XIIII. Ov.

#### Sectio XV. Magnitudo Aesthetica. — Section XV. Aesthetic Magnitude.

§ 177. Let the second care, in things to be thought charmingly, § 115, be MAGNITUDE, M. § 515, but AESTHETIC magnitude, under which name we embrace 1) the weight of objects, § 18, and their gravity, M. § 166, 2) of thoughts proportioned to their objects, 3) together with the fecundity of both, M. § 166. § 22. *That is truly great which repeatedly occurs to us as something to be thought over and considered,* (οὗ πολλὴ ἡ ἀναθεώρησις [of which there is much reconsideration]) *which can scarcely, or not even scarcely, slip from the mind, but is retained in a constant, firm and indelible memory.* Longinus. S. VI.

[p. 98] § 178. *He who has wit, who has a diviner mind, and a mouth / that will sound forth* great things, § 177, *to him grant the honour of this name,*

that he be called a poet, and a musician, if he sings of great things, and a painter, if he paints great things, etc. *Magnitude adds to the volumes themselves a certain authority and beauty.* Plin. ep. l. 20. Now let us first divide aesthetic magnitude again, as we did richness, § 116, into ABSOLUTE magnitude, necessary for all beautiful thinking, § 177, and RELATIVE or comparative magnitude, that degree of the absolute which is peculiarly and specially required for certain things to be thought beautifully. Let him know both, and distinguish them well,

*If anyone burns for the effects of the severe art,* / *and applies his mind to great things,* as soon as *he is skilled in the custom* / *of frugality,* § 164, *by an exact law.* Petr.

§ 179. Just as those who think truly beautifully, when they do not pursue comparative breadth, § 116, and much less strive to fall into the drawn-out and diffuse and overflowing manner of thinking by luxuriating, § 165, accuse themselves of poverty, § 120, by attenuation, § 175, sometimes perhaps ironic: so the same men, perhaps for the same reasons, when they are not thinking sublime things, and much less love [p. 99] to swell, call themselves of petty mind, yet do not want their hearers to believe them. Thus Horace:

*The gods did well to fashion me of a needy and petty / mind, one who speaks seldom and very little. / But you, with goatskin bellows, enclosed airs …* etc.

Thus Martial: IX. 50.

*You prove, Gaurus, that my wit is petty,* § 177, / *because I make poems that please by their brevity,* S. XIIII. / *I confess it. But you, who write of Priam’s great battles / in twice ten books, are a great man.*

§ 180. Relative and comparative aesthetic magnitude has indeed many degrees, § 178; now, however, as befits the aesthetician, § 15, 16, let us divide it into only the three degrees customary among grammarians, so that some things are great, absolutely that is, § 178, 177, in the positive, some in the comparative, some in the superlative. Let the first be *the lowly tamarisks,* the second *the shrubs,* the third *the woods.*

*Not everyone delights in shrubs and lowly tamarisks; / if we sing of woods, let the woods be worthy of a consul,* Virg.

§ 181. [p. 100] AESTHETIC MAGNITUDE, § 177, both absolute, § 178, and relative, § 180, is further either NATURAL, befitting things not more closely connected with freedom, or MORAL, to be attributed to objects and thoughts insofar as they are more closely connected with freedom. Natural is, e.g., Virg. Aen. V. 421, when

*Entellus threw the double cloak from his shoulders / and bared the great joints of his limbs, his great bones and sinews, / and stood huge in the midst of the arena.* etc.

Let themes set within the aesthetic horizon, S. VIIII, be thought richly, S. VIII, with topics, S. X, and enriching arguments, S. XI, if you know how to use them, S. XII; they will at the same time have magnitude, absolute at least, natural, § 177. Hence for this there is little need of new rules.

§ 182. Moral aesthetic magnitude, i.e. that which is possible as determined through freedom in conformity with moral laws, § 181, I find 1) in objects and their various consequences, if they can be thought at length without detriment to virtue, and otherwise elegantly, 2) in the same, if, thought in the ways mentioned, they can sooner or later finally agree with virtue, 3) in thoughts, if [p. 101] they can be proportioned and made equal to their objects and materials in such a way that no detriment, 4) but rather at last certainly some benefit, accrues to the most wholesome, most beautiful cause of virtue and good morals. § 177. Let it be permitted to call this moral aesthetic magnitude more briefly AESTHETIC DIGNITY, and now, after § 115, to see

⁠2) *What is fitting, what is not, whither virtue, whither error leads.* § 22.

§ 183. I would not want this charm of thoughts, as if by a μετάβασιν εἰς ἄλλο γένος [transition into another genus], and as if a sickle were being put into another’s harvest, now to be derived only from the severer and higher laws of the blessed life, or altogether from the most holy oracles of genuine Christianity. I would rather have it considered here as a condition, without which not, of a truly beautiful work of wit; and where it is wholly lacking, a certain deformity shows through, disfiguring whatever other elegance there is, not to the spirit alone, not to reason alone, § 15, but to the very analogue of reason, when it is supported by a greatness of heart that is inborn, § 45, and confirmed by practice, § 50, and discipline, § 63; because one may not dwell on such things without loss to morals, except by an exception that is certainly not elegant, § 25, or at the discretion of one who would measure morals by taste alone, and that not a public and meretricious taste, § 35.

§ 184. [p. 102] For from this it will now be clear that Catullus, although he threatens the most shameful punishments on those who think otherwise, is nevertheless deceived, and shamefully deceived, when: c. 16.

*It is fitting,* he says, *that the pious poet himself be chaste, / but his little verses need not be so. / Only then do they have salt and charm, / if they are soft and not quite modest, / and can rouse what itches.*

Although Martial, l. I. 35, is no more truthful:

*This law has been given to jesting poems:* / They *cannot please unless they itch,*

yet he found a lid worthy of the dish, approving his shameful law, as is fitting, thus:

*who has permitted a matron’s / modesty to harlots?* § 165.

More rightly in any case Persius, sat. 1, unless he sins in form:

*Here you may see, neither in seemly manner nor with serene voice,* § 183, / *the huge Tituses quiver, when the poems enter / the loins, and the inmost parts are tickled by the tremulous verse.*

§ 185. AESTHETIC DIGNITY, § 182, a part and [p. 103] species of aesthetic magnitude, will, like the latter, § 177, 178, be either ABSOLUTE, necessary for all beautiful thinking, concerning which, imitating the Roman jurists, we may judge that we cannot even in thought dwell upon whatever it would be contrary to good morals to think at length, § 183, 184; or RELATIVE, which is required as peculiar to certain things to be thought beautifully, not in the same degree in all, § 178. Of this Cornelius speaks, when *he does not doubt that there will be many* who judge his *manner of writing slight, and not sufficiently worthy of the persons of the greatest men,* and Cicero, de Fin. l. I, *suspecting that there will be some who, though this manner of writing (committing to Latin letters what the philosophers treated in the Greek tongue) be elegant, deny that it befits his person and dignity.*

§ 186. Of the same relative dignity, § 185, alone is Cicero to be understood, when, Off. l. I. 130, he establishes *two kinds of beauty, in one of which there is charm, in the other dignity, taking charm as womanly, dignity as manly.* For he himself demands that the *countenance* of the perfect orator should bring not *dignity* alone but also *charm,* Or. 60, and to *dignity,* Fam. X. ep. 6, he opposes certainly not charm but *deformity.* Matrons too have their own dignity, and, more precisely, as befits a jurist, § 15, Ulp. de senator: *There is greater dignity in the male sex.* Meanwhile, with Cicero’s leave and authority, [p. 104] it will be permitted to call deformed all things in which dignity, even absolute dignity, is wholly lacking, § 185, 177, and in true, especially masculine, beauty, e.g. of delivery, to demand dignity above all for charm, de Or. l. I. 142, and to lay down that a thing and a person *are deformed by taking away and stripping off dignity,* Or. pro Caelio 3.

§ 187. Nor do I deny that I, a German, writing above all for Germans, read dignity the more confidently among the first charms of things to be thought beautifully, § 186; since I know from experience that, among the other respects in which the German nation is especially like the Roman, this merit too is common to both peoples, of which Cic. pro Mur. 23: *All the arts that win for us the favour of the Roman people* (of the German nation) *must have both an admirable dignity and a most welcome utility.* Perhaps, for our aesthetic art, which commends absolute dignity universally and strenuously, and sets relative dignity too more clearly before the eyes, not only will its own absolute dignity stand firm, but a certain relative dignity too will be attributed to it by serious, though not grim, judges among our fellow citizens. § 185.

§ 188. There will meanwhile be, unless I am mistaken, even among German citizens, those who grant § 182 n. 1 and 3 liberally enough, yet judge that in n. 2 and 4 [p. 105] something is demanded which it would seem, more rigidly than is just and charming, to require everywhere, whenever one thinks beautifully. But I shall be more easily excused by these, when 1) they have learned to distinguish more carefully what is expressly and explicitly paraenetic from those things which tacitly and implicitly and, as it were, at long range, quite mediately contribute not nothing at all even to the precepts of the lesser morals, 2) they have looked more deeply into life, the primary gift of beautiful cognition, § 22, and into the various ways and degrees in which it is wont to display itself finely. Now, § 169, I shall defend myself thus, by denying that it can be counted a fault in me if I have demanded either the same or altogether less, in respect of the discipline of morals or of probity, than what he who calls himself

*a pig from Epicurus’ herd,*

Horace, asserts again and again of poets in general, e.g.

*Poets wish both to profit and to delight, / and to say at once what is pleasant and what is fit for life. / He has won every vote who has mixed the useful with the sweet, / delighting the reader and admonishing him alike.*

§ 189. Finally, AESTHETIC MAGNITUDE, § 177, and hence DIGNITY too, § 182, both ABSOLUTE and RELATIVE, § 178, 185, will be either OBJECTIVE (of things, of the material), insofar as the principal reason why they can be painted in proportionate magnitude and dignity by a beautiful [p. 106] wit and heart, S. II, lies in the objects and the things to be thought themselves, or SUBJECTIVE (of the person): the physical possibilities and powers of a certain man, even such as are so in a certain respect and hypothetically, together with his appetitions and his resolution to set the given material before the eyes charmingly in magnitude and dignity, as far as he can, as far as is right. Let us call these latter AESTHETIC MAGNANIMITY AND GRAVITY. E. § 489.

§ 190. There are things and materials at the first sight of which, as they are to be described with some beauty, there is at once wrung from us what once was wrung from Virgil:

*A greater order of things is born for me, / I set in motion a greater work.* VII. 44. Aen.

Then happy is he who, a just judge of himself, can nonetheless truly predict, of such a theme to be beautifully executed by him, what the same poet predicted of the golden ages that would return in his lifetime:

*Neither Thracian Orpheus shall surpass me in song, / nor Linus, though his mother stand by the one, and his father by the other, / Calliopea by Orpheus, fair Apollo by Linus. / Should Pan, with Arcadia as judge, contend with me, / Pan too, with Arcadia as judge, will own himself beaten.* § 189.

#### Sectio XVI. Magnitudo Materiae Absoluta. — Section XVI. Absolute Magnitude of Matter.

§ 191. [p. 107] The absolute aesthetic magnitude of the material, § 189, demands that there be not taken, as things to be thought and as themes by one who would meditate beautifully, objects and materials which, set within the aesthetic horizon of mankind, § 119, 127, wholly lack magnitude, § 177—TRIFLES TRULY SUCH (the lowest and trifling things, rubbish, inanities). *I do not listen to the poet in trifles,* Cic. Par. 3. Whence, sat. V. 19, Persius excellently:

*I do not indeed strive that my page should swell / with bubbled trifles, fit to give weight to smoke. / We give our hearts to be searched,* § 182.

§ 192. *If anyone wrangles, as often, over goat’s wool, and / fights armed with trifles,*

here, § 191, the matter of the dispute truly belongs, unless he wishes to lift his quibbles altogether beyond the aesthetic horizon, § 121. Catullus and Martial, in those of their thoughts which they wrote according to the shameful law of which § 184, are more lenient than is just when they call them trifles; yet, since they wrap other things more beautifully conceived by them under the same [p. 108] title, they are to be interpreted according to § 179. Things are also often counted among trifles which are not, such as § 100, and perhaps here belongs the opinion of Democritus, properly understood, which Cicero, having badly refuted it, de Diu. II. 20, counts not more fairly among great trifles, when he says that *nothing is more arrogant than the tribe of physicists.*

§ 193. AESTHETIC DIGNITY, § 182, both absolute and relative, § 185, both objective and subjective, insofar as n. 1 and 3 of § 182 are found in it, let it be permitted in what follows to call NEGATIVE, but insofar as n. 2 and 4 of the same § are at the same time considered, POSITIVE. And since we are now entering upon the noble part of the AESTHETIC ΗΘΟΥΣ [character], I shall define it, perspicuously at least for psychologists, in general before all else, by the study of moral possibility, M. § 723, that is aesthetic or observed in aesthetic matters. *For the name Ἤθους [character] the Roman tongue, as I at least think, has no word. They are called mores — — — — But to me, considering the nature of the thing itself, it seems that not so much mores are signified as a certain property of mores.* Quint. VI. 3.

§ 194. The AESTHETIC ΗΘΟΣ [character] also has its species and part by which it may be called NEGATIVE, § 193, 182, of which this is the formula: let the things, the order, the signification, e.g. the diction, § 13, if [p. 109] they are intended to be beautiful, be so arranged that nothing morally absurd shows through in them, even to the analogue of reason itself, § 16, 22; and another species and part by which it may be called POSITIVE, according to which the same things I have just mentioned, truly agreeing with morals too and consonant with them, beautifully harmonising with those things which can morally, ethically, economically, civilly come about from usages and customs, become a phenomenon observable even to the analogue of reason itself, § 23.

§ 195. Let us come to one part of ἤθους [character] among the first, § 194, which the natural order of meditating will now supply. Aesthetic dignity, even if you understand only the absolute kind, § 182, 185, demands that no things to be thought, much less themes, be taken which, set within the aesthetic horizon and to be perceived richly and vividly etc., § 22, would wholly lack all dignity—VILE AND PLEBEIAN OBJECTS, which you may compare to mud rather than to tamarisks, § 178, and, calling the latter lowly, you may call the former THE LOWEST AND ABJECT.

§ 196. *As each man is best born and brought up* (and this is the person of the practical aesthetician, § 45, 54, 63) *so they think nothing abject, nothing lowly,* § 195. Cic. de Fin. V. 57. None of them

[p. 110] *would stray with lowly speech into obscure taverns, / for those who have a horse, and a father, and property, take offence.* Hor.

Nor do I bring under this head only the most shameful obscenity, of which § 184, 192, but also adulation crawling on the ground,

*ready to praise / if he has done well — — — —, or if a friend has done anything / which a modest tongue could not bring out openly,* Iuu.

and the abusiveness, luxuriating in insults and taunts fit for sutlers and camp-followers, which once certain critics—good heavens! what Critics!—wanted as a proof of their richness and abundance. § 195.

§ 197. In judging the absolute magnitude and dignity of materials, lest great things with petty ones,

*things having weight without weight,* § 177,

worthy with unworthy, § 182, speakable with unspeakable, § 196, be rashly mixed, there are not excluded from the materials to be thought beautifully 1) things truly having some magnitude, sometimes the highest, § 178, in which, however, many a one, a little brother of the giants, who thinks that he alone is something great, and refers everything to the grand measure which he alone admires in himself, can find nothing at all except things to be made nothing of, to be held cheap, mere trifles. There is no need to [p. 111] cite *the music of Epaminondas,* so long as religion itself, to many,

*unless it comes with property, is cheaper than seaweed.*

§ 198. 2) Those which indeed lack a certain relative and comparative magnitude, yet not an absolute one, § 178, e.g. in familiar conversations and letters, in which even two of us are sometimes *a theatre great enough, the one for the other.* Sen. Such does Corydon present himself:

*I am scorned by you, nor do you ask who I am, Alexis,* / *How rich in snowy flocks, how abounding in milk.* / *A thousand lambs of mine wander on the Sicilian hills.* etc. Virg. Ecl. II. 19.

§ 199. 3) Those which can be thought with a pure mind not only in themselves, but also together with their various and sufficiently numerous consequences, even copiously, vividly, etc., § 22, although an impure mind might at the same time devise in them and in their various features and consequences not a few things hostile to virtue, e.g. if,

*Tityrus, at ease in the shade,* / *You teach the woods to echo fair Amaryllis.*

⁠4) those which bring tedium to a morose virtue, or even to a counterfeit one, when they are thought by others in innocence, not without a suspicion of envy: e.g. Mart. IX. 98:

*that we are loved, and that we are approved.*

[p. 112] § 200. 5) Those which are not suited to all persons and ages, times and places, but certainly to some, with absolute magnitude as well as absolute dignity remaining intact, § 197, e.g. those with which, in the company of Lucilius,

*when they had withdrawn from the crowd and the stage into privacy,* / *the virtue of Scipio and the mild wisdom of Laelius* / *used to trifle,* § 192, easy-going, *and, ungirt, to play, until* / *the greens were cooked.* Hor. Serm. II. 1. 75.

⁠6) those not explicitly, immediately, or more closely inculcating the virtues, e.g. Lucretius I. 49:

*Of the supreme order of heaven and of the gods* / *I shall begin to discourse, and shall unfold the first beginnings of things,* / *From which nature creates all things, increases and nourishes them,* / *And into which the same nature again resolves them when they perish.* etc.

§ 201. 7) Those which indeed lack a certain relative and comparative dignity, yet not an absolute one, § 188, e.g. *that Epaminondas danced gracefully and played the pipes skilfully.* Corn. To this belong Anacreon and Catullus’s famous sparrow, alive, c. 2, and dead, c. 3:

[p. 113] *Mourn, O Venuses and Cupids,* / *And all you men of finer feeling!* / *My girl’s sparrow is dead,* / *The sparrow, my girl’s delight.* etc.

#### Sectio XVII. Magnitudo Materiae Relatiua. — Section XVII. Relative Magnitude of Matter.

§ 202. The MATTERS to which both absolute magnitude and absolute dignity belong, § 182, 185, will have also a relative magnitude, § 180, and a relative dignity: I.) the tamarisks, the least, with some latitude, such as befits the aesthetician, § 15, 16, worthy of one of the lesser honours, the SLENDER. That persons too, personal objects, belong here, do not doubt; let us also, after Demetrius, refer here

*Vulteius, Mena by name,* / *An auctioneer, of slender means, born without reproach,* / *Who both hastened and rested in due season, both earned and spent,* / *Delighting in his humble companions and his own small hearth,* / *And in the games, and in the Campus when business was done.* etc. Hor. I. ep. 7. 55.

§ 203. II. The forests, the greatest, with the same latitude [p. 114] befitting the aesthetician, § 202, worthy of one of the greatest honours, eminently GREAT, GRAND AND SUBLIME. I shall not contend against Seneca when, considering the intrinsic magnitude and dignity of things, he says: *All vices, it matters not how far they advance and extend themselves, are narrow, wretched, low.* § 195. *Only virtue is sublime and lofty* (morally too). *Nor is anything great, unless it is at the same time also calm*, except perhaps as regards the last pronouncement, de Ira I. 16. But our discourse here is of objective magnitude and dignity, not insofar as it inheres in the objects themselves, but insofar as, inhering in the objects, whatever it may be, it contains the more proximate ground why great and worthy thoughts can be formed about the objects, § 189, 18.

§ 204. Just as, therefore, certain right habits, though not abject and lowly, can nevertheless be slender, § 202, partly in those destitute of a certain relatively greater natural magnitude, partly in those lacking many various consequences, even moral ones, yet more observable through the analogue of reason, § 181, 182: so certain depraved habits and deeds may seem able to be a great matter, or even an altogether grand one, for one who is to think beautifully, when 1) their various real features, pertaining [p. 115] both to the remote and to the proximate material, M. § 914, and their formal element too, have at the same time natural magnitude through a number of consequences as well, flowing anew either from the material or from the formal element of the evil and to be detected through the analogue of reason itself; 2) not so much the formal element of the said deeds themselves as the enormous deformity of this is taken as the theme to be beautifully set before the eyes by one who is to meditate aesthetically, which can indeed be done most worthily, § 18, 203. Thus the crime of Catiline may seem to have been a grand theme both for Cicero and for Sallust, § 89. Nor easily, except in themes of this kind, will anyone, on Petronius’s advice,

*shake the arms of mighty Demosthenes,* / *And brandish the grand words of untamed Cicero.*

§ 205. Nor shall I exclude from the aesthetically grand objects, § 203, those which have natural magnitude only, § 187, relatively one of the greatest, even if morally they seem indifferent, provided they be set forth in dignity, though only a negative one, § 193, which nevertheless you must guard with the utmost diligence, § 203. Thus Lucr. I. 720, grand things about Sicily:

*Here is great Charybdis, and here the Aetnean rumblings* / *Of the flames threaten,*

Is it worthy enough, when he continues:

*to gather their wrath once more?*

[p. 116] For my part, to the words:

*A region which seems in many ways wondrous* / *To the races of men,*

had I been about to think grand things, I would not have added:

*and is said to be worth seeing.* cf. Aen. III. 571. sqq.

§ 206. Meanwhile, among those which are generically the greatest, § 203, those stand out in which, to the natural magnitude and the negative dignity which you would call among the greatest, there is added also some positive dignity, § 193. Here I would refer, if I thought Cicero had written from the conviction of his heart, de nat. deorum l. II. 166: *No man was ever great without some divine inspiration.* 167: *To great men all things always turn out prosperously, if indeed enough has been said by our philosophers, and by the prince of philosophers, Socrates, on the richness and abundance of virtue.* 168: *it is an evil and impious habit to dispute against the gods, whether it be done in earnest or in pretence.*

§ 207. *There are* indeed, as Quintilian has it, VIII. 3, *some things more honourable, more sublime than others,* etc., just as there are also more or less slender ones, § 202. Neither the slenderness nor the sublimity to be considered here is that which perhaps the intellect and reason alone would at last detect in things represented as distinctly as can be, § 15, 16; but let the whole complex of matters, rising gradually and elegantly, not without dignity, from the least [p. 117] to the greatest of the things observable and beautifully to be set forth through the analogue of reason, be divided into three equal parts. The lowest will be the aesthetically slender, the highest the aesthetically sublime, each again comprising its own degrees both of magnitude and of dignity, § 203.

§ 208. III. The shrubs, occupying the middle part between the slender and the sublime, § 207, the MIDDLING MATTERS, *interposed, intermediate, and as it were tempered, neighbouring on* the slender and the sublime, *sharing in both, or rather, if we seek the truth, destitute of both.* Cic. Here belongs e.g. the description of Pamphilus in Ter. Andr. I. 1.:

*What nearly all young men do,* / *To devote their mind to some pursuit, either to keep horses* / *Or hounds for hunting, or to the philosophers,* / *In none of these did he engage eminently beyond the rest,* / *And yet in all of these moderately.* etc.

§ 209. Longinus seems to confound the great, § 177, even the relatively great, § 203, and the greatest among such great things, when he says: οὐδὲν ὑπάρχει [p. 118] μέγα, οὗ τὸ καταφρονεῖν ἐστι μέγα [nothing is great, the contempt of which is great], S. VII, and he adduces as examples, in general, honours and dignities, and the things which have πολὺ τὸ ἔξωθεν προστραγῳδούμενον [much outward pomp tacked on], interposing as the reason that they cannot seem eminent goods to the wise man, the contempt of which belongs to the golden mean, and that we admire those who despise them, when they can have them, more than those who enjoy them, § 207. When, further, he exhibits as the character of sublime goods that they are διὰ παντὸς ἀρέσκοντα καὶ πᾶσιν [pleasing always and to all], the question is whether anything can ever with certainty be subsumed under this major premise. If it can, that will surely be not great, but the greatest, e.g. when in S. VIII he demands τὸ σφοδρὸν καὶ ἐνθουσιαστικὸν πάθος [vehement and inspired passion]; compare Seneca as cited, § 203.

§ 210. Rightly does Longinus, S. VIII, distinguish the sublime, § 203, from the pathetic, since certain

*Light cares speak, great ones are dumb.* Sen.

and the sublime can exist without perturbations of the mind, § 205, while at the same time he confidently affirms that nothing is so magnificent as a vehement affect, where there is need of it, § 206. In the question which he weighs at length, S. XXXIII sqq., whether magnitude (understand relative sublimity, § 203) amid certain faults is preferable to middling things that are sound throughout and free from fault, § 208, he will have altogether an easy assent to his affirmation from the [p. 119] aesthetic philosopher, provided the sublimity and what surrounds it, when rightly thought, are so beautiful that, when the sum of the adhering blemishes has been subtracted, the aggregate of the former nevertheless remains more beautiful and more charming than some other middling theme thought in such a way that, even if no blemishes appear which must be subtracted from its degree of beauty, nevertheless the aggregate of the things to be beautifully thought about it remains below the former sum. Let us add that, on account of the companionship of sublimity, many things which in middling matters, § 208, would not only seem but also be blemishes, are not blemishes, but only seem so, being truly exceptions not inelegant, § 25.

§ 211. We consider virtue and good morals here only insofar as they become phenomena, or are observed through the intercourse of men and the external manner of living, not excluding the very shades of the virtues, § 45, whose difference from true virtue would be detected only through the intellect and reason, § 15, 16. Whence it will be evident that the external worship of God, manners, rites, ceremonies and customs, declared perspicacity, the discipline of a more graceful body, the care of one’s external condition, the duties of humanity in general and especially of decorum, positive as well as negative, stand in a somewhat different relation to the weightier ethics than is usually understood by one who meditates on the precepts of the happy life, as far as [p. 120] that can be done, through pure reason, E. § 110, -- 383, etc.

§ 212. Nor are virtue and good morals always, nay rather very rarely, regarded by the aesthetician in the natural state of man in general and, as it were, unlimited, as they mostly are by the more exact ethical writers alone, E. § 1, 9. Here they are rather considered as they exist, not only in the special modifications of the natural state, E. P. II, but also at the same time in the social states of men, in the ties of families and kinships, of the diverse kinds of life, of the societies variously differing therefrom, of civil states and of the almost infinite relations arising out of the civil bond, and not only the universal ones among these, which the philosopher abstracts, but the particular, nay the singular, insofar as they depend on the position of the body in this or that place, changeable according to places and ages, § 22. Here above all attention must now be paid to those things which *are not honourable and base alike for all, but are all judged by the institutions of one’s forefathers.* Corn.

§ 213. If from these points of view, § 211, 212, you contemplate virtue and praiseworthy morals, and that they certainly differ from one another by several degrees, you will find that they can nevertheless be divided with some roundness, § 166, just as diligence is divided [p. 121] into degrees for those who are to determine culpability or malice, and just about everything that is to be predicated, for grammarians, § 180, 207, into the SIMPLY HONOURABLE MANNER OF LIVING, that degree of the well-mannered kind of living, for the attainment of which a person of any condition, education, customs and connections whatever, if he wishes to be good, seems to have sufficient strength; into NOBLE MANNERS AND MANNER OF LIVING, that degree of the kind of living well-mannered according to § 211, 212, which you would find ordinarily only in those better born and already more happily educated, ordinarily not without practice, or indeed some discipline, so that you would judge, not without verisimilitude, that hardly any man whatever could reach it merely because he had resolved to; and finally into HEROIC VIRTUE AND A MANNER OF LIVING JOINED WITH SINGULAR MAJESTY, that best mode of living, conceived according to § 211, 212, which either to attain, or to preserve for oneself even-tenored and, in the good sense, always the same, which is harder,

*few, whom just Jupiter* / *has loved, or whom ardent virtue has raised to the heavens,* / *sons of gods, have been able.*

§ 214. He who knows how to live, E. § 390, will himself deliver to himself more fully the differences, [p. 122] § 213, briefly but clearly enough noted, nor will he stick at conceiving this as it were scale of aesthetic dignity, § 182, relative, § 185, and objective, § 189: the simply honourable kind of living: slender matters, § 202 = the noble: middling, § 208 = the heroic: sublime, § 203.

§ 215. Such is the limitation of human affairs that there is hardly anything which does not have, as Epictetus says, a double handle, the one to be borne, the other less so. Not only objects that are absolutely worthy, but the sublime themselves, if they are considered from one side of themselves, have at the same time another side, from which, if they are inspected more deeply, not only sublimity perishes, but often all dignity as well. The same holds of natural magnitude, according as it is compared, S. XV, XVI. Hence it is all the easier to observe that certain objects, certain matters, which at first sight seem always the same, will nevertheless, according to the diverse respects, the various features and moments, to which you chiefly attend, be now lowly, or at least slender, now middling; others now middling, now sublime. Certain objects can run through all the degrees of relative magnitude and dignity, according as they are presented to the eyes of the spectators from diverse sides, § 202, -- 214. *How much does it matter by whom each thing is done? For the same deeds, according to the fame or the obscurity of the doers,* [p. 123] *are either exalted to the highest or sunk to the lowest.* etc. Plin. VI. ep. 24.

§ 216. In the Bucolics, where Virgil for the most part pursues slender things, Ecl. V. 35, Vossius reads from the manuscripts:

*The luckless darnel and the barren oats spring up,*

which seems to him, not without reason, less than the same, § 215, thought thus in the Georgics, where middling objects are sought, l. I. 154:

*The luckless darnel and the barren oats hold sway.*

If meanwhile someone, like Prudentius, about to meditate a grand Hamartigenia, were to inscribe on the picture of the fall:

*The luckless darnel and the barren oats spring up,*

on the picture of the miseries and corruptions which it even now drags after it:

*The luckless darnel and the barren oats hold sway,*

and finally on the picture of the last outcome, natural indeed, but deplorable:

*The luckless darnel and the barren oats* are burnt,

among other things conceived more magnificently in proportion to the gravity of the matter, I do not think any of these thoughts would lower arduous matters, § 215. The fruits of peace can be slender, and middling, and grand matters.

[p. 124]

#### Sectio XVIII. Ratio Cogitationum ad Materias Generatim. — Section XVIII. The Relation of Thoughts to Matters in General.

§ 217. Aesthetic magnitude demands that thoughts be proportioned and made equal to their objects, personal and real, and to the thinking subject itself, insofar as it, or some state of it, is at the same time its own object, or an object to which thought must be congruent, § 177. A manner of thinking smaller than its great objects, whether absolutely or relatively such, is ΒΑΘΟΣ [bathos] (the lowly manner of thinking, creeping along the ground).
