A manner of thinking greater than its objects is TUMIDITY (the frigid, the swollen and inflated, the too lofty, the too exaggerated manner of thinking). The equality of thoughts with absolutely great objects is a merit, the opposite of which, erring by defect, is βάθος [bathos], by excess, tumidity.

§ 218. Thus Cicero, following *Aristotle, approves neither a lowly nor an abject speech, nor one too lofty and exaggerated, yet he wishes it to be full of gravity,* § 189, *so that it can carry those who hear it to greater admiration,* [p. 125] Or. 192. As soon as lowliness is opposed to dignity in general, as in Cic. pro Rosc. 156, let it be banished from beautiful meditations, § 195. But let tumidity be banished along with it, § 217. *Is a hand in a sound state when it is swollen? or is any other limb, swollen and turgid, not in a faulty condition? So then an inflated and swelling mind is in a fault, but the mind of the wise man is always free from fault, never grows turgid, never swells.* Cic. Tusc. quaest. III. 19.

§ 219. *Do you swell with the love of praise?* § 217. *There are sure expiations which, with the little book* / *Thrice read in purity, can restore you.* Hor.

But here he will be showing, either practically, through examples, or theoretically at the same time, that even then, when we call thoughts things, § 18, this is true:

*There is a measure in things, there are, in short, fixed limits,* / *Beyond and short of which the right cannot stand.*

For, well mindful of this most sacred law, you will not only avoid lowly things, but on account of the same you will also say to yourself within your breast:

*Restrain, then, your unseasonable swellings.* Ou. Trist.

[p. 126] § 220. *We are deceived by the appearance of right, the greatest part of us.* / *One who professes grand things swells.* / *One too safe and too fearful of the storm creeps along the ground.* Hor.

*More rightly* will you hold your course *neither by always* / *pressing the deep, nor, while, cautious, you shudder* / *at the storms, by pressing too close upon* / *the treacherous shore.* § 217.

For then you follow the natural manner of thinking, § 104, since

*assuredly, this is how it is, I think:* / *as things present themselves to all of us, so great or lowly,* § 195, *are we.* Ter. Hec. III. 3.

§ 221. To be avoided, therefore, is I. the TRIFLE-PEDDLING (trifle-vending) MANNER OF THINKING of those who trifle about trifles truly such, § 191, i.e. of those who form and exhibit thoughts equal to their objects. For since this manner of thinking is smaller even than the absolutely great, § 178, it is rightly, § 177, constituted the FIRST KIND OF ΒΑΘΟΥΣ [bathos], § 217.

*Assuredly, it is useful to be wise, having cast trifles aside,* / *And to leave to boys the play that suits their age.*

[p. 127] For from a cake of this kind no one will have sought a laurel-wreath, except perhaps some *nugipolyloquides* of Plautus.

§ 222. II. The manner of thinking about trifles truly such, § 191, as if they were something, § 177. 178, is the FIRST KIND OF TUMIDITY, § 217, of which the first species is, if under ill omens you place trifles among the absolutely great, albeit slender, § 202; the second, whenever you have tried to insert them among middling things, § 208; the greatest, if you think of them as of grand and sublime things, § 203. It is always ridiculous when

*The greatest story is born of nothing.*

But above all when

*Not about violence, nor slaughter, nor poison,* / *But my suit is about three she-goats;* / *I complain that they are missing through my neighbour’s theft,* / *This the judge demands to have proved to him;* / *You, of Cannae and the Mithridatic war* / *And the perjuries of Punic fury* / *And the Sullas, the Mariuses, the Muciuses,* / *Sound forth with a great voice and with full gesture.* / *Now, Postumus, speak of the three she-goats.* Mart. VI. 19.

§ 223. Yet lest we seem to be writing mere laws of what is forbidden, or prohibitions, [p. 128] it is positively recommended to apply to matters, at least absolutely great, a MANNER OF THINKING equal and proportioned to them, which one may say, from the truth of the thing, is at least absolutely GREAT ENOUGH, conducive to the purpose, and aptly adhering to it, § 177, 217. That will at the same time be a MANNER OF THINKING WORTHY ENOUGH, at least absolutely and negatively, § 182, 185, 193, and at the same time sufficiently adequate to the relative magnitude and dignity of its matters, § 162.

§ 224. Hence now again the LESS WORTHY MANNER OF THINKING is dissuaded, on account of a defect disproportioned to its objects and matters, whether absolutely worthy or relatively such, a species in its own kind of βάθους [bathos] always more eminent in baseness, § 217, 182. Among the diverse species of this manner of thinking, attention must now be paid to the VILE MANNER OF THINKING ABOUT ABJECT THINGS, proportioned and equal to its lowest objects, § 195, the baser species of the first kind of βάθους [bathos], § 221, e.g. Hor. ep. 8, Serm. l. 8, Cat. 15, 16.

§ 225. No less to be rejected here is the species of tumidity which can have a place in the first kind, if, in the case of trifles truly such, nay abject and lowest, there is nevertheless a mind to rub on to them some [p. 129] dignity or other, not only absolute, but also relative, and perhaps even something of majesty, if it please the gods, § 222, e.g. if some parasite has licked the sauce of some petty Trimalchio and, on the return of the day whose name he here bears,

*Now he likens the long neck of a weakling to the neck* / *of Hercules holding Antaeus far from the earth.*

§ 226. The MORAL MANNER OF THINKING IN THE WIDER SENSE is the name given to every one that strenuously observes ἦθος [character], § 193. Thus on Horace’s A. P. 319,

*Sometimes a play striking in its passages, and rightly drawn in character,* / *Though of no charm, without weight and art,* / *Delights the people more strongly and detains them better* / *Than verses empty of matter and melodious trifles,*

Acron already noted that *a play is rightly drawn in character* *in which the characters of the individual persons are best expressed.* But the greater part of all ἤθους [character] is demanded in things to be beautifully thought only on account of verisimilitude, then persuasion, and finally life, and so cannot yet now be unfolded by the natural method of the science, nor therefore may the moral manner of thinking in this signification now be exhausted, or demanded, except by a glimpse and as it were by anticipating what follows, § 22.

[p. 130] § 227. The MORAL MANNER OF THINKING IN THE STRICTER SENSE is the name given to one which always preserves a way of thinking proportionately equal to matters and objects that are worthy, either absolutely at least or also comparatively, and thereby as it were extorts from the spectator a favourable presumption about the morals of the thinker. Quintilian, professing to follow Cicero in these things, demands a *statement of facts* that is *moral: but in a speech*, he adds, *all things which can be should be moral, with dignity,* IIII. 2. And this is now that moral manner of thinking which it is not only permitted here, through what precedes, but even necessary to demand, if anyone wishes to aspire to truly beautiful thoughts, § 182, 185.

§ 228. THE MORALLY GOOD MANNER OF THINKING IN THE STRICTER SENSE will again be either ABSOLUTELY SUCH, or also comparatively and RELATIVELY, § 227, 185, either negatively only, or also positively, § 193, 194. For now let it suffice, by arguments drawn even from the very form of beauty, § 183, to dissuade the MORALLY BAD MANNER OF THINKING IN THE STRICTER SENSE, but ABSOLUTELY, whether impious, or dishonourable, or indecorous, even if it be proportioned to vile and lowest matters, perhaps basely chosen, § 224.

[p. 131] *If I know you well, most free-spoken Lollius, you will fear,* / *having professed yourself a friend, to present the appearance of a buffoon.* / *As a matron will be unlike and of a different colour from a harlot,* / *so will a friend differ from a faithless buffoon.* Hor. I. ep. 18.

§ 229. This morally good manner of thinking, which I called such in the stricter sense, § 227, I have the more willingly taken pains to distinguish from the moral manner of thinking in the wider sense, because I have rather often observed charming wits, through the very ardour of verisimilitude and of expressing character more happily, i.e. while they pursue one part of the moral manner of thinking, slip so far as to lose sight of the other, which dignity demands, § 227, absolute dignity, § 228, or verisimilitude itself recommending an exception not inelegant, if the collision of the two perfections cannot be avoided, § 25. Hence the obscenity of Juvenal and the licences of many other satirists.

#### Sectio XVIIII. Tenue Cogitandi Genus. — Section XVIIII. The Slender Manner of Thinking.

§ 230. It concerns aesthetic magnitude that thoughts be made equal also to the relative magnitude of their [p. 132] objects, S. XVII, § 217. Hence a threefold manner of thinking arises, each with its double παρεκβάσει [deviation]; which those who of old detected it only in speech called either its threefold character, or idea, or form, or even figure in general.

*Threefold is the kind of speaking: the sublime, the modest,* / *And that of slender thread.* Aus.

§ 231. I. THE SLENDER MANNER OF THINKING (subtle, attenuated, drawn fine, thin, Attic, ἰσχνόν [lean]) is one so proportioned, § 230, to slender matters, § 202, that, being both sufficiently tenacious of absolute dignity, § 223, and moral in the stricter sense, even relatively, § 228, it is not only not contrary to the simply honourable kind of living, § 213, but even agrees well with its morals as judged according to § 211. 212, § 214. To this long ago, in Terence’s time, *an old poet*, but a censor irregularly appointed, objected, as a fault:

*That it was of slender speech and of light writing,* / *Because nowhere did he write of a young man in his madness* / *Seeing a hind flee and hounds pursue her,* / *And her weeping and begging him to come to her aid.*

[p. 133] § 232. With this manner of thinking, laudable yet still aesthetic, many, with Cicero as their leader, confuse the manner of inquiring into the weightiest matters philosophically and, so far as can be done, scientifically, because they agree in this, that both lack that splendour which is wont to surround the weightier phenomena, the truly good, § 211, and any most determinate grand things, which are all but singular, § 212, e.g. when one must think *of the immortal gods, of piety, of concord, of friendship, of the common right of citizens, of men, of nations, of equity, of temperance, of magnitude, of every kind of virtue.* *When I have granted to the philosophers that they may discourse of all these matters in corners, for the sake of using up their leisure: yet this I shall assign and grant to the orator, that what they dispute about in a certain thin and meagre speech* (logical, § 122, in that it does not measure its greater part by the rules of aesthetics, which it transcends), *he should set forth with all weight and pleasantness.* (aesthetic) § 124, 123. See Cic. de Or. I. 56, 57. de Fin. III. 40. Or. 46.

§ 233. The slender manner of thinking in its turn admits of degrees, § 231, the first and lowest of which you might call the RATHER SLENDER MANNER OF THINKING, § 207. Let the example be *grammar,* [p. 134] *not occupying parts that belong to others.* For it is *thin* (indeed most of all) *at its source*, when

*the coaxing teachers once give little cakes to boys, / that they may be willing to learn their first elements.* Hor.

*having taken on the strength of the poets and the historians, it now flows in a channel full enough*, Quint. II. 1, yet in the whole compass of the disciplines it does not rise above slenderness, § 202. To the higher degrees of slenderness let us refer Cicero’s oration for Caecina, since he himself refers it to the subtle manner of thinking, as contradistinguished from the tempered and the weighty.

§ 234. If the slender manner of thinking expresses its argument and matter more happily, you might not unaptly say of it:

*In a slender matter the labour, but not thin the glory.* Virg. cf. Prop. III. 1.

*For it has its own sure devotees, who pursue not so much a plump habit of body as slendernesses, and whom, provided only the health be good, slenderness itself delights, — — — — It has its own praisers, who take great delight in this very subtlety.* Cic. de cl. Or. 64. Nor are these praisers without a nose, § 230, 202,

*nor does Apollo always / keep his bow bent.*

[p. 135] § 235. Nor, in general, is an opinion to be formed beforehand about the threefold character of thoughts which we are now setting out, as if even through a longer aesthetic meditation one of these characters ought to run evenly through all the things to be thought beautifully and through each one of them: nor about the slender manner of thinking, § 230. In a long work, as matters necessarily unequal are in some fashion joined together, so it has its own ἄνεσιν [relaxation] and remission of thoughts, proportioned to the matters and aptly cohering, now its ἔπαρσιν [raising] and elevation, while the denomination of the whole remains from that character which reigns and is predominant. § 217.

§ 236. In the same oration Cicero, de Inu. II. 51, bids *that some argumentations be handled more slenderly, more acutely and more subtly, but that some topics*, e.g. *those that heighten the atrocity of a deed, and the one which denies that one ought to pity the wicked, on the part of the accuser, who should show the calumny of his adversaries with indignation, and who should seek to win pity with complaint, on the part of the defender*, he wishes to be handled *more weightily, more ornately, and with excellence both of words and also of thoughts.* Who does not know that the sock of comedy demands the thin, the buskin of tragedy the sublime manner of thinking?

[p. 136] *Yet sometimes comedy too raises its voice, / and an angered Chremes rails with swelling mouth, / and the tragic poet for the most part grieves in pedestrian speech, / Telephus or Peleus, when poor and exiled each of them / throws away the bombast and the foot-and-a-half-long words, / if he cares to touch the spectator’s heart with his complaint.* Hor.

§ 237. We have already seen Cicero call the slender manner of thinking subtle, § 233, and Quintilian VIII. 3 expressly sets speaking *subtly* and *magnificently* as opposites. Although this happens from another signification of the subtle than the one M. § 576 exhibits, yet the likeness of the words has perhaps contributed something to the confusion of things, even of the philosopher’s more acute distinctions, with the thin and least weighty way of thinking. § 232. The slender manner of thinking does not spurn all ornament; rather it not seldom pursues wit and jests, § 202, but ones simply honourable, § 231. This character, if any, bears the playful way of thinking, by which, among other things, you utter ironically μικρὰ μεγάλως [small things grandly]. Thus Horace seems to make sport of those who seek the primary virtue of ὕψους [sublimity] in long and great and sonorous words, when at Serm. I. 2, right at the beginning:

*The guilds of the flute-girls, the drug-sellers,*

[p. 137] Oho! what *foot-and-a-half-long words!* But go on, and you will see simple slenderness.

§ 238. Yet in the slender manner of thinking the *subtle palate* of a judge well versed in things will not have sought anything *most acute* and *most subtle*, partly because to expend the utmost force of acumen and perspicacity on slender matters, § 231, would not be far from empty quibbling, M. § 576; partly because *that subtlety, when truth itself is filed down in disputation*, either does not belong at all to the aesthetic horizon, § 121, or at least would not agree elegantly enough with manners simply honourable only and aesthetically conceived, § 231, 228. In the thin and simpler way of living it is not the custom *to speak subtly rather than lucidly.* § 213. In this signification the *elegant subtlety* of thoughts, not repugnant even to the *copious* way of thinking, is to be sought rather in the tempered and in the lofty characters of thoughts themselves, § 230.

§ 239. The attenuated manner of thinking could perhaps be proved, from the passages of Cicero where he speaks of such a manner of speaking, to be one of the middle characters, to be opposed to the elevated and ample ways of thinking, if I wished to spend much effort in digging out the Roman denominations of thoughts. § 231. When Horace:

[p. 138] *More rightly you spin the Iliad’s song into acts*, etc.

he seems already to hint that the epic is so much grander than tragedies themselves that things transferred from the former into the latter seem to be attenuated. Hence the manner of thinking simply called fine-spun seems in any case to denote one lowered all the way to slenderness. Such is, for the most part, that of the bucolics.

*When I was singing of kings and battles, Cynthius plucked / my ear and warned me: A shepherd, Tityrus, ought / to feed fat sheep, to sing a fine-spun song*, § 155.

— — — — *I shall meditate a rustic muse on a thin reed.* Virg.

— — — — *Of you, Varus, our* tamarisks, § 180. / *Of you every grove shall sing.* Virg. ecl. VI. 3.

But when Horace, *cutting down his own vineyards*, says that *the poets*:

*complain that our labours are not apparent, / and our poems spun out on a thin thread.* ep. II. 1. 225.

without doubt he wished the εἴρων [ironist] to be understood from § 179.

§ 240. Not only is Virgil the one who

*once modulated on a slender oaten pipe,*

Aen. I. 1, but in orations too a certain slenderness and subtlety was referred to the Attic [p. 139] manner of thinking, § 237, 238, which, being proper to those Greeks, § 56, they maintained the Romans could not imitate, § 187. Quintilian consoles them thus, XII. 10: *Can we not be so slender? let us be stronger.* M. § 515. *Are we beaten in subtlety? let us prevail by weight.* M. § 166. At the same time he rightly warns that *Attic savour* is wrongly attributed *only to those who flow in a thin little vein over pebbles, because* Pericles (an Attic), *whom the comic poets compare to thunderbolts and heavenly crashing while they revile him, could not be believed like the Lysian slenderness.* The same writer elsewhere ascribes *slenderness* to *the fables of Aesop, which come next after the tales of nurses.* On account of which, nevertheless, the best appraisers of talents,

*The Athenians set up a huge statue to Aesop, / and placed a slave on an eternal base,* § 234, / *that all might know the way of honour lies open, / and that glory is given not to birth but to virtue.* Phaedr. II. 9.

§ 241. Letters and dialogues are generally, and wrongly, assigned to this manner of thinking. § 231. Granted: Cicero seemed *to deal in plebeian speech with* Papirius Paetus *in his letters.* Go on reading, and you will see that “plebeian” here is to be understood from the stricter notion of the Roman plebs, and not from that in which we sometimes so call something vile and abject, [p. 140] as being so. But even if it had been put for *thin*, does the consequence hold: If Cicero used slender speech to Paetus, then in no letters is thought to be raised above slenderness? Granted: *we are wont to weave letters out of everyday words.* Fam. IX. 21. Is it therefore not even permitted to insert into letters anything but everyday matters? § 96, 114 will teach what truth underlies the error.

§ 242. Some count as the virtues in the slender manner of speaking, 1) either propriety or purity, 2) perspicuity, 3) probability, 4) evidence. Others rightly say that all these are endowments common to all the characters of thinking beautifully, § 22. If, following the mind of the former, we conceive propriety through the absence of tropes, and purity through the absence of ornament, the latter beauty will involve the former. By the same reasoning evidence will involve perspicuity and probability, or verisimilitude, M. § 531. There would remain, then, the absence of ornament, and evidence. But the former is neither a virtue, nor is it present in every manner of thinking which is nevertheless thin. § 247. The latter is everywhere required on a double title. § 22. What, then, remains proper to this character? Not only both, provided they be rightly understood, but still more. cf. Cic. Or. 76-90.

[p. 141] § 243. I. Purity from the sordid things which would depress the slender manner of thinking below absolute aesthetic magnitude and dignity, § 202, joined with a solicitous caution against all that ornament which would raise thoughts about slender things either beyond the bounds of the magnitude natural in their matter, or beyond the limits of the dignity suited to the objects and of simply honourable manners, § 231. II. Evidence, as much as, on the side of verisimilitude, on the side of perspicuity, and on the side of persuasion, § 22, you could expect to be attained by all who are given to the simply honourable kind of living, § 213; by all, I say, in whom one may suppose a culture of wit proportionate to such a disposition. § 45.

§ 244. I had promised more virtues proper to the slender kind of thoughts, § 242. Let it be permitted now to adduce one by way of example. III. That rounded brevity, § 166, which you could fairly expect from a simply honourable man whose wit is proportionately cultivated, § 243, § 231, if not to be delivered by him, yet to be grasped without difficulty and praised with sober judgement, if it be delivered by another. How far these differ, and yet meanwhile agree among themselves, let us see in a twofold description of Rome:

[p. 142] I. *The city which they call Rome, Meliboeus, I thought, / fool that I was, like to this one of ours, to which we shepherds / are often wont to drive down the tender offspring of the sheep.* etc. Virg. ecl. I.

This rather slender one, § 233, I should expect from anyone simply honourable who knew how to use Latin metre, which does not pertain to the matter. § 18.

II. *All this that you see, stranger, where mightiest Rome now is, / before Phrygian Aeneas was hill and grass.* etc. Prop. IIII. 1-35.

This one, greater among slender things, I think is nevertheless to be properly grasped by any honourable man and, from his own simple way of living, the lesser among honourable ways, to be approved, yet not without consideration.

§ 245. From the slender manner of thinking let there be properly distinguished, I. the manner of thinking about things thin indeed, yet absolutely great, § 202, which so depresses them that, against their nature, they are transformed into mere trifles and ditties. § 191. THE SECOND KIND OF ΒΑΘΟΥΣ [bathos], § 217, and the first manner of thinking OF TRIFLERS IN A SERIOUS MATTER, who cherish about greater things only thoughts proportioned to trifles. cf. § 221.

[p. 143] § 246. I am not unaware that it is usual to oppose to the slender character, as though it were a single vice, the dry and the meagre, the arid and the bloodless, the jejune and the frivolous manner of thinking. But 1) it also has a species of tumidity opposed to it; 2) dryness is sometimes not only wrongly censured, even in the more severe meditations of the intellect and of reason, even of the purer reason, by irregularly appointed censors, who do not know how to distinguish the logical and the aesthetic manner of thinking, but is also praised by a competent judge even in aesthetic matters themselves. I shall not appeal to that woman of Plautus who *reckoned her husband dry, frugal, continent, a lover of his wife above all.* Cicero, when he says that *the compressed orator*, § 239, *must beware of poverty and jejuneness*, § 120, 128, being about to set forth in the example of Cotta how this ought to be done, says of him: *There was nothing in his oration but what was sincere, nothing but what was dry and sound*, and goes on to praise. de cl. or. 202. Surely the dry is not blamed here.

§ 247. Quintilian seems altogether to place the dry among the praises of manly dignity, when at II. 4 he most excellently urges that *the still tender age should dare more things*, S. VIII, *and invent, and rejoice in its inventions, even though these be not dry and severe enough for the time being. The remedy for richness* (excessive) [p. 144] *is easy; barren things are in no way overcome.* Macrobius praises *the eloquence of Maro* as *now dry, now florid.* But when Gellius, XIIII. 1, says that he *touches on*, in a *dry and unarranged*, § 113, *and almost single discourse, what Favorinus had carried out not without copiousness and charm, more broadly, more pleasantly, more splendidly and more fluently*: it is clear even from this that *the dry*, if it be counted a vice, is to be opposed to breadth and copiousness, S. VIII, as a παρεκβάσιν [deviation], rather than to aesthetic magnitude, S. XV, whether absolute or any relative one. But we shall rather often find that even diligent critics of these matters have confused the beautiful extension and the charming intension of thoughts. § 187.

§ 248. The DRY MANNER OF THINKING, then, will be rather the comparatively brief, either worthy of praise, § 246, S. XIIII, where aesthetic parsimony has commanded it, § 164, 246, or of blame, if a certain aesthetic breadth would have been preferable, § 116, 247. From this definition I understand Cicero, de opt. gen. or. 12: *If they place understanding in a fastidiousness of hearing,* § 242, 243, *and nothing lofty and magnificent delights them: let them say that they want something subtle,* § 237. 238, *and polished, and despise the weighty and ornate. But let them cease to say this, that only those who speak subtly speak Attically,* § 240, *that is, as it were dryly and soundly. To speak both amply, and ornately, and copiously, with the same soundness, is also Attic.*

[p. 145] § 249. 3) The meagre seems to be almost wholly synonymous with the dry, § 246–248, if we follow Pliny, in whose times perhaps the theory, I say the theory, not the practice, of aesthetics had been carried higher than in the time of Cicero himself. For he, V. ep. 17, when he says: *Aptly and variously he now rose, now subsided; he varied the lofty with the lowered, the meagre with the full, the pleasant with the severe, all with equal talent.* etc. who does not see that he a) wishes here too to praise meagre things, b) that these, however, are contradistinguished not from the lofty but from the full (relatively, i.e. the broad and diffuse), c) that the lowered are rather expressly contradistinguished from the lofty, and subsiding from τῷ [the] rising. Nor would Cicero seem to disagree, when he opposes *a meagre copiousness to a most rich one.*

§ 250. Then too Cicero, when he falls into the error noted in § 246, proves the congruence of the meagre and the dry, § 249, when at de Or. II. 159 he says that *the Stoic*, § 122, *finds many things which he denies can be dissolved in any way*, § 121, *and brings a kind of speech* (first, of the meditation itself too) *not liquid, not flowing and fluent, but meagre, arid, concise and minute*, § 121, *which if anyone* (a patron of the intellect and of the purer reason) *shall approve, he will approve it in such a way as yet to confess that it is not suited to the orator. For this oration of ours* (aesthetic) *must be accommodated to the ears of the multitude,* [p. 146] *to delight minds, to impel them, to prove things which are weighed not by a goldsmith’s balance, but by a certain popular scale.*

§ 251. I should never concede that all things are *thorny and meagre*, § 249, which are *far removed from our senses*, § 250; yet would that, in the manner of thinking of philosophers, which ought to be more distinct and more solid than the aesthetic, it were not so easy, to this very day, to find examples by which you could cover, or even defend, this enormous confusion, as it was for Cicero in the example which he brings forward in de Or. I. 83. Meanwhile Cicero is not excused, or, if you prefer, Crassus in Cicero, who asserts that *certain men disputed jejunely and meagrely about the same things* about which Democritus and Plato etc. disputed well, *as Chrysippus, whom they report to have been most acute, and not on that account to have failed philosophy.* de Or. I. I. 50. For he goes on: *What then is the difference? or how will you distinguish the richness and copiousness in speaking of those whom I have named* (Democritus and Plato etc.) *from the meagreness of those* (Chrysippus etc.) *who do not use this variety and elegance of speaking? There will be one thing, assuredly, which those who speak well bring as their own: a composed oration, ornate, and set off by a certain artifice and polish.*

§ 252. For either Chrysippus, about the same things about which [p. 147] Democritus and Plato etc. set forth the naked truth somewhat wrapped in the peplos of aesthetics, philosophised as wretchedly as Mnesarchus, of whom l. c. 83, and satisfied philosophy much less than the rules of aesthetics; or else he set forth completely, weightily, exactly, distinctly, scientifically and, so to speak, compellingly to reason and intellect, in natural brevity and simple dress, what those men, Democritus, Plato etc., offered to the analogue of reason through a composed oration, ornate artifice, and (aesthetic) polish, and it is surely with a less than mature judgement that the jejune and meagre manner of thinking is objected to him, as to the aptly connected skeletons of the osteologist it is objected that they lack a belly. But however these things may stand, this at any rate you may gather even from this passage of Cicero, that meagreness signifies rather a defect of richness and a certain jejuneness, § 120. 128, than a stain upon magnitude and dignity. § 249.

§ 253. The matter will be settled, I think, by Cicero’s judgement on Rutilius, de cl. Or. 114: *His orations are jejune; much that is excellent on law* (not slender matters, unless I am mistaken). *A learned man and versed in Greek letters, a hearer of Panaetius, almost perfect in the Stoic doctrines* (nor do these belong to the simply honourable kind of living by the aestheticians’ formula), *whose most acute and artful kind of* [p. 148] *oration* (nor do these savour of slenderness, nor do things like them, § 237, 238, and tumidity rather than excessive lowering befits the Stoics) *is yet meagre, and not sufficiently accommodated to popular assent* (because, against the precepts of Zeno, they did not always distinguish sufficiently in practice which things required the hands compressed into a fist and which required them open, § 122). Nor do I urge anything against this decision of Cicero’s, unless perhaps against the universality with which he reproaches the Stoics in general with meagreness. If anyone, out of the lectures of the more accurate philosophers, which he attended assiduously, having strenuously followed their logical and intellectual meditation, descends to the practice and use of things to be thought aesthetically, and there still clings to his way of thinking something of that distinct accuracy and logical precision to which he has grown accustomed, now excessive, and to be laid aside for the present time, he himself may perhaps be excused by fair judges, yet his way of thinking, in so far as it savours of dialectic, is not to be defended as though it deserved to be called beautiful and itself elegant. § 121, 124.

§ 254. 4) The arid manner of thinking, as a vice sinning by defect in the slender way of thinking, § 245, especially in that it is destitute of sufficient dignity, I should almost say is to be expressed by a more apt denomination, from this passage of Ovid:

[p. 149] *You, lighter* § 189 *than the leaves then, when, without the weight* § 177 *of sap, / they fly, grown arid, on the shifting winds.*

If anyone, however, should think that the same vicious brevity of which § 247–253 treat is marked by the same term, he will have reasons not to be despised, e.g. when Quintilian II. 4 wishes the historical narration to be *neither wholly arid, nor jejune, nor again sinuous and wanton with far-fetched descriptions, into which most are led by imitation of poetic licence.* § 165, and adds: *Each is a vice, yet worse the one which comes from poverty than the one which comes from copiousness.* (namely in boys, § 247, 248.) Who does not see here that the arid is a vice in defect, which is opposed to the luxuriant manner of thinking, a vice sinning in excess, so that we may go by the middle road between poverty and a lavish apparatus?

§ 255. Yet I shall state my opinion more openly, by which I judge the ARID MANNER OF THINKING, more broadly, to be all that which can be observed to have less force and strength, M. § 515, than manly beauty would require, of which the languid manner of thinking is a certain modification. § 111. In this sense the aridity of thoughts will be the φαινόμενος [apparent] defect of everything, whether of richness, or of dignity, or of verisimilitude, or of vividness, or of persuasion, or of [p. 150] life, in the measure that a given beauty would require. § 22.

§ 256. From this notion Quintilian II. 4 seems to protest so strongly against *the arid master*, comparing him to *dry soils without any moisture*, which are above all *to be avoided by tender plants*. If boys light on such a one, they become *at once humble, and as it were looking at the ground, daring to raise nothing above everyday speech* (whether in richness, or dignity, or verisimilitude etc. § 255), *and take leanness for health and weakness for judgement, and while they are content to be free of fault, fall into this very fault, that they are without virtues.* From the same source, perhaps, he says that in his Institutes he has mixed in something of *brilliance* and *pleasantness*, lest *a jejune and arid* (which at least to the analogue of reason would seem of that kind) *handing-down of precepts should turn minds away and grate upon ears, especially ones so delicate.* III. 1. cf. § 122.

§ 257. When the same author, VIII. pr., says: *Neither did the Asiatics, or those corrupted in whatever other manner, fail to see the matters, or fail to arrange them: nor were those whom we call arid fools or blind in their causes, but the former lacked judgement and measure in expression, the latter lacked force!* I interpret these *Asiatics* and those *arid ones* from book XII. 10, where, of the *arid,* [p. 151] *the sapless* (exsucci) *and the bloodless: These are they*, he says, *who hold out for their feebleness the name of health, which is most contrary to it, who, because they cannot bear the clearer force,* M. § 515, *of eloquence, as though it were the sun, hide in the shade of a great name.* But of the *Asiatics*: *that they most of all lack judgement and measure.*

§ 258. 5) The bloodless manner of thinking I hold among the synonyms of the arid, from the signification, § 255–257, in the way that Cicero generally explains *bloodless* by *enervated*: *As if you should give a sword*, he says, *to a small boy, or to a feeble or weak old man, he would by his own impulse harm no one. — — — — so, when the consulate, like a sword, had been given to enervated and bloodless men, who by themselves could never have pricked anyone*, etc. Or. pro Sext. 24.

§ 259. 6) The jejune manner of thinking is a synonym of the dry, when the latter is read among the vices. § 248, 128. Thus Quintilian, § 254, and when he has taught that *for grammar*, the interpreter of the poets, *there is need of music, astronomy, philosophy, and no mean eloquence*, the last so that it may *speak of the matters of those arts properly and copiously*, he goes on: *So much the less are they to be borne who cavil at this art as thin* (which, signifying something sinister, coincides with the meagre, the dry and the subtle, [p. 152] when it is blamed) *and jejune.* l. 4. By the same reasoning Cicero reproves the opinion of the crowd, by which *Antonius* was held *more jejune, Crassus fuller*, and Quintilian sets forth the malevolence of *Cicero’s* detractors, when he reports that he was called by some *jejune and arid*, by others *Asiatic and redundant and excessive in repetitions.* To be blamed through envy for contrary things

is the praise of the golden mean.

Meanwhile, by a certain law of retaliation, with a certain Nemesis as it were conniving, § 251, 252 will show that the former accusation was brought against Cicero.

§ 260. Of the latter accusation he himself speaks in such a way as to seem once more to fix the aesthetic notion of jejuneness sufficiently, when at Quaest. Tusc. II. 3, of his own orations: *Some*, he says, *when they were overwhelmed by the copiousness of thoughts and words, said that they preferred jejuneness and hunger to richness and copiousness. Whence had arisen the kind of the Attics,* § 240, *unknown to those very men who professed to follow it, who have now fallen silent, laughed down almost by the forum itself.*

§ 261. 7) The frivolous manner of thinking I hold among the synonyms of the trifling, § 221, 245, so that I grant [p. 153] indeed that the second kind of βάθους [bathos] is frivolous, but not that all things frivolously thought can be drawn hither, when we consider the defect of absolute magnitude in those things which demand the slender manner of thinking. § 246. I have hesitated somewhat in drawing out the notions of the words which, as equivalents across various languages, the common herd of critics uses like terms of abuse, and which not one in a hundred of the little women who use them understands: so that, if ever they are to be employed by sound critics, the aestheticians may first consider with themselves what they have to prove, and whether they can set forth openly enough that what is signified by words of this kind has a place in the thing under judgement.

§ 262. In the second kind of βάθους [bathos], § 245, the uglier species will be that which depresses a matter thin indeed, yet great and worthy enough to be described in conformity with simply honourable manners, into what is unworthy even of simply honourable manners, § 224: the VILE MANNER OF THINKING ABOUT THIN THINGS. Into this deformity Plautus rather often falls, and Virgil’s Menalcas tends toward it, who, among other things, having rather harshly reproached:

*What will the masters do, when thieves dare such things? / Did I not see you, you wretch, catch Damon’s goat / by ambush, while Lycisca barked loudly?* etc. ecl. III.

[p. 154] But he is first drawn back by Damoetas’ apt admonition:

*Remember, however, that such things are to be cast up at men more sparingly,* etc.

and nonetheless goes on, with an honourable invitation to a musical contest. If we admit the mystical sense of the eclogue which they report, we shall not impute it to Virgil if Menalcas at first seems to speak somewhat coarsely.

§ 263. As the slender manner of thinking has opposed to it in general all the stains which disfigure the charm of thoughts, so also those noted in § 246–261, especially

*If the sap is withdrawn from the flock, if the milk from the lambs.*

But now, curious only about the παρεκβάσεων [deviations] proper to it, we observe II. THE SECOND KIND OF TUMIDITY, when about slender matters, or about slender matters made greater by the various consequences of greater ones, either truly or apparently such, thoughts are conceived greater than both their relative magnitude and their comparatively very small dignity would seem to permit. The first species of this will again be one that lifts slender things to the height to which, as a phenomenon, only middling matters ascend, § 208; the second, raising them still higher, § 203, until

*they strike the stars with their lofty head.*

§ 264. [p. 155] The second species of the tumidity of which § 263, Plautus lashes with the example of the *braggart soldier*, whose *Pyrgopolinices* opens the scene straightaway thus:

*See to it that the splendour of my shield be brighter* / *Than the rays of the sun are wont to be when the sky is clear,* etc.

with Artotrogus ready to answer, saluting *the man*

*Brave and fortunate, and of regal form,* / *Then a warrior: Mars would not dare to say* / *Nor to set his own virtues on a par with yours.* cf. Ter. Eun. IV. 6. v. 3 - 5.

From the same that saying of Quintilian VI. 2 draws us back: *To stir up tragedies in small (lawsuits) is just as if you wished to fit the mask of Hercules and the buskins on infants.*

§ 265. How wonderfully Petronius’s Trimalchio illustrates the same second species even then, when he does not seem to be playing the fool altogether, *holding the caduceus, with Minerva leading him, learning to reckon, made a steward, and at length snatched up by Mercury, who lifts him by the chin, into a lofty tribunal, with his own charm furnished with wings, so that it might look like Pegasus,* etc.; thus he paints the first, § 263, in Trimalchio’s *steward counting gold pieces*, who, to those pleading for his slave, replies: *It is not so much the loss that moves me as the negligence of a most worthless slave. He lost my dinner-clothes,* [p. 156] *which a certain client had given me on my birthday, Tyrian without doubt, but already once washed. Whatever it is, then, I make you a present of it.*

#### Sectio XX. Medium Cogitandi Genus. — Section XX. The Middle Manner of Thinking.

§ 266. II. THE MIDDLE MANNER OF THINKING (the even-tenored, mediocre, intermediate, and tempered) is that which thinks about mediocre matters, § 208, in such a way that it not only contains nothing repugnant not merely to their natural magnitude, but also to their relative dignity and nobility of character, but is even eminently suited and proportioned to them. Whether it may be called the intermediate of the thin and the sublime, or the middle between the thin and the sublime, is disputed by the commentators on Cic. Or. 21, § 208. Where Cicero: *It flows in one tenor*, he says, *as they put it, in speaking* (thinking), *bringing nothing but facility and evenness,* etc.

§ 267. I see that Cicero speaks not from his own definition but from that of others. But since the same man distinguishes the *forensic manner* of thinking of his *orations* *from the even-tenored and tempered* manner [p. 157] of thinking which he follows in his books *on philosophy*, de Off. I. 3, the suspicion grows in many that he himself wished to speak of the middle manner of thinking, § 266, when the discourse is of philosophical matters, though all the while he attributes to orations in general *a greater force of speaking*. From the same error this middle manner of thinking is ascribed to histories, § 126, as if proper and universal to each of them. Rather, that is called the EVEN-TENORED MANNER OF THINKING, MORE BROADLY, which everywhere conforms to its matters, lowering itself with them, rising with them; MORE STRICTLY and comparatively, that which handles those matters, and in that respect, in such a way that there is no need of so great a variety of thinking with respect to relative magnitude and dignity as would appear if either other things, or the same, but in a different and more varied respect, had to be beautifully weighed.

§ 268. The middle character of thoughts always has a certain evenness, since it neither transgresses the limits of slenderness by descent nor the bounds of sublimity by ascent, § 266; but not every evenness in thinking expresses at the same time the middle character; there is an evenness proper to the thin too, there is one proper to the sublime, § 267.

§ 269. I would have called the middle manner of thinking mediocre, were it not that a twofold ambiguity stood in the way: 1) [p. 158] that of those who, with the peripatetics, love that *mean* (mediocrity) *which lies between too much and too little*, in which sense too everything praiseworthy is mediocre; 2) that of those who not only side with Horace when he decrees:

*that poets be mediocre* / *Neither men, nor gods, nor the booksellers’ pillars have allowed;*

but also, for a similar reason, extend the same to all things that are to be thought more charmingly, which, as soon as they fall even a little from the highest pleasure,

*As, amid pleasant courses, a discordant music,* / *And thick ointment, and poppy with Sardinian honey,* / *Give offence, because the dinner could have been served without them:*

In this sense nothing praiseworthy is mediocre in aesthetics. And so the middle manner of thinking would seem mediocre to the former not alone, to the latter not in a good sense, § 266.

§ 270. Why the same manner of thinking is called tempered, § 267, we have already seen, since truly the TEMPERED MANNER OF THINKING either signifies, § 269, MORE BROADLY, a praiseworthy moderation in every force of thoughts, § 22, or, MORE STRICTLY, the same applied to such a theme amid respects of such a kind in which more vehement exertions of the mind could not [p. 159] be beautiful. The first is the manner of thinking such as you would expect of one strong enough

*An even mind, in hard things,* / *To keep, and not otherwise in good ones,* / *Tempered from insolent* / *Joy.*

When Cicero, de Or. II. 212: *There is no*, he says, *speech more tempered than that in which the harshness of the orator’s contention is seasoned by his own humanity, while the relaxation of mildness is strengthened by a certain gravity and contention:* assuredly he did not wish to give precepts of the middle manner of thinking, of which we now speak. He seems to me here to be speaking of the manner of thinking which I called tempered in the broader sense.

§ 271. In the Orator, however, 91 - 96, Cicero at first sight would be judged to speak of nothing more certainly than of the middle manner of thinking, of which we too, § 266. He interposes it between *the humble and the most ample*, calling it *moderate* and *tempered*. Meanwhile he describes it as *more robust, indeed, than the humble, in which, however, there is the very least of sinew, fuller than the humble, but less ornate and copious, more subdued, in which commonplaces are spoken without contention, such as* (orators) *generally turn out from the schools of the philosophers, each of whom is approved on his own, unless that stronger one be set beside him face to face,* (of whom afterwards, *ample, copious, grave.*) Already [p. 160] from this you might suspect that the tempered manner of thinking is here still, like the middle one in force and strength, § 270, being considered in general and, so to speak, in the abstract, and that it will only then win praise or blame when we have seen to which matters and forms of thinking beautifully it is applied, insofar as they differ among themselves, as it were in the concrete. Cicero himself will remove all doubt, at last recognising not the tempered manner of speaking among the eloquent, but only him as *eloquent* *who can speak of humble things subtly*, S. XVIIII, *of great things gravely, of mediocre things temperately.* And so it is not Cicero’s tempered manner, but the manner of thinking that thinks mediocre things (middle things, shrubs) temperately, which deserves to be called middle in truly beautiful things, § 266, and the same will be, more strictly, tempered in the sense of § 270, 268.

§ 272. The middle manner of thinking again admits its own degrees, § 266, according as it stands nearer either to the thin or to the sublime. Just as among the thin ones in Virgil’s bucolics, § 239, Scaliger finds the lowest manner of thinking, § 233, in *Moeris*, the middle in *Gallus*, the sublime in *Silenus*: so, in the middle manner of thinking of the same poet’s Georgics, he again places the lowest in *ploughing* and *sowing*, the middle in the *tale of Aristaeus*, the sublime in the *pestilence*. In like manner it seems to me that the middle manner of thinking in aesthetics, [p. 161] and the middle in orations, § 271, properly differ, though I do not deny that the latter too has its own degrees in turn. To this are referred Cicero’s orations *for the Manilian law, for Marcellus, for Cornelius Balbus, after his return, to the Quirites, and in the senate*. Among which, meanwhile, I gladly concede some difference of magnitude.

§ 273. In a long work the middle manner of thinking does not exclude certain sublime things, certain slender things, § 235. These not only by the foot and measure of § 272, but also such as, if they stood alone, would not be reckoned, without tumidity, even among mediocre matters. E.g.

*often the tiny mouse* / *Has set its house beneath the earth, and built its granaries.* Georg. I. 181. Virg.

*Often too the ant has carried out its eggs from its inmost chambers,* / *Wearing a narrow path.* Ib. 380.

Let this be an example of grand things mixed with middle ones

*The sun too, when Caesar was extinguished, pitied Rome,* / *When it veiled its shining head in dark rust,* / *And the impious ages feared eternal night.* etc.

unless here you wish to concede that Virgil, Georg. I. 465 - 510, has truly been carried somewhat higher than is just:

[p. 162] *As, when the four-horse chariots have poured out from the starting-gates,* / *They add themselves to the course, and, vainly pulling the reins,* / *The charioteer is borne along by the horses, nor does the car heed the bridle,* v. 512.

§ 274. Of the evenness of the middle manner of elegant thoughts we have already seen, § 268. Hence among the virtues proper to it, by Cicero’s account, § 266, facility alone would remain. But what is this virtue, and how indeed is it to be conceived, so that it remains proper and peculiar to the middle manner of thinking alone? I am not now playing Cicero’s interpreter. I shall rather indicate what facility peculiar to itself the very nature of this character demands: namely such and so great a measure of rounded brevity, and such graces of the remaining charms, § 22, neither less nor greater, as anyone who has attained a noble way of life and a culture of wit proportioned to it can be confident that he will either easily supply himself, or at least, without longer labour, beautifully understand and detect, § 266, if we again abstract from the difficulty of elocution, § 244, which does not belong here, § 18. Hence too the sweetness so greatly praised by Cicero in the tempered manner of speaking is attributed to this middle manner of thinking with respect to the spectator, in whom a noble way of life may be supposed, Cic. Or. 91, § 271.

§ 275. [p. 163] On account of the same facility, restricted in § 274, even the middle manner of thinking, when it is prudently aimed at, does not now set up as its personal object, i.e. those for whom, or for whose sake, it thinks above all, any but those to whom a noble way of living, and a charm of cognition proportioned to it, may be ascribed:

Not, *whatever he has once smeared upon paper, will he* / *Be eager that all returning from the oven and the cistern should know it,* / *Both boys and old women;*

*He does not labour that the crowd should admire him*, whether of those destitute of all honourableness, or of those remaining within the horizon of simply honourable morals and of the cognition which is wont to be its companion,

*Content with few readers.*

If he gains more, as happens, following the herd of those who went before, where the road goes, and now at the same time, by an almost fortuitous chance, where it ought to go, to these too he is sweet and easy, so far as that can be done short of βάθος [bathos], all the while silently feeling with Horace:

*It is enough for me that the knight applauds.*

But we should be egregiously deceived if here we wished to conceive in our mind either only those, or all those, born of noble station, § 211–214.

§ 276. [p. 164] From this praiseworthy manner of thinking middle matters, § 266, there departs, by sinning in defect, the THIRD KIND OF ΒΑΘΟΥΣ [bathos], which unfolds the same matters with lesser and lower ways of thinking than befit an object of middle magnitude and dignity, § 217. Whether its first species remains within those limits of thinking slenderly which would sit not so unfittingly upon slender matters, or whether its second species, at the same time the second way of thinking of triflers in a serious matter, § 245, depresses its mediocre matter altogether into the sphere of trifles and ineptitudes, § 191.

§ 277. Plautus will again give examples of both species, when *he fetches his matter from the middle*, and yet does not fail to slip either into an almost humble slenderness, or altogether into illiberal jests.

*Look how Plautus* / *Keeps up the parts of a youthful lover,* / *Of an attentive father,* — — — — / *How, with a sock not tightly bound,* § 235, 236, *he runs across the boards!* / *For he is eager to drop the coin into his coffer, after that* / *Careless whether the play fall, or stand on an upright heel.* Hor. II. ep. 1. 170.

[p. 165] While Catullus arraigns those guilty especially of the latter crime,

*Full of rusticity and witlessness,* / *The Annals of Volusius,* 32. al. 36.

he falls into the same, or a kindred, vice, which he more happily avoided in c. 39, in a quite similar matter, expressing the middle manner of thinking more beautifully. I mean that poem which for others is 44:

*O our farm, whether Sabine or Tiburtine,* etc.

§ 278. A more disgraceful degree of each species, especially of the second, § 276, is when one must think of middle things with some nobility of character, § 213, 214, but one thinks of the same with morals shining through everywhere that are scarcely simply honourable, or altogether dissolute and illiberal. This last will be the ABJECT WAY OF THINKING ABOUT MIDDLE THINGS. One wearies of obscene examples, to which belong the poem of Catullus 25, al. 29, and six hundred epithalamia. Here rather, to illustrate the first species, the mind of Menedemus in Ter. Heaut. Act. I. sc. 1 entire, to whom Chremes: *You have*

*Several slaves. Yet just as if there were nobody,* / *You yourself so attentively perform their duties:*

He himself of himself, a. V. sc. 1:

*Anything of these things fits me,* / *Which are said against a fool: blockhead, stump, ass, leaden.*

§ 279. [p. 166] From the middle manner of thinking, § 266, there departs in excess the THIRD KIND OF TUMIDITY, adorning middle objects with such great thoughts as sublime ones have the right to claim for themselves alone. Here belong the parenthyrsi of the literati who think themselves alone, with their own, to be something, whenever they think of their own affairs; the Seraphic and irrefragable doctors of the scholastics; their successors, the perpetual dictators, the greatest commanders and masters of horse of the republic of letters, who never speak except with Cicero, nor against anyone except as against Catiline; who, by their heart’s judgement, bestow and decree upon the towns where they hold their schools a forum, rostra, a capitol, upon the capitol conscript fathers and consuls, upon the consuls curule chairs, fasces, axes, upon their own selves triumphs,

*Do you, son of Syrus, of Dama or of Dionysius, dare* / *To hurl citizens from the rock, or hand them over to Cadmus?* Hor. I. Serm. 6.

§ 280. From these vices, § 276–279, confounded with one another, there arises at last the FLUCTUATING AND UNCERTAIN MANNER OF THINKING, insofar as it deserves to be opposed to the middle character of thoughts, when someone who has pursued the middle manner of thinking in meditating, while he cannot attain it, wanders now into the bounds of thin, now of sublime [p. 167] objects, not knowing where to plant his foot and halt his step. In vain does the nature of the manner he has chosen counsel a wit of this sort what the father counselled Phaethon;

*Neither press down, nor drive the chariot through the highest aether:*

like Phaëthon, nonetheless, also

This *will now seek the heights, now through declivities and ways* / *Headlong be borne, with the expanse of earth nearer.* Ov. Met. II. 205.

#### Sectio XXI. Sublime Cogitandi Genus. — Section XXI. The Sublime Manner of Thinking.

§ 281. III. THE SUBLIME MANNER OF THINKING (the magnificent, great, lofty, highest, full, cf. § 158, grave, copious, cf. § 116, ornate, strong, μεγαλοπρεπές [magnificent], ἀξίωμα [dignity], ὕψος [height], ὑψηλόν [lofty]) is that which so conforms to its truly grand matters of thinking, § 203–207, that, being at the same time sufficiently worthy of them, § 223, and best in character, § 228, it not only is not contrary to heroic virtue and to the way of thinking which this is wont to have as companion, if not as leader, but also properly agrees with character joined with singular [p. 168] majesty, § 213, 194.

§ 282. Not only in jest does Terence order *Davus to be snatched sublime indoors, and bound hand and foot*, but Horace too laughs at

Him who, *while he belches sublime verses, and wanders,* / Who, *as if intent on blackbirds, has fallen, the fowler,* / *Into a well, or a pit.*

For us here the sublimity that is placed seriously and in its proper place is to be explored. E.g. *Not only with brave, but also with shining arms did Cicero fight in the cause of Cornelius, when he had achieved by his speaking that the Roman people confessed their admiration not by acclamation only, but even by applause. Sublimity, assuredly, and magnificence, and brilliance, and authority wrung forth that thunder. Nor would so unwonted a praise have followed the speaker if his speech had been ordinary and like the rest.* Quint. VIII. 3. Here belongs the comparison of Elegy and Tragedy, Ov. Am. III. 1, where the former confesses:

*I would not compare sublime songs with mine,* / *Your palace overwhelms my little doors.*

§ 283. The magnificent is still more often besprinkled, as with a certain stain of slight reproach, by the concept of the superfluous and of ostentation that clings to it. Thus [p. 169] Clitipho in Terence, Heaut. I. 2, congratulates Clinia, who

*Has one well and chastely brought up, ignorant of the meretricious art.* / *Mine,* he adds, *is powerful, forward, magnificent, extravagant, noble.*

In like manner Tibullus: II. 6.

*I seek the camp; farewell Venus, and farewell girls.* / *I too have strength, and for me too the trumpet has been sounded.* / *Great things I speak. But when I have magnificently spoken great things* / *The closed doors shake the brave words out of me.*

about to return to his humble complaint, l. 5:

*Burn the wild one and rack him: let him not wish to say anything* / *Magnificent hereafter; tame his rough words.*

More clearly Cornelius, by whom *Atticus* is praised as *elegant, not magnificent, splendid, not extravagant, one who with all diligence affected a neatness not affluent; his furniture moderate, not much, so that it could be remarked in neither direction.* Cicero himself, the patron of the magnificent against the Attics, or as it were, § 248, does not abstain from this poorer signification of the word. Pro Rosc. Com. 5, *Do you so far love yourself, and look about you so magnificently?* he addresses him whom he will soon accuse of madness.

§ 284. [p. 170] By us, however, magnificence is here thought, § 281, according to Cicero’s description, de Inv. II. 163, as *the agitation and administration of great and lofty things, with a certain ample and splendid resolve of mind.* According to which he calls the *Roman people* lovers of *public magnificence*, enemies of *private luxury*, § 281, 165, Or. pro Mur.; from which most rightly, Off. I. 72: *By those who take up the commonwealth*, he says, *no less than by philosophers, and I do not know whether even more, both magnificence and a contempt of human affairs, of which I often speak, must be employed, and tranquillity of mind, and security, if indeed they are not to be anxious, and are to live with gravity and constancy.* According to the same notion of magnificence the philosopher concedes that it can also agree with private life, together with simplicity, § 213, l. c. 92: *One who keeps these precepts* (of private life) *may live magnificently, gravely, and spiritedly, and also simply, faithfully, and in a manner friendly to the life of men.* From the same I conceive as most true the saying of Agamemnon in Petronius, *that nothing is magnificent to him which pleases boys.*

§ 285. Hence then let us gather what idea of the magnificent manner of thinking the orator formed for himself in his mind, who himself knew, and hinted, [p. 171] l. II. de Or. 89, that he was as it were a certain model of it, when he relates that *Sulpicius*, who had promised to imitate the speaker and Crassus, *had altogether been led by nature herself into that magnificent manner of Crassus*, S. II; adding, however, that *he could not have made sufficient progress had he not fallen in with the same zeal and imitation, and so grown accustomed to speak as to behold Crassus with his whole mind and all his soul.* Hence his first precept is imitation, § 56.

§ 286. Nor does the denomination of the *great* lack its own ambiguity, § 291. I shall not drag in here that a great man seems sometimes to have been said for a dolt, § 179. Assuredly, that a great tongue is not said in praise, mythology teaches from Horace, where he thus addresses Apollo:

*God, whom the offspring of Niobe, as the avenger* / *Of a great tongue, and Tityos the ravisher,* / *Felt.* IV. od. 6.

Nor do I think Juvenal’s *great friends*, nor Horace’s *great boys*, are sincerely praised by this appellation. Meanwhile let us in good faith call that which is thought great relatively and comparatively, § 178, aptly conformed to truly great things, § 291. For a deeper understanding of this let us note further, on § 203–207, concerning great matters, 1) that *some are clearer, others greater.* To confirm which saying Pliny devoted the whole ep. XVI of l. III, [p. 172] illustrating it with examples bearing on this. 2) that the great things pertaining here seem scarcely verisimilar to most. Horace relates to someone

*known to him by name only*

that in Maecenas’s house he has no need to push others aside, so that he may penetrate more intimately into his favour, nor does it *stand in his way*

*That this one is richer, or because more learned. To each one* / *His own place. A great thing you tell, scarcely credible.*

the other, I know not who, retorts.

§ 287. Let the lofty manner of thinking, § 291, and the too lofty, § 217, 218, not be confounded. The former Horace paints, IV. od. 9.

*As judge he preferred the honourable to the useful, and* / *Rejected the gifts of the guilty with lofty* / *Countenance, and through the opposing throngs* / *Victorious unfurled his arms.*

And Cicero, Tusc. quaest. II. 11: *The brave are aided not only by fortune, as the old proverb has it, but much more by reason, which by certain quasi-precepts confirms the force of fortitude. Nature begot you as one lofty, plainly, and high, and looking down upon human things. And so a discourse held against death easily settled in a brave mind,* etc.

§ 288. [p. 173] If you see some holding the copious and full manner of thinking for the sublime, § 281, then, mindful, § 247, 187, in order to judge what truth there is in their assertions, I fix the eyes of my mind before all things on the summary formula, as it were, of the virtue proper and peculiar to this manner of thinking: Bring into your grand theme in thinking so much rounded brevity, so much verisimilitude, etc., § 22, neither more nor less, as you can be confident that anyone raised to heroic virtue and to its companion, the way of thinking, and to elevation of wit, would either himself have sprinkled upon themes of this kind, or at least, when brought forward by others, would beautifully understand, § 281.

§ 289. Hence the sublime manner of thinking, much less still than the middle, § 275, chooses just anyone from the crowd as its spectator, its personal object, and as him for whose sake above all it thinks. Not caring much for the smallest wits or breasts, nor fearing to dazzle rather than refresh eyes of this sort, it designates for itself, as it were, a twofold race of mortals as its temple, in which it labours gravely in delighting. 1) The highest men, excelling not only in superior wits, but also distinguished in natural disposition, and having attained a singular majesty in character by habit, as a certain second [p. 174] nature. To these, when it thinks of the gravest things, in which however they are to be supposed to be engaged daily, with which to be familiar, in which as it were to dwell, it thinks elegantly and magnificently, and fully indeed and perfectly, § 158, yet so far is it from communicating its thoughts to them with a certain exceptional copiousness and a manifest breadth of meditation, that it is rather studious even of relative brevity, S. XIIII, and of a truly grand Laconism.

§ 290. Jupiter to Mercury after a brief exposition of the things that displease him in Aeneas, Virg. Aen. IV. 236:

*Let him sail. This is the sum, let this be our message.*

Mercury, the faithful interpreter of his parent, to Aeneas, when he had reported the rest by way of reproach, does not expressly add even this very mandate, brief as it is, v. 275, since pious Aeneas could gather it from what was set forth:

*He burns to go away in flight, and leave the sweet lands,* / *Thunderstruck by so great a warning and command of the gods.* / *He calls Mnestheus and Sergestus and brave Cloanthus:* / *Let them silently fit out the fleet, gather their companions to the shores,* / *Prepare arms, and what the cause may be of changing their plans* / *Dissemble.* v. 290.

[p. 175] Is it not briefly enough that great Aeneas speaks to the chief of his companions of great matters, in which these had long been expert?

§ 291. 2) The other race of men, whom the sublime manner of thinking should strive above all to serve and to please, § 289, are those who either seem able to be referred, at least aesthetically, to those described above, yet are to be supposed still to be ignorant of some lofty and signally sublime thing, or altogether through error to hold it cheap; or who indeed bear beneath the breast the sparks of a great soul and of heroic virtue, having that which can be raised and incited to any sublime things whatever, yet do not always have ready to hand as much present magnanimity as suffices for immediately embracing, with the impetus they deserve, certain grand things which are quite new to them, or set forth to them very briefly, § 288. To both the sublime manner of thinking, now with gravity preserved, not only richly, but even more copiously, will splendidly declare the unheard-of magnitude of its matter, its most ample dignity, its incredible weight, and its majesty most worthy of a certain divine ardour, in the chaste and matronly ornament of the most exquisite figures, § 116.

§ 292. Let us now imagine to ourselves in mind either the [p. 176] Athenians, for whom Demosthenes, or the Romans, for whom Cicero, laboured at times in their orations to think truly beautifully of sublime things. Or let us even substitute for them, from our own way of living, a sermon to be held on the gravest moments of sacred things. How few can here be thought of as belonging to the first kind of personal objects, § 289? But even among these themselves, however many they be, there can nonetheless be some who, otherwise the best, and rigid in a stricter virtue, or strenuous defenders of the public good, would meanwhile, now perhaps ignorant of that which is now being treated, especially if it be very determinate, or altogether singular and individual, now altogether prejudiced against it, attribute to it little value and esteem, unless we were taught better, § 291.

§ 293. All the rest, for whose benefit the orator can make the sublime manner of thinking his own, will be those in whom more generous instincts certainly lie hidden, in whom there are things which can be illuminated by divine light and inflamed by sacred incitements, but now a good part approach less attentive, others with a mind quite slack, or for this reason, that they rashly enough assume that what they are to hear is already known well enough to them. In these circumstances the sublime manner of thinking of the orator, a good many of whose words to these can [p. 177] fly past the hearer, which a reader would perhaps have weighed more maturely on a repeated reading, § 170, will certainly require much copiousness, much richness and amplitude for its perfect fullness, and an almost Platonic breadth, § 158, 116.

§ 294. For tragedy the copiousness and richness, even comparative, that are still more necessary along with its sublimity have been made so by the one who is

*to be detained by grateful novelty, / the spectator, who has done with the sacred rites and is drunk and lawless,* § 297–293. H. A. P.

*Because those who are more in number, less in virtue and honour, / unlearned and stupid and ready to fight it out / if the knights disagree, call in the middle of the songs / for a bear or for boxers; for in these the rabble delights. / But even the knight’s pleasure has now all migrated from the ear / to the ungrateful eyes and to empty delights.* Hor. II. ep. 1. § 275.

§ 295. Since these things are so, let us draw the controversy together to its sinew. § 288. 1) There is a certain sublime manner of thinking which is at once [p. 178] not only full but also copious, and this, if it has once crossed the bounds of relative brevity, must be strenuously opulent and display a truly regal apparatus and a magnificence that bears witness to public state, § 289–294. 2) Not every sublime manner of thinking is at the same time copious, § 289; in relatively great things rather the greatest things are relatively brief as well, § 207, 209. 3) Not every copious manner of speaking is sublime. S. VIII. § 281. So let those who do not wish to be adorned with the titles of the Asiatics cease to hold the copious and the sublime as synonyms. Longinus generally attributes to Demosthenes the ὕψος ἀπότομον [abrupt sublimity], to Cicero the sublime ἐν χύσει [in diffusion]. S. XII.

§ 296. If the sublime manner of thinking is called *grave* (δεινόν [forceful]), § 281, it has received that name from a certain relative and comparative gravity and from its highest degree, since every aesthetic magnitude demands some gravity too, an absolute one at least, § 189. If it is called ornate, § 281, the ambiguity can be removed after the pattern of § 295, and it is generally corrected by the addition of another word as a kind of synonym, e.g. *ornate* and *grave*. If we interpret Scaevola’s *men ornate and grave in speaking*, in Cicero, de Or. I. 42, as those who can attain sublimity in thinking and speaking, we shall see that they, set between the philosophers, rightly refute *Crassus,* [p. 179] *who had assumed, as if by his own right, that the orator can range most copiously over the discussion of every subject.* § 125. The incongruity of what Crassus asserts would be increased if we confounded the most copious with the sublime, § 295, and with the ornate, l. c. 50. § 257.

§ 297. Those who at last call the sublime manner of thinking *strong*, § 281, seem once more to wish to speak of a certain relative and comparative strength, which generically must indeed be ascribed to this manner of thinking in the highest degree, § 288. Now the slender manner demands some sinew and blood, § 242–244, the middle more, § 274, the sublime the most. M. § 515. Let us look at its remarkable force and efficacy a little more distinctly, so that it may be the easier to distinguish it from the mask of a spurious sublime.

§ 298. 1) The sublime manner of thinking has generically the greatest riches, § 158, 288, which it sometimes almost wholly leaves aside, § 160, shining with only a single, but most precious, diamond, as it were, § 289, from which you may silently infer for yourself the remaining wealth, § 177, 148; and sometimes it displays them more magnificently, § 293. Hence then the περιαγωγαὶ καὶ περιβολαί [circuits and amplifications], a certain circuitous and accumulated manner of meditating, not without synathroisms, § 148. Yet not every sublime meditation, nor the highest among the sublime [p. 180] ones, is circuitous and accumulated, much less is every circuitous manner of meditating, and still less a certain length and diffusion or distortion of the signs alone, e.g. of the diction and the words, grand and sublime. § 295. Aristophanes has a word of six verses, and there creeps along Plautus’s

*Whatyouhaveoncesnatchedyouwillneverafterwardsbesnatchedof,* § 237.

§ 299. 2) It preserves the magnitude of the things to be thought, even the natural magnitude, § 181, such that it neither, compared with the principal theme or with the persons, whether speaking or personal objects, passes away into a comparative nothing, vanishing, e.g. § 198, nor, on the contrary, under that same comparison which I have just mentioned, does the principal theme, for instance, when the greatest magnitude in great things is always expected of any thoughts whatever, if those things, the theme for instance, have indeed been grand, yet not the greatest among great things, § 207, rather lower, obscure and diminish than amplify, adorn and exalt. The sublime manner of thoughts has a care for a dignity that is generically the greatest, but at the same time, in the most dignified things, for a degree that is, so to speak, specific, § 207, so that it is neither raised too high nor lowered, and here too it requires that even of those parts or sides from which the eminent things are to be contemplated as such, those that tend to mediocrity, or to slenderness altogether, be carefully guarded against among the various sides and manners [p. 181] of thinking that have been not badly chosen, § 215, unless in a long work this too has its place at some time. § 235.

§ 300. As an example of this manner of thinking Cicero himself cites his own oration for Rabirius, and by the same right his oration for Milo, rising little by little, the second Catilinarian, the second Philippic and the speech against Piso are referred to it. In the Aeneid Scaliger finds the lowest of the grand manners of thinking in the description of the games, Book V, the middle in Book I, the highest in Books II and VI. Let me add an example from Scripture. Let the great among the greatest be that which strongly pleased Longinus, S. IX: *Let there be light, and there was.* Greater is: Τετέλεσται [It is finished]. The greatest: *Let God be all in all.*

§ 301. 3) The sublime manner of thinking pursues verisimilitude, § 22, but properly only that which can be comprehended and approved by superior wits and more elevated minds, § 288, though the same may be scarcely credible to the rabble, § 286, and may not find easy credence even among sufficiently noble minds. § 213. This verisimilitude of its own it either leaves to the silent ἀναθεωρήσει [reconsideration] of the spectator, § 177, 289, or proves so luminously, § 291–294, that the truth which at first was abhorrent, or most abhorrent, to our senses now at last produces not only no hatred, [p. 182] but even admiration and longing for itself. § 293.

§ 302. Pompey’s sublime saying, when he was embarking in a storm against the sailor’s dissuasion, It is necessary that I sail, it is not necessary that I live, is condemned by a certain Frenchman as an open contradiction. Let no mind equal to Pompey’s defend it, and let no Caesar, or enemy, accuse it. For both will understand it beautifully. § 288, 301. *It is a great thing to bring it about that someone understands what that true and simple good is which cannot be separated from honour.* Cic. qu. ac. l. 7. What sort of thing that good is, surely, not only *Epicurus denies* in words *that he can conceive it without pleasures that move the senses*, but many more down to this day deny it by their deeds, among them even those in whom the aesthetician would suspect the highest morals and an excellent virtue. And yet the naked truth of that good is not only argued *acutely, subtly, obscurely*, as it seems to the analogue of reason, § 121, *in the* more severe *study of philosophy*, *than which* no *greater or better gift given to man by the gods* seems to Varro and Plato to exist: its verisimilitude has already been set forth in so many aesthetic and truly sublime meditations too, and is set forth almost daily, so that on this side of human affairs little or nothing seems left to be desired. § 301.

[p. 183] § 303. 4) This great manner of thoughts shines forth in the highest light, § 22, but in a light for the bearing, the grasping and the enjoying of which the eyes of eagles are chiefly required § 288. These it sometimes already presupposes, § 289, and sates with that splendour at which Minerva’s own owl would be dazzled; sometimes, by a wonderful art, it so gradually entices those who are naturally good but not yet accustomed to drinking in so many rays, or who have grown unaccustomed to them again, and, by gently feeding them, at once sharpens and strengthens them, that at last they marvel at the midday sun and at everything plain and perspicuous there where not so long before they had seemed to themselves to find blindness and darkness and obscurity. § 291.

§ 304. That you may be able in an example to distinguish this truest light of sublime things, an apparent obscurity, from darkness, read, if you will, the Sibyl’s prophecy, Aen. VI. 83-97, where the poet would have had, as it were, the privilege of forgetting true perspicuity on account of the verisimilar mist of oracles. Although he himself says

*With such words from the shrine the Cumaean Sibyl / sings her dreadful riddles and bellows in her cave, / wrapping true things in obscurities,*

yet you will find things truly sublime, whose meaning and whose weight [p. 184] anyone from the common crowd of the lettered may now understand, with meanwhile nothing of obscurity in the words and in the manner of thinking, except what is ordinary in things to come and, because man, even the greatest, but still a mere man, lacks the knowledge of their complete determination, strongly necessary, and so, after the event and the fulfilment, sufficiently passable for one versed in the history or the story.

§ 305. 5) The sublime manner of thinking persuades, § 22, but it labours to persuade only, or at any rate chiefly, those in whose inmost hearts a more remarkable magnitude of heart, § 45, has already sown, with certainty, the seeds of virtue and of right morals. Since these men have within them principles not only known but also, for their own sake, not in need of proofs, it now builds up its persuasion in three words, as they say, or even in a single one, in the briefest terms, as upon unmoved foundations, and now, according to the different condition of its personal objects, § 289–294, it heaps up so many weights, so many maxims, so many supports, to make its theme more certain, not those trite ones, but ones always breathing something great, so that the greater minds are most fully persuaded, the lesser are overwhelmed and are driven to the most prudent thing that can be hoped of them, to silence.

[p. 185] § 306.
