An example of persuasion, and indeed of the most efficacious kind, by a single word Suetonius has in his Caesar, 70. *When the men of the tenth legion at Rome, with enormous threats and even the greatest danger to the city, were demanding their discharge and their rewards, while the war was then blazing in Africa, he did not hesitate to go to them, though his friends tried to deter him, and to dismiss them. But with a single word, in that he addressed them as Quirites instead of soldiers, he so easily turned them about and bent them that they at once answered that they were soldiers, and of their own accord followed him to Africa, though he refused them.* A little more diffusely the same Caesar, versed in true aesthetic sublimity, persuaded his men to lay aside their fear of the approaching enemy *by amplifying and falsifying* the number of his forces, 66.

§ 307. *Asinius Pollio thinks that Caesar’s commentaries were composed with too little regard for the truth*, 56; yet how much persuasion there is in most of what he writes! Reading them you would think that those had acted most rightly who, if Catullus is to be believed, could do nothing

*Except devour greasy patrimonies.*

I am not deterred by the opinion of those who judge these commentaries humble from making mention of them here. Because even boys grasp their words well enough and some sense, as far as they can for their age, they are not on that account humble, they do not display the slender manner [p. 186] of thinking, and, unless you think that all history must embrace the middle manner of thinking, § 267, I have no doubt in ascribing to them an even-tenored manner of thinking indeed, § 267, 268, and a tempered one, § 270, and, with Cicero, one *naked, straight and, with every ornament of speech stripped off as it were a garment, charming*, yet, as the matter requires, sublime, so that, *while he wished* (or pretended to wish) *that others should have materials ready from which those who wanted to write history might draw, he has perhaps gratified the fools who want to curl those materials with the curling-iron, but he has deterred sensible men from writing.*

§ 308. 6) The sublime manner of thoughts reigns in this, that it is able, § 288, now to incite minds, but minds more capable of a nobler impulse, § 289–294, to the highest inflammation and an almost divine frenzy, now to draw them back from the most turbulent commotions of the mind, arisen from some trifling matter, to a becoming serenity and the tranquillity of a loftier spirit, according as this or that state of minds is more suitable for heroes. § 213.

§ 309. Aeneas, Book II, though Hector in a dream dissuades him in vain, v. 295, *madly takes up arms,*

*fury and anger drive / [p. 187] his mind headlong, and it comes to him how beautiful it is to die in arms,* v. 315.

*At the words of Othryades he is borne into the flames and into arms, whither the grim Erinys, / whither the roar and the shouting raised to heaven call.* v. 338.

*He addresses his companions, who join him, met by moonlight.*

*Thus fury is added to the spirits of the young men,* v. 355.

Still *with maddened mind* Aeneas *is borne along* v. 588, and there is

*a knot worthy of a deliverer,*

to draw the pious hero back from the defence of his country. Lo! the god is at hand, his mother, taking him by the right hand and strengthening her speech, which soothes her son’s mind and heart, with an ecstatic vision. v. 589-625.

#### Sectio XXII. Vitia Sublimi Opposita. — Section XXII. Vices Opposed to the Sublime.

§ 310. In thinking about grand matter one must beware of I. A FOURTH KIND OF (MANIFEST) ΒΑΘΟΥΣ [bathos], in which it at once appears that the thoughts formed by the mind [p. 188] are smaller and lower than the magnitude of the objects, which is comparative and generically the greatest, § 207. § 217. In this you may again easily detect four species: 1) if, within what is generically the greatest, excellent things are indeed thought again in a manner suitable and worthy enough of the greatest things in general, but not so excellent as the themes themselves seem to deserve, 2) if forests are made equal to shrubs, 3) if forests are made equal to tamarisks, 4) if the highest things are pressed down altogether to the ground and reduced to mere trifles by one who means, if the gods please, to think charmingly within his mind. The last species will constitute the third class of triflers in a serious matter, and indeed the most shameful, § 245.

§ 311. To the first species of the βάθους [bathos] belonging here let us refer the manner of thinking which Longinus already censures in Homer, S. VIIII, by which he, *out of the men* who took part in *the affairs of Troy*, *as far as he can*, while he *makes gods*, makes *men out of gods, whose wounds, quarrels, revenges, tears, bonds and unhappy immortality* he sings. Against this vice Horace’s precept seems to be directed:

*It will make much difference whether a god speaks or a hero.* A. P. 114.

For from this verse, with Bentley, I order *Davus* into exile. To the same species of the sublime lowering itself somewhat beneath its own majesty, § 310, belongs Homer’s *decline from the Iliad into the love of stories in the Odyssey,* [p. 189] observed by the same Longinus, S. VIIII. On account of which he compares Homer in the Odyssey to *the setting sun, whose magnitude remains without the intensity* of its heat and strength § 298–309. There is not, he says, the ἴσον τόνον [same tension] of Homer in the Odyssey as in the Iliad, οὐδ' ἐξωμαλισμένα τὰ ὕψη καὶ ἱζήματα μηδαμοῦ λαμβάνοντα [nor are the heights kept level, nowhere admitting a sinking]; in short, *old age, but the old age of Homer.*

§ 312. Of the second species, § 310, let the example be Timaeus in Longinus, S. IIII, who says that *Alexander the Great subdued all Asia in fewer years than* those within which *Isocrates composed his panegyric oration on the war against the Persians. An excellent comparison, forsooth, of that Macedonian with the Sophist. Isocrates surpasses the Lacedaemonians in energy. They spend thirty years in the conquest of Messene, he only ten in composing his panegyric oration.* Here too, besides comparisons, I would bring the thin, or at any rate middle, parts of sublime things and the various matters they call circumstances, externals, especially if the mention of them, being less necessary, does not seem very brief either. Does even great Maro nod sometimes? e.g. Aen. l. 177.

*And first Achates struck a spark from flint, / caught the fire in leaves, and gave dry / nourishment round about, and quickly took the flame in the tinder.*

[p. 190] § 313. To the third species of a manner of thinking about sublime things that is lower than is right, § 310, I do not yet dare to refer all comparisons of heroes with beasts and living creatures. Meanwhile, however, in this age in which we live I think that only lions and eagles still hold their place in the greater theatre, especially with the aid of the art which they call heraldry or blazonry. Almost all the remaining animals, moved from their place and rank, have vanished from heroic works when praise is intended, until the philosophers have settled more clearly among themselves whether they are machines or spirits. Homer’s heroes still, like dogs, if they do not bark, at least fight. Virgil’s heroes, Aen. II. 355,

*like ravening / wolves in a black mist, whom the wicked madness of the belly / has driven out blind, and whose whelps, left behind, / wait with dry throats: through weapons, through enemies / they go.*

You may recognise among arms and men Tityrus, who had learned how

*grievous the wolf is to the folds.*

§ 314. Horace’s Drusus, now, IV. od. 4, is compared more handsomely 1) to the eagle, whom not *the wicked madness of the belly*, § 313, but

[p. 191] *once youth and inherited vigour / drove from the nest, ignorant of toils.*

But is it sufficiently worthy of him

*that the king of the gods has entrusted to it the kingdom over the roaming birds, / Jupiter, having found it faithful / in the matter of fair-haired Ganymede?*

⁠2) To the lion. Let it pass. But are there also, *beneath the Rhaetian Alps, right hands of the Vindelici armed with Amazonian axes,*

*and troops long / and widely victorious,*

*a roe intent / on the glad pastures?*

If there are, is the name of Drusus *memorable in the punishment* of them?

*Has the victory its praise?*

§ 315. *Ho there! that is enough now, ho there!* poet! / *You still go on, and seek to go further.* / Greater comparisons than with eagles and lions / do you know, greatest of poets?

He compares the princes of the Roman youth, whom Augustus held in the place of sons, 3) to bullocks:

*There is in bullocks, there is in horses the virtue / of their sires.*

[p. 192] In this too will be found an example and a likeness of itself,

*To thin great things by small measures,*

and, although I am not unaware that oxen were once held in greater reverence than now, nevertheless, not for the sake of carping at Horace, but for love of the truth, I judge it βάθος [bathos], and if anyone now slips into it on a similar occasion, if not the *Romans*, § 212, at least

the Germans *will raise a laugh, horse and foot,*

and *the fathers*, whom Bentley substituted for *the foot*.

§ 316. The fourth species, § 310, of the vice mentioned may be graced by that old line:

*Jupiter meanwhile bespat the Alps with white snow.*

and by the more recent one: *mortals of a cleaner nose*, when it pleases them to parade the witty and the elegant

*in grand-sounding measures and with tragic bellowing.*

For let Horace’s *man of a well-wiped nose* be allowed to pass,

*and the childhood of a dripping nose*

in Juvenal, together with the *belching* persons of the same, since they did not then intend to write sublime things. § 310. Here rather belongs the *Sorrow, from whose nostrils the humours flowed,* of Hesiod, or whoever is the author of the poem called the *Shield*, which Longinus censures, S. VIIII.

*You are a burden to us, and you often wipe your nose; go out / quickly and make haste. Another comes with a dry nose.* Iuu.

[p. 193] Longinus accuses *Plato and Xenophon* of the same vice, *as sometimes forgetting themselves* διὰ τὰ οὕτως μικροχαρῆ [through such petty conceits], *although they come from the Socratic wrestling-school*, e.g. there where *Xenophon, writing on the Lacedaemonian republic, calls the pupils that are in the eyes modest maidens.*

§ 317. Of all these degrees of manners of thinking about grand things more lowly than is right, those are always the more shameful in which not only the magnitude, perhaps the natural magnitude, but also at the same time the dignity and the love of rigid honour and of severer virtue that is necessary in great things are wounded by THE VILE MANNER OF THINKING ABOUT SUBLIME THINGS, e.g. the reward for abominable pandering ascribed to Jupiter, § 314, and a great part of Ovid’s Metamorphoses, which sometimes proceeds to such shameful things that the poet himself, who was certainly not chaste, thought it advisable to preface them, X, 300.

*I shall sing dreadful things. Far from here, daughters, far from here be, parents. / Or if my songs shall soothe your minds, / let me in this part be denied belief, and do not believe the deed, / or, if you will believe it, believe also the punishment of the deed.*

Most rightly and most gravely Horace warns, A. P. 227,

*lest whatever god, whatever hero shall be brought on, / [p. 194] lately seen in royal gold and purple, / should migrate with humble speech into dark taverns.*

Here belong the mutual insults of heroes, examples of which there is no need to seek in Homer, since a few centuries ago they still stained the public writings of illustrious princes, or even their very titles.

§ 318. Horace, continuing, l. c.,

*lest, while he avoids the ground, he catch at clouds and empty things,*

leads these men to the vice that those who would think greater things must avoid, II. A FOURTH KIND OF TUMIDITY, which in vain wraps excellently great things in the empty appearance of sublimity in thinking. Since its meteors are not truly matched by an illustrious matter and object, every tumidity of this kind will be a βάθος κρυπτικόν [hidden bathos], § 217, 310. We shall see its chief species, as it were in their very source, if, mindful of the principal truths about the sublime, we attend to their false conversion, hence to the distortion, hence to the mask and the shade of sublimity in those who embrace a tumid and puffed-up cloud in place of Juno.

§ 319. I. The sublime manner of thinking is very beautiful. This is most true, § 210. Hence [p. 195] badly converted propositions are inferred: 1) Whatever is to be rendered more beautiful must be made sublime. Hence the kinds of tumidity considered up to now; 2) pretty, neat and dainty things will be sublime. This first source will yield the first species of the tumidity now to be considered, § 318, if, through an affected pursuit of beauty, heaping up all cosmetics and flowers, and whatever there is anywhere of pigments and ornament, periods rounder than any ball, subtle antitheses and the very figures of diction to the point of nausea, men dream that they have written sublimely, § 165–307.

§ 320. II. Sublime things are sometimes very brief, sometimes excellently copious and overflowing, § 298. Hence by conversion they are most wretchedly twisted: 1) the briefer, 2) the more diffuse you are, the more sublime you will be. Hence a twofold species of tumidity, 2) that by which a man seems to himself very sublime whose

*sentence did not differ from the oracular Delphi,*

provided only it be brief, even if it falls into what may be called a very long brevity. § 171. Here belongs the sublime *urbanity* of Trimalchio, where, *forcing out Carpe* repeatedly *in the slowest voice, he called Carpus, who was carving the dish, and gave him his order with the same word.* Petr. But here belongs more closely the case when someone, e.g. concerning something truly grand, about which above all his mind would have to be set forth if he wished to think anything sublime, [p. 196] applies that trite saying: *About this it is better to say nothing than little*, and so says neither nothing, nor little, nor much, nor anything great. Meanwhile he applauds himself as if he had said the greatest things. For why not? Has not everything been dispatched most briefly?

§ 321. 3) That by which another man seems to himself sublime when he so circumscribes everything that he does not think even “farmyard cock” a long enough expression, but prefers that phrase of Juvenal’s:

*that by which the hen is bitten by her husband.*

Here belongs rather the example of frigidity adduced by Vossius. Sextus Rufus has: *through D. Brutus we obtained the Spains, and reached as far as Gades and the Ocean.* The same thing Florus relates thus tumidly: *D. Brutus, somewhat more widely, having traversed as victor the Gallaeci and all the peoples of Gallaecia, and the river of oblivion dreaded by the soldiers, and the shore of the Ocean, did not turn his standards back before he had beheld, not without a certain fear and horror of sacrilege, the sun falling into the seas and its fire overwhelmed by the waters.*

§ 322. III. The sublime manner of thinking thinks the greatest things (generically) in such a way that it never slips down to minute things (destitute of all aesthetic magnitude), § 299. Hence the principle is converted: Whoever, then, thinks about some matter as about [p. 197] the best and greatest thing that ever is, was or will be, in such a way that he raises everything that surrounds it, stands about it and in any way concerns it to the point beyond which there is no further even for the imagination, he, assuredly, will think sublimely. Here you have the fourth species of the tumidity belonging here, § 318. This is that *tumidity* which, according to Longinus, S. III, *wishes to rise higher than the sublime, but, being puerile, is altogether opposed to the sublime*, § 318. An example will be given by the description of the storm in Aen. l. I, compared with the one which Lucan has, swelling as much as his own waves, l. V, in which

*the earth was added to* (Neptune’s) *second kingdom, / when the sea rolled the nations together, when Tethys / would not endure any shores, content to be held by the sky. / Then too so great a mass of sea would have grown up to the stars, / had not the ruler of the gods pressed down the waves with clouds. / The wave receives the rain in the clouds,* etc.

§ 323. IV. Sublime things exceed the belief of some people, § 301, 286. Hence, by what consequence? it is nevertheless inferred: the more lavishly you heap up things that exceed all belief, the more sublimely you will think. This is the fifth species of the tumidity now in question, § 318, that of those who seek sublimity in incredibility of whatever sort. In these Ovid will seem to have transcended himself [p. 198] when he says, Met. VII. 104:

*Behold! the bronze-footed bulls breathe out Vulcan from adamantine nostrils, / and the grass touched by the vapours / burns, and as full furnaces are wont to resound, / so their breasts, rolling the flames shut up within, / and their scorched throats resound.*

Petronius laughs at these sublime men, *who hear and see nothing of the things we have in common use, but tyrants writing edicts in which they command sons to cut off the heads of their own fathers*, etc.

§ 324. V. Sublime things do not shine with everything to everyone, § 303. Hence it is foolishly concluded: the more obscure, the more sublime. Here you have the sixth species of tumidity and of foolish sublimity, § 318. To its friends Tacitus surpasses Livy, Lucan and Statius surpass Virgil, as regards sublimity, because they are often harder to understand. Let us look at an example. Virgil to Augustus:

*Long since the palace of heaven has grudged you to us, Caesar, / and complains that you care for the triumphs of men,* Georg. I. 503.

Statius, because he cannot have the glory of invention, will nevertheless, on account of his obscurity, seem to its partisans to have snatched the palm of sublimity from Virgil, addressing Titus thus:

[p. 199] *O glory added to the fame of Latium!* / *Whom, as you take up the new undertakings of a father ripe in years,* / *Rome desires for herself for ever: though a narrower* / *bound drive all the stars, and the shining tract of heaven* / *free of the Pleiades, of Boreas and of the gaping thunderbolt* / *call upon you; though the curber of the fire-footed horses* / *himself press upon your locks his arch that gleams on high,* / *or great Jupiter yield to you an equal* / *share of the sky: may you remain content with the reins of men,* / *lord of waves and land, and bestow the stars.*

§ 325. This Statian periphrasis of Virgil recalls to my mind a new modification in this species of tumidity, since it explicates the tumid manner of thinking, which is quasi-obscure, § 303, through what is truly obscure, and at the same time, with some Asiatic apparatus or other, crushes all sublimity. If the sailor to whom, as he feared the storm, Caesar said, *Be of good cheer; you carry Caesar*, did not understand Caesar—a point I shall not contest—the question is whether it is credible that he understood Lucan’s Caesar, when the latter, verbose indeed, addresses the ship’s helmsman, book V:

*Scorn the threats*, he says, *of the sea, and to the raging wind* / [p. 200] *give your sail. If, with heaven for your warrant, you refuse Italy,* / *seek me. The only just cause of fear for you is this,* / *not to know your passenger, whom the powers never* / *desert, of whom fortune then deserves ill,* / *when she comes after prayers.*

Thus Lucan’s Caesar goes on commenting somewhat obscurely on the true Caesar for ten more verses, and yet the poet is almost angry with his own Caesar

*for having said no more:*

§ 326. VI. Sublime things persuade so much, § 308, that Longinus, S. I, says they rather *carry one away out of oneself*, and, *bringing an irresistible force and impetus, raise themselves above every hearer*, than persuade. Hence the prejudice: if I draw great arguments from great things, great arguments from great things will be most persuasive. From this again a seventh species of tumidity, of which § 318, shows itself, now in a vast abundance of persuasives, when there would have been need of almost none, now in a train of enthymemes fetched from the greatest height, now above all in an empty shade of magnitude in arguments that are in fact most trite. Here belong similes that have nothing at all of certainty, provided only they are drawn from Phoebus, the rainbow, hail, and [p. 201] whatever meteors there are anywhere. Here belong examples, though they truly prove nothing, so long as they rattle off Cambyses and Epaminondas, Alexander the Great, *Xerxes, the Jupiter of the Persians*, and the Scipios. Here belong the testimonies of the Stagirite, of Euphorbus reborn, of the Attic nightingale, of the swan of Venusia, of the bard of Bilbilis, of the swan of Mantua, of the philosopher κατ' ἐξοχήν [par excellence], or of Christina, a virgin taught to despise a throne, etc., when the occasion has no need of those defenders.

§ 327. VII. Certain sublime things move most vehemently, § 308. Hence the worst conversion: the more fervently you rave, the more sublimely you will think. Whence an eighth species of tumidity, of which § 318, by which one about to think sublime things suddenly falls into parenthyrsus; and this, when in sublime matters it carries someone off, as vertigo did Phaethon, is described by Longinus, S. II, after Theodorus, as *an unseasonable and empty affect, where there is no need of affect, or an immoderate one, where there is need of a moderate one. For often, as if by the force of drunkenness, certain men are carried away to affects that are not grounded in the matter, affects proper to themselves and scholastic. Then they deservedly incur the mark of ignominy before hearers not at all moved. While they are in ecstasy before people who have undergone nothing of the kind.*

[p. 202] § 328. The baseness of this tumidity is increased if not even the unseasonable and excessive affect is conceived in the manner that befits, so that *you weep in little maxims, you make demands in periods*, and in the utmost horror you play the child with your witticisms. Hence the tumid elaborations of the themes recorded by Petronius: *pirates standing in chains on the shore, and responses given in a pestilence, that three or more virgins be sacrificed.* Now imagine a young man worn down by inflated declamations of this kind, and set on blending all these species of tumidity into a single theme, like a cook’s sauces, and you will grasp Petronius’s witty saying: *Those who are nourished among these things can no more have taste than those who live in a kitchen can smell good.*

[p. 203]

#### Sectio XXIII. Argumenta Augentia. — Section XXIII. Augmenting Arguments

§ 329. Let us call AUGMENTING ARGUMENTS those whose sole, or principal, or at present chiefly to be attended-to force, § 143, is to procure for our thoughts a magnitude congruent with the things to be thought, M. § 162. For let it be permitted here to mark with the name of augmenting arguments only augmentations that are truly such, truly beautiful, since the arguments of tumidity have already obtained the name of METEORS, and may be called ARGUMENTS OF βάθους [bathos], DEPRESSING ones, from which I distinguish the EXTENUATING, which invite the mind to form a smaller magnitude of the things to be thought than at first appeared; for these, when verisimilar and otherwise beautiful, I call augmenting, as augmenting not indeed the theme, but the magnitude and dignity of the thoughts, which it certainly concerns that the one who meditates should seem to measure each thing with a just foot.

§ 330. Among the augmenting figures, § 329, 26, there is also αὔξησις [auxesis], augmentation or increment, whose signification, however, is vague. Longinus first describes it, S. XI, in such a way that it coincides with the figure which some now call climax, others gradation, [p. 204] a figure surely augmenting, § 329; but then he rejects the definition of αὐξήσεως [auxesis] which he concedes to be that of the writers of the art, according to which it is λόγος περιτιθεὶς μέγεθος τοῖς ὑποκειμένοις [a discourse that invests its subjects with magnitude], and according to which I would more beautifully call every augmenting argument, in the sense of § 329, auxesis, or augmentation, § 26. Finally he himself defines αὔξησιν [auxesis] in such a way that, so far as can be gathered from a passage corrupted by a lacuna, he seems to wish to comprehend under the same term whatever is ἰσχυροποιοῦν τῇ ἐπιμονῇ [strengthening by dwelling upon], whatever, by cleaving longer to a certain matter, intensifies its force and that of the thoughts about it, whether it promotes verisimilitude, or light, or persuasion, or life, or finally, more strictly, only a greater and more dignified manner of thinking about the given matter, § 172. It is easy to see that the last signification is the broadest, and nearly coincides with the notion of a longer argument in general; the middle one is broader, the first stricter. If I had need of a word, I should choose to fix the middle signification together with the τεχνογράφοις [writers on the art].

§ 331. Just as Longinus still calls the art of the sublime βάθους τέχνην [the art of depth], S. II, in the manner in which the sky is called deep, so he likewise still advises φοιβάζειν τοὺς λόγους [to inspire the discourse with Phoebus] in sublime matters, S. VIII, and refers the φοιβαστικούς [the Phoebus-inspired] to the first class of sublime geniuses, S. XIII. [p. 205] But from this, βάθος [bathos] signifies, in the most frequent usage, the vice opposed to the sublime, indeed the greatest of them, § 217, and the ironic *art of sinking* of the more recent Anti-Longinus has fixed it sufficiently. By a like reasoning, although it is sometimes permitted, without baseness, to speak of Phoebus and the meteors of nature even in truly sublime matters, § 126, yet those leaden men who, tumidly and frigidly calling meteors everywhere to their aid, as a god from the machine, whenever they dared to lift themselves from the ground, brought matters to such a point that already Longinus, S. III, following the usage of speech by which such heralds of meteors, such a one as we saw in § 202, were called μετεωρολόγοι [meteor-talkers], indeed μετεωρολέσχαι [meteor-babblers], opposes the sublime to meteors, and now Phoebus and φοιβολογεῖν [to speak in Phoebus’s manner] have come to the same vicious signification.

§ 332. In the augmenting figures and arguments let us investigate, only in general, the moment they have for absolute magnitude. From this it will be clear of itself how they stand to relative magnitude as well, and to slenderness, mediocrity, and sublimity. Longinus, S. XV, says that the image, which we call hypotyposis, contributes to the latter; yet it is rather an argument and figure of light, chiefly as it aspires to sublimity, § 303, than one that immediately or chiefly augments the magnitude of thoughts about any matter, § 329, [p. 206] 172; cf. Longinus S. XVII. By the same reasoning I should think that interrogation, of which Longinus S. XVIII, contributes rather to persuasion, and indeed to as much as a sublime meditation demands, § 305, than to augmenting magnitude itself immediately. Nor should I deny that asyndeton, of which Longinus S. XIX, looks more to richness and to a kind of synathroismus that befits sublime things together with a becoming brevity, § 295, 148, than to intensifying magnitude more closely and immediately, § 329.

§ 333. Repetition, however, of which Longinus S. XXI, I certainly call hither by its own right, whenever it is a figure, § 26; for although it can sometimes exert its own eminent efficacy both for persuading and for inciting, yet it always produces first that which we are now chiefly attending to, namely that the thing be judged something greater, worthier of our notice, and holding much weight in itself, which has seemed worthy to be recalled to mind again and again and set as it were before the eyes more than a single time. You will see an example in Longinus. But I do not want only the arguments of the sublime, § 332. Here, among slender things, belongs that repeated

*Fortunate old man.* Virg. ecl. 1.

[p. 207] from one who had said:

*Indeed I do not envy; I rather marvel.*

Likewise that of ecl. 4, of one coming back to himself again out of a rather disgraceful ecstasy:

*Ah! Corydon, Corydon! what madness has seized you!*

*And we shall raise your Daphnis to the stars,* / *Daphnis we shall bear to the stars; Daphnis loved us too*, ecl. V, etc.

§ 334. Hyperbaton, of which Longinus S. XXII, contributes rather to life and to the pathetic, especially such as befit sublime things, § 308, than immediately and more closely to magnitude, § 329. Of those figures which Longinus S. XXIII, mentions, climax and gradation above all belong here, of which already § 330: when, namely, either more wonderfully and magnificently, or at least in some manner for the sake of augmenting and adorning something with some elevation, that is thought first which befits the given matter considered as it were in the positive degree; and if that has been accepted as a unit and measure, something greater is at once added, thence again something greater, and so on, until at last the greatest, illuminated by these very premises, as by so many weaker lights, is perceived in all its amplitude. One example, also [p. 208] for the sake of other things, I shall set down: *O times! O manners! The senate understands these things, the consul sees them. Yet this man lives. Lives?* § 333. *Nay, he even comes into the senate, he becomes a partaker of the public council, he marks and designates with his eyes each one of us for slaughter.* Cic. in Cat. I.

§ 335. The vice opposed to this, a great increment of βάθους [bathos], which the *art of sinking* calls *anticlimax*, is vehemently to be guarded against, whereby there would follow, in things beautifully thought, after greater, worthier and weightier things, suddenly, things equivalent as it were in their primary marks, but in their secondary ones involving something less, lighter or less worthy than what went before. Longinus, S. XLIII, recording a rather amusing example of this vice from Theopompus, § 174, censures him among other things for running down from more sublime things into lowlier ones, when on the contrary he ought to have made an αὔξησιν [auxesis]. Here again Longinus uses the word αὐξήσεως [auxesis] not according to his own notion, nor according to the definition of the writers of the art, but more strictly, § 330.

§ 336. The arguments which Longinus runs through, S. XXIIII–XXVIIII, all augment rather mediately, through one of the remaining cardinal elegances of meditation, § 22, than immediately and more closely procure weight and magnitude. [p. 209] Metaphors, however, of which Longinus S. XXXII, similes and comparisons, of which the same, S. XXXVII, although when fine they mostly have at the same time another force and efficacy, § 142, are nevertheless to be drawn hither too, in so far as among shrewd authors they at the same time take on the role of measuring-rods, which they supply precisely so that from their known and given magnitude you may silently measure within yourself the magnitude, dignity and weight of the improper signification and of the compared theme, which may still be less known to you, § 329. Call someone a bad citizen: the quantity is still undetermined. Call the same man a plague of the republic. Does not the degree of public calamity which is known to be in a pestilence augment at the same time the depravity and the public detriment in the improper signification, less known before?

§ 337. Virgil, thinking it difficult to enumerate the species of trees, paints by comparison how great the degree of difficulty is which the aesthetician takes for physical impossibility, Georg. II. 105, saying that *the number and names* of the species

*if anyone should wish to know, the same would wish to learn* / *how many sands of the Libyan sea are stirred by Zephyr,* / *or, when Eurus falls more violently upon the ships,* / *to know how many Ionian waves come to the shores.*

[p. 210] § 338. But these same augmenting arguments, 336, are to be employed the more cautiously, the more certainly mathematical cognition is for the analogue of reason

*a work full of dangerous hazard,*

lest, even φαινομένως [apparently], § 16, you err in the calculation; which done, from the same sources comes also a rich harvest of depressing arguments, § 313–315, and of meteors, § 329. Of these I do not know whether Plato has not given certain examples in his metaphorical description of the human body, which Longinus partly inserts in his own work from the dialogue called Timaeus, and in which Plato is held to have already taught the circulation of the blood, calling the heart the source τοῦ περιφερομένου σφοδρῶς αἵματος κατὰ πάντα τὰ μέλη [of the blood that is carried vehemently round through all the limbs]. Is the neck called without tumidity *an Isthmus between the citadel of the head and the breast?* Are they mistaken who, in the comparison of *the republic* with *a cup*, in which *the raging god is chastened by the sober god* (water), seem to themselves to detect βάθος [bathos], and tumidity, both manifest and cryptic? § 310.

§ 339. An augmenting argument, and one primarily belonging here, is in Longinus S. XXXVIII, hyperbole, which, as much in thinking of something as great as in thinking of another thing as small, has transgressed the ordinary limits, if it is truly a figure, § 26. The latter, diasyrmus, is an extenuating argument, § 329, by some not very [p. 211] aptly called Tapinosis, which is rather to be taken as the Greek denomination of every depressing argument, at least among the vices. I have called hither the hyperbole that is a figure. For I have already often declared that those phenomena observed in things beautifully thought or said, and mostly adorned with Greek denominations, which we learn as boys as figures, I judge worthy of this title only when they are found placed with sufficient reason, and contribute something notable, for their part too, to the focus of beauty. Hence cryptic hyperboles please Longinus most, those which have so transgressed the limits of height and depth that not even this seems to have been done. I find an example in a common phrase of the Germans, when they say of one heavily bleeding from his wound that he swims in his own blood.

§ 340. Nor would I, according to the aestheticians’ measuring-rod, reject the hyperbole of Herodotus, by which he says that the Greeks fighting at Thermopylae were *buried by the javelins of the barbarians*, nor that comic one: *he has a field whose soil is smaller than a Laconic letter.* Much worse by now is the diasyrmus, ταπεινότητος αὔξησις [an augmentation of lowliness], which Quintilian ascribes to Cicero, VIII. 6:

*Varro calls it a farm, which I could throw with a sling,* / *unless it should fall out where the hollow sling opens.*

[p. 212] O Tully, a mediocre poet! Catullus’s diasyrmus on the poverty of Furius, c. 23, al. 20, would be formally more charming if the author had throughout taken even simply a greater account of decent manners:

*Furius, you who have neither slave nor coffer,* / *nor bug, nor spider, nor fire.* / — — — — / *Your teeth can eat up even flint.* / — — — — / *And yet you have bodies drier than horn,* / *or whatever is drier still,* / *from sun and cold and hunger.* etc.

§ 341. Nor do I dare excuse the excessive hyperbole of Isocrates, by which, 1) right at the start of the exordium 2) of an oration, not a poem, 3) in which he promised to show the true pre-eminence of Athens over Sparta in benefits conferred on Greece, attacking himself with his own sword and warning the hearer against himself, he attributes *such force to words that you can* with them *both make great things humble and clothe small things with magnitude*, etc. For what, Sophist, will you accomplish by daring such a thing with words alone, except either to creep or to swell? Let us prefer the hyperbole of Juvenal, on a certain woman, Sat. VI.

*With so many tiers she presses, with so many stories still she builds* / *her head on high. In front you will see an Andromache,* / [p. 213] *behind she is smaller. You would think her another; and she seems shorter* / *than a Pygmy maiden aided by no buskins.*

§ 342. The charming repetition which we chose almost first among the augmenting arguments, § 333, comprehends under itself many species of figures, which are ordinarily counted among the figures of diction, and which then, when they truly have the force of repetition mentioned above, become at the same time figures of thought, § 26. Repetition is either total or partial, e.g. with at least the relations and respects changed which the cases are wont to signify, whence polyptoton, which Longinus too mentions, S. XXIII, and to which figure Vossius, by a bold but true philosophy, has also referred repetition with varied numbers, degrees, genders, moods, tenses and persons. Here belongs that saying of Calvus: *That bribery was committed you all know, and all know that you know this.* By another division repetition is divided into immediate and conjoined, and into mediate and disjoined.

§ 343. Total disjoined repetition is anaphora, § 342, a figure if the thing itself, being weightier, is driven home again and again by the repetition of the sign placed in the first position, to be laid up deep in the mind, whence an ἀναθεώρησις [reconsideration] may truly be hoped for. [p. 214] Such is the figure, surely not one of diction only, in the anathema of Moses and the μακαρισμῷ [beatitude] of Christ. Let us repeat the example from Lucilius, after Lactantius:

*Virtue, Albinus, is to be able to pay the true price* / *in the affairs in which we are engaged, in which we live.* / *Virtue for a man is to know what each thing holds.* / *Virtue is for a man to know what is right, useful, what is honourable,* / *what things are good, what likewise bad, what useless, base, dishonourable,* / *Virtue is to know the end and measure of seeking a thing.* etc.

§ 344. The same force, if not a greater, is had by that which, repeating at the end, wishes to commend to deeper consideration, epistrophe, or epiphora, if it is truly a figure, § 342. Such is that of Plautus:

*Those who pretend to know everything, and know nothing at all,* / *what each man has in mind, or is going to have, they know,* / *and what the king has said in the queen’s ear,* / *they know, what Juno has chatted about with Jove,* / *things that neither will be nor have been done, yet they know them.*

[p. 215] Greater now is that in Cicero, II. philipp. *Do you grieve that three armies of the Roman people have been slain? Antony slew them. Do you miss our most illustrious citizens? Those too Antony has snatched from you. Has the authority of this order been struck down? Antony struck it down.* Of the worst omen for Maximinus was the epiphora contained in the Greek verses which Julius Capitolinus relates to have been spoken against him, to this effect: *He who cannot be killed by one is killed by many. The elephant is huge, and is killed. The lion is strong, and is killed. The tiger is strong, and is killed. Beware of many, if you do not fear single men.*

§ 345. Symploce, epanalepsis, anadiplosis, ploce, epizeuxis, differing in the position of the repeated words, about which I should care little in matters of substance, agree as figures in this, that they charmingly repeat what is to be considered more deeply, § 333. There is an elegance of this kind in Ov. Fast. II.

*One day had sent all the Fabii to war,* / *one day destroyed those sent to war*

In the slender manner of thinking especially, if in it anything is nevertheless to be judged to be something comparatively above the rest, they are wont to be employed together with a sweet imitation of the manners of those accustomed to a simple way of living and thinking, since men of this kind rarely think what they would not at least say to themselves, and so, thinking again and again what has seemed weightier to them, they signify it again [p. 216] and again. Hence these figures are so frequent in bucolics, in the hendecasyllables of Catullus, in anacreontics, etc.

*Let us rise. Shade is wont to be harmful to singers.* / *Harmful is the juniper’s shade; shades harm the crops too.*

§ 346. The gradation, the increment, which we have already counted with Longinus under the name of climax among the augmenting arguments and figures, § 334, some, bestowing the name of climax on a certain species of it, have also among the figures of diction, the increment being rightly referred to the figures of thought. Climax is then said more strictly, when that lesser thing, while we climb from it to greater things, is at the same time repeated, § 333. Let us see an example from Epicharmus in Athenaeus, in Vossius’s version, because at the same time, as it reads, it exhibits in its last members an example of the anticlimax that disfigures the rest, § 335: *From the sacrifice a feast, from the feast a drinking-bout, which indeed seemed festive to me, from the drinking-bout a wanton revel, from the revel madness, from madness a lawsuit before the judge, from the suit and judgement condemnation, from condemnation fetters, gangrene, or paralysis, and a fine.*

[p. 217] § 347. Although Vossius says that all figures of thought have a certain dignity in the things themselves, I nevertheless do not draw all of them hither, nor most of them, but chiefly those which, from the middle signification of the word, § 330, you would call αὐξήσεις [auxeses], § 329. For there is no need to call the increment, nor the lesser, still sufficiently verisimilar, hyperbole, auxesis merely by synecdoche, nor the μειωτικὴν [diminishing] exaggeration tapinosis by catachresis, § 339. Meanwhile let us see one more brief example of the increment, § 334, which is not strictly climax, § 346, from Seneca, who laments *the most shameful loss, through negligence*, of those of whom, *if you are willing to attend, a great part of life slips away while they do ill, the greatest part while they do nothing, the whole while they do something else.*

§ 348. Synathroismus, which some call congeries, we have already seen among the enriching arguments, § 148, and among the augmenting ones, up to sublimity, § 298. For in the slender and in the middle manner of thinking too, a complex of this kind, by which the heaped-up things are offered to minds as it were as a whole, avails to excite a perception great absolutely, and as great relatively as is sought. So Cicero for the Manilian law: *Finally, what kind of war can there be in which the fortune of the republic has not exercised him* (Pompey)*? Civil, African, Transalpine, Spanish, a war mixed* [p. 218] *of cities and of the most warlike nations, servile, naval war, various and diverse kinds of wars and of enemies, not only waged by this one man, but also brought to an end.* A greater congeries, and hence a swifter one, epitrochasmus, also belongs here, Virg. Aen. IV.

*I should have carried torches into the camp,* / *and filled the gangways with flames, and son and father* / *with the race I should have blotted out, and thrown myself upon them.*

§ 349. Among the augmenting arguments we should without doubt have Parrhesia, which shows implicitly or explicitly that, for a certain meditation or signification of a meditation to be resolved upon, we had need of a notable effort of liberty, and that only after some struggle did the better cause nevertheless prevail in the mind. For after a prologue of this kind, or a tacit insinuation, things surely not common and trite are wont to be expected. Whether this argument is always called a figure by the rhetoricians, or never, is all one to me. Sinon in Virgil uses it with the same effect, when, among the preliminaries already pertaining here, Aen. II, 77:

*All things indeed, king, whatever shall befall, I shall confess to you* / *truly*, he says, *nor shall I deny that I am of the Argive race.* / [p. 219] *This first. Nor, if fortune has made Sinon wretched,* / *will she, wicked as she is, make him also vain and a liar.*

§ 350. The feigned Sinon goes on μετὰ πολλῆς παρρησίας [with much parrhesia], until he thinks he has sufficiently sharpened their attention. Then indeed he again feigns a struggle within his mind, 101.

*But why do I in vain unroll these unwelcome things?* etc.

With what effect?

*Then indeed we burn to inquire and to ask the causes,* / *ignorant of such great crimes and of Pelasgian art.* / *He goes on, trembling.*

But when he was now almost touching with the needle the matter for whose sake the whole fable was being played, not a little word before

*he raised to the stars his palms freed from their bonds:* / *You, eternal fires, and your inviolable* / *godhead I call to witness*, he says, *you altars and unspeakable swords,* / *which I fled, and fillets of the gods, which I wore as a victim.* / *It is right for me to loose the sacred bonds of the Greeks.* / *It is right to hate the men, and to bring all things out under the air,* / *whatever they hide. I am held by no laws of my country.* / [p. 220] *Only do you stand by your promises, and, Troy, preserved, preserve* / *your faith, if I bring true things, if I repay great things.*

The event taught how highly this parrhesia was esteemed, and the narration wrapped up in it.

§ 351. Let exclamation close the train of augmenting figures, not only that by which such things are thought as must, in eloquent reading, be uttered with some intensity of voice, on account of their magnitude, but also that which cryptically designates that such things as we are now thinking present themselves to our mind not without admiration. For since small and proletarian things are not wont to carry one into admiration of them, it is denoted by the very fact that it occurs to one thinking beautifully to marvel, that what he turns over in his mind is something great. When you read in Terence:

*D. Woe is me! What shall I do? What shall I act? What shall I cry, or complain?* / *O heavens! O earth! O seas of Neptune!* / *M. There you have it!* Ad. V, 3.

do you not, at least comically, expect great things which he has found out as he cries? Pamphilus, in the Andria of the same writer, thus describes as it were the genesis of an exclamation that is to follow a little later:

*I am scarcely myself. So is my mind stirred by fear,* / [p. 221] *hope, joy, at this so great a marvel, so sudden a good,* V. 4.

And now he will seem to rejoice at no small things, concluding after a few words:

*O happy and fortunate this day!*

#### Sectio XXIIII. Grauitas Aesthetica Absoluta. — Section XXIIII. Absolute Aesthetic Gravity

§ 352. However great the magnitude may be in aesthetic materials, one will nevertheless not attain it unless one is at the same time capable of a subjective magnitude, which we have called aesthetic magnanimity and gravity, § 189. This will be the absolute magnitude of wit and heart, § 45, not innate alone, but also nourished by exercises, by practice, § 51, and by discipline, § 63; not a dead magnitude, but one sufficient for eliciting full desires, as great a magnitude, and hence also dignity, as all things to be thought beautifully require if they are to possess absolute aesthetic magnitude, § 178.

§ 353. Since, therefore, we now demand a great mind in everyone who is to think beautifully, that notion of Cicero’s does not sit well with our mind, which perhaps, on the very [p. 222] horizon of a stricter and purer reason, might be approved though exaggerated too much and more august than is just, where in Quaest. Tusc. IV, 61 he says: *We wish that man to be steadfast, calm, grave, pressing down all things human, whom we call a magnanimous and brave man. But no one can be such who grieves, or fears, or desires, or exults. For these things belong to those who hold human events to be superior to their own minds.* We shall here be content with a Cato of greater affability and humanity, provided he can and will pursue absolutely great things, and abstain from inept, cheap and most lowly things, § 191, 195.

§ 354. Nor do we require in everyone who is to think beautifully a man so proven,

*grave in piety and merits*

and venerable, that by a mere nod he could impose silence on the tumultuous; much less a man who with *a grave brow and a gloomy frown* *obstinately* forces his opinions upon others; least of all one of those who

*cannot be moved by sluggish gravity.*

For us it suffices to have a mind both capable of and constantly eager for absolutely great, and hence also dignified things, and diligently on its guard against what Lucilius already censured,

*while he laughs at the verses of Ennius as lesser in gravity.*

[p. 223] § 355. This magnitude of wit and heart we wish for both as potential, a part of the general character in the happy aesthetician, S. II–VI, and then, when he comes to thinking beautifully, as actual, by which, not only perhaps at some other time and on other occasions, but now too, and in the manner the matter now requires, he can, and does not merely will antecedently from incomplete solicitations, but also efficiently decides, to conceive in mind and heart things absolutely great, and hence dignified, in which there is nothing of the puny, nothing lowly and abject. Let there reign in his person that which Cicero describes in de Inu. II, 166: *dignity* (subjective), namely *an honourable authority, worthy of cultivation, of honour, and of reverence.*

§ 356. Those who are to think beautifully, but who lack the actual absolute magnitude of mind and the dignity of person which we have seen up to this point, are PRETTY LITTLE AND TRIFLING MICROLOGISTS (leptologists).

*And that you may know the souls to be light,*

read there the otherwise great poet Lucretius, where in Book III he lowers one of the greatest and gravest subjects with this exordium:

*Come now, that minds are born with living beings and mortal, / and that souls are light, that you may know it: / [p. 224] long sought and found with sweet labour, / I shall proceed to set in order songs worthy of your life* etc.

The rock of this vice will scarcely be avoided, not only by those who choose for themselves themes set below the aesthetic horizon, but also themes placed above it, whenever they nonetheless labour to expound these with a certain festive urbanity, not without witticisms and jokes, § 120, 121.

§ 357. Meanwhile I wish only absolute levity to be blamed in everyone who is to think beautifully, § 355, 356. A heart and wit truly great, as great as Virgil’s, can sometimes

*with gravity unimpaired attempt a jest,*

and the same can be carried into things comparatively less great and dignified, which are nevertheless absolutely so, § 198, 201; hence they may be called light by the author himself, interpreting according to § 179. Hither belongs the exordium of the bee-keeping, Georg. IV, 4.

*The wondrous spectacles of little things I shall tell you, / and magnanimous leaders, and in order the whole nation’s / manners, and pursuits, and peoples, and battles.*

§ 358. Let us leave undecided, now as aestheticians, the state of mind in Demosthenes which Cicero condemns in quaest. tusc. V, 103, when he examines it no longer on some [p. 225] popular balance, but on the goldsmith’s scales, which he approves, § 250: *Our Demosthenes*, he says, *was surely rather trifling, who said he was delighted by that whisper of a little woman carrying water, as is the custom in Greece, whispering to another: This is that Demosthenes. What could be more trifling than this? But how great an orator! But he had evidently learned to speak before others, not much with himself.*

§ 359. Pretty little habitual micrologists and trifling pursuers of minutiae are those PYGMIES OF HELICON, the reptiles of Parnassus. The tiny little souls among these, such by nature, the mercenary poetasters, and all of that kind, let us laughingly bid to abound in their own wit, and cheerfully dismiss them to their own images,

*Where the whole cohort is not taller than one foot.*

Those alone grieve us, and deserve an honourable compassion, whom the injuries of their times, and the barbarism of the places in which they lived, and the depraved taste of their contemporaries, or of the most honoured men, drove to this, that, frivolously prodigal of souls great enough, they wore away their leisure either in trifles and ineptitudes, or in altogether most lowly turpitudes, or, with a befouled heart, disfigured their wit at the same time.

§ 360. To one who properly contemplates the absolute magnitude of mind, wit and heart which we have hitherto considered, [p. 226] especially that which sometimes suffices beautifully, without disgrace, for painting even comparatively greatest things proportionately, there will be evident that moral necessity both of beautiful erudition and of praiseworthy morals in the happy aesthetician, which has sometimes carried away the teachers of the signally beautiful art so far that, speaking generally, they laid down a rule which to others has seemed harder than is just, and broader than is just.

§ 361. Horace’s law is granted, A. P. 309:

*Of writing well, to be wise is both the principle and the source.*

But when, comparing the context and what follows, we here interpret *to be wise* as being practically skilled in practical philosophy, then we shall seem to many to have demanded more than is just. Cicero is judged to be equally at fault, who *places this among the first things, that without philosophy the eloquent man whom we seek cannot be produced*, and confesses that he is *an orator, if indeed he is one, who came forth not from the workshops of the rhetoricians but from the walks of the Academy*, and finally describes *the orator* as *a good man skilled in speaking.* Nor will Quintilian escape censure, who says yet more expressly: *There cannot be an orator unless he is a good man. Let him be such a man as can truly be called wise, perfect not only in morals, but also in knowledge, and in every faculty of speaking.*

§ 362. But what? Just as erudition [p. 227] is ill feigned before the erudite, so too are good morals before those of better morals. As soon as there some supine ignorance, here some uncouth morals hidden beneath the fox’s skin, shine through, the matter is settled, and by the verdict of the most competent judges you are condemned as one who covers turpitude more basely still. Since, therefore, it is now placed beyond doubt that no shameful ignorance, nothing abject and plebeian, ought to become a phenomenon in things to be thought beautifully: first it is asked whether a mind awkward on both sides can be dissembled aptly enough; next, that will at least be more difficult than to present yourself with an open heart, such as you are both in wit and in morals, and it is the business of teachers to prescribe the easiest rules they can; finally, if it be granted that someone, through a prodigious habit of feigning, could so counterfeit a good man that not even the least depravity of mind and heart shows through in his manner of thinking, who forbids calling him a good man, aesthetically and verisimilarly, until the contrary is established? § 15, 16, 211.

§ 363. Now, therefore, it will be permitted to determine a little more carefully the absolute magnitude of mind which we required in § 45 as innate, and as strengthened by exercises, § 50, and by discipline, § 63: a mind not merely honourable but also noble, § 213, which at the same time has learned by custom, that second [p. 228] nature, to live more politely, and has acquired the cognition ordinary to such a class of good men as something familiar and easy to itself as well. For such a one it will suffice to express both simply honourable morals and a manner of thinking proportionate to them, by restraining his powers somewhat; and he will attain, if he wishes, the heroic manner of living and its majesty, and will strain all the sinews of his mind to represent certain eminent things in worthy ways.

#### Sectio XXV. Magnanimitas Aesthetica Comparatiua. — Section XXV. Comparative Aesthetic Magnanimity

§ 364. Since true magnitude of mind is no less hostile to tumidity than to a lowly and creeping manner of thoughts and of life, § 363, it will teach one who is to think slender things not only to be content with his own lot, even if he himself could attain no more than a simply honourable kind of life, but also, though conspicuous for nobility of morals and of wit, and even the heroes themselves, to fashion neither the things to be conceived about the theme nor the mode and manner of thinking more augustly than the matter, with which above all one has to deal, allows. If the labour is in the slender, every greater nobility and [p. 229] heroism itself is, as it were, to be put off, useful for this one thing, that the greater they are in someone, the easier it may be for him so to keep the bounds of slenderness that his thought be neither depressed into abject things, and thorns instead of tamarisks, nor swell up under ill-omened birds, § 245, 263.

§ 365. For expressing the true and natural magnitude of slender things a wit and heart that is, among beautiful things, one of the least does indeed suffice; nor yet is it necessary, since the greater, which can do the lesser too, and are apt for them, have been taught to spare their own powers, § 369. You have a new, and indeed a subjective, difference between the slender manner of thinking in aesthetics and the logical and concise manners of meditating. A mind and heart that is signally philosophical can, unless it wrongly despises beautiful things, express the slender manner of thinking; but not every mind great enough for this praise can rise to the philosophical manner of meditating. Leibniz could devise fables; a wit and mind suited only to fables cannot demonstrate his differential calculus.

§ 366. Let us not confound slender veins, and a mind content with its slenderness, but honourably so, with the trifling reptiles of Parnassus, § 356, 359. Even in the slender elegances of thinking [p. 230] the ἔπαρσις [elevation], however much of it is required, § 235, will indeed be difficult for a wit of this sort; yet among the pygmies of Helicon it will raise its head as far as tamarisks rise from the ground, § 180, 195, and, solicitously studious of the absolute gravity and magnanimity that is given in aesthetics, S. XXIIII, it will maintain some degree, though the least, of the comparative, § 189, 185.

§ 367. A mind great enough even for slender things, among praiseworthy aesthetic matters, is also governed by a subtle wit, though it be not the most subtle, § 238, and gladly accustoms itself to season it with pleasantries and honourable urbanity, with sayings worth saying, and with witticisms and jokes, in their proper season, § 237; but from the truly charming examples of the ancients, not from that ridiculous formula of certain moderns, which *Vavasseur, on ludicrous diction,* shows both by authorities and by reasons

*to go headlong into the mud,* § 195, *head over heels.*

§ 368. A mind apt and accommodated to thinking only slender things charmingly, more easily, and one not unequal even to greater things, with more difficulty, will each nevertheless well employ their effort in this too, that they do not rise higher, whether in knowing or in desiring, than the sphere of thoughts and of morals [p. 231] defined by slender things extends, § 364. *Admirable in his own kind is Theocritus. But that rustic and pastoral Muse shrinks not only from the forum, but even from the city itself.* Quint. X. 1; so that with equal awkwardness you would feign to think of the forum, and of the city too, in that manner of thinking which Theocritus used, and would describe bucolics out of that sphere of notions and wishes which those nourished in the city, nay in the forum itself, and constantly busied there, are wont to enjoy.

§ 369. Rightly does Servius praise the same *Theocritus* as *everywhere simple*, adding that *the persons in bucolics are rustic and delight in simplicity*, § 213, namely *those from whom nothing lofty, nothing urbane, nothing declamatory* is to be expected. *He excuses some figures of Virgil by necessity.* Yet they are mistaken who on this account bid one who is to think proportionately of slender things avoid all ornaments, all figures, § 237; the very example of Theocritus may prove it, in whom surely, if in anyone ever, the positive ἦθος βουκολικόν [bucolic character], § 194, is also preserved. Id. l. 76:

Ἁδεῖ᾽ ἁ φωνὰ τᾶς πόρτιος, ἁδὺ τὸ πνεῦμα, [Sweet is the voice of the heifer, sweet her breath,] / Ἁδὺ δὲ χὡ μόσχος γαρύεται, ἁδὺ δὲ χἁ βῶς, [sweet too the calf lows, and sweet the cow,] / Ἁδὺ δέ, κ. τ. λ. [sweet too, etc.]

and yet it has ornament, a figure, anaphora, § 343, 345. How full the same poet is of comparisons, thoroughly rustic, but figures nonetheless!

[p. 232] § 370. This attenuation of powers and parsimony in the eclogues, § 368, they say Virgil kept so strenuously that he has only two maxims in all of them taken together, one of which nevertheless has not escaped the severity of the critics, as though it were greater than is just in the mouth of a shepherd. That they are mistaken the maxim itself teaches:

*Each is drawn by his own pleasure.*

For why should not his own pleasure in maxims draw one of the shepherds, whom Aristotle generally calls γνωμοτύπους [coiners of maxims], with Cervantes’s Sancho Panza agreeing wonderfully?

§ 371. For my part I have observed not only maxims but also a great many other phenomena which bear the names of figures among the rhetoricians, chiefly in the common conversations of honourable men of the lowest rank, and many others have observed them with me; we do not call them figures, because they are not parts of beautiful thought, and, with their surroundings neglected and somewhat base, they are so obscured that no peculiar elegance is discovered in them, and they are rather imputed to chance, like little flowers springing up of themselves in meadows and at the crossroads, § 26. This, however, is noted for this reason, that we may be the more certainly persuaded that one does not sin against the verisimilitude of ἦθος [character] if, even in slender things, as though doing something else, [p. 233] one mixes in ornaments of this sort, not sought out, yet lying in the way, which can be marked with the trite names of figures, and does not on that account transcend his own sphere of slenderness, § 368. *And Terence gives to slaves for the most part maxims that are indeed most prudent, but such as offer themselves by nature to all; as: Nothing too much.* Servius on Aen. X.

§ 372. A singular proof of a great mind in slender things is a certain abnegation of erudition, when a man of greater wit, cognition and mind, who is nevertheless to express slender things, deliberately commits an error in the person whom he feigns, lest he injure verisimilitude. Here, after Theocritus, Virgil is called as an example, when he writes

*I. Amphion of Dirce on Actaean Aracynthus,*

and they interpret *Actaean* as Attic, not as if Aracynthus were in Attica, but as if Virgil had feigned the shepherd’s geographical ignorance:

*II. Some of us will go to Scythia and the swift Oaxes of Crete,*

nor does he employ the customary constructions concerning peoples, lands and rivers to which we come, nor is the river he assigns to Crete one that it possesses, since some seek the Oaxes in Scythia, others in Mesopotamia. With whom the truth lies, a shepherd may know as well as the most ignorant.

[p. 234] § 373. The servitude which Longinus, S. XXXXIIII, bringing forth, in the person of some philosopher, I know not whom, whom he feigns as speaking from the beginning, perhaps the opinion of his own mind, indicates as the principal cause why in his times there was so universal a dearth of sublime and magnificent wits, servitude that in a single day takes away half of virtue: this, even if it be complete, leaves to hearts that would dare greater things in liberty at least as much relative magnitude as suffices for thinking slender things beautifully.

*Servitude, exposed to harm, / because it did not dare to say what it wished, / transferred its own affects into little fables, / and eluded calumny with feigned jests.* Phaedr. l. III.

This genesis of apologues is excellently confirmed by the first of those that survive, Jotham’s fable of the trees seeking a king, though it still breathes something greater and more sublime in its application than the later Aesopic fables of slaves and freedmen.

§ 374. The relatively least magnitude of mind, whether only potential, or at least actual in those now occupied in elaborating slender things, is a wit and propensity apt and eager, both negatively and positively, [p. 235] to follow a simply honourable manner of living and a mode of thinking congruent with it, § 213, 214; it can, tends, and cherishes to present objects capable not only of a middle but also of the greatest measure, for contemplation at the same time from those of their sides which, though absolutely great and dignified, are nevertheless the lesser of these, § 215, and to escape βάθους [bathos], § 217, both its first, § 221, and its second kind, § 245, and tumidity, § 217, both its first, § 222, and its second kind, § 263.

§ 375. The same magnitude accordingly both loves and is able to exhibit a manner of thinking absolutely great enough, especially absolutely dignified, § 223; to beware of a cheap manner of thinking about abject things, § 224, or even about slender things, § 262; to seek a manner of thinking absolutely moral, in the broader sense, § 226, as in the stricter, § 228; to avoid the manner ill-moral in either sense, § 228; to measure prettily the degrees and intervals even within slender things, § 233; and, when occasion is given, to keep the mean between βωμολοχία [buffoonery], the scurrility that gapes after laughter in every way, and ἀγροικία [boorishness], the wildness of one naturally rough to all, which is sometimes signified by the name of εὐτραπελία [wittiness], *of those who bear themselves neatly and decently in play and jests*, § 237.

§ 376. I cannot throw in a mention of εὐτραπελία [wittiness] without its perhaps coming into some people’s minds that, by Eph. V, 4, it is judged altogether [p. 236] forbidden to Christians. I shall not inquire here into whether all things that are illicit by the most holy faith can also be considered as forbidden by the philosophical rules of aesthetics alone; I shall only give in passing an interpretation of the passage which I hold to be most true: *Let not even turpitude and obscenity be heard among you, nor morology.* But if you ask what I embrace under the title of morology, it is that εὐτραπελία (κατὰ) τὰ οὐκ ἀνήκοντα [wittiness (in) things not fitting], *all that manner of thinking and acting which they cover with the titles of urbanity and festivity, but which is truly contrary to decorum, and to Christian decorum at that*, § 212.

§ 377. It must surely be held most difficult, even by those considering the matter only psychologically, so to render the wit versatile by deliberate design, and to vary one’s manners so often and so amiably, yet always right and truly fine, that

*every colour, and condition, and circumstance may become you,*

and that you never suffer shipwreck on the rocks of the sharp-tongued, obscenity, slander, or any profanation of heavenly things, and that, with Cicero as adviser, no saying of yours, no jest, be either too frequent, for that is the buffoon’s, or somewhat obscene, for that is the mime’s, or petulant, for that is the wicked man’s, nor against the unfortunate, for that is inhuman, nor against criminals, for that is too light a punishment, nor sought out and brought from home, for that is cold, nor, [p. 237] generally, made to provoke, since the reply is both wittier and more humane, nor against those dear to us or to many, for that is odious, nor against the powerful and those endowed with dignity, for that is beyond the veneration and respect that are due, nor atrocious and too insulting against anyone, for that is incurable.

§ 378. For the same Cicero, off. I, *the distinction between a gentlemanly (elegant, urbane, witty, facetious) and an illiberal jest is easy.* The former *is made at the right time and with a relaxed mind, worthy of a free man*, and for it, *as there is a rule for beginning, so there is a measure for leaving off.* But let it be easy in theory and in the abstract, as they say; let it be easy in many cases, on the one side the more liberal, on the other those verging toward the lowest baseness and dregs, like lead; let it be easy for a mind serene, quiet, hastening slowly, placed beyond the cast of the weapon, nay outside the parties themselves, like a judge who does not make the suit his own. The ease will nevertheless vanish if, on the very point of saying a saying which, suddenly occurring, you already have on the tip of your tongue, with the opportunity about to pass most swiftly, you are seized with something which, of the honourable and the ill-moraled,

*plays about the borders,*

your mind somewhat more agitated, and carried away into a certain ἐνθουσιασμός [enthusiasm], as it were, of jesting, S. V; or if you examine a saying of another who is attacking either you yourself, or yours, or one whom you surely fear, etc.

[p. 238] § 379. I think that he will judge in agreement with us who further weighs whether the examples of elegant jests given in general by Cicero himself, l. c., are truly all such. 1) *Our Plautus*, § 262, of whom, however, Horace again speaks generally and indefinitely, A. P. 270:

*But your forefathers praised both the numbers and / the witticisms of Plautus, too patiently admiring both, / not to say foolishly, if only you and I / know how to separate the boorish from a graceful saying.*

⁠2) *The old comedy of the Athenians*, which however, on Horace’s authority, l. c. 282, needed a law to restrain the vice of its sharp tongue.

*The law was accepted, and the chorus / fell shamefully silent, the right of harming being removed.*

⁠3) *The Socratic philosophers*, whose leader himself, declared by the oracle the wisest of mortals, could not escape being named, as Cicero himself reports, *the Attic buffoon* by Zeno the Stoic. Let us add, as an argumentum ad hominem, the saying of those too who, with injustice, saluted this same Cicero as *the consular buffoon*, following Cato, who led the way, when he once said with a smile, speaking of Cicero: *What a ridiculous consul we have.*

[p. 239] § 380. But to what end all this, § 377–379? That we may not only assent to Cicero when he says, l. c., *It is shameful, and very faulty, to bring sayings of the dinner-table, or a dainty conversation, into a serious matter*: but also that, even in slender things, and things to be thought more loosely by a certain merit of their own, we prefer, by the most holy counsel, to pursue εὐχαριστία [graciousness], affability and amiable grace, not without salt, rather than either a εὐτραπελία [wittiness] strongly suspected of μωρολογία [morology], or one hedged about on every side with dangers; the more cautious, the more certain it is that

*Nothing is more inept than an inept laugh,* § 376, 191.

§ 381. The more readily will a nation find consolation whose genius perchance is indeed apt both for devising slender things with a certain dignity and for throwing out jests and sayings with grace, but is said to be surpassed by some of its neighbours in a certain slenderness and subtlety, I know not what, though a very amiable one; since, if some people counts among its citizens more beautiful wits born perhaps for this character alone, it is no wonder if they cultivate more happily the art which alone they can attain than do their neighbours, whose wits and the huge impulses of their hearts both can and desire to rise higher and to put on at once more numerous and graver persons, § 249. For these

*the loss of jests is easy.*

[p. 240] § 382. A heart and wit great enough for thinking slender things proportionately both can, and burns the more, to keep itself pure and unstained from the filth both of things and of signification or of elocution, the nearer it remembers itself to be to the common people, and the more easily, hence, it could be wrapped up in the crowd, from which it nevertheless shrinks no less than does that sublime one:

*I hate the profane crowd and keep it off,* § 243.

The same mind, bearing itself in absolutely great things as either the least, or at least now as one of the least, does not pursue what is paradoxical to almost all honourable people by the simpler formula of living, but is busy with its own affairs, to prove its own to them, to declare its mind evidently to them without roughness or thorns, to make them, if it can, more certain of what it wished to persuade the good of, and that without circumlocutions which few of its contemporaries would grasp, § 244.

§ 383. A great mind labouring elegantly in slender things prefers some dryness to wantoning in tumid or empty insertions, knowing that pomp is odious at the circus games, § 301, 284; it will prefer a certain thinness, § 249, to any muddy river, such as Lucilius’s, if

*often in an hour, as a great feat, / he dictated two hundred verses standing on one foot.*

but the arid, § 255, the bloodless, § 258, and [p. 241] the jejune manner of thinking, § 259, it will shun equally with the frivolous, § 261; finally it will think, will, and do all things by the old formula of the prudent: *As it is fitting to deal well among good men.*

§ 384. If one wished to call more briefly the aesthetic magnitude of mind relative to slender things, which has hitherto been so often described, THE SIMPLE HONOURABLENESS OF AESTHETIC MATTERS, it would be permitted to call that which is either only sufficient for middle things and the middle manner of thinking, or is at least now uniquely required for them, AESTHETIC NOBILITY OF MIND, the gravity and magnanimity of setting charmingly before the eyes some middle material in an equalising and worthy magnitude of thoughts, § 189.

§ 385. In conceiving this I by no means exclude *nobility* of birth, which Cicero somewhere calls a *bland little conciliator*, not a little *commending* one *to the opinion of men*; yet I wish there to be thought that habit of wit and heart, both natural and in its present and existing state, which one born in a noble place embraces,

*Unless one lives badly before the Lepidi, unless the all-night dice / are played before the Numantini.* Iuu. Sat. VIII.

[p. 242] or if rather a mind worthy of the illustrious line of its ancestors, whose morals

*bear the mark of lofty nobility.* Ou. Fast. III, 2.

or altogether, what is yet greater,

*though he be well-born by ancestral names, / he surpasses his race by the nobility of his morals*, Trist. IV, 4.

§ 386. *The Spartan who, being reproached by someone because he went down lame into the battle-line, answered: to fight,* I wish, *not to flee,* is said by Valerius Maximus to have answered *nobly*, III. 7; by our present notion you will find in his answer neither merely simple honourableness, nor, on the contrary, singular majesty, but something midway between the two, which is precisely that which we now wish; which, at any rate, being already in itself more arduous than a mind great enough for slender things, is more easily obtained by the well-born, when from their tender nails, as they say, they are led as if by the hand to a more upright manner of thinking and more polished manners of living, and has more difficulty for others, so that their minds are raised to it only the later, though it still cannot altogether fail to happen, if you exercise yourself diligently in the praiseworthy society of the truly noble, in disciplines and practice, and vehemently despise whatever [p. 243] is below the place to which you tend, and trifles everywhere, § 191.

§ 387. A mind that is to have nobility enough for the middle manner in thinking beautifully, § 383, is mindful and rather tenacious not only of absolute dignity, § 193, since no one would call a sow that is a friend of the mud noble, but also of the comparative dignity which the pursuits and the manners of true nobility demand, § 213, 1) the negative. Hence it will be able and willing to move every stone, even with a long arm, lest anything of ignorance, anything of error, anything of the depraved habit and sickness of minds be detected in the things it thinks more charmingly, which, even if it could agree well enough with simple honour, would nevertheless disgrace the higher order of persons and things, not without baseness.

§ 388. It was once *base for a nobleman to be ignorant of the law in which he moves*, and perhaps it will be so again some day. Hence Juvenal seems to himself to have reported something absurd when he says: *some eloquent fellow from the lowest of the plebs is wont to defend the causes*

*Of the unlearned nobleman; one from the gowned plebs will come* / *To untie the knots of the law and solve the riddles of the statutes.*

It was base in some places for a nobleman not to have served in war. [p. 244] Hence the same Juvenal thinks he has hit the absurd thing on the head when he continues:

*This youth makes for the Euphrates and the eagles that guard* / *The conquered Batavians, industrious in arms; but you* / *Are nothing but a Cecropid, most like a Herm’s trunk.*

Among these base things, and truly such, those also come into the reckoning which are judged so by the institutions of our ancestors, § 212.

§ 389. A mind noble enough to think middling things beautifully will further look round for the comparative dignity that befits the pursuits and the life of the nobility, 2) the positive, so that it has learned those things, and that indeed exactly enough, so that it both knows how to carry them through to their end, and with a constant mind decides upon them, things which simple honour perhaps cannot know, will, will constantly and do, or is the more easily excused by its own weakness if it has gone wrong, but which, if they can be displayed with decorum and set perfected before the eyes of those who doubted before, then indeed no longer leave it uncertain that he who is of such a kind is not one of the many, but conspicuous for the exceptional gifts of his wit, his virtue and his mind.

§ 390. [p. 245] Brevity does not allow us to gather together what belongs to this dignity, either all of it or most of it, that is, to go through almost the whole of practical philosophy, and of mathematics as well, and at the same time to be carried out upon the wide-spreading ocean of the manners of men differing in nations and ages, § 212. Some image of it, though inadequate to our limits, is offered by Juvenal’s counsels:
