*Be a good soldier, a good guardian, likewise an arbiter* / *Of integrity; if ever you are called as witness in a doubtful* / *And uncertain matter, though Phalaris command you to be* / *False and, with the bull brought near, dictate perjuries,* / *Count it the highest wickedness to prefer life to honour,* / *And for the sake of living to lose the reasons for living.* / *When the province* / *Receives you as its governor, set rein and measure to anger,* / *Set them also to avarice, pity the destitute allies,* / *Look to what the laws advise, what the senate-house commands,* / *What rewards await the good, with how just a thunderbolt* / *Both Capito and Numitor fell, when the senate condemned them.*

§ 391. [p. 246] The nobility of which we are now speaking, § 388, scorns whatever is lowest and abject, § 195.

*Or will what* / *Is base in a cobbler become the Volesi and Brutus?*

But even those things which perhaps do not conflict with simple honour, on the abstract formula of the philosophers, nevertheless take away, in the opinion and manners of men, by whose judgements even a nobleman’s reputation must stand, something of that dignity which, while it is middling, is not the least, § 212; and the aesthetician whom we are now forming in our mind, who is to paint middling things charmingly, resolves with himself, in thinking and deciding, to keep that dignity before his eyes and not to depart from it by the breadth of a nail. How harsh a censor is the poet?

*Lentulus played Laureolus nimbly, even well,* / *And deserves, in my judgement, a real cross.*

§ 392. A mind elegantly noble will, even in such materials as could be thought slenderly or could be thought more sublimely, seek a singular artifice in order to declare that it has meditated not only what it brings forward, but also what it leaves out, since the shrewd spectator, experienced in these things, will himself supply to himself many things, now on account of their thinness, now on account of their sublimity, which he will find not exhibited, but will nonetheless find to have come into the author’s mind, [p. 247] and that he did not slacken his powers, and at the same time nicely spared them, § 215.

§ 393. The nobility of mind which we now want thinks that it must guard against βάθους [bathos], § 317, especially its third kind, § 276, and against the third kind of tumidity, § 217, § 279; it rather follows the moral manner of thinking, inasmuch as it observes, even relatively, not only every kind of decorum, but also that degree of it which reigns almost alone in the society of the nobler sort of men, § 228; it will indeed afford some relaxation and elevation of its thoughts, especially in a longer work, § 273, but as it is always even-tenored in the broader sense, § 268, so for the most part it is at the same time such in the stricter sense, § 267; it will prefer a certain mediocrity, § 269, tempered to middling things even in the stricter sense, § 270, 271, and, according to the themes and the formula of signifying once chosen, now higher, now lower, § 272, bringing to a spectator of a nobility roughly equal to that of the mind that thinks something easy and sweet, § 274, not only with abject ways of thinking about a middling matter, § 278, but also with fluctuating and uncertain ones, § 280.

[p. 248]

#### Sectio XXVI. Magnanimitas in Aestheticis Genere Maxima. — Section XXVI. Magnanimity in Aesthetic Matters, the Greatest in Kind

§ 394. There remains AESTHETIC MAGNANIMITY SO CALLED PAR EXCELLENCE, a superior wit cultivated by the best things and the greatest in kind, born for immortality, and a breast, while it still lives in a mortal body, both equal to thinking the things greatest in kind in accordance with their majesty, and watchful, with every effort, that

Hence it often *marvels at the unaccustomed threshold of Olympus,* / *And sees beneath its feet the clouds*, § 318, *and the stars.* / Often *the life of the gods*, S. V. § 206, *it will receive, and will see* / *Heroes mingled with the gods*, and likewise *will be seen by them.*

§ 395. The same *burns with its own brilliance the arts which it outweighs,* / *Set beneath itself,*

not those trifling ones only, § 191,

*which* it fastidiously *overthrows with its foot,*

but also those habits of mind great enough for slender, nay for middling things, which, compared with [p. 249] this magnanimity, not rarely seem to fall from their titles, which they nevertheless hold by the best right when set outside fear of comparison. The things you have thought with a certain aesthetic dignity, § 193, are all of them indeed thought σεμνῶς [gravely]; but comparatively, Pliny II, ep. 11: *Cornelius Tacitus*, he says, *replied most eloquently, and, what is exceptional in his oratory,* σεμνῶς, as if those who do not attain the sublime gravity of Tacitus could not have replied σεμνῶς.

§ 396. It does not call away from the vile and the abject, § 195, only this

*Virtue, opening heaven to those who deserve* / *Not to die, essays its way by a path denied,* / *And the vulgar throngs and the damp* / *Ground it spurns on fleeing wing:*

but it is not even content with the absolute magnitude and dignity of things and thoughts, § 198, 201; higher, sometimes, it *snatches the blood of poor parents*

*On a wing not customary nor slender,*

so that *it does not linger longer on earth*, and *leaves the very cities, and, greater than envy, is borne upward through the liquid ether.*

§ 397. [p. 250] This magnanimity, the greatest in kind among aesthetic magnanimities, directly takes upon itself the painting of virtue in all its majesty.

*Virtue, knowing nothing of sordid rebuff,* / *Shines with unstained honours.* / *Nor does it take up or lay down the axes* / *At the whim of the popular breeze.* Hor. III. od. 2.

cf. od. 3. It belongs indirectly to the same magnitude of mind to abominate public vices, like so many epidemic plagues, § 203.

*Ages fruitful in guilt first defiled* / *Marriage, and the race, and the homes.* / *From this source the disaster flowed* / *Into the fathers and the people.* etc. od. 6.

cf. II. od. 15, 18. III. 1, 24. Epod. 7. § 204.

§ 398. The superior wit of minds that are excellently great does not permit them to be practical and pragmatic with neglect of theory, which they love and embrace even then, when in the contemplation of things naturally greatest it seems to cling so fixedly that it does not at once appear what such a consideration contributes to [p. 251] morals and the blessed life. § 205. Thus in a man not imbued with the truer sacred rites none but an eminently great mind utters these words:

*Nothing greater is begotten than he himself (the Father)* / *Nor does anything flourish like him or second to him;* / *Yet the honours next to him* / *Pallas has taken,* etc. Hor. I. od. 12.

§ 399. In matters conspicuous for natural magnitude, the greatest in kind, I have also thrown into the examples mention of the things specifically greatest, divine things, lest perhaps one who is to form for himself minds greatest in kind should fabricate in his mind some Polyphemuses or Enceladuses, or Mezentiuses, despisers of the gods, who are not truly great of mind, but tumidly proud. The first law of positive dignity through sublime things is rather this: subject all human things, even the specifically greatest, to the divine, § 206.

*Over kings to be feared by their own flocks,* / *Over the kings themselves is the empire of Jove,* / *Famed for his triumph over the giants,* / *Moving all things with his brow.*

§ 400. From which formula Horace, σεμνῶς [gravely], I. od. 12:

*Father and guardian of the human race,* / *may you reign with Caesar* / *second to you;* / [p. 252] *Less than you, he will rule the wide world justly,* / *You with heavy chariot will shake Olympus,* / *You will send upon the groves that are not pure* / *Your hostile thunderbolts.*

The same of the Roman people, than which the Roman knew nothing greater among human things. III. od. 6.

*Because you bear yourself as less than the gods, you rule.* / *From here take every beginning, to here refer the end.* / *The gods neglected have given many* / *Woes to mournful Hesperia.*

But when in ode 5

*We have believed*, he says, *that Jove thundering in heaven* / *Reigns: Augustus will be held* / *A present god,*

he seems already to have felt the effect of servitude, be it the most just or at least the most acceptable, of which § 373; and also, according to the philosopher whom Longinus cites, we turn out nothing but flatterers μεγαλοφυεῖς [of great nature].

§ 401. Magnanimity, whose sublime things Longinus calls its Echo, and of which we are now speaking, does not indeed wholly transcend the aesthetic horizon, nor does it conceive things only for itself in the way the pure science of philosophy does; yet because it cleaves chiefly and almost solely to the things greatest in kind among observables, it is occupied with those [p. 253] materials which are common to the logical and the aesthetic horizon of reason, and hence, and at the same time for other reasons, it has more of reason mixed with its analogue, more also of mathematical distinctness, and therefore measures and observes more carefully the degrees, so to speak specific, within the things themselves greatest in kind, than the great minds of which S. XXV. § 207. It belongs to princes and kings who are truly great to give, with immense ease, yet to each his own in all their intercourse, even in things that seem smaller, and to hold firmly the degrees of the higher honours and dignities, whether placed below or beside themselves, and according to this ladder, as it were, to render to every person what befits him.

§ 402. Hence a mind not even sufficient for the noble manner of thinking and living is not only unfit for conceiving this magnanimity, but those also are deceived who, always choosing for themselves the specifically greatest among these things greatest in kind, fly over the smaller and middling things among them, which they confess themselves unequal to thinking beautifully, while they are quite certain with themselves that they can nicely describe the greatest of grand things, § 209. One’s powers are rather to be tried first in grand things of which on another occasion the very neglect will be still more sublime, in grand things to which a good deal of pomp, of ceremonies, of gold and of royal purple still accrues from without, [p. 254] so that at length you may suffice to fashion more sublimely either naked majesty, majesty, I say, of a hero, one of the greatest things, or the purer sacred rites, while they are performed solely within the mind. *That, however, is a laughable arrogance, to confess oneself unskilled in smaller vessels, but to have learned to steer quinqueremes or even larger ships.* Cic. de Or. I.

§ 403. Although a mind great enough for sublime things is not that Stoic sage whom,

*If the world were shattered and fell in,* / *The ruins would strike undismayed,* § 353,

yet it is not affected by lighter cares, nor is it thrown out of a tranquil serenity that imitates the life of the gods above. It does not much care about the smallest things, nor is it moved more vehemently if some reptiles of Parnassus, censors irregularly elected, boast of having found a trifling stain on its garment, not what boys find in a bean; rather it thinks, in a true collision of rules, with a bold impulse, while it strenuously follows in a loftier orbit the strongest laws, not even known to the Pygmies, so to settle its accounts that to the lighter little souls of their own petty art, which alone they know, it may seem to spurn, to overthrow, to trample underfoot the most sacred rules, if it please the gods, really the lesser and weaker ones, not without [p. 255] a little tear from the pretty busybodies, yet truly with a not inelegant exception. § 210.

§ 404. We have placed absolute aesthetic gravity in noble minds, § 363, as Cicero himself describes the *moderator* of wits that strive for great things, *not as some master and craftsman, but as one of the number of the gowned, and out of forensic practice, a man of middling kind and not wholly unskilled.* But if a noble mind of this kind wishes in act to approach the thinking of grand things, it must, as if forgetful of itself and of its ordinary state, be roused and as it were snatched out of itself into a greater theatre than that in which it daily plays its part, and so mingled with gods and heroes that it appears to have contracted some lofty familiarity with them, and not as if carried off into an alien world, but as long dwelling in such company, § 213, 396.

*Do you hear? or does an amiable* / *Madness mock me? I seem to hear and to wander* / *Through holy groves, which pleasant* / *Waters and breezes enter.* Hor. III. od. 4.

§ 405. The magnanimity we are now seeking longs to consider not only great themes, but also from that [p. 256] side from which they appear greater and grand. § 215. Remember Dido 1) in that elegant epigram of Ausonius:

*Unhappy Dido, to no husband well joined!* / *When this one perishes she flees, when that one flees she perishes.*

⁠2) whom Ovid, her. ep. VII, paints with this epitaph:

*Aeneas furnished both the cause of death and the sword;* / *Dido herself fell by the use of her own hand.*

⁠3) that Dido of Virgil, thus performing her own funeral rites at a pyre built of Trojan garments and the familiar bed: IV. Aen. 651.

*Sweet relics, while the fates and the god allowed,* / *Receive this soul, and release me from these cares.* / *I have lived, and the course which fortune gave I have finished,* / *And now a great image of me will go beneath the earth.* / *I founded a famous city, I saw my own walls,* / *Avenging my husband I exacted punishment from a hostile brother,* / *Happy, alas! too happy, if only the Dardanian* / *Keels had never touched our shores.*

§ 406. Which Dido would heroic magnanimity prefer to have feigned by itself? For my part, without any hesitation, [p. 257] I name Virgil’s Dido. You ask the reason? Measure the predicates. The first can fit even some little woman of the Gypsies, who, when her first husband had been sent to the gallows for a crime, saved herself by flight, but when with her second husband, of the same bran, she had again committed an atrocious crime, and he had slipped out of the hands of the lictors, paid with her death the penalty of their common crime. The second will suit also the wife of any common soldier, who with cudgels has driven her into such despair of her conjugal fortune that, in his absence, she cut her throat with his sword. In Virgil, however, not only is she of whom we speak excellently great, but so also are the things that are predicated of her. § 405.

§ 407. Our magnanimity will now with every effort guard against both the fourth kind of βάθους [bathos] and all its species, according to the degree of their baseness, § 310. The gods of Virgil already speak, act and suffer θεοπρεπωτέρως [more befittingly for gods] than the gods of Homer. § 311. Virgil’s heroine comments more augustly on the things that concern her, § 408, than Ovid’s Dido:

*It endures to the last, and the tenor of my fate which was before* / *Follows the final moments of our life.*

Behold! an ordinary woman complaining of perpetual calamity! Virgil’s Dido, stronger, proclaims herself happy, [p. 258] except for the last calamity.

*My husband fell, slaughtered at the household altars,* / *And my brother holds the rewards of so great a crime.*

The one complains at length of her husband unavenged and his assassin unpunished, indeed holding rewards; the other in a few words congratulates herself on the avenging of her husband and the punishment of her brother. The first is any woman’s.

*An exile I am driven, I leave my husband’s ashes and my fatherland,* / *And am carried over hard roads with the enemy following.* / *I land among strangers, escaped from my brother and the sea,* / *And buy the shore which, faithless one, I gave to you.*

What have we read so far in Ovid that could not happen to the wife of any military prefect whatever? § 312.

§ 408. Let the gravity of the German nation, running with decorum through all the degrees of great things, have something with which to rebut its neighbours who exalt their own slenderness beyond measure. There was among them one — by what name shall I call the manikin? — who would lacerate with those playful little verses of the new jesters the torments of the best and greatest of men, which deliver the human race from infinite evils, and yet wished to be seen as a Christian in the kingdom of Christendom. Justice herself would sooner grant pardon to a hundred citizens for sinning grievously [p. 259] in slender matters than to this one man for applying, in one matter, indeed the gravest, a vile manner of thinking most lightly. § 317.

§ 409. That grand magnanimity, from which sublime things can truly be hoped for, is so averse to all tumidity, its fourth kind, and the species of this, § 318, that, if either were necessary, it would rather fall into manifest βάθος [bathos] than be entangled in the cryptic lowlinesses of childish things. Hence examples of the former are still less rare in the best authors than of these ineptitudes, with which Martianus Capella, Tertullian, Sedulius, and their equals, abundantly swell. Far from here be big-talking lies, and whatever there is of insipid magniloquence!

§ 410. A mind greatest in kind in aesthetic matters has the imitation of grand things so commended to it by Horace, Cicero, Longinus, by reason itself through the psychological doctrine of the expectation of similar cases, that it rejoices not only to turn the *exemplars* of sublime writers, painters, etc.

*With nightly hand, to turn them with daily hand,*

but also to look upon the deeds and sayings of illustrious men through all ages as into a mirror, and to compose both its own manners, as far as it may, and those which it is to express in its thinking, after the likeness of [p. 260] these. § 285. Nor will it stick only to the more famous: it does not blush to dig out any greater things from the darkness, to vindicate them from the injury of oblivion, and, even if they are found in the enemy himself, to praise them according to the merits of the cause. § 286.

§ 411. A mind great in the degree often mentioned is not suspicious and incredulous if it seems to itself to hear, or indeed to see, something outstanding and eminent, and, although it has long since learned to distinguish bronze from lupins, it nevertheless knows from its own experiment that not every arduous thing flowing only from singular virtue is by that very fact to be counted, on account of the disgrace of our race, among absolutely incredible things, § 286, 301. Just as certain small occasions move it, worthy of cedars, little to an impetus, S. V, so it sometimes feels, more strongly than others, a peculiar incitement, since, even in its own judgement,

*Hidden virtue differs little* / *From buried inertia,*

if ever it detects *battles that should be told by the Muses*, if a heroic love of country, if an outstanding sanctity of life and manners in many, and yet those

*all unwept* / *And unknown are pressed down by a long* / *Night, because they lack a sacred bard,*

or seem not long afterwards to be *plucked* at [p. 261] by *envious oblivions*, if it too should *be silent about them, leaving them unadorned.* Hor. IV. od. 9.

§ 412. The nature of the well-born is assuredly πρῶτον καὶ ἀρχέτυπον στοιχεῖον ἐπὶ πάντων ἐστί [the first and archetypal element over all things] of this magnanimity, as Longinus, S. II, liberally concedes; not that nature only which in divine wits and in hearts born almost solely for heroic things, but in very few, is αὐτόνομος [a law unto itself], but also that nature of noble minds, § 404, which, not indeed by its ordinary powers, but by those powers living and directed by good counsels toward something higher and more sublime, and more strongly incited by grand examples, can and resolves to rise to that height. S. II.

§ 413. Nor indeed do I think that nature alone, so far as it is inborn, here at least, makes up both sides of the page, or that I can from this bring about what experience confirms: that the best and greatest natures, or at least excellently good ones, § 412, can be depressed, constricted and diminished by the barbarism of the ages and of their own time, by depraved examples, by the preconceived opinions of the great, by servitude, as it is in Longinus, S. XLIV, as if by *a certain little casket for the mind and a public prison*, § 359, *just as the little caskets (if the report be true) in which dwarfs, called Pygmies, are reared, not only hinder the growth* [p. 262] *of those shut in, but also contract them by the bond of the body set round them.* § 359.

§ 414. If any reins of external liberty, if any external servitude, are especially made for depressing minds and blunting all their greatest pursuits and incitements, which I by no means deny, nor would I seem to myself to wander beyond the olives if, on account of the opposite reasons of opposites, I were to count among the first aids for raising minds to the truly lofty an intimate persuasion concerning the copious system of the greatest events, not of those past and present only, but also, for the greatest part, of those still to be expected; a persuasion which not only sets forth simply for imitation the best and greatest examples of sublimity and venerable virtue, but also supplies, with the most evident counsels, the most salutary remedies for bringing forth command over oneself, for fleeing all internal moral servitude, and for obtaining victory over oneself; a persuasion, I say, about true things of this kind, both done and to be done, which at the same time bestows divinely on souls such force and power that not even by the fear of death can they be driven to that which a tyrant demanding wicked things has designed, the supernatural complement and supplement of internal and psychological liberty.

§ 415. [p. 263] Longinus, l. c., goes on to recount the causes of the dearth of sublime wits in his times: 2) the peace of the whole world, a cause in which, however, he himself puts little trust, and the Discord of brothers has sufficiently provided that the want of heroes cannot long be imputed to it, and it would be imputed to it falsely, if ever it were permitted to hope for a universal and lasting peace of the habitable world, and men at the same time were to learn the lofty arts of peace, by which the father of his country transcends even the supreme subduers of kingdoms and nations. 3) *The infinite war that holds our desires in its grip*, about which the interpreters dispute what sort of thing it is.

§ 416. The ordinary state of excellent magnanimity is a state of tranquillity, E. § 445, set above the common perturbations of the mind and the internal tumults of the mind. Through a deep harmony of reason and the lower faculties of knowing, of the will and the lower faculties of desiring, which often remains in eminently great men, even if they are moved rather vehemently, and not without affections, since they are men. They are carried away into πάθος ἐνθουσιαστικόν [enthusiastic passion], if the occasion brings it, but with reason approving, and with a rational conscience, antecedent, concomitant and consequent. So far there is no internal discord. If, however, [p. 264] some struggle sometimes has a place in heavenly minds, it is not one of merely sensate desires against others no less sensate, παθῶν εἰς πάθη [of passions against passions], but of decrees, with which something sensate is mixed, fighting against mere sensate appetitions, and neither for long nor without end, but for the most part with the better part winning, whose victory brings back again deep peace and the old serenity to tranquil minds: M. § 693.

§ 417. With this state compare the state of bestiality, E. § 434. No rest has this, no tranquillity, no concordant harmony of lulled reason and of the affects and the instincts, nor of these among themselves. You would now believe it

*The homeland of the storm-clouds, places teeming with raging South winds,*

which the whole man

*Indeed the rapid winds would carry off with them and sweep through the air.*

Yet you are deceived. Not long afterwards

*Together East wind and South wind rush forth, and the South-wester* / *Thick with squalls,*

and to each its own crime. Let external servitude set some bar to them,

*They, indignant, with a great murmur roar around* / *The barriers,*

[p. 265] *struggling* within continually, and at the first opportunity again,

*as if in a formed column,* / *Where a gate is given, they rush out.*

Thus wretchedly is such a mind driven now by the accursed hunger for gold, now by ambition and pride, now by the whirlwind of pleasures, now by sloth, now by some of these, now by all, disagreeing among themselves. This is that infinite war, § 415, most hostile to the magnanimity pregnant with sublime things, § 416.

§ 418. Already one of the storms mentioned breaks the mind sufficiently. Rightly Longinus, among the causes of the failure of the excellent and the sublime, recounts 4. φιλοχρηματίαν [the love of money], charmingly calling φιλαργυρίαν νόσημα μικροποιόν [avarice a disease that makes small], § 45, and feigns as its children, together with great expense (Sumptuousness), Pride and Luxury, from which indeed, no degenerate offspring, are begotten in turn Insolence, Injustice and Shamelessness, amid which progeny the magnitudes of minds waste away and wither.

§ 419. *Or, when once this rust and care for property has imbued our minds, do we hope that songs can be fashioned worthy to be smeared with cedar oil and kept in smooth cypress?* A. P. 330.

[p. 266] Phaedrus himself denies it of the fables, such as he wrote, or is said to have written. III. prol. *Of the nine arts, although the care*

*of possessing I have utterly erased from my heart, / And have thrown myself upon this life with praise unwilling, / Yet I am received fastidiously into their company. / What do you think befalls him who seeks to heap up great wealth / with every vigil, / Preferring sweet gain to learned labour?* cf. Hor. od. II. 15, 18. III. 2, 24.

§ 420. 5) φιληδονία καταβυθίζει [the love of pleasure sinks it], § 417, 418. An intemperate desire for external delights softens morals to such a point that at last no masculine strength at all remains in the mind, and least of all as much worth as grand things require.

*Do you see how pale every man rises / from a doubtful dinner? Nay, the body, burdened / with yesterday’s vices, weighs down the mind too together with it, / And dashes to the ground that particle of the divine breath.*

Hor. Serm. II. 2, a satire which almost entirely pertains here. Only count the expenditures of time that must be spent in acquiring the theory, and an empirical one at that, raised by rather frequently repeated experiments to that certainty which befits an arbiter of elegances of this kind, [p. 267] and you will wonder that any leisure at all was left over to be granted to weightier matters.

§ 421. 6) Finally Longinus numbers sloth among the causes of minds rising little or not at all to the sublime. For while exercises are interrupted, the habit does not grow, and by that very fact there grows the disuse opposed to the habit, that is, difficulty, degenerating at last into true impotence.

*Are you preparing to placate envy, with virtue abandoned? / Be despised, wretch. The wicked Siren / Sloth must be shunned, or whatever you have gained by a better life / Must be laid down with an even mind.* Hor. Serm. II. 3, v. 13.

Ambition and the pride of haughtiness, the natural parents of tumidity, Longinus omitted, perhaps not distinguishing these vices sufficiently from a laudable philotimia, or fearing, in the manner of many, lest, once these were utterly overthrown, the latter be killed off along with them, or at least enervated. But one versed in a sounder ethics perceives by reason, what experience and history sufficiently confirm, that just as they can and should properly be separated, so in those ages and places in which the ambitious pride of haughtiness was substituted for the love of true honour, it was not the reign of sublimity that flourished, but the tyranny of tumidity that raged.

[p. 268] § 422. Loving true beauty in thinking, so that you may be able, as to great things, S. XV, absolutely, S. XVI, in proportion to their relative magnitude, S. XVII, with thoughts proportioned to the matter, S. XVIII, whether in the slender, S. XVIIII, or the middle, S. XX, or the sublime manner of thinking, S. XXI, without the vices which in the greatest things are wont to be most conspicuous, S. XXII, to augment, S. XXIII, the innate magnitude of your breast, of which you must have some, § 45, compose it also to absolute gravity, S. XXIIII, and raise it as far as it will be able to rise, S. XXV, happy if it has sufficed even for attaining sublimities. S. XXVI.

*Not, though Maeonian Homer holds / the first seat, do the Pindaric lie hidden, / and the Cean, and the threatening ones of Alcaeus, / and the grave ones of Stesichorus, the muses; / Not, if Anacreon once sported anything, / has age blotted it out.* Hor. IV. 9, od.

[p. 269]

#### Sectio XXVII. Veritas Aesthetica. — Section XXVII. Aesthetic Truth

§ 423. Let the third care in things to be thought elegantly, § 115, 177, be TRUTH, M. § 515, but AESTHETIC truth, § 22, i.e. truth insofar as it is to be cognised sensately. We know the metaphysical truth of objects to be their agreement with the most universal principles, M. § 92, and thence we understand Leibniz, who in Theodicaeae T. II. p. m. 312 says: *It can be said in some way that the principle of contradiction and of sufficient reason is included in the definition of the true and the false.* For the representation of what is metaphysically true in some object, insofar as it is carried out within the soul of a certain subject, is that agreement of representations with objects which most people call *logical truth*, others *mental* truth, truth of affection, of correspondence and of conformity, while they call metaphysical truth material.

§ 424. Metaphysical truth could be called objective, the representation of objectively true things in a given soul SUBJECTIVE TRUTH, or also, being easy-going in words, let us call the same *logical* with [p. 270] most people, but *more broadly*, so that we may agree in the thing itself, for whose sake chiefly these matters are being repeated from somewhat higher up. For now I think it is clear that TRUTH, when you have called it metaphysical or objective, as you please, so represented in a given soul that it yields in the same a logical truth more broadly so called, or mental and subjective truth, now presents itself to the intellect chiefly in a spirit, while it is among the things distinctly perceived by it, LOGICAL TRUTH MORE STRICTLY SO CALLED, now presents itself to the analogue of reason and the lower cognitive faculties, either solely or chiefly, *aesthetic* truth. § 423.

§ 425. Read, if you please, the counsel which Chremes in Terence’s Heaut. III. 50. 1 supplies to Menedemus, and you will catch him speaking in reply of a certain aesthetic truth:

*To see the truth, and to say it so, as the thing is.*

Remember the satires, when they do not hesitate to set forth *truth, which* often *begets hatred*, nor

*to scrape tender ears / with biting truth,*

safe as it were by their own privilege:

*although laughing, to tell the truth, / Who forbids?*

and compare these with the practical counsels, of what would seem the same argument, [p. 271] of some ethical philosopher demonstrating his points more carefully and scientifically: and you will catch in the example the difference between aesthetic truth and the more strictly logical. § 424.

§ 426. Common to logical and aesthetic meditations is the *virtue* which Cicero off. II. 18 describes almost genetically, which consists *in perceiving what in each thing is true and sincere, what is consonant with each thing* (agreement with the principle of contradiction), *what is consequent* (agreement with the principle of the consequent, M. § 23), *from what each thing is generated, what is the cause of each thing* (agreement with the principles of reason, M. § 20, and of sufficient reason, M. § 22). But whereas the former strive for a distinct and intellectual insight into these things, the latter, keeping within their own horizon, are busy to behold the same elegantly with the senses and the analogue of reason. § 424.

§ 427. If we call TRUTH mental and subjective, every truth of representations, which thus far has been called only logical, AESTHETICO-LOGICAL: it is not the opinion of those who make this distinction, as if 1) some, nay many, aesthetically true things were not [p. 272] at the same time logically true, which we willingly concede. In that address of Nature to one refusing to die, which Lucretius feigns, III. 940-957, and to which he truly enough subjoins:

*What should we answer, except that Nature brings a just suit, / and sets forth in words a true cause?*

almost all things are at the same time logically true, M. § 265. E. § 252.

§ 428. Nor do we deny, nor are we ignorant, 2) that aesthetic truth in parts beautifully depicted often yields the logical truth of the whole, and that it can hardly happen otherwise, if the enumeration of the parts is completed and carried through to the end. This one thing we observe, that truth, insofar as it is intellectual, is not aimed at directly by the aesthetician; if indirectly one truth emerges from several aesthetic truths, or coincides with what is aesthetically true, the rational aesthetician congratulates himself on it, § 38, yet that is not what was now chiefly being sought, § 423.

§ 429. But if there be some logical truth, strictly so called namely, § 424, to be thought only through the intellect; whether for that subject which is supposed to be about to think beautifully, or for those personal objects for whose sake you chiefly think, and [p. 273] both again, either always, or at least under the circumstances now actually obtaining: it is set above the aesthetic horizon, and rightly, at least for the present time, is omitted. § 15, 121. Think to yourself, as an astronomer, not a physicist only but also a mathematician, or with astronomers, of the annular eclipse of the past year; but think of the same as a shepherd, either to your companions or to your Neaera: oh! how many true things you thought before, now entirely to be passed over!

§ 430. There are certain truths so small that to pursue or mention them is below the aesthetic horizon, at least below beautiful magnitude, now absolute magnitude altogether, now at any rate relative. § 120, 178. These infinitely small truths the aesthetician does not care about. § 191, 221. Not even for the historian does he think this severe law written without exception: *That he say nothing not true*, reading himself in Aen. VI.

*The band of youths leaps out ardent / upon the Hesperian shore. — — — — / But pious Aeneas seeks the heights over which lofty Apollo / presides, and at the same time the retreat of the dread Sibyl, / the vast cavern.*

he neither cares nor thinks with which foot Aeneas first touched Italy, and yet it is most true [p. 274] that it was either the left or the right, unless with both, which is less seemly.

§ 431. Aesthetic truth demands of objects to be thought elegantly I. possibility, § 426, 1) absolute possibility, M. § 15, 90, insofar as it is to be cognised sensately, § 423, i.e. that in the object, while it pleases to regard it even in itself, nothing of marks contradicting one another be observed, whether by the senses or by the analogue of reason. M. § 8. A certain inequality of sins has this possibility, and hence is also aesthetically true. M. § 272. On the contrary,

*Those who have decided that sins are nearly equal are in difficulty, / when it comes to the truth. Sense and morals rebel, / and utility itself, well nigh the mother of the just and the fair.* Hor. Serm. I. 3.

§ 432. Aesthetic truth requires the possibility of its objects, § 431, 2) hypothetical possibility, § 426. M. § 16, and this again A.) natural, M. § 469, insofar as it is not more closely connected with a certain freedom, and can be judged by the analogue of reason. § 423. This I detect in Aen. IX. 100.

[p. 275] *Then the almighty father, who holds the supreme power over things, / begins. As he speaks, the lofty house of the gods falls silent, / and the earth, shaken to its foundation; the high aether is silent.*

§ 433. Aesthetic truth requires in its objects possibility B.) moral, a) more broadly so called, M. § 723, so that the things which are to be derived only from freedom be also such and so great as would seem to the analogue of reason to flow from a given freedom, a given person and the moral character e.g. of a certain man. This is that *coming nearer to the truth of life*, according to which indeed it is not all one whether

*A mature old man, or one still fervid in flowering youth, / and a powerful matron, or a busy nurse, / a wandering merchant, or the tiller of a green little field, / a Colchian, or an Assyrian, one bred at Thebes, or at Argos*

speaks, whether the object be one of occupation, or a personal one. Hor. A. P. 115.

§ 434. *You, hear what I and the people with me desire: / if you need an admirer who waits for the curtain, and will sit / right up until the singer says: applaud: / the manners of every age must be noted by you,* / [p. 276] *and a seemliness given to changing natures and years.* 153.

*We shall always dwell on what is adjoined and fitted to the age,* 178.

Hence Horace commended also practical philosophy, applied, so to speak, § 126. 361. 212, because he who holds it properly,

*he, assuredly, / knows how to render to each person what befits him.* § 433.

and perceiving already in his time the utility of that art or knowledge which the *Characters of Manners of Theophrastus* began, and the Gallic Theophrastus thereafter enriched,

*he bade him look to the exemplar of life and manners* v. 317. / *the learned imitator, and draw from thence true voices.*

§ 435. Aesthetic truth requires moral possibility b) more strictly so called, M. § 723, not only in the thinker himself, S. XXIIII–XXVI, but also in the objects to be explicitly or implicitly approved by him, more briefly, in beautiful thoughts, even if they describe Acheron, § 422, but only that which falls within the senses and under the scales of the analogue of reason. § 211. This is that MORAL TRUTH by which, according to Horace,

*It is true that each should measure himself by his own measure and foot.*

[p. 277] Just as I would call the truth demanded in § 433, 434 MORAL TRUTH MORE BROADLY SO CALLED, so I would more gladly call this moral truth MORE STRICTLY SO CALLED, and that MOST STRICTLY SO CALLED the agreement of signs with our mind, which, if it be a virtue, already has the title of sincerity, if it be a vice, already carries with it the ignominy of leakiness. E. § 339.

§ 436. Of this moral truth, but aesthetic, of this dignity in thinking more charmingly, § 435, 182, Cicero beautifully shows the aesthetic limits pro Mur. when, 74, he says, *But Cato deals with me austerely and Stoically; he denies it to be true that goodwill is enticed by food*, etc., and on behalf of the aesthetic truth of the morals against which Cato had inveighed, as against bribery, he replies: *A horrible speech, but usage, life, morals, the state itself reject it.* etc. 75. *Wherefore, Cato, do not reproach with too severe a speech the institutions of our ancestors, which the republic itself, which the long duration of our empire approves.* etc. 76. *As to what you say: that the minds of men ought to be enticed by nothing to the entrusting of magistracies except by dignity* (insofar as it is conceived solely intellectually by philosophers, § 211, 212): *this you yourself, in whom there is the highest dignity, do not observe. For why do you ask anyone* [p. 278] *to support you, to assist you?* etc. 77. *What? that you have a nomenclator.* etc.

§ 437. Aesthetic truth requires of objects to be thought beautifully II. a nexus with their grounds and consequents, § 426, 431, insofar as it is to be cognised sensately, § 423. M. § 24, through the analogue of reason, M. § 640. Let the example be Livy’s Coriolanus, II. There is the ground of his very name and of his first authority, c. 33; hence his loftier spirit against the tribunician power, c. 34; whence the anger of the plebs, from this the exile of Coriolanus, and his hostile mind, carrying him off to the Volscians, not without a reason manifest from the antecedents; hence the counsels of war against the Romans shared with his host Tullus, c. 35; the consequence of these, the cunning artifice of Tullus, and the new indignation of the Volscian plebs against the Romans, c. 37.

§ 438. Hence war is decreed, the leaders Tullus and *Marcius*, our Coriolanus, *a Roman exile*. Owing to his bravery, a prosperous beginning for the Volscians, trepidation of the Roman plebs, hence the first embassy of the Romans, the *harsh answer* which it brought back; returning to the enemy, it is not received. Hence suppliant priests. Since these too are in vain, a well nigh womanish fear now pervades Rome, c. 39. His mother, his wife, and the throng [p. 279] of women at last move his hard mind, and that this may not seem to happen without reason, the historian feigns a truly pathetic speech of the mother. Hence the hostile camp is moved back, yet Coriolanus does not disappear except after some mention of the rest of his fate. At Rome, however, a monument to Women’s Fortune is erected. c. 40. How great a harmony sounds together in these things for nourishing and refreshing at least the analogue of reason of the readers? § 437.

§ 439. Aesthetic truth requires the possibility of objects, both absolute and hypothetical, as it is sensately perceived, § 431–436; all possibility demands unity, M. § 73, absolute possibility absolute unity, hypothetical possibility hypothetical unity, M. § 76. Hence aesthetic truth too requires both unities in its thoughts, insofar as it can be sensately detected, the inseparability of the determinations in the things thought, saving the beauty of the total perception. § 73. This UNITY of objects will be, insofar as it is a phenomenon, AESTHETIC, either of internal determinations, M. § 37, whence the unity of ACTION, if the object of beautiful meditation be an action, or of external determinations and relations, M. § 37, of circumstances, M. § 323, whence the unity of PLACE AND TIME, M. § 325. 281.

*Let it be, what you will, only simple and one,*

[p. 280] and you will obtain at the same time that pleasing rounded brevity, S. XIII. XIV, and beautiful coherence, § 437. Hence *unity* pleased Augustine so much that he called it *the form of all beauty*.

§ 440. AESTHETICO-LOGICAL TRUTH, § 424, is either of universals and notions, and of general judgements, or of singulars and ideas, M. § 148. Let the former be GENERAL, the latter SINGULAR truth. In the object of a general truth never so much metaphysical truth is detected, especially sensately, as in the object of a singular truth. M. § 184. And the more general an aesthetico-logical truth is, the less metaphysical truth is represented in its object, both altogether and especially to the analogue of reason, M. § 150, 184. You have one reason why the aesthetician, most studious of the greatest truth he can observe, § 22, prefers, as far as he can, to more general truths, the most abstract and most universal, the more determinate, less general, less abstract, and to all general ones the singular. The same is urged by richness, § 115, since the more determinate an object you have, the more differences at the same time, M. § 151, and thus the more it is granted to think beautifully about the same. The same is commanded by magnitude, both natural, and by aesthetic dignity, [p. 281] if you reckon in, over and above the magnitude of the universal, the weight, gravity and fecundity that at the same time accrue to any inferior difference of it, § 177.

§ 441. The aesthetico-logical truth of a genus is the perception of a great metaphysical truth, the aesthetico-logical truth of a species is the perception of a greater, the aesthetico-logical truth of an individual, or singular truth, is the perception of the greatest metaphysical truth, in its kind. The first is of the true, the second of the truer, the third of the truest. § 440. Singular truth is either of the internal determinations of the best and greatest being, or of absolutely contingent things. Contingent things are not represented as singulars except as possibles of some whole universe. Hence singular truth about contingent things either presents them as possibles and parts of this universe, M. § 377, and this truth, together with the truth of absolutely necessary things, is called the greatest truth, truth MOST STRICTLY so called, and in popular speech simply TRUTH; or as possibles of another universe and its parts, belonging to the middle cognition of men, M. § 876, HETEROCOSMIC TRUTH.

§ 442. *Truth* most strictly so called, § 441, *is* that which Cicero describes de Inu. II. 162, *through which those things which are, or were before, or are to be, are said* (are perceived) *unaltered* (not altered), [p. 282] and which he seems to compare with lesser truths Quaest. Tusc. V. 13, saying: *That chorus of virtues (constancy, gravity, fortitude, wisdom, the rest), set upon the rack* (by truth either general and abstract, as *those things of the Stoics delight more when tasted than when drunk*, or heterocosmic, § 441, 440) *sets up images before the eyes with the fullest dignity*, S. XXI, *so that the happy life would seem about to hasten to them, and not to suffer them to be deserted by it* (to middle cognition, § 441). *But when you have led the mind away from that picture and the images of the virtues* (either general and abstract, or heterocosmic) *to the thing and the truth* (most strict): *this bare question* (stripped of the heterocosmic objects of middle cognition and separated from all of them) *is left, whether anyone* (in this universe, singularly and in the numerical concrete) *can be happy as long as he is tortured.*

§ 443. Of general aesthetico-logical truths only those are aesthetic which, and insofar as they, can be sensately represented to the analogue of reason, saving charm, § 440, 423, either manifestly and explicitly, or cryptically in the omitted propositions of enthymemes, or in examples in which, as in concrete things, these abstract truths are detected. In this way the very principle of identity, M. § 11, is in [p. 283] Plautus, who would believe it?, in the prologue of the Captiui:

*These two captives whom you see standing here, / they who stand by, these both stand, they do not sit. / You, you are my witnesses that I speak the truth.*

§ 444. Aesthetic truth is truth of things most strictly true insofar as they are sensately perceived as true, by sensations or imaginations, or also by foresights not without presage, and no further; and under the same hypothesis heterocosmic truths are aesthetic truths, neither more nor fewer than are to be perceived by the analogue of reason. § 441. You think the distinction Leibnizian; it is already Tibullus’s, who in the Panegyric of Messalla, L. IV. 1, had told many things about the wanderings of Ulysses, ending them thus, v. 80:

*And whether these things, once known, lie within our lands,* (most strictly true, § 442) / *Or whether fable gave a new world to these wanderings.* (heterocosmically true, § 441)

[p. 284]

#### Sectio XXVIII. Falsitas Aesthetica. — Section XXVIII. Aesthetic Falsity

§ 445. AESTHETIC FALSITY is subjective falsity and the disagreement of thoughts with the truth of the things to be thought, insofar as the latter can be sensately perceived. § 423, 426. Excellently Cicero: *That is false, in which there is perspicuously a lie*, if it be understood of aesthetic falsity, and sensate perspicuity be thought of. M. § 531. But when he wants to illustrate his notion by an example, whether that properly serves the purpose, let the wise men of our times see. *In this way*, he says: *He cannot be wise who neglects money.*

§ 446. *Not he who, cunning, does not know how to compare with Sidonian purple / the fleeces that drink in the dye of Aquinum, / will receive a surer loss, and one nearer to his marrow, / than he who will not be able to distinguish the false from the true.* Hor. l. ep. 10. 26. § 423, 448.

Yet not all subjective falsity, § 424, belongs here, but such as Horace paints at the head of the Art of Poetry:

[p. 285] *If a painter should wish to join a horse’s neck to a human head, / and to lay on varied plumage / over limbs gathered from everywhere, so that a woman fair above / should end foully in a black fish: / admitted to the sight, could you hold back your laughter, friends? / Believe, Pisones, that very like that picture would be a book / whose empty forms are fashioned, like a sick man’s dreams, / so that neither foot nor head is rendered to a single / shape.*

§ 447. Not from the egg, but from false things does Horace begin, and, what would seem witty, from the same Antonius in Cicero de or. II. 30. The former, however, from the aesthetically false, § 445, 446; the latter from the aesthetico-logically false in general, § 427, since among advocates of causes, such as Crassus and himself, he affirms that it is sometimes *necessary for one or the other to say what is false, and for both of them to defend now one thing, now another, about the same matter, since more than one thing cannot be true* (in itself, objectively, and most strictly). Hence he concedes that the whole business of disputing well in the forum *rests upon a lie*, nor does he take it ill that Catulus taunts him, that *he thought it no more glorious to begin from truth than from some dignity or other.*

§ 448. Let there be some things aesthetico-logically false, § 427, [p. 286] nay even lies in this signification, § 447; yet they will only then be aesthetically false, if they are perspicuously lies to the analogue of reason itself, § 445. As an example let the very dispute about truth serve. Let dogmatic philosophers on this side, academics and sceptics on the other, contend subtly among themselves *about the true and the false* (logically and metaphysically), *about* its *rule* and criterion (the first, abstract and scientific), about the *notion* of each, *by which they can be told apart* (distinctly, completely, scientifically). Let them digress into *what the difference is between right and wrong* (conceived more universally and logically). Let them return to the catholic and first *mark of truth*, and to logical *judgement*. Suppose each side defends its opinion as carefully as can be done. The analogue of reason will look up at both somewhat astonished, like a silent spectator, unless perhaps at some point it applauds the versatile wit of the sceptics. Yet imagine some of the academics at last proceeding so far that they altogether *deny that anything can so appear as not in the same way* (and degree of evidence) *also to be able so to appear false.* Then perhaps it will chime in with the dogmatists, already stirred more harshly than is just: *they act childishly.* Cic. quaest. ac. IV. 33. For the last point is also aesthetically false. § 445.

§ 449. Ask from Lucretius, § 448,

*What thing has created the knowledge of the true and the false,* / [p. 287] *And what thing has proved that the doubtful differs from the certain.* III. 477.

he will answer, what is aesthetically true, § 423.

*You will find that the knowledge of the true has been created first from the senses, / and that the senses cannot be refuted, / — — — — / since of their own accord they can overcome false things by true. / — — — — / Or will reason, arisen from a false sense, be able / to speak against them, reason which has wholly arisen from the senses? / If these are not true, all reason too becomes false.*

and he will show the contrary to be aesthetically false, § 445: To distrust sense

*is to let slip from one’s hands things manifest by one’s own hands, / and to violate the first faith, and to tear up all / the foundations on which life and safety rest; / for not only would all reason fall: life itself too / would collapse straightway, unless you dared to trust the senses, / and to shun precipitous places.* etc. 511.

§ 450. If it should seem strange to anyone that not every falsehood is a falsehood in aesthetic matters, § 448, he will perhaps cease to wonder if he reads that a far more restricted signification of the false has been sanctioned by the law which is reputed the most perfect, [p. 288] D. tit. de L. Corn. de falsis, l. 23: *It is asked what the false is; and it seems to be this: if someone imitates another’s handwriting, or suppresses or copies a document or accounts — not those who otherwise lie in a computation or in an account.* Behold the aesthetician, § 15, nevertheless stricter in judging falsehoods than Paulus the jurist himself! § 445.

§ 451. Meanwhile, under the tutelage of Roman Justice, I exempt from the number of falsehoods to be shunned by the aesthetician as such those which are indeed patently false to the purer intellect, but only then, when they are raised above the aesthetic horizon, § 16, 121 — unless certain of them are assailed by so great an evidence of reason as, like a greater light, not only obscures all sensate perception of them, but even wholly dispels and suppresses it, like some mist vanishing as the sun climbs higher. § 423, 429.

§ 452. Suppose that in two materials for thinking beautifully no falsity or absurdity shines through to the analogue of reason left alone and to itself, and yet that both are logically false. Now the place and the age in which, and the personal objects for whom, you chiefly think, must be looked into more closely. Let the first of the given materials be logically false in such a way that [p. 289] there, where you live, in your times, and among those for whose sake you chiefly think, either the demonstration of its falsity can be presumed to be wholly unknown, or at least that they, while you meditate beautifully, will not have it very present to their minds. This can be thought without the stain of aesthetic falsity, § 451.

§ 453. Let the second of the materials to be assumed, § 452, be one that among many people has seemed true to the sensate faculties of souls for many centuries; but now, and there where you live, and among those to whom above all you would wish to commend your work, let its falsity be already so patent and manifest through the powers of reason, even enlightened reason, that you cannot hope that they will forget their rational certainty while they examine your work, but rather must fear that, with all your effort, you will wring from them, in place of applause, an acclamation like this one:

*The greatest history is born of nothing.*

This second material labours under falsity, even aesthetic falsity, because reason prevents whatever truth may perhaps still be in it, now infinitely small, § 430, from being perceived sensately with any force and delight. § 423, 451.

§ 454. There are certain falsities so small that caution against them lies below the aesthetic horizon, § 120. Whether these creep at times [p. 290] in among the more elegant thoughts, or not, the aesthetician does not care, § 191. In the description of night in Virg. Aen. IV. 522:

*It was night, and weary bodies were plucking placid sleep / over the lands, and the woods and the savage seas / had come to rest, when the stars roll in mid-course, / when every field is silent, the cattle and the painted birds, / both those that haunt far and wide the liquid lakes and those that haunt the fields rough with thickets, / laid to rest in sleep beneath the silent night, / were soothing their cares and hearts forgetful of toils.*

the poet took care that you should not object to him the wild beasts that roam most of all by night, by his determination of the kinds of living creatures of which he wishes to be understood. But if you were to go on insisting, as if in earnest, that Maro speaks indefinitely and thus universally, and that nevertheless certain sick persons, or some single nightingale, should probably be excepted, he would, I believe, scarcely deign you a reply, were he alive.

§ 455. Let that which you set out to think elegantly be a dream taken objectively, M. § 91, in which there already lies hidden, inwardly and under the skin, an absolute contradiction that will sooner or later destroy it, M. § 15; or let a fabulous world arise in your mind out of several such empty appearances of things, [p. 291] or out of things true in themselves yet empty and labouring under a structure of incompossibles, M. § 91. But let the former, or here the latter too, be so disposed that 1) the vice of internal impossibility does not at all appear to the analogue of reason, 2) it is even hidden from the reason and intellect that you must presume in yourself and in your principal spectators, or at least does not confront them with that evidence which ought to be expected to scatter, like spiders’ webs, the whole fabric of a work meant to please beautifully: this dream, this fabulous world, is not annihilated by the aesthetic tribunal. § 452.

*Thus they pursue one road, and empty dreams of things / do Homer and keen Apelles both conceive.* Prud.

§ 456. Should such a dream, or fabulous world, § 455, either 1) present even to the analogue of reason itself things that conflict, that cancel one another, absurdities, or 2) have so often already had the black theta branded upon it by the reason and intellect of your spectators that you can be certain they will judge that you, now too, are thinking what you think against all reason — then such dreams, such fabulous worlds, are to be banished from the field of charming meditations on account of a falsity that is also aesthetic. § 453, 431.

*To painters and to poets / [p. 292] there has always been an equal power of daring anything whatever, / but not so that the tame should couple with the savage, not so that / serpents be paired with birds, lambs with tigers.*

§ 457. Livelier wits possess a certain NATURAL DYNAMOMETRY, a measure of forces referred to what they call common sense, the analogue of reason weighing by wit, in a sensate way, the proportion between cause and effect. Let, then, an effect be made by you greater or smaller than the living forces of the cause to which it is ascribed, and hence naturally impossible, yet in such a way that the said disproportion becomes evident neither to the mathematical knowledge of forces as it has just been described, nor yet to reason and intellect with the highest evidence; then a naturally impossible thing of this kind will sail past the aesthetic censor without the mark of disgrace, like Atlas of old, who had borne the heavens.

§ 458. If a god should intervene where no knot worthy of such a deliverer appears, if something that cannot happen by the nature of things is most evidently manifest, either to the analogue of reason, of which § 457, or to the intellect, even now, when it is to be thought — such a thought is to be banished from the beautiful. § 432, 453.

*Let the fable not demand that whatever it pleases be believed of it, / [p. 293] nor draw a living boy from the belly of a Lamia that has dined. / The centuries of elders drive off what is void of profit*

§ 459. It will be difficult for the natural dynamometry of which § 457 to form in its mind not only Homer’s but also Virgil’s shield, even though it remembers the divine craftsman. 1) How, on a shield defined by human stature, albeit heroic, so many pictures, vv. 626-729, found room, each so large that they could be made out by human eyes; and among other things 2) how, in the same picture, Cleopatra,

*the queen in the midst calls the ranks with her native sistrum,*

borne in her ships as an enemy to meet Augustus, and yet

*her, pale amid the slaughter with her coming death, / the fire-lord had made to be carried by the winds and the Iapygian gale?*

If you say that she, with the whole army of the East, was painted twice, not without some subtle and artful interval, 1) giving the signal for battle, 2) fleeing — the first difficulty is increased.

§ 460. I shall not provoke the prophesying horses of Achilles in Homer. It is better to go to Statius. In his Theb. II. 494, the enemies

*[p. 294] pour out in order from the fifty lofty gates of Thebes*

against the single and solitary Tydeus.

*Hail to thy spirit, worthy to be entrusted with such arms,*

and by so great a poet as Statius is, who never sang things greater than the truth! They lie in wait for Tydeus in ambush, and yet do not attack unless provoked by him alone. When at last they are present, summoned from all sides by a single foe, they allow him to clamber with hooked hands onto the rock of the Sphinx, safe and sound, without the cast of even a single weapon. With one stone he now kills the first four enemies, and among these

*the lightning-swift Dorylas, whose ardent valour / equalled kings,*

although with forty-nine nocturnal robbers he had attacked a single envoy. More than forty remain. Upon these Tydeus leaps down *headlong* from the rock of the Sphinx, and not before he has armed himself with the enemy’s shield and helmet do they

*mass themselves densely into one.*

Now he *draws his sword*, and *unconquerable, withstands* them all, as *Briareus* withstood all the gods,

*when, beset in vain by all Olympus, / he complains that so many of his hands are idle.* § 399.

§ 461. While we draw breath, many enemies have already received sheer deadly wounds, one in his very tongue; but Tydeus has

*[p. 295] often suffered rough / wounds, yet none has been received into the inner recesses of life.*

And now there remains the last single enemy. Lest the victorious Tydeus should enter hostile Thebes still in triumph, there is need of Pallas dissuading him through a vision. To the last man his life and messages to the Thebans are granted; he himself, so many times wounded, has the strength and the leisure to raise a trophy out of the arms of all forty-nine corpses, and, with the Echo of the long ridges resounding, to dedicate it to Minerva with prayers in twenty-five verses.

§ 462. If it can be perceived and judged, by the moral dynamometry of the analogue of reason of which § 457, that what is nevertheless ascribed to someone does not accord with freedom in general, or in particular with that of a certain person, of a certain character that you have once attributed to someone — that it is not possible through that freedom, or is greater or less than its living forces — this will give a new species of aesthetic falsity to be avoided, § 458, 433.

*Lest perchance the parts of old men / be entrusted to a youth, and manly parts to a boy.* / — — — — / *So that it makes no difference whether Davus is speaking, or the bold / Pythias, who has gained a talent by wiping Simo’s nose, / or Silenus, guardian and servant of his divine foster-child.*

§ 463. [p. 296] Let something be, through a certain freedom, truly ἀδύνατον [impossible], but let this moral impotence partly be evident only to the intellect and to reason, and to its more accurate dynamometry, and partly not even to these, at least not now, and among the spectators whom you especially wish to win over, let it not be presumed to be going to confront them so evidently as to scatter all the moral possibility that appears to the analogue of reason. Even if a morally impossible thing of this kind is mingled with your thoughts that are to be composed according to the rules of elegance, there is still no reason for you to fear being charged with falsehood by aesthetic verdicts, § 445.

§ 464. Although the aesthetic court does not extend its moral censure of falsity and of the illicit directly beyond the bounds of the sensate horizon, and hence does not immediately accuse of aesthetic falsity thoughts that are to be tested against the Lydian stone of the elegances, except those which offend against that licit, pious, honourable and decorous which is evident as such to the analogue of reason as well — yet suppose that someone has woven a morally evil action so cunningly that to the analogue of reason it at first seemed indifferent, or wholly good; but that it belonged to beautiful dignity in that same person to abstain from frauds of this kind, in one who, if not eminently good, nonetheless wished to be accounted a good man; suppose that the analogue of reason does not detect [p. 297] the frauds themselves except with the help of reason, but that, once the deceits are detected, it is most manifest, even in a sensate way, that they ill befit such a person as the thinker had taken upon himself to represent: then, to administer so subtle a poison of vices, if not directly on account of the aesthetic falsity itself of the thoughts, yet on account of the base defect of dignity, S. XV, in the cosmetic enticements of evils, the aesthetic court itself will condemn as baseness. § 435, 445.

§ 465. Yet there are also things to be shunned aesthetically which conflict with the moral possibility, more strictly so called, of things to be thought beautifully, § 435, which Claudian directly joins together, de IIII. cons. Hon. 278.

*Nor act in doubtful suspicion, nor be false to your friends.*

⁠1) Do not, throughout your whole meditation, charming as you may wish it to be, appear deliberately ambiguous between virtue and vice, so that the spectator’s analogue of reason, attending sufficiently according to its powers, cannot nonetheless determine to its own satisfaction, at least at the end, whose side you are on: that of those who strive to defend the morals which are ordinarily reckoned good, or that of those who strive to commend the morals ordinarily held to be bad. For thus, doubtfully suspect to the good, doubtfully pleasing to the bad, you will not win even the latter’s vote. To whom, then, are you beautiful?

§ 466. [p. 298] 2) Do not, as what they call a crass dissembler, so carry before you the mask of virtue that even to the analogue of reason the minds lurking beneath the fox show through. For example, it is base to feign a singular friendship, whether some *beam so falsely* that even at first glance

*they are found false by their falsities, / who, when they deceive themselves, stupidly think they are deceiving others, / factious in tongue, idle in deed, of unsound faith,*

or whether others conduct their business more cunningly, and only then, when they have *wrongfully induced* someone’s good analogue of reason, too confidently credulous, *to surrender its soul, as if all were safe,*

*then they draw back, and let all their words and deeds / be carried void by the winds and the airy clouds.* § 464.

§ 467. Here will perhaps be the most fitting place to affirm expressly, once for all, and to ask that anyone who wishes, having read these things, to approach the thinking of beautiful things, should regard it as said to him, as if it were written at the head of every page, that the falsehoods which we shall now call directly aesthetically licit are indirectly not licit, even aesthetically, § 464, much less by the graver rules of a good mind, as soon as you abuse them to the detriment of venerable virtue and the ruin [p. 299] of souls. Of this rule, and of the practice nevertheless contrary to it:

*If you have forgotten, yet the gods remember, Faith remembers, / who will make you repent hereafter of your deed.* Cat. 28.

§ 468. Let there be mingled with beautiful thoughts things which do not have the reason and the corollaries which, of such a kind and in such measure, a philosopher would rightly demand, but which nonetheless do not lack on either side such a connexion, and so much of it, as the analogue of reason of those for whose sake above all you write is wont to demand: the irrationality of unconnected things of this kind will not be charged with aesthetic falsity, § 445. But should there be wanting also that connexion, that coherence, of such a kind and in such measure as the analogue of reason desires in the things it approves, you will be condemned forthwith of aesthetic falsity, § 437.

*Nor let anything be sung between the middle acts / that does not conduce to the plot and fit it aptly.*

§ 469. The unity of his materials, both absolute and hypothetical, which is to be discerned by the reasoning of the metaphysicians alone, the aesthetician cares for little, being quite secure that, even if he disregards it, he will not be ill received by a competent judge as if he had thought aesthetically false things. But if [p. 300] he violates that unity, even of place alone or of time, which the analogue of reason bears at the same time before its eyes, § 439, he is not without shame relegated to that judge as guilty of aesthetic falsity, § 445.

*He who,* wishing *to vary a single subject prodigiously, / paints a dolphin in the woods, a boar in the waves.*

§ 470. All general falsities, in so far as they can be perceived by the senses and the analogue of reason, are also aesthetic, § 440, 445. But those which reason alone and the purer intellect have discovered to be such will not bear the mark of aesthetic falsity, unless either they are administered to those whose reason, thoroughly convinced of the contrary, suddenly scatters all the appearance of truth that remains, or they are disfigured by an accompanying defect of dignity and probity. § 464, 467. The graver and more dangerous an error is in notions, and in those that can be rashly applied to many things, the more seriously should the aesthetician look to himself in general matters too, lest he seem to have poured forth with himself, before his beautiful meditation, these silent prayers:

*Fair Laverna, / grant me to deceive, grant me to seem just and holy, / cast night over my sins and a cloud over my frauds.* Hor. ep. l. 16, 60.

§ 471. [p. 301] But what is this ambiguity? Now falsehoods are conceded, now again the aesthetician is dissuaded from them? State your opinion explicitly. As I have said hitherto, not without the necessary determinations, so I shall proceed. Things opposed in whatever way to what is most strictly true, or even those alone which seem opposed to them, are called FALSE IN THE WIDEST SENSE, and simply false in popular speech. Hence whatever in this universe neither was, nor is, nor will be, is all commonly declared false, and, as soon as one grows angry, most false. Let something to be thought rather beautifully be finite, possible in itself, possible naturally and morally, in the wider and in the stricter sense, by the most exact judgement both of reason and of the analogue of reason, and yet not possible in this universe: it will be false in the widest sense, and by the common formula of speech simply false.

§ 472. So great is the pursuit of truth, § 423, in one who would think beautifully, that he will not offend even against the most strict truth without a beautiful necessity, nor without the same meditate things false even in the widest sense, § 471. Hence elegant historians think it said to themselves especially: *Nothing false* (i.e. nothing that is not most strictly true). Tacitus, when he had narrated the history of Drusus’ death as it seemed to him most strictly true, adds a rumour [p. 302] about the same contrary to his own narrative, Ann. IV. 11. *My reason,* he goes on, *for relating and refuting the rumour was that by a clear example I might dispel false hearsay, and might ask those into whose hands our work shall come not to prefer things divulged and incredible, greedily received, to the true and to what has not been corrupted into the marvellous.*

§ 473. For this reason indeed it is not counted by good arbiters of the elegances among the Graces of thoughts, if these thoughts, to no purpose and from no observable necessity, contradict rigid and severe history, genealogy, geography, chronology, and the other disciplines which deal in the most strictly true. How often has Virgil himself been blamed for leading the perhaps chaste Dido with the Trojan leader into the same cave, chronology protesting, by those who seemed to themselves to observe no necessity for the falsehood? In Pythagoras’ declamation in Ovid these verses:

*Sparta was famous, great Mycenae flourished, / and the citadel of Cecrops too, and that of Amphion. / Sparta is cheap ground, lofty Mycenae has fallen. / What is Oedipus’ Thebes but a name? / What remains of Pandion’s Athens but a name?*

since without necessity they charmingly clash with chronology, have been held by many to be glosses.

§ 474. [p. 303] Whatever anachronisms and ἀνιστορησία [ignorance of history] are introduced without necessity into what is to be thought beautifully, all of it belongs to the falsities that are also aesthetic, as soon as the vice appears to the analogue of reason. § 473, 445. Here belong Virgil’s *triremes*, unless, subtly facetious, he purposely calls the ship *Chimaera*, L. V. 118.

*A city’s bulk, which the Dardan youth in triple tier / drive on; the oars rise up in threefold rank.*

Its *bronze-clad sterns*, at least, we cannot for that reason banish from this universe into exile, because beaks were invented after the heroic times. Let whoever will chastise Plautus, since not only his Sosia but Mercury himself swear frequently *by Hercules* in the Amphitryo — he who was only later to be Hercules’ *father, whom lawful wedlock declares.*

§ 475. But let there sometimes be, for one who would think beautifully, a certain necessity according to which, with the beauty of the whole preserved, he cannot tarry longer in this universe, but his wit must rather range abroad through other possible worlds; as soon as on such a journey he lingers about the regions of the fabulous world described in § 456, there is in his thoughts a heterocosmic falsity that is also aesthetic; but if he does not leap beyond the limits [p. 304] described in § 455, he is rightly not accused of aesthetic falsity in heterocosmic matters, M. § 445. Epaphus had perhaps said what is most strictly true to Phaethon, Ou. Met. l. 752:

*Madman, you believe your mother in everything, / and are puffed up with the image of a false father.*

§ 476. In the world of the Ovidian metamorphoses, however, through the prayers of Phaëthon, l. II. 35,

*O common light of the immense world,*

is most strictly true, and at the same time heterocosmically, § 441.

*Phoebus, father, if you grant me the use of this name,*

is heterocosmically true, § 441; though a dream from a fabulous world, nevertheless for the places and times in which Ovid lived, and for the persons for whom especially he wrote, it is not aesthetically false, § 455.

*Nor does a false Clymene hide a fault beneath a pretence,*

this would have been false in the said world of Ovid, the fable of Phaëthon being supposed, and aesthetically false too.

*Give pledges, father, by which I may be believed your true offspring, / and remove this error from our minds.*

You have a heterocosmic truth, which was not aesthetically false either, then, and for those when and for whom Ovid chiefly wrote.

§ 477. [p. 305] And now I think it manifest in some measure that one who would think beautifully is indeed always a friend of truth, S. XXVI, and yet is not the slave either of that most abstract truth about universals, § 440, or of that most strict truth, § 441.

*The fertile licence of the bards goes out into the immense, / nor binds its words by historical faith.* Ou. Am. III. 12.

where many examples are heaped up to confirm the maxim that bears upon this:

*It is not the custom to hear poets as witnesses.*

For, that I may at last seem to everyone to write perspicuously, § 471: the more charming wit,

*When it lies thus, so mingles false with true / that the middle may not clash with the beginning, nor the end with the middle,*

its Muse mindful of § 467, is, to the aesthetic censors,

*Splendidly mendacious, and a maiden noble / for all time.*

[p. 306]

#### Sectio XXVIIII. Verisimilitudo Aesthetica. — Section XXVIIII. Aesthetic Verisimilitude

§ 478. *How long, pray, will you abuse our patience? How long will that frenzy of yours mock us too? To what end will your unbridled audacity flaunt itself?* Do you, then, publicly appointed a teacher of logical and ethical truth, commend lies, as sometimes splendid, and the mixing of false with true, as a work in the highest degree noble? In roughly this fashion I seem to hear certain persons railing at me from the school which I have painted, § 279. But with calmed spirits, good men, let us return to our philosophical phlegm, which sometimes sits ill with you. The safety of Greece is not at stake. Meanwhile, however,

*long is the wrong, long / the windings; but I shall follow the chief heads of the matter.*

§ 479. Do you think it so easy, as Claudian’s panegyric to Honorius does, even for the analogue of reason,

*to draw forth the truth sunk in hiding-places?* v. 513.

That I too may deal with you by great authorities: do you not remember that truth, according to Democritus, [p. 307] lies at the bottom, whence it is drawn out with difficulty? You will surely listen to Cicero, who, while he says more than I would approve, will nonetheless illustrate the point now in question, at the end of book I de Or.: *Not only in this matter* (concerning the orator, hence the aesthetician too) *which looks to the assent of the crowd and to the pleasure of the ears* (and of the analogue of reason, as regards the matter), *which two are the slightest things for judging; but not even in the greatest matters have I found anything firmer to hold to, or by which to direct my judgement, than whatever seemed to me most like the truth, while the truth itself lay hidden in obscurity.*

§ 480. Not only against Cicero, § 479, and the sceptics and academics, whether ancient or recent, do I gladly admit that it is granted to the purer and more distinct reason and intellect sometimes to rise through the sciences above verisimilitude, to a knowledge and insight of the true which is not indeed full and most absolute in every respect, yet complete, and such as excludes all fear of the opposite; but I add this too, which few of the more recent dogmatists would perhaps concede, that there is already in the sensate and confused perceptions of the soul themselves some certainty, which is nonetheless complete, and a sufficiency of consciousness for distinguishing certain true things from all false ones.

§ 481. [p. 308] Let a certain truth, even an aesthetic one, § 423, nevertheless be completely cognised by the analogue of reason, as such, § 480, that is, completely, with certainty and persuasion, M. § 531. But which will this be? 1) Certain first and most universal principles of human cognition, which nature has so implanted already in almost all wits, as natural metaphysicians, M. § 3, that, at times adorned with the title of common sense, they are evident enough and more than enough, M. § 531.
