But on account of this very evidence of theirs, by which they seem of themselves to be plain to anyone, it is only rarely, or never, permissible to think them expressly and manifestly in fuller form, not without weighty considerations, in all the light of their truth, with many arguments and solicitations to assent. For the most part they are, as they say, supposed, and, omitted by a charming enthymeme, are left to the spectator among the things he supplies to himself.

§ 482. 2) Those very few intuitive perceptions truly such, which we sense immediately, unviolated by any vices of subreption. Experience in the strict sense alone I count here, not that in the wide sense, the complex of all cognition in which there is something of sensations, and which even those prove to have little certainty who, while truly contradicting one another, nonetheless both [p. 309] confidently appeal to it. Experience in the strict sense is to be set apart from the many other perceptions of the remaining lower faculties of the soul besides sensations, if one has a mind to think out something rather beautiful, § 140. These very things, which I have conceded can be completely certain even to the analogue of reason, are not so always and for everyone; but their credit is often not attended to at all, and sometimes, by sophisms and quibbles, empty though they be, it is nonetheless shaken and almost overthrown, unless it is confirmed by the notions of reason and intellect.

§ 483. Now I should think it established by the plainest reckoning that very many of the things to be apperceived in the course of charming thought are not completely certain, nor is their truth beheld in complete light, § 481, 482. Yet in none of them can anything of sensate falsity be detected without disgrace, S. XXVIII. But such things, of which we are indeed not completely certain, and yet in which we apperceive no falsity, are VERISIMILAR. Aesthetic truth, S. XXVII, named from the predominant feature, is therefore VERISIMILITUDE, that degree of truth which, even if it has not been raised to complete certainty, nevertheless contains nothing of observable falsity.

§ 484. [p. 310] *Of which* spectators *or hearers have within the mind*, when they see or hear, *certain anticipations: what mostly happens, what is wont to happen, what is placed in opinion, what has in itself a certain likeness to these, whether it be false* (logically, and in the broadest sense) *or true* (logically and in the strictest sense), *what is not readily abhorrent to our senses:* this is that εἰκός [the likely] and verisimilar which, with Aristotle and Cicero assenting, the aesthetician should pursue, § 483. For in things of this kind the analogue of reason is not wont to observe anything of falsity, although it is not altogether convinced of their truth. Hence for Cicero invention is described as *the thinking out of things true or verisimilar, which render the cause probable* (aesthetically).

§ 485. Aesthetically true, that is, verisimilar, § 484, will therefore be 1) things completely certain both sensately and intellectually, which come first, § 480–482; 2) things completely certain sensately only, § 482, in which the intellect has not yet exercised its work; 3) things logically and aesthetically probable, which come later. Since PROBABLE things are those for which there is more reason to grant assent than to withhold it, and IMPROBABLE things those for which there is more reason to withhold assent than to grant it: [p. 311] when the reasons for doubting and for deciding, for assent and against it, are distinctly cognised, LOGICAL PROBABILITY arises; if sensately, AESTHETIC. The denomination is taken from the predominant feature whenever there is a certain aesthetico-logical probability. The same reasoning holds for improbability. The fields of verisimilitudes therefore extend more widely than the territory of probability, even if everything probable is at the same time verisimilar, § 483.

§ 486. Aesthetically verisimilar things will be 4) things perhaps logically doubtful, indeed improbable, provided they are aesthetically probable, § 485; 5) things even aesthetically doubtful and improbable, not only to others than those for whom above all you think charmingly, but perhaps even to these very persons at another time, when they have imbibed more fully the reasons against assent, provided that now, and within the series of your meditation, they either have, concerning the things you set before them, an aesthetic probability, or at least do not have present enough to the mind the reasons preponderating on the opposite side, so as to obscure all that of elegant verisimilitude which remains to your thoughts, § 484.

§ 487. Let us compare the advice given by Cicero to accusers, or. pro Rosc. Am. 57: *You ought above all to make your attack upon those who deserve it* [p. 312] (whose crime is completely certain, logically and most strictly true, or even logically and aesthetically probable). *This is most welcome to the people. Then, if you will, even when it is verisimilar that someone has committed a crime, bark away in suspicion* (even if the crime is not completely certain or logically probable, nor aesthetically so to everyone, yet not aesthetically improbable to those whom you especially wish to please). *That too may be allowed. But if you proceed in such a way that you accuse someone of having killed his father, and cannot say either why or how, and merely bark without suspicion* (without any aesthetic verisimilitude whatsoever), *no one indeed will break your legs; but if I know these men well, that letter to which you are so hostile that you hate even all the Kalends they will fasten so violently to your head that afterwards you will be able to accuse no one else but your own fortunes.*

§ 488. Another example will be given by Cicero the philosopher, de nat. Deorum l. I. 65: *These*, he says, *oracles of the natural philosophers (that there is no void) I now pour forth, whether true or false I do not know.* (Completely certain logically, yet not to the analogue of reason, § 482.) 66. *But still more like the truth than yours. For those scandalous doctrines of Democritus, or even before him of Leucippus, that there are certain corpuscles, some smooth, others rough, others round, some angular, some curved and as it were hooked,* [p. 313] *and that from these the heaven and the earth were made, with no nature compelling, but by some fortuitous concourse.* (These scandals will illustrate § 486.)

§ 489. At the same time I think it evident, by the reasoning of opposites, that the aesthetically false, S. XXVIII, is to be named from the predominant feature the false-seeming, § 483, of whose falsity we are indeed not completely certain, yet from which nothing of truth at all shines forth notably. Let something be completely certain to the intellect, let something else be probable to it: if both are nevertheless aesthetically doubtful, or improbable, or suggest no arguments at all for their truth to the analogue of reason of those whom you chiefly wish to please, then although their falsity is not clear to it either, still, being destitute of aesthetic verisimilitude, destitute of anticipations within the mind of your personal objects, since they seem unusual at least to these, § 484, one must abstain from them, as false-seeming, § 485, 486.

§ 490. *Nocturnal spirits and Thessalian portents are laughed at*

by some. Whether they know why, or do not know, the aesthetician will not care. It will suffice him that to spectators of this kind whatever there is of spectres and of the arts they wrongly call magical seems so ridiculous to the analogue of reason that in [p. 314] visions of this kind nothing of elegant verisimilitude remains for them; hence for this reason alone he will remove from the eyes all such false-seeming things, for those for whose sake he chiefly writes. If Virgil’s readers had been such, holding judicial astrology, as many now do, in contempt, he assuredly would not have written

*Who would dare to call the sun false? / He too often warns that blind tumults are at hand, / and that fraud and hidden wars are swelling.*

Georg. l. I. 463, fearing lest one of them should retort: we do not call the sun false, but you false, a petty truth-teller from its colour, § 489.

§ 491. We have already at some point hinted at a certain aesthetic necessity of exhibiting not only those things which are true with complete certainty and most strictly. Now is the place to recall some of these necessities. 1) Let the aesthetician, who is not obliged to be a pansophist, much less omniscient, have to think about things whose metaphysical truth he does not completely know, § 423. 2) Let there come before him things to be thought whose more strictly logical truth he cannot rigidly demonstrate, § 424. 3) Let there occur things of which he is not completely certain even aesthetically, § 427. 4) Let him fall upon such things to be thought whose exact truth does not go beyond the horizon [p. 315] of distinctness, so that the better it is known to him, the more certain he is that it is not suited to the grasp of those for whom above all he thinks, § 429: in all these cases refuge will have to be taken in some verisimilitude, § 479.

§ 492. In the first case were almost all the Romans, if the question were directed to them:

*It is a small thing that I labour to know. / What do you think of Jove?*

In the second I should think Iopas was, as he sang, Aen. I. 748:

*Why the winter suns hasten so to dip themselves in Ocean, / or what delay stands in the way of the slow nights.*

In the third was that woman of Virgil’s, who, ecl. VIII. 106,

*Look,* she says, *the ash itself, while I delay to carry it, has seized the altar with trembling flames / of its own accord; may it be a good sign. / Something it is, surely, and Hylax is barking on the threshold. / Do we believe? or do those who love make up dreams for themselves?*

In the fourth case will be the more accurate philosopher, who rather often treats aesthetically those moral themes which I adduced from Horace, § 126.

§ 493. [p. 316] The aesthetic necessity of withdrawing from completely certain and most strict truth will arise for one who is to think beautifully, 5) when it is not worth so much to examine whether what must be set down is accurately true or only verisimilar, § 430. 454. 6) When there comes into his mind a thing of which it is little clear whether it is possible in itself or not, § 431, yet this much is clear, that to one meditating the thing beautifully nothing of absurdity is brought before the analogue of reason, § 431, 455. 7) When in thinking you fall upon an event about which your dynamometry does not decide exactly whether the full effect is accurately equal to the living forces of the causes, and yet to natural dynamometry, with the forces sensately cognised, it does not seem in any respect either disgracefully less or greater, § 432. 457.

§ 494. In the fifth case Virgil, Aen. I. 706, sings:

*Fifty maidservants within, whose charge it is in long order / to lay out the provisions and to make the hearth-gods glow with flames; / a hundred others, and as many attendants matched in age, / to load the tables with dishes and set out the cups.*

[p. 317] content with round numbers, allowing for an error of reckoning. In the sixth case was Aeneas, when:

*O! how,* he says, *shall I name you, maiden? for your countenance is not / mortal, nor does your voice sound human. O! a goddess surely, / either Phoebus’ sister, or one of the blood of the nymphs!*

In the seventh case read Virgil, when Nisus and Euryalus are described by him, l. IX. 176–450, so that the verisimilitude of this, set beside the falsity of Statius, § 460, 461, may shine forth the more.

§ 495. The aesthetic necessity of descending rather often from completely certain and most strict truth to verisimilitude will arise, 8) when one who, in the moral manner of thinking in the broader sense, § 226, is about to observe a new species of ἦθος [character], § 193, § 433, falls upon an event to be painted which seems to agree exceedingly with the moral characters of persons once established, with the customs of their ancestors, and with usages, age, country, etc., § 434, and in no respect ill-treats the moral dynamometry of the analogue of reason, § 462, yet whose most strict truth is not completely certain. In this case was Virgil, arming Aeneas for his country Troy, l. II. § 309.

§ 496. [p. 318] The aesthetic necessity of embracing verisimilitude in place of exactly cognised truth will arise 9) when, as to what a good man, whether simply honourable, or noble, or altogether a hero, truly did in certain circumstances surrounding him, on occasions for acting rightly, history is silent, experience is silent, but ἦθος [character] and the moral manner of thinking in the stricter sense teach what he ought to have done; for then refuge must cautiously be taken, § 463–467, in this moral truth, though only verisimilar, § 435. In this case was Virgil, about to describe what Aeneas said or did at the funeral of Pallas, l. XI. 25–100, and almost all the ancient historians, when they insert into their works the speeches that were delivered.

§ 497. 10.) When the most strictly true reasons, with complete certainty, for thinking beautifully, and true consequences of this kind, are either unknown, or placed above the aesthetic horizon, or for other causes, e.g. dignity and decorum, cannot be adduced, and yet neither a disgraceful gap nor anything at all done in vain is to be set before the senses and the analogue of reason, § 436. 468. In this case was Virgil, seeing that he had to supply his hero with many and great reasons for exercising fortitude, many and great enemies, L. VII. 641.

[p. 319] *Open Helicon now, goddesses, and stir your songs: / what kings were roused to war, what battle-lines, following each, / filled the plains, with what men the Italian land, / the nourishing land, even then flourished, with what arms it blazed; / for you remember, divine ones, and you can recount, / to us scarcely a thin breath of fame glides down.*

§ 498. 11) When certain things, perhaps on account of the most strict truth itself, or at least on account of their fame, and because there is already some anticipation of them in the minds of the spectators, are to be thought beautifully, which nevertheless might seem to offend more gravely against aesthetic unity, § 439. 469. For then there is need of much verisimilitude, lest you be judged to have disgracefully joined things that cannot be associated. Coming to the fulfilment of the paradoxical and unexpected oracle,

*the first way of safety, / which you least expect, shall be opened from a Greek city,* VI. 96,

which had to be described, Virgil, lest Greek and Trojan arms badly forced together under one leader be judged so, even by the analogue of reason, takes how great pains to soften [p. 320] the unlooked-for event, l. VIII. A god intervenes, since a knot worthy of such a deliverer is at hand, father Tiberinus, who gives the counsel of approaching the Arcadians,

*who wage war continually with the Latin people,* 55.

Aeneas himself sets out, he does not send envoys, nor does he merely call himself and his men *Trojan-born*, but also at once

*weapons hostile to the Latins,* 117, etc.

§ 499. 12) When concerning general matters, and through the abstract region of notions, logical truth either lies so hidden at the bottom that it cannot be dug out from there even through the intellect and reason of one who is to think beautifully, or will seem, at least to your principal personal objects, to be fetched from too deep and too far, if not altogether false, since it does not contain those things which are wont to happen and of which they have anticipations within the mind; while another opinion about the same general matters, which cannot be true together with the former judgement, can nevertheless be commended by innocent reasons, and without evil deceit, without nefarious fraud, § 470, being more popular and breathing something of a greater truth, § 440. E.g. Although it is logically true that some public safety is truly procured not without war and through arms: nevertheless

*There is no safety in war.*

[p. 321] Although there are those who shudder at everything except idle leisure, and judge that everything they find to be above them is no concern of theirs, in the sincere opinion of their mind, nevertheless

*Nothing is too arduous for mortals, / we seek heaven itself in our folly.*

§ 500. 13) The very pursuit of truth, which can be very great, sometimes compels the aesthetician to lie, i.e. to think things false in the broadest sense, or things of which he himself does not know whether they are altogether true logically and most strictly. Suppose he can think an exactly cognised truth, very abstract, general and universal, = about 20°. Suppose there comes into his mind something more determinate, less general, less abstract, which, the rest of the beauty being preserved, he could substitute for the former. Let there be in this 40 various things. Of ten of them let him either doubt whether they are true in every way, or see altogether that they are not to be defended before the examination of purer reason, although they do not yield aesthetic falsity, S. XXVIII. Let the remaining 30 agree properly with the catholic principles of truth, especially in so far as they lie open to the analogue of reason, and at the same time give the theme greater richness, dignity, light, etc. than the former, the logically true and completely certain, = 10°. In this case, choosing the former, you will give truth = 20°. Preferring the latter, you exhibit truth [p. 322] at least = 40° − 10° = 30°. Hence the very pursuit of truth can impel one to set the latter falsehood before the former truth, § 441.

§ 501. It is a general truth: a good man sometimes yields even to the unjust suspicions of those whom he loves. But does not that truth appear greater to the analogue of reason in those who read Liv. II. 6, that *against Valerius the consul there arose not only envy, but even suspicion, with an atrocious charge, that he was aiming at kingship, because he was building on the top of the Velia; that there, on a high and fortified spot, there would be an impregnable citadel?* And that, having summoned *the people to an assembly*, Valerius then said among other things: *The house of P. Valerius will not stand in the way of your liberty, Quirites. The Velia will be safe for you. I shall not only bring my house down to the plain, but shall set it beneath the hill, so that you may dwell above me, a suspected citizen. Let those build on the Velia to whom liberty is better entrusted than to Valerius.* That *brought down* afterwards *straightway* were *all the materials below the Velia, and the house built at the lowest part of the slope.* That hence opinions were so *turned to the contrary* that from then on he was held *a friend of the people, whence the surname Publicola.* If this Livian truth is rightly judged greater in some respect than that general one, let us remember that Livy invented the speech, § 496.

§ 502. [p. 323] 14) Let something be most strictly true at some time, which happens not so rarely, so constituted that not only in the opinion of the crowd, but even to good critics of facts, it seems singularly like falsehood, nor yet does one who is to think charmingly have the strength or the room to vindicate its verisimilitude to many; or let it be destitute, so far as it is cognised, of the things required for the beauty of thoughts, richness, dignity, light, etc., § 22, if it stands alone and is adorned with nothing false in the broadest sense. You have gathered into one the many cases in which there seems to be need of heterocosmic truths, which are either substituted for the most strictly true or mingled with it, § 444, 475.

§ 503. Since therefore one who is to think beautifully is pressed by so many necessities of passing from truth, as they call it, to some mere verisimilitude, § 491–502: not rarely *he will become a poet.*

*The poet, when he has taken his tablets to himself, / seeks what is nowhere in the world, and yet finds it, / he makes that like the truth which is a lie.* Plaut. Pseud. l. 4. v. 8.

But the aesthetician, distinguishing metaphysical and aesthetico-logical truth, as the object and the perception of it, as the thing [p. 324] and the concept of the thing, notes that this aesthetico-logical truth is either completely clear or less so. The former they call truth, the latter verisimilitude. The philosopher calls the former complete certainty; the other, truth in uncertain, probable, doubtful, improbable things. Hence the aesthetician advises that the beautifully true be sought not only among things completely certain, but that the same be searched out at the same time through the uncertain, through probable, doubtful, improbable things, so long as he does not withdraw himself from the lover’s eyes, ending in a disgraceful likeness of the false, or in the very disgrace of the false, S. XXVIII.

§ 504. One who is to think beautifully follows the counsel, § 503, and, amid the surrounding din of rumours of falsehood, pursuing the one truth, mostly through uncertainties,

*and unteaches the people / to use false*

*words* of falsehood and of lies, or, easy in words, congratulates himself in his bosom on the truth of things and of life, when sometimes, through the midst of what they call the forest of Falsity and Lies, there offers herself, shining back in full light,

*Modesty, and Justice’s sister, / uncorrupted Faith, and naked Truth.*

[p. 325]

#### Sectio XXX. Fictiones. — Section XXX. Fictions

§ 505. Those things which we have not sensed in as many ideas as we think anew, and which nevertheless are to be sensately cognised, must be feigned, M. § 589. Hence FICTIONS IN THE BROADER SENSE, M. § 590, perceptions formed by combining and abstracting phantasms, make up by far the greatest part of the things to be thought beautifully.

*Things feigned for the sake of pleasure should be very near the true,* § 504.

§ 506. Not only things very near the true, but even very many things themselves most strictly true, cannot be thought beautifully and sensately except by fictions in the broader sense. How many are those objects of one who is to think beautifully of which he could say: *which I myself saw,*

*And of which I was a great part?*

Nor are these without fictions, save only experiences strictly so called, if they are narrated altogether purely and nakedly. The remaining things of this universe, whether past, if I narrate them according to historical faith, yet without having strictly experienced them, or present [p. 326] in time, if I sensately perceive them absent in place, or future, even if at last they come to pass in this universe from my preconception, as a tower from the anticipation of the architect, are nevertheless FICTIONS (in the broader sense, § 505) which it is permissible to call MOST STRICTLY TRUE.

§ 507. Fictions, therefore, like truths, falsities and verisimilitudes, will be either general or singular, and the latter will either enter into the cognition of this universe or be heterocosmic, § 445, 441. The general ones have their rules, S. XXVIIII. The singular ones, a part of the charming cognition of this universe, will either be most strictly true, § 506, presenting matters of fact, or present events, or things truly future, which however the thinker himself has not strictly experienced, or less so. Those which are perceived in the cognition of this universe, yet not with accurate intuition, hence fictions in the broader sense, § 505, are HISTORICAL FICTIONS IN THE BROAD SENSE.

§ 508. The Trojan origins of the Romans are narrated by Virgil and by Livy, l. I, neither having experienced them, hence each through a historical fiction in the broad sense, § 507. The former narrates that Aeneas escaped by flight unbeknown to the Greeks and the enemy; the latter, that against *Aeneas*, both on account of *the ancient right of hospitality* and [p. 327] because *he had always been an advocate of peace and of the restoration of Helen*, *the Achaeans* exercised no *right of war*. The former first leads Aeneas into Thrace, the latter into Macedonia. The events that next followed the arrival of Aeneas in Italy the two conceive in their minds differently. For the former, Ascanius and Iulus are the same, the son of Creusa. For the latter, *Ascanius* is *born of Lavinia*, though he adds: *I shall not deny some doubt (for who could affirm a matter so ancient as certain?) whether this was Ascanius, or an elder one than he, born of his mother Creusa while Ilium was still unharmed, and thereafter the companion of his father’s flight, the same whom the Julian clan, calling him Iulus, names as the author of their name. Wherever and of whatever mother born, it is agreed at any rate that he was the son of Aeneas.* Wherever and of whatever father the most strictly true fiction be born, § 506, these, besides others, are historical fictions in the broad sense of both authors, § 507. Ascanius in the Aeneid is a verisimilar person, S. XXVIIII.

§ 509. Historical fictions in the broad sense, embracing all the cognition of this universe in one who thinks beautifully which he has not himself experienced, either present, at least to one who has not experienced them, most strictly true events of this universe, being most strictly true fictions, § 506, or such things as, all the events sensately known to us in this universe and the moments surrounding the event being supposed, nevertheless could have happened or could happen; and these will be FICTIONS, even if they are thought by [p. 328] poets, HISTORICAL IN THE STRICTER SENSE.

§ 510. Virgil’s Turnus makes war on Aeneas only; Livy’s *Turnus makes war on Aeneas and Latinus together*, and this with the outcome that *the Aborigines and the Trojans, victorious, lose their leader Latinus*. Virgil’s Mezentius is killed by Aeneas. Livy’s Mezentius is indeed conquered by Aeneas, but, this *last of mortal works* accomplished, Aeneas is buried, Mezentius surviving. To one who reads only one of these authors, so far is it from being the case that his narratives could be suspected of being fictions, even historical in the stricter sense, that he would rather hold them among the most strictly true fictions, § 506. Yet to one who compares the two it is not permissible, on the authority of Livy, to count the Virgilian fables among the historical fictions in the stricter sense, § 509.

#### Sectio XXXI. Fictiones Poeticae. — Section XXXI. Poetic Fictions

§ 511. If such things are feigned as, on account of a circumstance known both to the thinker and to those who are to think under his guidance, as must be presumed, and on account of a certain hypothesis and event of this world, have no place in this same universe, so that, as [p. 329] possibilities of it, on the supposition however of some other hypothesis which is not a possibility of this universe, they could have happened or could happen beautifully, or disgracefully, through middle cognition: such heterocosmic FICTIONS, § 441, since their inventor as it were creates a new world by feigning, even if they are most of all put forward by a historian, are called POETIC.

§ 512. The wrath of Juno, Aen. I. 19–85, is a poetic fiction; the storm, – 127, historical. Neptune, the pacifier of the seas, poetic, – 160. The landing of the Trojans in Africa historical, – 227, unless the stags raise a doubt. The conversation of Jupiter and Venus and the embassy of Mercury are a poetic fiction, v. 308.

*But pious Aeneas, turning over many things through the night, / as soon as the kindly light was given, resolved to go out and to explore / the new places, what shores he had reached by the wind, / who hold them (for he sees them uncultivated), whether men or beasts, / and to report his findings to his companions* – 317,

is a historical fiction. Here begins the poetic fiction of Dido, extended beyond l. I–V, § 509, 511.

§ 513. [p. 330] The complex and rather badly coherent system of fictions of every kind, already adopted and supposed by many of the more elegant wits, let us call synecdochically the WORLD OF THE POETS. For here most of the cosmogonies of the philosophers, not even the Cartesian excepted, find a place no less than the theogonies of the ancient theologians, and whatever there is of mythology, not only of that better-known one, Greek and Roman, but also of what is πατροπαράδοτα [handed down from the fathers] among other nations from the Indians to the Edda. Here belong the acts of the saints in every part of them that is feigned either historically or poetically, their very signs, the lineaments and postures with which they are wont to be painted and sculpted. A certain new land of this world is the cabbalistic world with its inhabitants of the four elements, who had once dwelt very much in obscurity, etc.

§ 514. In the world of the poets there is a certain very widely extended region of chimeras, fictions destitute of all the metaphysical truth that might be in their objects, which let us call the UTOPIA of the world of the poets. A great part of the fabulous world, after it has entered the world of the poets, will not be touched beyond its limits, § 456, by one who is to think beautifully. Against this rule offend 1) those who now think it equally [p. 331] permissible to themselves to trifle much out of the mythology of the ancients as that was once permissible to the Greeks and Romans, whose principal spectators had not, through the analogue of reason, penetrated so far into its absurdities as now all do who scarcely bathe for a copper.

§ 515. 2) Those who altogether mingle trifles of this kind into the best and greatest system of the truest religion, or chant them to those hearers to whom serpents would seem to be paired not with birds, but with angels, or at any rate with doves, if they examine, even by their own sensate judgement and taste alone, not public indeed, yet properly mature, so wretchedly incoherent a cementing-together of light and darkness of this kind, § 415. Virgil himself, calling to his aid whatever rustic divinities there are anywhere, Georg. I,

*All ye gods and goddesses, whose care it is to guard the fields, / And who nourish the new crops by no seed of man,* v. 21.

carefully takes care not to mingle with his own any of the gods whom only *holy nations* venerate,

*among whom these divinities grow in gardens.* Iuu. XV.

[p. 332] § 516. A certain poetic fiction, § 511, of the poetic world, § 513, not shamefully touching upon utopia, § 514, 515, in conformity with a certain part of that same world, even positively, i.e. it will so conceive its own orb, which it creates by heterocosmic feigning, that this coheres well, so far as it ought aesthetically to presume that its principal hearers will scrutinise the matter, with the properly known part of the poets’ world, could be formed in the mind from the anticipations of that part, so presents its events, as is customary, in a given region of the poetic world, has aesthetic unity with the same, and receives from the same in turn its own verisimilitude. Let us call a POETIC FIGMENT of this kind ANALOGICAL.

§ 517. The precept for these fictions is Horace’s, according to S. XXVIIII.

*Writer, if perchance you put honoured Achilles back upon the stage, / Let him be tireless, wrathful, inexorable, keen, / Let him deny that laws were born for him, let him claim everything by arms, / Let Medea be fierce and unconquered, Ino tearful, / Ixion treacherous, Io a wanderer, Orestes mournful.* A. P. 120.

I am not lightly moved here to read with Bentley *Homereum* for *honoratum*, [p. 333] though I see a reason why Achilles may have been called honoured, to whom fell the highest honour, to be envied even by Alexander the Great, of being consecrated to eternity by the best of poets. In these poetic fictions almost alone did Virgil rest content.

§ 518. A POETIC FICTION so creating a new orb that it makes it neither very similar to any region of the poetic world, nor conducive to it and more aptly cohering with it, is WHOLLY UNKNOWN. Since the verisimilitude of the poetic world, now to be presupposed in the spectators, does not come to its aid, § 512, since there are lacking in the same a good many anticipations, which an analogical fiction would already have found, § 516, since by the poetic world itself it is unaccustomed, and could not have happened in this universe, § 511, and so will seem to many altogether like the false, § 489, it must have a remarkable internal verisimilitude, a singular order of things more charmingly conjoined, a harmony and agreement of successive things striking the eyes with much light, a more notable unity, and in general an altogether singular charm, if it is nonetheless to stand upon a straight ankle and win enough votes, S. XXVII. § 22.

[p. 334] § 519. Hence Horace thinks it more right to draw off some small part from a poem of such fame as the Iliad is, A. P. 130,

*Than if you were the first to bring forth things unknown and unsaid.*

Hence I have taken the name of the fictions of which § 518. If anyone nevertheless dares such things, he warns him:

*If you commit to the stage something untried, and dare / To form a new character, let it be kept to the end / Such as it went forth from the beginning, and be consistent with itself. / It is hard to say common things in one’s own way;*

which I think I interpret rightly thus: to present general truths, which no one thinks himself ignorant of, for the first time in some singular example, beautiful enough, which no one before us has yet in part painted more charmingly, is a thing still wholly unknown; but that is of greater difficulty than it seems to many.

§ 520. A poetic fiction which indeed bears before it some analogy, I know not what, with the poetic world, yet not much later runs heavily against that same region of that world which it had made its own, affirming things that contradict it, setting before the spectators things that fight against its ἤθος [character], things that would happen in it by pure chance, things that would [p. 335] happen in vain, things that separate what is inseparable by the same — such a fiction will be an ANOMALOUS FIGMENT.

§ 521. Since an anomalous figment can neither happen in this universe, nor in that poetic one, nor can be internally, singularly verisimilar, because it has already as it were received into its bosom things which it nonetheless contradicts, from where in the world is it to receive the truth, or any verisimilitude at all, so necessary to elegance? S. XXVII, XXVIIII. See with how much effort Livy, l. 2, takes care not to break the fame of the poetic fiction about the death of Aeneas, since he either would not or could not bring forth a most strictly true fiction: *He lies buried,* he says, *by whatever name it is right and lawful for him to be called, above the river Numicius: they call him Jupiter Indiges.*

§ 522. Lest, however, things be drawn into anomalous figments of this kind, 1) if there is disagreement between the historians, who are to be supposed to feign things strictly true, and the poets, who are to be supposed to feign heterocosmic things, § 519. Hence this does not belong here: *Whether Aeneas loved Dido*, a question on which the philologist Atteius is said to have published a special book, because Justin, after Virgil and contrary to him, narrates that she was the wife of one husband and most chaste, l. XVIII, and chronological reasons favour Justin. Ausonius here [p. 336] and Silius rather had the freedom to follow whichever world they wished, this one or the poetic. Hence Ausonius followed both in different epigrams, surely with no grave fault. The poetic one in the epigram of which § 408. This one in ep. 111.

*Believe the historians about me, readers, rather / Than those who sing the thefts and couplings of the gods, / Lying bards, who profane the truth with their song, / And liken the gods to human vices.*

§ 523. Silius, however, followed Virgil’s poetic world, yet not without poetic liberty, L. VIII. In him things are drawn apart which in Virgil follow one another more closely with greater unity. At last Dido, about to die,

*Gods of the long night, whose divinity imminent death now makes greater for us, / I pray, she says, be present, / And calmly admit to the shades one conquered by passion. / Aeneas’ wife, Venus’ daughter-in-law, having avenged my husband, / I have seen the citadels of our Carthage built, / Now the shade of a great body will descend to you. / [p. 337] Me too, perhaps, a man once known to me by sweet love / Awaits, desiring to equal his former cares,*

Compare § 405, 406, and this Dido of Silius, unless I am mistaken, as much as you rank her below Virgil’s Dido, so much will you still rank her above Ovid’s Dido: just as the Ausonian Dido, of whom § 520, is greater than the Dido of Ausonius, of whom § 405.

§ 524. 2) If our disagreement with the preceding poetic world is so slight that the falsity seen to arise from it is so slight that it may rightly be judged referable perhaps to § 454. Here a fair censor will refer Aeschylus’ departure from Homer’s poetic world, since in the latter Patroclus is older than Achilles, yet Aeschylus feigns Patroclus the younger, and was on that account ill treated by Plato, the great enemy of poets. Hence Martial was not deterred by the authority of so great a philosopher from following Aeschylus rather than Homer, XI. ep. 44, nor did Statius restrain himself from choosing altogether, as it were, a new middle path of his own, in whose Achilleid Patroclus is to Achilles

*equal in pursuits and in measure of years.*

§ 525. Someone might object: these fictions you will surely grant to be lies. Why then do you define, illustrate, [p. 338] distinguish them, and seem at least to commend the analogical ones? § 516. I myself indeed despair of *unteaching the people the use of false words*, § 504; hence from its manner of speaking I have stated my opinion openly, § 477. Perhaps, however, St Augustine is happier than I, when he, l. II. quaest. eu. 51, says: *Not everything that we feign is a lie, but when we feign that which signifies nothing* (which is of no truth, but is the offspring and shameful sign not only of aesthetic, but even of moral falsity), *then it is a lie. But when our fiction is referred to some signification* (as in some comparative, whose perception is sufficiently worthy, if we cannot exhibit a completely certain truth, at least an incompletely certain one, if not some greatest truth, yet the one nearest to it, with a candid breast and pure hands), *it is not a lie, but some figure of truth*, § 26. *Otherwise all things which have been said figuratively by wise and holy men, or even by the Lord himself, will be reckoned lies, because according to the customary understanding* (of truth most strictly so called) *the truth does not subsist in such sayings.* — — — — *A fiction that is referred to some truth* (and this can also be poetic) *is a figure; one that is not referred is a lie.*

[p. 339]

#### Sectio XXXII. Fabulae. — Section XXXII. Fables

§ 526. A general judgement, or DOGMA, is either theoretical or practical. A practical dogma aesthetically thought is a MAXIM. A lower concept DECLARING its higher concept, under which it is contained, or rendering it clearer, is an EXAMPLE. An example of a maxim strictly feigned, § 506, is a FABLE. For although any little narrative, even a most strictly true one, since it generally grows as it goes and acquires spurious strength from feigned additions, is sometimes called a fable in the broader sense, as someone who has died before his day through luxury

*Goes the round of every dinner as a new fable, and not a sad one,* Iuu. Sat. I.

yet we shall have to use the word more strictly and simply, so that it is opposed to most strictly true narratives.

§ 527. A fable will be either a HISTORICAL FICTION MORE STRICTLY, § 526, 509, which, interspersed with no poetic fictions, § 511, is called SIMPLE, [p. 340] or a poetic fiction, § 511, or one mixed from historical and poetic fictions. Fictions of the second and third kind are called FABULOUS. Let us be permitted, by analogy with fictions, to call FABLES of the first kind HISTORICAL, § 526, and fables of the second and third kind POETIC.

§ 528. Historical fables, § 527, are those which, though sometimes interposed in the midst of little fables, nevertheless receive the name of narratives, indeed of histories; the poetic ones, however, are those of which Cicero, de Inu. l. 27, speaking as of a *kind of narratives remote from civil causes, which is spoken and written* (thought more charmingly) *for the sake of delight, not without useful exercise*, describes them thus: *those* (narratives) *which contain things neither* (most strictly) *true, nor* (most strictly) *verisimilar, of which sort is:*

*Huge winged serpents, joined in a yoke.*

§ 529. Quintilian, l. 4, enumerates *three species of narratives*: 1) *history* (most strictly true, at least predominantly), 2) *fable, which is found in tragedies and poems, remote not only from truth* (most strictly so called), *but also from the form of truth* (most strict), 3) *argument, which comedies feign as* (most broadly) *false, but like the true* (most strictly such). Hence [p. 341] a little fable, too, is for us a poetic fable, § 526. And the fable which he calls argument all the comic poets grant to be truly a fable; yet generally the argument of a Comedy, and rightly so, is a species of the fables we have called historical, § 527. Behold the first prologue of Terence:

*When first the poet applied his mind to writing, / He believed this alone was the business given him, / That the plays he had made should please the people.*

§ 530. Just as truth, therefore, is either most strictly so called, singular (historical), or heterocosmic, poetic, § 441, so also aesthetic verisimilitude, § 483, the universal endowment of things to be thought beautifully, § 22, is either that in which not even falsity most broadly so called is apperceived, VERISIMILITUDE MOST STRICTLY SO CALLED (historical), or that in which falsity most broadly so called is indeed detected, yet for spectators who, in the lovely dream of the waking, have once been carried into another orb by elegant middle cognition, once the sufficiently possible things of that other world have been supposed, all things cohere well and aptly, without any more vividly noticeable contradiction, gap, leap, etc. This is HETEROCOSMIC VERISIMILITUDE (poetic). The former belongs to historical fictions and fables, [p. 342] the latter to poetic ones, § 509, 527.

§ 531. In the oath which Ovid has, Met. III. 657:

*By you* (Pentheus) *now himself,* (Bacchus) *for no god more present than he / Is there, I swear, that I tell you things as true / As they are greater than belief.*

in which reason sees with complete certainty that nothing is sworn, the analogue of reason will see historical verisimilitude: that some good and poor fisherman, a drinker of water from his earliest years, such as Acoetes is depicted swearing, could be carried away by the orgies and all the Bacchic apparatus into such enthusiasm that he swears such things in good faith. But poetic verisimilitude only in what follows, if in heterocosmic fictions you follow the lovely dreams of the waking, according to the analogy of Graeco-Roman mythology: that the rest, the abductors of Bacchus, were changed into dolphins, that Acoetes alone was now cast into chains by Pentheus, but that

*The doors opened of their own accord, and of their own accord / (so the story goes) the chains slipped from his arms, with no one loosing them.*

[p. 343] § 532. Hence too the fairly customary division of fables into probable and improbable can be conceived. For suppose that the analogue of reason, having noticed some verisimilitude or other of a fable, § 530, is as it were roused to more careful weighings of the reasons for assent or dissent, and among the reasons for assent counts only those which prove truth most strictly so called, neglecting the reasons for any other kind of truth; then almost only the historical fables will remain in the balance of the probable; all the rest, or all but all, the lightness of the improbable will lift upward. Hence a FABLE MORE STRICTLY PROBABLE will be one for establishing which as possible in this world there seems to be more reason than for sending it into exile from this world into heterocosmic things, or altogether into the fabulous world. But a FABLE MORE STRICTLY IMPROBABLE will be one for not holding which among the possibles of this world there seems to be more reason than for the opposite, § 485, 486.

§ 533. In the same way, therefore, as truths, fictions, fables, verisimilitudes, we shall also distinguish AESTHETIC PROBABILITIES into HISTORICAL, or preponderating indications of truth most strictly so called, and POETIC, preponderances of heterocosmic truth. § 441, [p. 344] 509, 511, 527, 530. An improbable fable (historically and in this universe) is not deprived of all aesthetic probability of every kind (even heterocosmic), § 531; most broadly like the false, § 489, it can nonetheless contain much aesthetic truth, § 441, 423.

§ 534. The first fable of Phaedrus is more strictly improbable, § 532, although the manners of the age often prove that nothing is truer than it, so that we may dispense with Spinoza’s demonstrations; but the tenth fable of Book III, which is prefaced thus,

*Truth must be searched out well beforehand, / Lest foolish opinion judge wrongly,*

after the matter of the narrative has been properly weighed, will not be deprived of its historical probability, § 533, although it is more unusual for such things as it narrates to happen than the things that are feigned in the first fable.

§ 535. Let us subjoin some species of fables, even if they do not more closely concern verisimilitude, so as to complete the things in which those who would think beautifully must sedulously take care for verisimilitude. In fables a remarkable change of unhappiness into happiness is the KNOT (περιπέτεια [reversal]), and the fables which contain it are COMPLEX [p. 345] and connected FABLES, whose parts are δέσις [tying] or BINDING, μετάβασις [passing-over] or TRANSITION, and finally λύσις [loosing], SOLUTION.

§ 536. Who does not know that the plots of dramas are examples of complex fables of this kind, now comic, now tragic? As, meanwhile, those who undertook to feign tragedies with a happy outcome did not meet with so happy a success, so neither would those manage their affair well to whom it occurred to attach to comedies a truly sad ending. The reasons can be more beautifully drawn from the proper form of each kind of poem. For now let it suffice to note that dramatic fables are not the only ones to be thought of as having a knot. In epics themselves many, and still more that are in them, such as episodes, belong here. Indeed, if one chose to draw out some of the Aesopic fables, not a few would pass over here, whose knot would now be sought in a bulrush.

§ 537. The examples of fables are either endowed with reason, whose fables are called RATIONAL FABLES, or such as are judged devoid of reason, and the FABLES of these are called by some moral, by others FEIGNED, apologues, aeni, αἰσωποποίητα [Aesop-made], or joined partly from the first, partly [p. 346] from the second class, whose FABLES are MIXED.

§ 538. Of the rational ones let us add three species, or rather lower orders, by way of example. 1) Examples divine or of divine things, yet rational, if there are any such, are THEOLOGICAL FABLES, and more strictly mythological. 2) Examples of love, whose fables are MILESIAN, and more strictly romances, which more broadly coincide with poetic fictions. 3) Examples of sublime virtue that has earned glory, whose fables are heroic. Just as other species or orders could be added to these, so some greater fable, e.g. an epic, sometimes embraces them all at once, when *through windings and the ministrations of the gods and the fabulous torment of maxims it hurls the free spirit headlong, so that it may appear rather the vaticination of a raging mind*, about the chaste loves of heroes, *than the good faith of a religious speech before witnesses.* Petr.

[p. 347]

#### Sectio XXXIII. Argumenta Probantia. — Section XXXIII. Proving Arguments

§ 539. PROVING ARGUMENTS are now for us aestheticians those whose sole, or chief, or at any rate now principally to be considered power is, § 143, to exhibit to the things to be thought, and as it were to surround them with, beautiful verisimilitude, and that truth which, however great it may be, in however many falsities it may seem to be wrapped, nevertheless proves itself and presents itself pleasant and easy to all good men who know how to penetrate these pleasure-gardens of truth.

§ 540. As an exordium fit for all arguments of this kind we read in Lucretius, L. II,

*Now apply your mind to us for a true reasoning.* — — — — / *Cease therefore, terrified by novelty itself, / To spit reason out of your mind, but rather weigh it with keen / Judgement, and, if it seems true to you, / Give in, or, if it is false, gird yourself against it. / [p. 348] For the mind seeks a reason.*

Yet it is not everything, though not nothing either, that this not so small promiser says, after so great a prologue, about the multitude of worlds. Quintilian, compiling a great heap of arguments that serve this purpose, V. 10, now indeed applies the word argument to signify only proving and persuading ones, yet he himself notes that *all matter destined for writing* can be called an argument. Therefore every matter for thinking, too, the ground of another perception, is a perception, § 26.

§ 541. I have already observed that Quintilian does not distinguish proving arguments from persuading ones. Hence he refers to proofs all *enthymemes, epicheiremes and apodeixes*, *evident proofs*, as among the Geometers. But it will appear more fully below why I now abstain from them. An argument of aesthetic truth is one thing, an illustrating argument another. Put these together, and then at last you will have a persuading argument. Everything that persuades proves, but not everything that proves persuades, § 22, 539. But though it may also persuade, it is now attended to solely as proving.

§ 542. Let us rest content with the description in Quintilian, [p. 349] l. c., when, he says, *an argument* (a proving one) *is a reasoning that affords proof, by which one thing is inferred through another, and which confirms what is doubtful through that which is not doubtful.* But oh! what great things does he demand, continuing: *The force and nature of all things, and what each of them generally effects, must be known to him who would handle arguments rightly. For from this come the things called εἰκότα* [likelihoods]. But let us interpret *known* aesthetically, and *all things* as those about which you set out to think beautifully. For in general he himself concedes that to satisfy his postulate *is long, impossible, or rather infinite.*

§ 543. But let us come to certain species of arguments of this kind, without shaking out the topics anew, of which enough has already been said above, S. X. They have among the figures of diction the Paregmenon, by which words related in root and origin are conjoined, e.g. Ou. de Ponto, II. 5.

*You too, studious one, are held by the study of the Pierian Muses, / And, ingenious one, you favour my ingenuity.*

Whenever this becomes a proving argument, whether taken from the *definition by etymology*, which Quintilian mentions, or from ὀνοματολογίᾳ [the account of names], or that argument *from conjugates, which he would have thought ridiculous, had not Cicero used it*, it becomes a figure of thought, to be employed sometimes with much verisimilitude, § 26, 539.

[p. 350] § 544. For in most of the spectators whom he who would think charmingly wants as his own there are not lacking anticipations of this kind: 1) Our judicious ancestors assuredly wished to signify by similar and related words things related and similar. 2) Tautological propositions, either wholly or for the most part, are also identical. These are the foundations of the argument both etymological and to be elegantly drawn from conjugates, § 543. Thus Cicero, from the word *conuiuium* preferred to the Greeks’ συμποσίοις [symposia], could now make it verisimilar even to our Germans themselves, if he wished, that one should rather live together at banquets than drink together. Nor among the Germans who favour their own language will there be no argument, drawn from their essential word by which they designate happiness, for proving to them aesthetically that virtue and blessedness belong to it no less than fortune, if anyone has learned to turn it with an artist’s hand, so that it does not smell of the school.

§ 545. Among the figures of thought that are enumerated, I cite here AETIOLOGY as the first of them, by its own right, so that I dare to define every argument that proves more charmingly as the same, and COLOUR, a strictly feigned aetiology, which however is not to be confused with the colours of which below, just as a certain care is praised, [p. 351] while cares are all blamed, conveniently enough. There is a multiplied aetiology of this kind in Cicero pro Plancio: *Who is there who would deny that the zeal of that order* (the publicans) *for the honour of Plancius was singular? And not without reason, whether because his father was the man who has long been chief of the publicans, or because he was uniquely loved by his partners, or because he canvassed most diligently, or because he was petitioning for his son, or because this man’s greatest services towards that order in his quaestorship and tribunate were well known, or because they thought that in adorning this man they were adorning the order and providing for their own children.*

§ 546. An example of colour Juvenal has, Sat. VI. 229, or am I mistaken? or is it anywhere in the world most strictly true?

*But* (the wife) *lies in the embraces of a slave, or of a knight. Say, / Say some colour here, Quintilian, if you please,* § 545. / *We are stuck. Say it yourself: It had been agreed long ago, she says, / That you should do what you wished, and that I likewise might / Indulge myself. You may shout, and confound sea / With sky; I am human. Nothing is bolder than they / When caught. They take their anger and spirit from the crime.*

[p. 352] That very celebrated maxim of Virgil, § 499, is nothing but a colour, with which, Aen. L. XI, *Drances*

*hostile, whom the glory of Turnus / Was goading with slanting envy and bitter stings,*

covers a mind *powerful in sedition*, v. 362, gravely rebuked by Turnus: v. 399.

*No safety in war? Chant such things, madman, over the head / Of the Dardanian, and over your own fortunes.*

§ 547. The dictum de omni et nullo teaches with how much right the maxim belongs here, § 525, since the truth of singulars and of the inferiors contained under general things is usually inferred from these. But a proving maxim will also be either an aetiology, now completely certain, now aesthetically probable, or a colour, § 545. Let the opening of Sallust be an example of the first: *All men who strive to excel the other animals ought to exert themselves with the utmost effort, lest they pass through life in silence, like the cattle which nature has formed bent downward and obedient to the belly.* Of the second: *It seems to me more right to seek glory by the resources of wit than by those of strength, and, since life itself, which we enjoy, is short, to make the memory of ourselves as long as possible.*

§ 548. [p. 353] A maxim, which is a colour, § 546, persons of the best character not rarely insert among their own thoughts when, having to establish the verisimilitude of deeds wickedly done, they are compelled to bring forward some of those practical principles from whose perverse opinion evil minds have acted most wickedly. Here belongs the maxim of Euripides, uttered however from the sense of another, which Cicero translated and Caesar so often made his own:

*If right must be violated, it must be violated for the sake of ruling; / in other matters you may cultivate piety.*

Yet much caution will be needed in these, partly so that it be sufficiently evident that such things are uttered from the mind of a villain, partly lest they have so much ambiguity, or be conceived so wittily and charmingly, that it is rightly to be feared that they please more than is fair, § 465, 467.

§ 549. Nor is it without use that MAXIMS are distinguished into those uttered DE FACTO and those which are conceived DE IVRE, concerning the just and the equitable. The former set a certain moral matter before the eyes in general terms, as it mostly tends to be, as it tends to happen, not to be twisted to the point as if what is the argument of the meditation were being approved and commended for imitation.

[p. 354] *Accursed hunger for gold, to what do you not drive / mortal breasts!*

The latter display certain general things pertaining to morals in such a way that they at the same time show what the one affirming posits as true and praiseworthy in them:

*Learn justice, you who are warned, and not to scorn the gods.*

Here the caution of which § 547 will be well applied, since daily experience teaches how often those things are confused which are done and which ought to be done. *To how many is there the same care for honour in secret as in public? Many fear reputation, few conscience.* Plin. III, ep. 20. Finally let us remember, in maxims, § 174, and let us not heap them up rashly wherever we do not wish to seem *teachers of living*, lest, when we perhaps seem to ourselves most weighty, we seem to imitate the thin veins of the γνωμοτύπων [maxim-coiners], of whom § 370.

§ 550. Philosophers, exact after the geometers, will have learned how much a true definition, especially a genetic one, avails for drawing out truth. If such a definition, conceived even in logical rigour, of the many things to be thought beautifully has become known to him who is about to meditate elegantly, even if it is not itself expressed in just as many syllables and marks as the intellect has distinctly cognised it, but is either omitted altogether, or, interspersed with more marks and characters, is [p. 355] diluted into a fuller description, rounder for the analogue of reason, whether nominal or real, nevertheless, in the manner of a directing notion, just as it will pave the way to many elegances, so it will guard against the ugly internal impossibility of a utopian figment, will furnish the internal, sometimes also the external, possibility of the thing described, its first unity, its connection with its causes and its first consequences, to be adorned with easy effort by the rules of the art of aesthetics into aesthetic truth, S. XXVII. Hence I now cannot but read the definition in proving arguments, either omitted, or not explicitly thought, or transformed into a fairly pretty description, § 539.

§ 551. Concerning aesthetic definitions and descriptions there is a special place for treatment in our discipline. Here only their force and efficacy in proving beautifully are to be attended to, § 550. Just as of old the Greeks, entrusting themselves and their goods to the ocean, looked up to the course of the Great Bear as to the guide of their way, whereas the Phoenicians steered more directly by Cynosura: so the truer, the more exact will be the definition conceived in the mind of the things to be thought beautifully, the more verisimilar, the truer, the more certain will be the whole course of a charming meditation; but the coarser, the more teeming with many erroneous adherent concepts the first concept of thinking elegantly is, the easier will be the [p. 356] declension into aesthetic falsity itself. Imagine one about to think beautifully about chance, fortune, fame, honour, riches, friendship, public affairs, etc., who does not know better what these things are by their nature than everyone usually does, who think that nothing is more perspicuous to them, while they know as much as the most ignorant. Hence those so many false maxims, so many disfigurements of images, not even verisimilar, so many circumlocutions of words by which nothing at all is said.

§ 552. Let us commend one more thing among the more elegant arguments of proofs: epanorthosis, or correction. First, let us recall to mind that the true and the exact, in which truth itself is filed smooth, certainly differ. M. § 515. Hence let us infer: just as truth of whatever kind is not exact, so there can also be thought a certain COARSE AESTHETIC TRUTH and verisimilitude, too popular, indeed almost plebeian; the same can be tried on the Lydian stone, if not of purer reason and of the, so to speak, scientific intellect, yet of a more delicate taste and a mature judgement, even if a sensate one, as to its predominant part. From it, by polishing, adherent concepts can be removed, if not all the false ones, yet those which you might fear would bring some falsity, even aesthetic, into your materials. This is EXACT AESTHETIC TRUTH and verisimilitude.

§ 553. [p. 357] That study, to be commended above all, S. VI. § 22, 423, either, right at the first impulse of thoughts, substitutes exact notions of things and of general maxims and of other things for the ideas poured out, as it were, with the coarse ones wholly suppressed, or, anticipating the anticipations of these in its principal spectators, first thinks these same expressly κατ’ εἰκός [according to likelihood], such as they usually are, but then by correcting hews them into shape and cleanses them of the filth of falsities, even aesthetic ones, even if minor. This is epanorthosis. E.g. imagine with Horace, Serm. II. 6, among guests charmingly merrier:

*Talk arises, not about others’ villas or houses, / nor whether Lepos dances badly or not; but what more / concerns us, and is bad not to know, we discuss: whether / men are happy by riches or by virtue, / and what draws us to friendships, utility or the right, / and what is the nature of the good, and what its highest.*

Behold a cryptic epanorthosis of conversations, even familiar ones, or rather charming ones, and an example of people inquiring more exactly into what is true in aesthetics, since

[p. 358] *Among these things neighbour Cervius chatters old wives’ / tales to the purpose.*

§ 554. The fruit of such confabulations, studious of the more exact truth in aesthetic matters, you will surely read as a lovely one, a correction of a fairly common opinion, IV. od. 9.

*You will not rightly have called the possessor of much / happy. More rightly does he take / the name of happy who knows how to use / the gifts of the gods wisely, / and to bear hard poverty, / and fears disgrace worse than death; / he who is not afraid to perish for dear friends / or for his country.*

Here belongs the artifice of raising by ironic hyperbole, or rather of casting down into manifest falsity, even aesthetic falsity, S. XXVIII, the false adherent concepts, which most people have infinitely small, in some somewhat coarse notion, e.g. of wealth; e.g. Hor. Serm. II. 3. v. 94. *Everything,*

*Virtue, fame, honour, things divine and human, obey / beautiful riches; he who has heaped them up will be / famous, brave, just. Wise too? Yes, and king, / and whatever he wishes.*

[p. 359]

#### Sectio XXXIIII. Studium Veritatis Aestheticum Absolutum. — Section XXXIIII. The Absolute Aesthetic Pursuit of Truth

§ 555. The greater is the forest of things aesthetically true, verisimilar, of fictions and fables, through which he who is about to think beautifully can wander, lest he roam in it licentiously, and rather let him think that all will see his sins disfiguring it by falsity, in the elegant person of such a wit and breast a singular PURSUIT OF TRUTH must now be attended to separately, by which name let it be allowed to comprise not only a wit that is apt, but also one exercised by much labour and accustomed to perceiving aesthetico-logical truths, and not any propensity whatever, but a firm resolve of the mind to bring into its meditations the greatest truth which they admit with the beauty of the whole kept safe. This character of mind, since I judge it necessary for all things universally to be thought prettily, § 22, let us call the ABSOLUTE and universal PURSUIT OF TRUTH, § 29, 45.

§ 556. The same could be demonstrated from absolute gravity, [p. 360] S. XXIII, to those who know that all virtue is placed by many not inept philosophers in the love of truth. One of the highest arbiters of elegances among the English decrees *that all beauty is truth*, that *in poetry itself, where all things are fable, truth nevertheless rules and effects the perfection of the whole.* But it will be better for the stricter friends of truth to retrace the degrees of aesthetico-logical truth somewhat further back. The least is the least perception of the least metaphysical truth. Hence 1) the richer, 2) the greater and more worthy, 3) the more exact, 4) the clearer and more distinct, 5) the more certain and solid, 6) the more ardent the perception of an object is, 7) the more things, 8) the greater and weightier things, 9) the things of stronger rules, 10) the more fitting things it comprises, the greater is the aesthetico-logical truth, § 437. M. § 184.

§ 557. I should not think there is now need of a demonstration 1) that no greatest truth is aesthetico-logical, but more strictly logical, § 424, 2) that no such truth falls to man, that no thing is cognised in the greatest logical truth by any human intellect, since he who cognises one thing thus knows all. Hence the metaphysical evil of all aesthetico-logical truth in man, the lack of the highest logical truth met with only in omniscience, is infinitely great. [p. 361] And so, since the pursuit of truth of a quite sound mind, most loving of the true, cannot nevertheless be borne towards those things which it can know cannot come about, nor will nothing at all as soon as it has cognised that it cannot obtain everything: it must be content with an infinitely small part of the highest logical truth taken more broadly, namely that part which it can attain, § 556.

§ 558. To logical truth taken more broadly, § 556, nos. 1–6 procure formal perfection, nos. 7–10 material. Hence the human pursuit of truth at one time aims chiefly at the formal, which cannot happen without loss of the material, at another embraces chiefly the material, nor can it do so except with detriment to the formal, § 557. There is attributed to someone a cognition not only copious, but also complete; not only sufficiently great, but also equalling its object and adequate to it; not only true, but also exact, in which there is nothing at all false; not only clear, but also completely clear, sufficient for distinguishing the object from all others, and also distinct, all of whose marks are clear, not rarely through several degrees; not only certain, but also completely certain, rigorously demonstrable and excluding all fear of the opposite; not only moving, but also compelling assent, extorting approval and some necessary pleasure and appetition.

§ 559. [p. 362] All this is well. But what sort of thing is that object which a man contemplates with such perfect reason? A certain universal, arisen from individuals comprising very many greatest things, most fitting to the strongest rules, each according to its own measure, as innumerable marks of differences, down to the numerical difference of very many — arisen in such a way that in these individuals, in which alone the most determinate metaphysical truth is, and which would hence procure for perception a generically greatest material perfection, if they were perceived as individuals, 1) the abundances beyond a perception that is complete are abstracted away, 2) things greater and weightier than the degree that was posited in a cognition equalling the object are abstracted away, 3) those things about which the given exact cognition does not sufficiently perceive whether they are true or not, lest any falsity remain for the cognition, are abstracted away, 4) those things which are not needed for distinguishing, together with those which refuse to become clear to a given subject, are abstracted away, 5) those things which for a given subject are not yet completely certain, not rigorously demonstrable, in which a fear of the opposite remains, are abstracted away, 6) those things which could move to the contrary, recall assent, perhaps even create tedium, are abstracted away.

§ 560. [p. 363] Thus, the objects of the human disciplines and sciences having arisen, universals, § 559, there is born together in the minds of the solidly learned a truth certainly perfect, often beautiful, even more strictly logical. But now it is asked whether to such a universal there belongs a metaphysical truth equal to that which belongs to its individual, which is contained under it? For my part I think it can now be most evident to philosophers that whatever chief formal perfection is in it had to be bought with the loss of much and great material perfection in cognition and in logical truth. For what is abstraction, if it is not a loss? By the same reasoning you would not make a marble globe out of marble of irregular shape except with at least as much detriment to the material as the greater price of roundness will demand.

§ 561. Now let it be supposed that the pursuit of aesthetico-logical truth is at some time borne especially towards its material perfection, § 558, and hence embraces the objects of the most determinate metaphysical truth that it can. Here the intention is not deliberately to cut off by abstraction the impediments to any roundness whatever, yet neither is it within human power to mark out completely for oneself the widest compass of all-sided determination; things exceeding the magnitude [p. 364] of our intellection are not deliberately cut off, yet neither can the human mind attain all the weights of determinate and almost or wholly actual things and balance them in their just moments. We have not resolved to cut off whatever could by any reasoning take on the appearance of falsity, yet neither is the true everywhere clear in the indefinite moments of an all but singular object of this kind. It is not the plan to omit and relegate to voluntary darkness what does not have some required clarity, and yet strength, place and time do not permit raising the single moments of the object to that clarity. It is not the intent to omit entirely things somewhat uncertain, yet neither can the thinker rigorously demonstrate all the things which befit an object of the most determinate truth. We have not resolved to cut off many things which may please or be hateful, yet neither do our powers suffice to exhibit everything in such a way that he who has meditated could efficaciously desire or shun them by certain and deliberate decrees.

§ 562. You have the composite perfection of aesthetico-logical truth, § 556; you see the inevitable exceptions, if you wish to attain both even moderately, § 560, 561. The purer intellect and reason of the stricter friends of truth so follows, through the exact sciences, the formal perfection described in § 560 that it leaves to the analogue of reason and to cognitions sensate in whatever degree the task of adding, as far as their powers allow, the complements and supplements of materially perfect truth, [p. 365] once its universals and more abstract things have been brought to the formal perfection of scientific things.

§ 563. But the no less strenuous friend of truth who follows it with the analogue of reason does not in turn have a lower sensate faculty to which he might commend what he has left aside and omitted; yet for the sake of the material perfection of truth he does not dare to neglect altogether the things which have in themselves much of the most determinate metaphysical truth. Hence for him it is more necessary sometimes to make exceptions from the rules of the highest formal perfection in tracking down aesthetic truth, lest the material perfection of the same truth suffer too much detriment, § 558.

§ 564. The most universal, most abstract things, and those which are to be raised by the art described in § 559 to the highest formal perfection of truth obtainable by men, being partly left altogether to the logical horizon, partly drawn from it and wrapped up again somewhat in the not so few more determinate differences from which reason had abstracted, but especially in the singular, [p. 366] individual, and most determinate things which exhibit the greatest material perfection of aesthetico-logical truth — in these the aesthetic horizon takes its delight, as its own forest, chaos and matter, § 129, out of which it so carves aesthetic truth into a form, if not altogether perfect, yet beautiful, § 558, 14, that during the elaboration as little as possible of materially perfect truth perishes and is worn away in polishing for the sake of elegance, § 563.

§ 565. Let him who is about to think beautifully therefore take for himself a matter either more determinate — one of the lower genera or altogether species of things — or, if he seems to ascend higher into the superior genera, let him nonetheless be held to clothe them with many marks and characters which purer science omits, or finally let him choose for himself singular themes, in which the perfection of material truth reigns. Let him surround himself with a huge multitude of marks. He will have rejected those which the beautiful form does not admit. Let those remain of which a brief, but elegantly full, roundness, a charming dignity, both absolute and relative, the perfection of material truth itself, elegant vividness and the brilliance necessary to a meditation, intimate persuasion, life above all and efficacy in delighting and moving will suffer scarcely one to be missed. But in these very many marks let the metaphysical truth not be perceived by the meditator completely, right up to the light, provided no uglier falsity is in them. Now I should think that I have reduced to some general formula [p. 367] the aesthetic necessities, some special cases of which I enumerated, § 491–502, of making exceptions, in a collision of the rules of perfection in cognising the true, from the more perfect form of truth, for the sake of wrapping the materially more perfect truth in the more lovable form of verisimilitude, § 483.

#### Sectio XXXV. Studium Veritatis Comparatiuum. — Section XXXV. The Comparative Pursuit of Truth

§ 566. The absolute, but aesthetic, pursuit of truth, § 565, will comparatively exert itself chiefly according to the threefold species of truths, and the degree of those materially perfect, with which it is occupied. Of its matter there are 1) general things, 2) the actual things of this world, 3) heterocosmic things. The MANNER OF THINKING that expresses general things, but elegantly, is the AESTHETICO-DOGMATIC; that which charmingly paints the actual things of this world, on account of the small number of future things to be set forth in the strictest truth, let us call the AESTHETICO-HISTORICAL. Finally, the manner of meditating heterocosmic things elegantly let us name, by some new synecdoche, [p. 368] even when it is not expressed in verses, the POETIC MANNER OF THINKING.

§ 567. Since the aesthetico-dogmatic manner of thinking 1) when named after the orders of matters by disciplines — theological, philosophical, etc. — has a name in common with the theological, philosophical, etc., scientific and acroamatic manner of thinking, 2) by ascending higher more often contemplates things common to the logical and the aesthetic horizon, 3) rightly exhibits its primary heads and the chief moments of its meditation to the beautiful intellect through extensive distinction: it is mostly confused with the scientific and formally philosophical manner of thinking, is often not ineptly combined with it, yet always, as it is truly different, is distinguished from it by singular advantages to the sciences no less than to the elegances. § 566.

§ 568. From the confusion of the two dogmatic manners of thinking, the logical and the aesthetic, a twofold prejudiced opinion holds sway: 1) that of those who, even when it is a question of the more rigid intellectual and rational theory of the first principles of some universal cognition, to be treated as exactly, distinctly and solidly [p. 369] as can be done, stop short at a diluted, though not altogether light, verisimilar, sufficiently perspicuous, howsoever little certain exposition of truth, provided it pleases the ears or the eyes enough, and seems to bear the reckoning of reason, while in truth it bears rather that of the analogue of reason. 2) That of those who think that some dogma is to be propounded to the grasp of people less exercised in the sciences, or to altogether popular understanding, and that by the living voice, and yet labour in vain to adorn it with the briefest definitions, the most exact axioms, the most distinct resolutions of notions, and completely certain demonstrations. § 567.

§ 569. The logico-dogmatic and the aesthetico-dogmatic manner of thinking, their names taken from the predominant feature, differ from one another not only in form, but also in the matters in which at first sight they seemed most of all to agree. As the former follows chiefly the principles of its theme, so the latter follows what is derived from the principles and the consequences. As the former chiefly draws out the universals under which its theme is contained, so the latter chiefly attends to the lower concepts of the same theme, which it contains under itself. Even if the matters have received their name from a whole discipline, there are nevertheless in any discipline certain things first in order, and certain things as it were last in the synthetic method. The former the logical, [p. 370] the latter the aesthetic manner of thinking so pursues through the disciplines that it can roughly be said, in any one of them, that where the logical manner of thinking leaves off, the aesthetic manner of thinking begins. § 566.

§ 570. Those who have looked more deeply into things see well that between those genera of things with which the sciences strictly so called have hitherto been concerned, and individuals, there still lies a great interval of lower genera and species, into whose territory demonstrators rarely descend, into which exact experimenters and observers rarely ascend with success. This is above all the course on which the aesthetico-dogmatic manner of thinking should run. Hence either it chooses its theme itself best of all, or it draws a theme sought from even higher down to that point, towards greater material truth, § 440, even if it thereby becomes formally a little less exact according to the scientific balance of the logicians, i.e. elegant to the point of verisimilitude. § 565.

§ 571.
