The comparative pursuit of truth in the aesthetico-dogmatic manner of thinking, § 566, 1) will solicitously seek even the more strictly logical truth about the matter itself, if it belongs to the aesthetico-logical horizon, or about its genera and superior notions, [p. 371] even if it is not to be expressed acroamatically, yet as the directrix of the verisimilitude to be thought explicitly in the general notions and in those to be adopted, § 429, 424. 2) Notions in which the intellect and reason have already detected something of contradiction reaching to absurdity, or almost reaching it, it will guard against the more carefully, the more probable it is that there will also be such spectators, etc., of an aesthetico-dogmatic meditation of this kind as can themselves perceive the latent incongruity most certainly by reason, and show it sufficiently aesthetically to other companions in watching, reading, etc. § 431–435.

§ 572. The pursuit of truth in aesthetico-dogmatic matters 3) will apply itself especially to this, that it paint most vividly, as most verisimilar, the connection of its matter, more briefly with its principles, more richly with its weightiest consequences, not without a persuasion that is desirable, or else fertile in the tedium to be drawn from aversion, § 437. 4) If it also attempts to advance into lower things, and reason shines immediately before it, it will nonetheless move every stone to set forth its own supplements, inseparably joined with this, for what is to be cognised with complete certainty, § 439.

§ 573. The pursuit of truth in aesthetico-dogmatic matters [p. 372] 5) will most gladly set out to its own people those things which he who is about to think also knows with certainty to be logically true, § 571, through the harmony of the lower and higher cognitive faculties, now to be exhibited as aesthetically true; if this is not granted, it will prefer what is logically probable to it over what is logically improbable to it, § 485, on account of the same harmony. These it will either judge will now also be verisimilar to its principal spectators, and rests secure in them; or it will suspect that they will have little verisimilitude with these. In the latter case either it has an occasion for giving its thoughts verisimilitude by charmingly proving arguments, and then it is not torn a finger’s breadth from the true, as it seems to itself, and labours rather to draw its own people to its own opinion than to think contrary things with them; or place and time do not permit reconciling verisimilitude to what is probable to it, but doubtless improbable enough to its own people.

§ 574. If he who is about to think beautifully falls upon a dogma probable to himself, which he perceives neither to be verisimilar to his own people, nor able now to be made such to them by himself, § 573, the friend of truth will either posit neither this nor its opposite as verisimilar to the spectators, if it is permitted to abstract from both, or, if one or the other must be posited by aesthetic necessity, weighs with much gravity whether what is verisimilar to the spectators [p. 373] especially to be attended to by him is perhaps an error, common indeed, yet quite innocent, or a more dangerous one, whose supporters could deservedly be charged, even indirectly, with moral falsity, § 470, 467. In the former case he will use the opinion of his own people, as philosophers and mathematicians use their heuristic fictions; in the latter he will rather wish to seem to have thought something false, erring most broadly, § 471, than expose his conscience or his reputation to the future reproach of moral falsity, § 464.

§ 575. The pursuit of truth in aesthetico-dogmatic matters 6) not for the sake of illustration alone, but also on account of § 572, 569, will be much occupied with examples of its matter, § 526, and in them will prefer singulars to universals, ideas to notions, the most strictly true to heterocosmic and fabulous things, § 472, or, when a charming necessity has called it to these, will not dissemble that they are fables, § 565, or aesthetically heuristic fictions, § 574.

§ 576. The AESTHETICO-DOGMATIC MANNER OF THINKING either has a theme that more closely enunciates what is to be done or omitted, or not. The latter is the THEORETICAL, the former the PRACTICAL. The practical in turn [p. 374] either has a theme to be commended, the PARAENETIC, or one to be dissuaded, the ELENCTIC. Just as Cicero’s writings, both the rhetorical and the philosophical, are examples of the aesthetico-dogmatic manner of thinking, so the *Offices* are especially of the practical. Lucretius is an example of the aesthetico-theoretical manner of thinking. The satires, of the elenctic. In these Juvenal, rather than promise wholly fictitious examples, prefers *to try*

*what is allowed against those / whose ashes the Flaminian and the Latin way cover.*

§ 577. The pursuit of truth in aesthetico-dogmatic matters of whatever kind, § 576, 7) even if it treats things to be cognised with the most complete certainty, will nevertheless now not ascend beyond a certain VERISIMILITUDE, which we shall call DOGMATIC, by which the dogma is not indeed rendered completely certain, yet no falsity in it, no internal repugnance or contradiction with completely certain things, or at least with things more certain than this dogma, no incoherence with the principles from which it would have to be deduced, or with the consequences to be derived from it, total, etc., is apperceived, whether by the intellect, by reason or by the analogue of reason, as great as these faculties are to be supposed in the principal personal objects, § 483.

[p. 375] § 578. For when definitions are not straightway hewn to the severe rules of the logical art, or are again skilfully resolved into beautiful descriptions; when general propositions, with the intuitive judgements from which they were formed enthymematically omitted, are nevertheless exhibited as experiences; when not only secondary axioms and postulates are mixed with primary ones, but many theorems and problems too are assumed as though indemonstrable, or are proved by leaps logically illegitimate, though beautiful, by cryptic forms, of which the one to be convinced is not completely certain, yet elegant and oratorical; when scholia reign and absorb most of the space: what else will now come forth from such a meditation, even if it has a theme that is most completely certain, but dogmatic verisimilitude? § 577.

§ 579. If space permitted, it could be shown more fully from the history of philosophy that the confusion of this dogmatic manner of thinking with the accurately logical and intellectual one, § 568, is the source from which 1) the philosophers and almost all the other learned have for so many centuries been surpassed in solidity and certainty by the mathematicians, for whom, in expounding their own matters to their own followers, it has always been the true course to instruct the intellect and especially reason, not only by promising, but also by performing. 2) The disciples of Plato, who always [p. 376] combined, not to say confounded, these two manners of thinking, have fallen into such great uncertainty, excepting the Aristotelians, whose teacher showed in his acroamatic writings how much difference there is between the logically true and the aesthetically verisimilar, if we are to philosophise. 3) The eternal disputes of the dogmatists and the sceptics, and of those who are nearer now to the one, now to the other. § 9.

§ 580. The absolute aesthetic pursuit of truth, S. XXXIIII, will comparatively display itself in the highest degree of its perfection, even material perfection, as most curious in the aesthetico-historical manner of thinking, § 466, of which 1) the most ordinary kind is that which describes matters of fact, past singulars; 2) a kind not to be neglected by the aesthetician is that which expresses the present state of the mind; 3) one to be observed not so frequently, yet more often than it seems, is that which foresees things to come. If you call the middle one the empirical, the third the mantic manner of thinking: THE AESTHETICO-HISTORICAL MANNER OF THINKING will be either the MORE STRICTLY historical, or the EMPIRICAL, or the MANTIC.

§ 581. Examples of the first kind of thinking elegantly, of which § 580, are *the famous writers by whom the prosperous or adverse fortunes of the ancient Roman people have been recorded*; of the second, both the not so [p. 377] few letters that Cicero and Pliny wrote about matters concerning themselves at the time when these were happening, and the poems written in the midst of loving or grieving, about love or sadness.

*Wretched, I waste in vain words that avail nothing,* / *The heavy waters themselves drench the mouth that speaks* / — — — — / *Surely we are perishing, nor is there hope of safety, save a vain one,* / *And while I speak, the wave overwhelms my face.* / *Ah me, how swiftly the clouds flashed with flame!* / *How great a crash resounds from the ethereal vault!* etc.

§ 582. I would not have the aesthetico-mantic manner of thinking sought only in true or fictitious prophecies, e.g. Virg. ecl. IIII, but it is better to observe it intermixed with others, whenever the mind has to be directed towards things to come from things present, or past, or wholly general, even without any extraordinary divination. e.g.

*Perhaps one day it will be a joy to remember even these things.* / *O you who have suffered worse! God will give an end to these things too.* Virg.

*Wherever a fortune better than our parent shall bear us,* / *We shall go. O comrades and companions* / — — — — / *O brave men, who have often suffered worse things* / *With me, now drive away cares with wine,* / [p. 378] *Tomorrow we shall set out again upon the vast sea.* Hor. I. od. 7.

§ 583. The more strictly historical manner of thinking beautifully, and in aesthetic matters the empirical as well as the mantic, differ 1) from the more severe logical and critical care of the historian, at least a good preliminary one, by which through memory and intellectual judgement, and much use of reason, he selects his matter for himself out of the great farrago of traditions, rumours, report, calumnies, fictions, fables etc., before he weaves the things that have pleased him into the elegant web of a pleasant narration; 2) from the logical solicitude of the experimenters and observers, truly so called, among the philosophers, by which they set the thing perceived before the intellect by intuitive judgements and experiences strictly so called in such a way that the vice of subreption is guarded against as far as in them lies, as are enthymematically omitted major premisses and preconceived opinions; 3) from the logical and rational foresight by which a politician, e.g., measures the future state of his republic, as if from some watchtower.

§ 584. This pursuit of truth in aesthetico-historical matters, since it is for the most part exercised by the lower faculties of the mind, § 583, needs not only fictions in the broader sense, § 505, but [p. 379] in meditations that are to be somewhat extended it will also intersperse among the things known to itself as completely certain, as most strictly true, both generals that are less certain, and singulars of which it has not been completely convinced whether they are truly possibles of this universe. § 507. Hence this pursuit of truth will neither rise above verisimilitude, § 483, nor yet busy itself to attain just any verisimilitude whatever, but just as in its interspersed generals it will embrace the dogmatic verisimilitude, § 577, so in its ideas the one most strictly so called, § 530, not shunning all historical fictions even in the stricter sense, § 509, yet even in these averse to everything that could justly be accused of moral falsity, of slight partisanship, or of credulity, and to all poetic fictions, and to utopias and worlds best apart from this one, whenever it binds its thoughts by historical faith. § 566.

#### Sectio XXXVI. Studium Veri Poeticum. — Section XXXVI. The Poetic Pursuit of Truth

§ 585. The absolute aesthetic pursuit of truth, S. XXXIIII, can be moved, for other reasons already indicated, to the poetic manner of thinking, § 566, when 1) history does not supply enough for the charming fullness [p. 380] of the requisites, and the good man openly prefers for this reason to lie, by devising a more beautiful abundance, rather than cunningly to corrupt historical faith. 2) When fictitious heterocosmic examples will give more dignity, or even majesty, than historical ones, which have always undergone something human, which not even the most strictly so-called verisimilitude often permits to be suppressed, § 584. 3) When more moral truth, a greater unity of characters, place and time, and a more observable and, so to say, more compact connexion can be hoped for from heterocosmic things than from things which in this universe are, because of higher reasons, more scattered, or, because of human weakness, more inconstant. 4) When, because of the anticipations of its principal elements in the spectators, it can hope that the heterocosmic will be better known to them than an event of this world, but one situated in a part of it quite unknown to them. There are many well versed in the world of Milesian tales or of Aesopic fables who are strangers in historical matters. 5) When it hopes for easier belief in fictions than in things most strictly true. That this can be hoped for not so rarely will be found by those who know how often, and for how many men, there is need to say: *I would not have thought it*; 6) when truth most strictly so called is foreseen to be going to have anything but the life which is chiefly intended. § 561.

[p. 381] § 586. But if the absolute pursuit of truth has impelled the one who is to think beautifully to feign heterocosmic things, § 585, it is now transmuted into the pursuit of poetic verisimilitude, § 530, which is so far from being the same as the historical and most strictly so-called verisimilitude that, if it were necessary to refer it to one or the other, either to the dogmatic, § 577, or to the most strictly so called, I should rather refer the εἰκός [the likely] of the poets to the dogmatic, with the sufficient support of Aristotle, who bids *the historian* report *what has happened, the poet what kind of things ought to happen, and hence praises poetry as better and more philosophical than history, because it speaks more of universals, history however of singulars. For to be universal is that it befalls such a man to say or do such things according to the necessary or the verisimilar; singular however is what Alcibiades did or suffered.*

§ 587. Hence certain less well-instructed critics are deceived who examine poetic verisimilitude by the laws of historical verisimilitude, when they straightway condemn the poet, i.e. the one now feigning heterocosmic things, of a sin against verisimilitude, when he feigns that someone was killed at a time and in a place at which and where it is historically either verisimilar or wholly certain that the same man was still alive, without hearing any further exceptions in the matter. Authors are deceived who, truly creating a new world [p. 382] even about weightier matters, nevertheless stop short at merely historical fictions, and then think they have managed their business best when they have brought some good man to the point of perceiving no fraud beneath their thoughts, taking whatever they feign for things most strictly true, and for anecdotes perhaps dearer than any gold. This artifice seems to have in it something of falsity, by an indirect route, even of aesthetic falsity. § 464.

§ 588. Nor, above all, is poetic verisimilitude to be placed in the probability, whether logical and disciplinary, or historical and most strictly so called, of that hypothesis under which the objects of a charming middle cognition might perhaps, through poetic fiction, have come to pass even in this universe, and which is, as it were, the basis and foundation of the new world. § 511. Nor is it the business of the analogue of reason ordinarily to examine more deeply the first causes, elements and primary threads of the universe, while it holds fast to the phenomena in their effects. But when an analogical figment sets before its eyes things, § 516, of which it already has within itself certain anticipations from the world of the poets long since known to it, § 573, it seizes upon any hypothesis whatever, after the fashion of a lemma, and by means of it, as by some familiar bridge, *it stands ready to leap* into your new world, *and does not fear*

[p. 383] *The legs of the little bridge, standing on planks that cannot be renewed,* / *Lest it go over on its back and lie down in the hollow marsh.* Cat.

§ 589. Since the poetic manner of thinking, § 580, is nothing but a certain charming, or at least not inelegant, exception, § 585, 25, all its verisimilitude consists in this, that it be evident even to the analogue of reason that those things have been feigned which, saving the beauty of the whole, could be the fewest; or at least that the contrary be not evident to it, that nothing should seem able truly to be called feigned without aesthetic necessity, § 491–503, 565. Let it be permitted to call the first POSITIVE POETIC VERISIMILITUDE, the other NEGATIVE.

§ 590. The first question, then, in judging the verisimilitude of the poetic manner of thought, would be whether it was aesthetically necessary to choose that manner at all, or whether perhaps the contrary is evident even to a well-instructed analogue of reason. Thus Quintilian rightly objects *poetic licence* to *the Greek historians* whenever they mix into their own writings things poetically verisimilar, however verisimilar they may be, since there is no place at all for these throughout histories, § 584. This verisimilitude, external, so to say, and relative to the condition of the author [p. 384] and the dignity of the work or of the whole into which the poetically verisimilar is to be mixed, and to the dispositions of the chief spectators whom you wish to please, we generally suppose is to be deduced hypothetically, after the fashion of a postulate, from the definition of the more restricted manner of thinking which someone has once chosen.

§ 591. Theological and heroic fables befit the epic, the poetic manner of thinking befits feigned fables, and hence a peculiar verisimilitude, etc. These things are evident axiomatically. Whence the postulates: whoever wishes to be an epic poet, an Aesop etc., let him seek this verisimilitude. Further than this, aesthetic judgement of the meditation itself will scarcely ascend. Whether an author has decided well, wisely, and in a manner suited to himself and to his own hearers, especially those he may hope for, to become an epic poet, an Aesop etc., does not directly touch the verisimilitude and hence the internal beauty of the meditation.

§ 592. The second question will be: whether that new world, into which the poet, whether a prose writer or a writer in verse, or a painter, or a sculptor, etc., is about to introduce us, is, from the standpoint of his own intentions to be elegantly carried out, the best world after this one? Nay, whether the theme could in it truly be set, more perfectly [p. 385] and more elegantly, in a certain pleasant dream of waking men, than it could have been thought in this world? whether the new world differs from ours only in those moments that make for the charming end, or whether many other and different things are also placed in it in vain? Ovid, about to describe in his Metamorphoses some best and greatest world of the poets, yet wishes to observe a certain chronology in agreement with this universe:

*and from the first origin of the world* / *To bring down a continuous song to my own times.*

§ 593. Though about to feign poetically as much as any poet ever could lie, he nevertheless begins from those histories which not a few would concede to be wholly true dogmatically, and almost all would concede to be verisimilar, and at first he inserts among them none but fictions which either no one, or at least the readers he had chiefly to attend to, could have guessed to transgress the limits of historical fictions. Behold! first you have Chaos. v. 5–20.

*A god and a better nature settled this strife.* / And now fire, water, earth and air are separated, / *which, by as much as the weight of water* / *Is lighter than the weight of earth, by so much is heavier than fire.* 53.

Did the air-pump, then, only now discover the [p. 386] gravity of the air? Meanwhile all things come to be, down to the animals, 75.

*An animal holier than these, and more capable of a lofty mind,* / *Was still lacking, and one that could have dominion over the rest.* / *Man was born. Whether that craftsman of things, the origin of a better world,* / *Made him from divine seed,* / *Or whether the fresh earth* / was that which Prometheus / *Fashioned into the likeness of the gods who govern all things.* / *First was sown the golden age, which, with no avenger,* / *Of its own accord, without law, kept faith and right.* 90. / — — — — / *Straightway there bursts into the age of worse vein* / *Every wickedness; Shame and Truth and Faith fled,* / *Into whose place came Frauds and Deceits* / *And Plots and Force and the wicked Love of possessing.* 130. / — — — — / *You would think they had sworn to crime. Let them all speedily pay* / *The penalties they have deserved to suffer; so stands the sentence,* 243. / — — — — / *Yet the loss of the human race is a grief to him.* 246. / — — — — / *The king of the gods forbids them to tremble, and promises an offspring* / [p. 387] *Unlike the former people, of wondrous origin,* 252. / — — — — / *And now sea and land had no distinction* / *All things were sea, and the sea too lacked shores,* 292.

And now the bard seems to have given his readers enough to drink of the most strictly true and of the historically verisimilar, so that, unawares, gradually snatched away altogether out of this universe, as if through a dream, they are carried into that world where men come from stones, the laurel from Daphne, a heifer from Io, Io from a heifer, and whatever else there may be of

*the splendid miracles that he then brings forth.*

§ 595. These questions, § 590, 592, are not the only ones, nor the primary ones, § 585, 588. Let it be necessary to posit heterocosmic things; let their difference from this universe be no greater than is necessary. Now a third question is asked: whether you have rather taken your new fiction from the whole world of the poets and its richer, worthier, more verisimilar, better-known regions etc., § 22, than led your guests into unknown mazes of fables, without aesthetic necessity? Whether this, the contrary of perfection, can be detected even by the analogue of reason? § 513, 516. You have Horace’s precept repeated:

[p. 388] *I shall follow a song feigned from what is known.*

The verisimilitude of a poetic fiction demands above all that it be, with respect to the world of the poets, in those things in which it departs from this universe, as similar and consonant to it as the beauty of the whole which you are thinking permits, § 484.

§ 596. Hence the necessity of the study of mythology, not only of that of the Greeks and Romans, but of all times and places, whose report, cabbala and traditions will be able to furnish a region of the poetic world especially commendable to someone thinking for the sake of certain people about a certain theme, § 594. Nor should you object the disagreements of the ancients in this very world of the poets, if you distinguish its primitive chaos from the derivative one gradually overlaid with adornment and grace, and consider with yourself the difficulty in which poets once found themselves, not so far distant from one another in time, of making known to themselves a poetic world scarcely born or enriched before them, a difficulty which has now almost wholly disappeared. Ovid’s argument is to be carefully weighed, proving, when he is already plunging deeper into heterocosmic things:

*Who would believe this, unless antiquity stood as witness?* Met. I, 400.

With which agrees that saying of Virgil about the ships changed into nymphs:

*The belief is ancient, but the fame is perennial.*

[p. 389] § 597. The fourth question is asked: whether the one feigning poetically has touched upon and entered the utopia of the poetic world, beyond the limits of § 456, and that in such a way that he could be caught on the lightning-struck ground by the very analogue of reason, or whether he has altogether enlarged such dismal fields with broken glass and the interpretations of mere dreams? § 574. Far more charmingly does the prince of the French epic bards feign a prophetic hermit, the demon of fanaticism, and Saint Louis as the guide of Henry the Great through the heavens and the underworld, than if he had called back the gods of greater and lesser rank from the utopian intermundane spaces of the Greeks or Romans.

§ 598. Let the fifth question be: whether the poet has not joined together things which, taken separately, could indeed be in a good enough state, as far as their possibility is to be aesthetically perceived, but which, fused into one, are incompossible? This could happen chiefly for three reasons, 1) if regions of the poetic world severed by eternal enmities were confounded, an example of which case is in § 515. For the world of the poets has its islands and peninsulas, nor did a prudent god of the bards

*In vain sever* / *By Ocean the unsociable* / *Lands*

[p. 390] between which lie *shallows* that are not well *leapt across*; 2) if something is fastened onto the world of the poets which in itself implies no aesthetic contradiction, but which would so conflict with a portion of the poetic world already once assumed that it would become an anomalous figment without necessity, § 520.

§ 599. 3) If, once we had passed over into heterocosmic things, such phenomena of this universe, and these most strictly true, were set before us again when we did not expect it, by which, since they cannot stand together with the feigned world, we should be compelled, as if awakened from a spontaneous dream of waking men, perhaps somewhat against our will, and at a time when it is not yet time, to recognise as feigned, and that in the stricter sense, all the things which, as though present, we wished to gaze upon, gladly yielding to the poet’s illusions, right up to the curtain. To avoid this fault Aristotle rather wittily judged the poet worthy of excuse *if he composes things that have happened*, and writes things most strictly true, which however he does not concede to him, except when and in so far as they are verisimilar, which I here interpret in its full compass, that they have poetic verisimilitude too, so that they have a place in that new world which we feign just as much as in this one. For then, looked upon as portions of that world and not of this, they will not rashly interrupt the spontaneous dream dreamt for pleasure’s sake.

[p. 391] § 600. The sixth question is asked: whether the poet, choosing the world best after this one for his own ends, § 591, has conformed his well-analogical figment, § 516, 595, among the unsociable portions of the poetic world, § 598, above all to that part and region which is best for those same ends of the poet? Almost all religions have some part of their own in the world of the poets. Almost any history of a commonwealth, if you wish to go back to the first times of dominions,

*hides its head among the clouds*

of fictions and of rather uncertain report. Hence he would assuredly sin against verisimilitude, even poetic verisimilitude, who ascribed, e.g., to a Turk the mysteries of the ancient Egyptians, and to the Chinese the mythologies of the Goths. But would those not sin who, being Christians, feigning something about a Christian for the sake of Christians, always and everywhere inhabit the world of the superstitions of the Greeks and Romans? Milton’s world is more verisimilar.

§ 601. Let the seventh question be: if someone has dared to bring forward a wholly unknown figment, and the eighth: if someone has formed an altogether anomalous figment, whether each has enough internal verisimilitude to compensate for whatever at first seemed like falsity in such fictions, [p. 392] so that the last fiction may in the end be held rather a correction of the poetic world than a disgraceful anomaly in it. § 518, 520. The ninth: whether the maxim of fables, § 526, has in point of fact the dogmatic moral verisimilitude in the broader sense, § 577, and in point of right the one more strictly so called at the same time? § 433, 435. Whether it is sufficiently evident from the outcome of the fable whether the author wishes his maxim to be understood as true only in point of fact, or also in point of right? § 548.

§ 602. Phaedrus, b. II. f. 1, has a maxim true in point of right, the example of which is the lion leaving to the innocent traveller the part of the prey denied to the robber.

*An example altogether outstanding and praiseworthy;* / *But greed is rich, and modesty poor.*

F. 2 teaches that the maxim is to be understood only in point of fact, by the baldness of a disgraceful uxorious man:

*That men are in any case despoiled by women,* / *Whether they love or are loved, we learn, to be sure, from examples.*

But b. III. f. 5, since it would yield this maxim as the most obvious one: a prudent man sometimes bears injuries by giving thanks and by inciting against a more powerful avenger, which, to be sure, would scarcely have been a maxim true in the stricter moral sense: the fable is indeed narrated, but a truer maxim is ascribed to it, though too general:

*Success calls many down to their ruin.*

[p. 393] § 603. This moral truth of the maxim is the primary thing to which attention must be paid in the verisimilitude of fables, § 600; to which, however, secondary things can be joined, a tenth question: whether a fable which, adorned more strictly with the title of narration, ought not to proceed beyond historical fictions, nevertheless falls into poetic licence? Whether the same, skilfully observing the laws of historical fictions, can in a weightier matter deceive to such a point that it is taken for most strictly true by many, to their notable detriment? § 527, 587. An example of a fable which both lacks dogmatic truth, § 601, and in which both the faults inhere which we have already indicated, may be Philostratus in his fable about Apollonius of Tyana.

§ 604. The eleventh question: whether a fable, even a poetic one, § 527, if there seemed to be need of it, could not have expressed the dogmatic truth of the maxim just as well while more strictly probable as while more strictly improbable? § 532. Equal goodness here is equal beauty of the whole, which those enjoying historical probability will not always obtain in preference to those in which a more verisimilar probability, though only poetic, shines forth. § 533. Whether a fable, even one beautifully and rightly improbable, nevertheless contains no improbabilities except as many and such as are required for sufficiently illustrating the maxim? § 589. [p. 394] This seems to have been the reason why Longinus turned the love of fables into a fault not of the Iliad, a fable no less poetic and, in my sense, even more strictly improbable, but of the Odyssey, § 311.

§ 605. The twelfth question will not consist chiefly in this, whether some poetic fable and fabulous fiction is more rightly rational than some feigned apologue, and more beautifully so, especially in this age of ours, in which some measure of intellect and reason is once again, with truth aesthetic enough, attributed to animals other than man, or whether in the end the purely rational is more right than the mixed, § 537. Let it rather be this: whether to feigned persons, even to those which we otherwise reckon brutes, nay, vegetables, there is nevertheless attributed their own verisimilar ἦθος [character], which suits them either from the analogy of the poetic world, or from the insufficient traditions of natural history, or from the opinions of those for whose sake we chiefly write? § 495.

§ 606. In the rational fable of the Aeneid, pious Aeneas, sustaining the ἦθος [character] of the person which the poet once imposed upon him, first comes on the scene thus, B. I, 96:

*Straightway the limbs of Aeneas are loosened with cold,* / [p. 395] *He groans, and stretching both palms to the stars* / *Utters such words aloud: O three and four times blessed,* / *Whose lot it was to meet death before their fathers’ faces, beneath the high walls of Troy!*

and he so concludes the final scene that he was even beginning to be gentle towards his enemy Turnus, when

*on his high shoulder appeared the ill-fated* / *Belt, and the sword-belt flashed with its familiar studs,* / *Of the boy Pallas, whom Turnus had laid low with a wound,* / *Conquered, and was bearing on his shoulders the hostile emblem.* / *He, after he had drunk in with his eyes the memorials of cruel grief* / *And the spoils, kindled with fury and terrible* / *In his wrath: Are you, clothed in the spoils of my own,* / *To be snatched from me here? Pallas with this wound, Pallas* / *Sacrifices you, and takes the penalty from your criminal blood.* / *So saying, he buries the steel in the breast that faces him,* / *Burning: but the other’s limbs are loosened with cold,* / *And his life with a groan flees indignant beneath the shades.*

The groan and the limbs loosened with cold belong to Aeneas as he begins, and, in his enemy, as he ends; but pious towards the gods, his country and his own, Aeneas, always consistent with himself, remains one and the same, § 605.

[p. 396] § 607. The thirteenth question: whether in fables that are complex, which seem to be called connected with this one simply and by eminence, § 535, that charming connexion is observed by which all things are prepared for the catastrophe and the resolution, more remotely already in the very knotting, more closely in the transition, in such a way that this comes about, unforeseen indeed to the unwary, yet not without verisimilitude, by so grafting the past onto the present that the future, though most unexpected, nevertheless appears to be born from it, and all these things preserve that pleasant unity by which nothing of what is thought seems to be lacking, and after the resolution has been made nothing further seems able to be desired? § 437, 439. Nor is there here less care for moral verisimilitude, § 604, and for a certain analogy in the very imposing of names, § 594.

*But if it is not permitted to use the same persons as others,* / *How is it any more permitted to write running slaves,* / *To make good matrons, wicked courtesans,* / *A greedy parasite, a boastful soldier,* / *A child substituted, an old man deceived by his slave,* / *To love, to hate, to suspect?* Ter. Eun. prol.

Nay rather, not only is it permitted, but it is also often necessary, § 433.

§ 608. The fourteenth question: whether the maxim is grand enough for divine examples to be subsumed under it without a gross anthropomorphism, such as would seem to be one even to the analogue of reason? [p. 397] whether the examples of divine things so conduct themselves that from them, by an argument from the lesser to the greater, divine things can be beautifully inferred by the analogue of reason? Whether in both cases everything is truly as it should be, according to the exact judgement of the analogue of reason, θεοπρεπῶς [in a manner befitting the gods]. § 435, 399. In Phaedrus, b. III. f. 17, one may first ask whether it is fitting to mix divine things into Aesopic fables? But let us leave that aside. Then, to Minerva wondering *why the gods chose for themselves barren trees,*

*Which they once wished to be under their protection,* / *Jupiter gave the reason:* / *Lest we seem to sell the fruit for the honour.*

Minerva prefers the olive on account of its fruit, and corrects her opinion, and now thus holds

*The begetter of gods and sower of men:* / *Unless what we do is useful, the glory is foolish.*

§ 609. The maxim of the said fable is stated:

*The little fable warns us to do nothing that is not useful.*

Now then it is asked whether this is declared by the feigned example otherwise than by supposing, through Jupiter’s own later concerns, that the glory had been foolish, while

*The oak pleased Jupiter* / *And the myrtle Venus, the laurel Phoebus,* / *The pine Cybele, the lofty poplar Hercules,*

and that there had been an empty colour in that grand reason and aetiology which [p. 398] Jupiter had first adduced, which is indeed so great that many of the interpreters have not even grasped it, and have laboured in vain to change the reading: therefore we make our trees barren, leaving the fruit-bearing ones to mortals, lest we seem to sell them fruit in return for the divine worship to be rendered to us. Meanwhile the trees that are told of are nevertheless under the protection of the gods who are mentioned, throughout the poetic world, down to this very day. How many verisimilitudes are observed to be lacking at once? § 608. But where would you not uncover disgraceful things of this kind, as soon as you have departed from true religion?

§ 610. The fifteenth thing is asked, whether, beyond the verisimilitudes common to it with other fables, a Milesian tale, especially one of love, now blind, now thoroughly clear-sighted, has everywhere preserved truth and verisimilitude, especially moral, in the broader sense, § 433, 495? Whether, as to the maxims which it sets out to illustrate — sayings of praiseworthy persons, and such as pronounce only in point of fact something which is wont to befall him who

*Either fears sweet loves, or experiences bitter ones*

— it has sufficiently distinguished them, at least implicitly, through the course of the feigned narration and the outcome itself, and the characters of the persons who use them as practical principles? § 549.

§ 611. The maxims of Milesian tales that are more charmingly [p. 399] practical, § 610, those namely which are the final end and aim of the whole work, come back to these two most general formulas, either that of Venus:

*Now let him love who has never loved, and let him who has loved, now love!*

or this one about a good many loves, no less true, even aesthetically:

Let him not love who has not yet loved, and let him who has loved, not love.

Hence, according as the author has taken as his primary maxim a paraenetic or an elenctic one, § 576, he must scrupulously observe moral verisimilitude even in the stricter sense, § 435, so that he commends nothing repugnant to good morals, and dissuades from nothing by teaching through an example what virtue, considered according to § 211, 214, either altogether demands or at least does not censure, except when it is peevish, § 467, 470.

§ 612. Let the sixteenth question close the ranks: whether the heroic fable, beyond the verisimilitude common to it with other fables, strenuously preserves this one proper to itself, by which the person of the chief hero always remains consistent with himself and one, having indeed at some time undergone an ἄνεσις [relaxation] and some remission, yet sublime even in this, that of his own accord he collects himself and is raised again to his former and eminent glory. S. XXI, XXVI, § 433, 538. Aeneas at Carthage seemed to Jupiter himself to be *lingering,*

[p. 400] Whom *no glory of great things kindles,* / *Nor does he himself undertake any labour for his own praise,*

yet not so very long after he had himself again so shaken the slumber from his eyes that he could magnificently, yet without incurring the charge of grandiloquence, exhort Ascanius, b. XII. 435:

*Learn, boy, virtue from me, and true labour,* / *Fortune from others.* / — — — — / *Let your father Aeneas and your uncle Hector rouse you.*

§ 613. Rightly distinguishing aesthetic truth, S. XXVII, from aesthetic falsity, S. XXVIII, exhibiting it more charmingly in verisimilitudes, S. XXVIIII, through various fictions, S. XXX, and these sometimes poetic, S. XXXI, not without fables, S. XXXII, which it narrates, feigns, nay, is deemed to tell as fables, where they need proofs, if it proves beautifully, S. XXXIII, following its own pursuit of truth everywhere, S. XXXIIII, whatever species of verisimilitude it may follow, S. XXXV, even then, when it becomes a poet, S. XXXVI, charmingly coming forth: he will be,

*In my judgement, no mean author* / *Of nature and of truth,* § 104. Hor.

## Aestheticorum Pars Altera. — Aesthetics, Second Part.

WRITTEN BY ALEXANDER GOTTLIEB BAUMGARTEN, PROFESSOR OF PHILOSOPHY.

Frankfurt on the Oder,

at the expense of Johann Christian Kleyb, 1758.

### Praefatio. — Preface

Meaning to redeem the pledge which I had given in the preface when I published the first part of the Aesthetics, I was returning, not so very long after it had been made public, in my spare hours to the web which I had begun and which the synopsis then prefixed briefly indicates, so as to weave it through more carefully. The printer followed my writing without delay, and we had quickly enough got so far that what must now be offered to readers was finished, when an illness threw me on my bed, so grave and so stubborn that I often seemed given up for lost not only to myself and my own people but even to the most skilled physicians. Long enough, I think, has the little work hung interrupted, and it would hang still, if I thought it fair to resist the bookseller, who has patiently borne the delay so far and who now can the less be persuaded to hold back any longer what he already has in print, since the state of my health is not yet such as to promise strength fit for the writing of books of this kind rather than death. I am not unaware that authors who tell of their own affairs, as if the public cared about them, are generally tiresome. I only wished to give, in three words, the reasons why I have not touched even the not yet wholly exhausted matter of aesthetic certainty, the life of charming cognition, its sweetest beauty, lucid method, and that elegant semiotics which in beautiful speeches is usually called elocution and delivery, and why there is no hope of advancing a step into the practical part of the Aesthetics, which I once called the second, and which I had already drafted piece by piece in the dictations of my lecture course, which had furnished the nearer occasion for these meditations. Yet if any of you survive, friendly reader, who care for me, who know me, who, in short, love me, learn from others’ fortune, and from mine, who now for the eighth year wander through windings of sickness that seem inextricable, how necessary it is to grow accustomed in good time to thinking well about the best things. For what I should do, being as I now am, unable to do this with all my might, I truly do not know. Given at Frankfurt on the Oder, on the second day before the Kalends of April, 1758.

### Synopsis. — Synopsis

e. Aesthetic light.

א) in general, S. XXXVII, § 614–630.

ב) in particular

α) obscurity, its opposite. S. XXXVIII, § 631–653.

β) aesthetic shade. S. XXXVIIII, § 654–665.

γ) the right dispensation of light and shade. S. XXXX, § 666–687.

δ) aesthetic colours. S. XXXXI, § 688–703.

ε) cosmetic, its opposite. S. XXXXII, § 704–729.

ζ) illustrating arguments

A) in general, S. XXXXIII, § 730–733.

B) in particular,

a) comparison in the broader sense. § 734.

I) assimilation, § 735–741.

II) comparison of the greater and the lesser, S. XXXXIIII, § 742–762.

III) antithesis, S. XXXXV, § 763–772.

IIII) comparison in the stricter sense, S. XXXXVI, § 773–779.

b) tropes, S. XXXXVII, § 780–807.

η) aesthetic wonder-working, S. XXXXVIII, § 808–828.

f. Aesthetic persuasion

א) in general

α) persuasion itself, S. XXXXVIIII, § 829–846.

β) the evidence which it affords, S. L, § 847–854.

ב) in particular

α) confirmation. S. LI, § 855–885.

β) refutation. S. LII, § 886–898.

γ) persuasive arguments. S. LIII, § 899–904.

[p. 401]

### Aestheticorum Generalium. — General Aesthetics.

### Caput I. Heuristica. — Chapter I. Heuristics

#### Sectio XXXVII. Lux Aesthetica. — Section XXXVII. Aesthetic Light

§ 614. One who is studious of truer beauty in thinking, § 18, § 17, and of its elegances, will in the fourth place, § 115, 177, 423, pursue LIGHT, clarity and perspicuity in all that he thinks, M. § 531, but AESTHETIC light, such as suffices even for the analogue of reason to perceive the distinguishing features of a thing, § 22, M. § 531. Quintilian VIII. 2, luminously commending perspicuity as one of the first virtues of eloquence, most rightly distinguishes *perspicuity in words*, which is not of this place, § 13, from *perspicuity of things*, by which the objects [p. 402] of a charming meditation are *lucid and open even to those who listen* and attend *carelessly*.

§ 615. If here one is pleased to call him too a *careless* attender *or hearer* who does not strain too stiffly the powers and sinews, so to speak, of his understanding and reason in perceiving the things that are treated, I gladly go over to Quintilian’s opinion. And I could almost persuade myself that this was the best of rhetoricians’ meaning, since he adds as the reason for his assertion that *the attention of the judge* (spectator, reader, hearer) *is not always so keen that he himself dispels the obscurity for himself, and brings into the darkness of the speech some light of his own intelligence*. But if one is pleased to call a *careless hearer* or attender only him who, *called away by many thoughts* and wholly distracted, is either turning over in his mind things utterly different from what you are thinking, or is drunk, yawning, and next door to sleep; in short, him who neither will nor can turn even the analogue of reason to your thoughts: then, even if some things which you must think out with greater charm are less open to such a man, I would not yet concede universally that they are on that account destitute of beautiful light.

§ 616. That everything we meditate according to the rules of elegance ought to be *so clear that the speech bursts into the mind* of the hearer *as the sun into the eyes, even if he* [p. 403] *is not straining towards it*, I should deny, not without reason, § 615. Let it be *the care of an advocate in a cause, not that* the judge *may be able to understand, but that he may be wholly unable not to understand*. Not everyone who is to think charmingly is the advocate of a cause, nor is everyone whom he rightly regards as his chief personal object a judge of the kind that the Roman judges-delegate used to be.

§ 617. Truth itself bids us distinguish AESTHETIC LIGHT and perspicuity into ABSOLUTE, necessary to all beautiful thinking, § 614, and COMPARATIVE, that degree of the absolute which is to be poured over only certain things that are to be meditated with greater charm, of which we saw an example in § 616. The intensification of clarity through distinctness, adequacy, depth and, as it were, purity of intelligence, M. § 637, is not at all aesthetic light, § 614, and hence neither absolute nor comparative, but logical. Hence it is not aimed at directly, as a primary end, by one who is to think beautifully, § 14, although it may sometimes be of use to him in the course of charming thinking, if he has made it his own concerning the theme, § 551, and indirectly, when not so few parts of the theme shine out in sensate light, an extensively distinct concept of the whole, M. § 634, is at the same time obtained for the beautiful intellect, M. § 637.

§ 618. [p. 404] All aesthetic light, therefore, which you aim at directly in things, will be the sensate perspicuity of things, the extension of clarity through a multitude of marks, § 617, also the absolute; the comparative, however, will be the brilliance and splendour of vivid thoughts and of the matter, M. § 531. Tacitus, Ann. XIII. 3, ascribes *a vivid mind* to *Nero*, who *from his boyhood years at once was engraving, painting, practising song or the management of horses, and sometimes composing verses* (what a hodgepodge of various occupations!). Pliny, ep. III. 1, thinks that *Spurinna* has *an agile and vivid body* from this, that not only, *so long as it was honourable, had he discharged his offices, held magistracies, governed provinces, and earned his leisure by much labour*, but also *as an old man he walked three miles in the morning and exercised his mind no less than his body; presently he mounted his carriage*, etc. Where there is again a wonderful variation of motions and a changing of the daily manner of life.

§ 619. Hence I seem to myself rightly entitled to call those thoughts at last vivid in which a certain peculiar variety is detected, and a sudden, as it were, rapidity of marks pressing one upon another, from whose eminent diffusion that brilliance and splendour of a meditation rises up in a part, the whole of which must nevertheless be perspicuous and absolutely clear: thus Quintilian, [p. 405] IIII. 1, although he rightly demands that the exordium itself be *lucid*, § 617, nevertheless admits only *certain passages, when minds are more conciliated and already warm, whose natural richness does not allow* some *licences to be noticed, for the brilliance poured round them*. As he here venerates natural richness as, so to say, the mother of brilliance, so Cicero, de claris or. 36, ascribes to *Hyperides, Aeschines*, etc., *this copiousness, that sap and uncorrupted blood, in which there was a natural brilliance, not a cosmetic one*, § 104, 115.

§ 620. Let not the brilliant vividness of a charming meditation be confused with its ardour and life, § 22, of which more carefully hereafter. They are rightly and beautifully joined, as often as it can be done, so that thoughts not only shine but also burn, § 142, 143, cf. Quint. VIII. 3. Yet by their nature these graces in thinking are disjoined, and are to be weighed separately through an accurate theory of them. One is also not rarely found without the other. In this way Cicero, de legibus I. 6, relates that *Antipater blew somewhat more vehemently* than his predecessors, and that *he had powers rustic indeed and* rough (not destitute of the force of moving and of life), *yet without brilliance and polish*, § 619, 256.

§ 621. The same Cicero, de finibus IIII. 5, though perhaps not about to object to Cato [p. 406] that this Stoic theory of politics does not move him and impel him to deserve well of the commonwealth as much as his own Academic one does himself, nevertheless freely objects to Cato that the Stoics *define and divide political matters rather squalidly*, while *the discourse of the Academics* on the same matters *shines*, since *they have written much about the commonwealth, much about laws, and have left behind many not only precepts in the arts, but also examples of speaking well in their speeches*. From this passage it suffices me now to draw out the true notion of brilliance; Cicero’s objection, however, I do not make my own, § 251, 252. For our purpose let us finally compare the same author, de claris or. 238, where he describes *a very brilliant and a plainly rough speech* as fighting front to front.

§ 622. We have now twice seen that brilliance is required to be natural, not cosmetic, § 619. Nor would an admonition seem more necessary about any one of the primary elegances of thoughts than about this brilliance of charming meditation, § 22, since in none is it easier to deflect from the right imitation of nature. *And likewise those who are captivated by show think that there is more beauty in men who are plucked and smoothed, who curl their hair with the branding-iron and are glossy with a colour not their own, than uncorrupted nature can bestow, so that the beauty of the body seems to come from bad morals*, Quint. II. 5. Now brilliance in thinking will be [p. 407] natural, 1) when the natural endowments of the mind, not twisted with violence, not strained too vehemently, suffice to grasp it, so that a meditation flowing *according to nature* may *seem to have nothing of contrivance in it*, § 104.

§ 623. Brilliance in thinking will be natural, 2) when it is so fitted, accommodated and, as it were, proper to the materials and the things of which you think, that it is found rather to spring necessarily from them and from the thinking of them than to be brought into them and smeared on for the sake of adornment. Hence a friend of elegance, if he *despairs that any things handled can shine* by their internal and inborn resources, *leaves* them rather than force them to glitter with foreign pigments; 3) when it is so proportioned to the natural capacity, horizon, wit, genius, disposition, the very eyes, so to speak, of those for whose sake above all you think, that it can be imbibed by them without fatigue, not dazzling them, yet sating them with light, illuminating them lovingly, not burning them uncomfortably. Those who do not sufficiently attend to this third requisite of natural brilliance deny that it is found anywhere, when, for instance, they read thoughts little in accord with their own nature, their way of thinking and feeling, their genius, their principal studies, although they ought to grant that they are not the ones whom above all the author had resolved either to teach or to [p. 408] delight. § 104, cf. Cic. de or. I. 81.

§ 624. *In paintings some are delighted by the rough, the unpolished, the retired and the dark; others, on the contrary, by the brilliant, the cheerful, the well-lit. What is there by which you could express any rule or formula, when each excels in its own kind, and the kinds are many? I have not been driven back from this attempt by such a scruple, and I have judged that in all things there is something best*, (most beautiful) *even if it lies hidden* (from many), *and that this can be pointed out by one who is expert in the matter*. Cic. orator. 36. If in aesthetic matters some are delighted by things destitute of the absolute light of beauty, § 617, absolutely rough, unpolished, retired and dark, they are deceived, and for all I care let them embrace a cloud in place of Juno. But in every charm in general, as in a painting, provided that all things are conspicuous in the light we have called absolute, not all, but only some are comparatively lucid,

*they shine with a flowery face*, / *as the white feverfew*, / *or the yellow poppy*, Cat.

some are truly, are prettily perspicuous, although, when they are compared with those brilliant and thoroughly illuminated things, they appear dark, and at first glance would be judged rough, § 621. Elegantly, not all, but

*more things shine in the poem*. Hor.

§ 625. [p. 409] If we call a MANNER OF THINKING not destitute of absolute aesthetic light SIMPLY LUCID and perspicuous (in the sensate way), § 617, just as one shining also with comparative light BRILLIANT and splendid, we follow Quintilian going before us, VIII. 3, when he says: *The reward of those who speak correctly and lucidly*, § 619, *is slight, and it is more a matter of being free from faults than of seeming to have attained some great virtue*. — — — — *Cicero would not have achieved, by merely instructing the judge, and by instructing him usefully in the end, and in good Latin and perspicuously*, what we saw, § 282, that he achieved *by the brilliance of flashing arms*. *But let this ornament* (*for one may repeat it*, § 622) *be manly, strong and chaste; let it love neither effeminate smoothness nor a colour made prominent by cosmetic; let it shine with blood and strength*, § 619. *And this is so true that, since in this most important part the vices lie close to the virtues*, § 622, *even those who use the vices nevertheless give them the name of virtue. Wherefore let none of the corrupt say that I am an enemy of those who speak with cultivation. I do not deny that this is a virtue; but I do not attribute it to them.*

§ 626. Horace laughs at the ape of natural splendour, cosmetic, in examples, A. P. 25.

*Often, to grave beginnings and great professions*, / *a purple patch or two is stitched on, to shine far and wide*, / [p. 410] *when the grove and altar of Diana*, / *and the windings of hurrying water through pleasant fields*, / *or the river Rhine, or the rainbow is described*. / *But this was not the place for these things*.

Here you have the stork for ill-starred painters of this kind, who choose themselves a subject pretty enough, and from which you might hope for things not only singularly beautiful but also distinguished by novelty, concerning which the author himself promises things almost greater than your hope; but when it comes to the point, the blind herd of imitators does not know how to set in full light anything new which the nature of the things in question would most readily supply, does not know how to illuminate what would require special splendour, but, neglecting unity, twists the attention aside to some other thing wholly different, or less connected, brought into the ill-cohering work only so that this thing, already painted in brilliance by truly beautiful wits, may now be painted again, if the gods please, more brilliantly, in truth with some obscure and Asiatic apparatus, I know not what. § 325, 623.

*No one’s own house is better known to him than to me the grove* / *of Mars, and the cave of Vulcan near the Aeolian* / *rocks. What the winds are doing, what shades Aeacus* / *is tormenting, whence another is carrying off the gold of the stolen* / [p. 411] *fleece, how huge the ash-trees that Monychus hurls*, / *the plane-trees of Fronto and the convulsed marbles cry out* / *continually, and the columns broken by the unceasing reciter*. / *You may expect the same from the greatest and the least poet*. Juv. I. 8.

§ 627. Other painters from among the least, of whom Juvenal speaks, are deceived by the bold sayings of certain great wits, such as we noted at § 125, such as Cicero’s in the proem of the Paradoxes: *Nothing is so rough*, § 621, *so unpolished, that it may not shine with speech and be, as it were, cultivated*. For they do not perceive that leading men of this kind, *relying* on their own powers, if we look more deeply into what they assert, *have laid down something false* about the *duty* of a man who thinks charmingly, *and have expounded, not what the art, but what they themselves* perhaps *could do*. Hence the pretty little men of whom we speak, the micrologists, § 356, generally thinking themselves something remarkable, conclude that it is now their business to fulfil the prophecies of those men and to dare, with Minerva unwilling but Fortune helping, what a certain Cicero, sometimes nodding, has declared can be done. So they seek out materials which they can devise as by their nature admitting the least splendour, and clothe them, even against the will of all the gods, with so many foreign flowerets, so many ornaments, that they indeed present to the chief of their spectators a theme not unlike [p. 412] the jackdaw in Aesop strutting in borrowed plumes, yet always rejoice in their hearts that they have thought the better, the less these very spectators confess that they could have drawn natural pleasure from the picture set before them, even if they were ignorant of the art. § 623, 119–121.

§ 628. Nor will only the pygmy of Helicon, such by nature, scarcely, and indeed not even scarcely, distinguish the cosmetic adventitiously added to things prettily thought from truly natural splendour, § 359: for mediocre wits too the same will be most difficult, when a bad habit contracted in adolescence counterfeits truly elegant nature, § 622, and the injuries of the times and the barbarism of the places in which they have lived, covering over the inmost nature of the most beautiful things, compel them always to stick in the bark, and a judgement sufficiently depraved among their contemporaries by the same fates has grown used to venerating only cosmetic graces as the daughters of nature herself, § 622, 623. Meanwhile a happier wit in aesthetic matters, S. II–VII, like Caesar in Cicero, de claris or. 261, *applying reason*, § 38, *corrects a vicious and corrupt usage by a pure and uncorrupted usage*, and when to a lucid elegance of thoughts everywhere, § 625, *he adds* certain singular *ornaments, then he seems to set well-painted pictures in a good light, and holds* a certain *splendid, and by no means crafty, manner* of thinking.

§ 629. [p. 413] Since the brilliance and splendour of thoughts is a species, indeed a certain degree, of sensate perspicuity, the logico-dogmatic manner of thinking lacks it just as well as the aesthetico-dogmatic manner lacks the more intensive degrees of intellectual perspicuity, § 617, 567. The more formally philosophical and scientific that manner of thinking is, and the more pervious and perspicuous to a more solid reason and a purer intellect, the less brilliance will it have, and the less perspicuity even to a translucent analogue of reason, § 578. The more vivid splendour a dogmatic manner of thinking has, the less is it to be referred to logic, and the more to aesthetics, § 568. Hence, against certain hodgepodge-makers among the moderns, Cicero himself judges the manner of thinking of his own philosophical books to be aesthetico-dogmatic, when in de fin. I. 6 he says: *What reason have they to prefer Greek works to those* (my writings on philosophy) *which are both splendidly expressed and are not translated from the Greek?* He expounds his mind still more openly in the proem of the Paradoxes, where he says: *You will taste this kind of my exercises, which I have been accustomed to use, when I transfer the things which are called in the schools* θετικά [thetic, general questions] *to this our oratorical manner of speaking*. § 569.

§ 630. *For my part*, with Cicero, off. I. 6, *I judge* [p. 414] *that Demosthenes, if he had held to what he had learned from Plato and had wished to declaim it, could have done so ornately and splendidly*, § 567. But if he had done it ornately, if he had done it splendidly, if in the philosophical manner of disputation he had shown himself a Demosthenes, then in my opinion he would not so much have furnished reason and the intellect with evidence, so far as can be done, as have illuminated the analogue of reason with a vast light of persuasion. § 629, 568.

#### Sectio XXXVIII. Obscuritas Aesthetica. — Section XXXVIII. Aesthetic Obscurity

§ 631. Since *obscurity* is the defect and opposite of light and clarity, while light and clarity are either sensate or intellectual, S. XXXVII, the ancients already most rightly distinguished obscurity κατ᾽ αἴσθησιν [according to sense] from obscurity κατὰ νόησιν [according to understanding]. A thing and a thought which, when to be perceived in a sensate way, does not have enough clarity, extensive clarity that is, and aesthetic light, is obscure κατ᾽ αἴσθησιν. Hence whatever is more distinct, purer, deeper by way of the sciences, since the analogue of reason does not attain it, if one wishes to consider it by this alone, one will not only complain that it is obscure, but will also feel it to be so, as it is, κατ᾽ αἴσθησιν.

§ 632. [p. 415] A thing and a thought which, when to be conceived through the intellect and reason, does not have as much clarity, intensive clarity that is, distinctness, purity and depth, as is required for distinguishing from clear marks and well-examined grounds, or for pronouncing in a more thoroughly analysed way, or for demonstrating completely, is obscure κατὰ νόησιν. Hence if anyone has laboured to conceive more distinctly, to search out more deeply with reason, a good many things which are not only aesthetically lucid but also splendid with beautiful brilliance, just as they are proffered to the analogue of reason alone, he will often not only complain that they are obscure, but will also feel it to be so, as they are, κατὰ νόησιν.

§ 633. As the praise of perspicuity and light is a primary one, so, to be sure, the blame of the obscurity truly opposed to this praiseworthy clarity is disgraceful, § 23. This, too, is an obscurity either of things and thoughts, or of signs, words and phrases. We are now concerned only with the first, § 614, and with that species of it which they once called κατ᾽ αἴσθησιν, § 631. Let there be, in the course of a charming meditation, not so few things obscure κατὰ νόησιν, whose accurate definition is hard to find, whose genesis lies hidden at the bottom, whose connection is but little evidently perceived by reason; a truly beautiful wit does not trouble about this obscurity, which is referred almost to the pure sciences alone, [p. 416] § 632, provided that it be granted it to escape AESTHETIC OBSCURITY, the defect of aesthetic light in its own things (the things and thoughts) which are to be examined by the laws of elegance. § 632, 614.

§ 634. Aesthetic obscurity will be either the defect of the light that is absolute in aesthetic matters, § 617, which let us call AESTHETIC DARKNESS, and sheer night; or of the comparative light only, of some brilliance and eminent splendour, which again will either arise through an exception that is not inelegant, § 25, an only apparent defect, AESTHETIC SHADE, or will be a defect truly such and a privation of the vividness required for true beauty, and will give a PLAINLY ROUGH MANNER OF THINKING, along with aesthetic darkness, § 621. Nor will he who is everywhere diligently to think beautifully shudder only at night itself, but also, no less than those for whose sake he becomes an Aristarchus, he will compel certain rather rough things, lucid indeed, § 625, but comparatively not without blemish

*and too little clear, to give light*,

while shades he will now follow, now flee, according to the advantages of the form, § 614.

§ 635. Under the name of shades we comprise all those artifices, imitating nature, by which certain things [p. 417] in the course of charming thinking are not set in so great a light, are not spread out with so great an extensive clarity, as could be done by those ignorant of charming parsimony, § 164; e.g. when things less connected with the theme are only shown as if from afar, gradually decreasing in magnitude, as in optics, as if through a mist. For thus by the nature of things a portico, supported on columns equally spaced and equal in size,

*though long, if it is seen whole from its upper end*, / *gradually draws the tapering of a narrow cone*, / *joining the roof to the ground and all the right side to the left*, / *until it has contracted into the obscure point of the cone*, Lucr. IIII. 430.

Thus through *the obscurity of beginning light* the brief noon is gradually unfolded, and between it and midnight stands the intermediate *obscure light after the setting of the sun*.

§ 636. A disgraceful obscurity in things to be thought beautifully will be begotten by the excessive 1) intension of such attention as you can expect from the analogue of reason among your chief spectators, when you counterfeit some popular manner of thinking, yet in truth you conceive things, and in that way communicate them to the same people, which, to be properly grasped, already require a great and learned use of the intellect and of reason, and which seem fit to be left to the horizon [p. 418] of reason alone, § 121, 615, 622; 2) if its extension and 3) its protension be attempted, M. 628, while things already sufficiently open and lucid for the ends of charm either 1) are nevertheless surrounded with yet new splendour, dazzling the eyes, § 633, with a vividness and a variation of very many marks that distracts the mind from the beautiful consideration of the whole, of the theme and of its primary parts, § 629, M. § 638, or 2) by a luxuriant diffusion, § 165, are illuminated with ever new and newer comments for so long that the mind, wearied by the mass of things and thoughts, grows faint and is rather buried under a kind of forest than refreshed, § 623.

§ 637. Cicero seems to touch at once the second and third genesis of vicious obscurity, de Inu. II. 156, saying: *We should not have been reluctant to supply an example of each kind of issue, had we not seen that, just as obscure things become clearer by speaking, so clear things become obscurer by speech*. For what? Those examples, illuminating in other contexts, he would either have adorned, short enough as they were, with the most flashing lights, and in this place they would have dazzled; or he would have expounded them drawn out at length in a long series, and here they would have wearied the eyes of the spectators, § 636.

§ 638. A more disgraceful aesthetic obscurity will arise, 4) [p. 419] if someone has either undertaken to explain elegantly a theme absolutely and in itself obscure, some chimera and a mere nothing that seems to be something, or at any rate a thing relatively so obscure, not only to the grasp of the spectators but also to that of the author himself, that he himself attains it neither by the senses and the imagination nor by the force of the intellect, and yet promises to expound it to others. To this belongs that *great interpretation of dreams by Antiphon, and that not natural*, § 622, 623, *but artificial*, § 110. *And in the same way* a good many lies *of oracles and prophecies*, and many *explanations* even of true ones, *in which many things are obscure and ambiguous*. Cic. de diu. I. 116, cf. II. 115.

§ 639. There are two kinds of those who try in vain to discourse, prettily nonetheless, about good things, perspicuous in themselves, of which they are wholly ignorant. The first is more sincere and simpler, and therefore its disgrace is more easily detected: those who stupidly persuade themselves that the business is well done whenever, concerning a thing which they know as well as the most ignorant, they boldly begin to pour out with great gapings signs without mind, though not without a stream of words, to expound the obscure by the obscurer, to illuminate twilight with darkness, which no one else can understand, since they themselves did not understand their own thoughts while thinking them. The other is more underhand and, as it were, practised in trickery, which in the same [p. 420] ignorance of the best things, with the same impudence, sets about a charming meditation, forsooth, on anything whatever, but with a sufficiently cunning design so covers the darkness of its own mind as conveniently to shift the blame for its ignorance on to the very matter and thing in question, and to grant its spectators, some of whom it is not difficult to find equally ignorant of the things, that the reason why those things cannot be understood is to be sought not in themselves but in the things to be thought. For thus, while an author of this kind cherishes and excuses the blemishes of his own people, he will depart from them not only excused in turn, but even often praised, as the only one who has seen the darkness of things, about which others, who wish to seem more learned, lie when they compare them to the noonday sun. § 638.

§ 640. Of this obscurity, § 636, 637, Cicero’s books on the nature of the gods offer us not one example only; in book III. 94 of them *Cotta makes an end of speaking*, saying: *This is roughly what I had to say on the nature of the gods, not in order to abolish it, but that you might understand how obscure it is, and how difficult the explanations it admits*. For since throughout their whole course the questions of a most evident natural religion are wretchedly confounded with Graeco-Roman superstitions, most of which the Stoics, under evil omens, were taking under the guardianship of philosophy, [p. 421] § 636, all of which, but together with the true anticipations of the truest religion, the Epicureans laughed at, at least in their sleeve, § 637: Cotta the Academic, while he assails them, now rightly and wittily laughs at the obscurity of the Porch, whenever these men were rattling off interpretations of dreams, § 638; now well censures the empty windings and impudent audacity of the Epicureans, when these, careless enough of divine things, nevertheless expounded at length the quasi-flesh and quasi-blood of the gods and the leisure of the intermundane spaces, lest they should seem to have said nothing at all about the gods; but now, *inveighing also more vehemently against that account which has been most holily and most providently established concerning Providence*, he cunningly feigns many things to be obscure in themselves which are open to anyone acknowledging the truer religion. § 639.

§ 641. Examples of the more disgraceful obscurity noted in § 638 will be given, labouring now under one, now under both of the diseases spoken of in § 639, by many chemists of old, especially the alchemists, to whom all things are secrets, especially the philosophers’ stone, and by many mystics among the moderns too, whenever, not content with the truly divine, they devise new mysteries for themselves and their own, every one of them counterfeiting magic and a nocturnal commerce with demons, if

*obscure in the windings of strange words* / [p. 422] *she murmurs thrice nine times a spell from her magic mouth*. Ov.

§ 642. One who is anxious to guard against the first cause of aesthetic obscurity, § 636, will refrain from distinguishing from one another all those things which, though each one is lucid, or even brilliant, § 625, are nevertheless, without harm to the beauty of the whole to which they belong, often confounded and mingled by the analogue of reason. Let there be in some charming picture a *Cnidian Gyges*, without harm to the beauty of the whole,

*whom, if you were to slip him into a chorus of girls*, / *the obscure difference would wonderfully deceive the shrewd guests*, / *with his flowing hair* / *and ambiguous face*: Hor. II, ode V. 21.

Do not then play the betrayer and wish to disturb the circles of pleasure; rather cover a superfluous sagacity in distinguishing with convenient shades. § 634, M. 645.

§ 643. The second way of obscurity, which disfigures even the best things, § 636, is rashly entered upon I) by one who thinks better and more highly of his chief spectators than he ought, and attributes to them a more penetrating acumen and a richer power of attending and perceiving than they truly either [p. 423] have or exercise, and so, while he thinks that he will scarcely satisfy such great wits with the small measure of his own wit, crowds and forces together, as far as he can, into one whatever brilliance there is anywhere, and in this way

*for the most part, being modest*, / *he takes on the appearance of the obscure*.

§ 644. The same road into darkness will be entered II. by one who thinks more loftily of himself, of the authority of himself and of his thoughts, than is right, and who hence tacitly demands that his works be read and, like riddles, re-read, remarkable for as many weights as they have words, and that the spectator, lingering on each stroke of his picture, halt and advance neither foot nor eyes any further until, having often turned over in his mind what he had seen and long weighed it, he has at last grasped what they mean. For this reason perhaps *Heraclitus is very obscure, Democritus not at all.* The former earned himself by his apophthegms the name σκοτεινός [the obscure]; the latter’s composed and ornate speech, by the artifice of those who measure the reader’s grasp, § 643, and their own authority, and by its distinct polish, has carried the vote of Cicero, nay, of readers less philosophical than he. § 251.

§ 645. This somewhat obscure fork in the road, § 643, 644, [p. 424] aesthetic dynamics, § 60, will beware of: so reckoning the powers of its principal spectators and the attention they can extend that, in a doubtful case whether these will sufficiently follow one who is to think beautifully and, so to speak, keep pace with his mind, it would rather appear too open than veiled in darkness; and so measuring his authority among the same that, in a doubtful case whether these will repeat ten times the reading of one who thinks beautifully or not, it would rather be understood at once on the first reading than on the last, § 636. Above all the *advocates* of causes have these reckonings rightly most commended to them, by whom a *right obscure and unknown* must sometimes be *laid open in the courts and illuminated*, § 615, 616. Should the author’s modesty, § 643, or his pride and arrogance, § 644, neglect these considerations, it will befit him to complain with Horace:

*I labour to be brief, / I become obscure.*

*The obscurity which follows upon those who cut everything too short must be avoided.* Quintilian IIII. 2.

§ 646. Yet not even with all his breadth could Plato escape every mark of obscurity, not even before a favourable judge, Cicero, quaest. ac. IIII. 123. Of such moment is rounded brevity, § 166, the best antidote against the third cause of obscurity to be avoided, [p. 425] § 636. In the same Cicero, de fin. II. 15, *Epicurus* is reproved, who *is not unwilling, if he can, to speak plainly and openly, nor speaks of an obscure matter, as the physicists,* § 639, *or of an artificial one, as the mathematicians, but of a thing illustrious and easy, and even commonly known to the vulgar,* pleasure, and yet cannot be understood. If there is something true in these objections, which are suspicious enough, since true pleasure is thought to be an easy thing and commonly known to the vulgar: assuredly Epicurus’ aesthetic sin is no other than that in drawing out his practical lectures through writings to be read by anyone he was a little too wordy and too negligent of rounded brevity, whence it came about that few drew the whole theory of pleasure from him with sufficiently sustained attention, while most seized on some part of it as though it were the whole, attending cursorily now to this, now to that scrap from the very many writings of Epicurus. § 636.

§ 647. To stop up the fourth and fouler source of obscurity, § 638, 1) do not dream of adorning beautiful thoughts with anything aesthetically false, with the dreams of the feverish, § 456. For not only the love of truth but also the pursuit of perspicuity calls us away from this, since true aesthetic light will always be lacking to such absolutely obscure things, § 614. 2) In the very fictions [p. 426] of the poetic world do not wish to dwell in utopia, § 514, through which even for the wanderer the road is,

*Such as, beneath an uncertain moon, under a grudging light, / Is a journey in the woods, when Jupiter has hidden the sky in shade / And black night has taken from things their colour.* Virg.
