§ 648. 3) Let it not be your mind to think splendidly of any theme before thinking of it lucidly, § 619, 625. 4) What you cannot even set forth lucidly, do not hope ever to illuminate with natural splendour, § 622, 623, and do not wish to besmear it with cosmetics, § 626, 627. 5) If something seems to you less obscure κατὰ νόησιν [according to the understanding], do not for that very reason, while you are about to think it by the rules of charm, conclude that you have escaped all aesthetic obscurity, § 632, 633. 6) Do not, confusing the perspicuity of your individual concepts with that of the whole you are to set forth, rashly infer from the former to the latter, which is nonetheless chiefly necessary. 7) Do not labour to impute the darkness of your own wit to the things and the materials. § 639.

§ 649. *All cognition is obstructed by many difficulties, and there is both an obscurity in the things themselves* (objective), *and a weakness in our judgements,* (subjective obscurity,) *so that not without cause both the most learned and the most ancient, as to being able to find what they desired,* [p. 427] *have lost confidence.* Cic. quaest. ac. IIII. 7. But where absolute obscurity lies hidden in the things themselves, so that they are in and through themselves inconceivable, these are not to be touched, beyond the limits, § 647. If some material should seem to be such that, though clear and distinct in itself, it nonetheless by its nature cannot even be lucid for us, much less shine: about it I shall not much dispute whether the darkness in which it is enveloped is to be ascribed to the thing itself or rather to the weakness of our judgements. This one thing I urge: that we do not, as though by a protest contrary to the deed, tell many that the thing itself is obscure, and that repeatedly, and yet not rather surround it with fitting shades than vainly attempt to explain or even to illuminate it through empty circumlocutions. § 634, 635.

§ 650. He who would guard against aesthetic obscurity in general does not indeed trouble himself about superfluous subtleties κατ’ αἴσθησιν [according to sense]; he leaves them untouched for those who would dispel all obscurity κατὰ νόησιν [according to the understanding], § 631, 632; nor does he fear to confound things which, the beauty of the whole being preserved, need not be dissected further into fragments, § 642: yet he avoids the confused and PERTURBED MANNER OF THINKING, which knows nothing of charming distinction and analysis. For as soon as you see, concerning the whole of a certain material, so many parts, so many various things to be noticed by [p. 428] aesthetic necessity as, confounded into one and heaped together into the same glance of the spectator, would dazzle his eyes and overwhelm by their mass the power of attending, § 636, 637, a *distinct* array of arguments is required, which presents to the beautiful intellect the whole of the parts shining with successive splendour, § 428, 618; cf. Quintilian IIII. 2.

§ 651. Hence, if it is permitted to draw out one’s thoughts at greater length, e.g. over several water-clocks, and a singular perspicuity is rightly demanded, as from the advocates of causes, § 616, 645, the beautiful intellect is more rightly fed by the light of extensive distinction through the contemplation of the whole, while the analogue of reason is refreshed by parts now stated most plainly, now more adorned with a certain brilliance, § 617, than if you were to follow the manner of meditating of a certain *Fusius* and *Pomponius*, of whom Crassus, in Cicero de or. III. 50, says that *as soon as they have begun to plead, he* does not *understand, unless he has attended most closely, so confused, so perturbed is their speech, that nothing is first, nothing second,* § 650, *so that the speech, which ought to bring light to things, brings obscurity and darkness, and that they seem in a manner to drown themselves out in speaking.*

§ 652. But just as aesthetic light is not [p. 429] that in which the logico-dogmatic manner of thinking chiefly rejoices and by which it is illuminated, § 629, 630: so the obscurity of which readers, labouring to perceive by the analogue of reason alone the pronouncements of reason not written for it, complain in philosophers and mathematicians writing scientifically, as though it were proper and natural to them, is indeed obscurity κατ’ αἴσθησιν [according to sense], § 631, yet not aesthetic, § 633. Nor will a more solid philosophy either attain or defend the evidence of the mathematicians before we assign *obscurities* of this kind *not to the fault of the writers, but to the ignorance* or inadvertence *of those who do not follow them*, with the same consciousness of truth and tranquillity of mind as, not *Caecilius* in Gellius XX. 1 those *most obscure* passages *of the twelve tables*, but the mathematician those shadows for which the common crowd arraigns the dust and the rod.

§ 653. Crassus himself in Cicero, who most broadly extends the bounds of the orator, and hence at the same time of the beautiful wit, when he feigns to dispute against those who would exclude the orator from all learning and from the knowledge of greater things, de or. I. 68: *Since,* he says, *philosophy is divided into three parts, into the obscurity of nature,* § 639, *into the subtlety of discourse, into life and manners: let us relinquish those two, and grant that to our indolence; but the third, which has always belonged to the orator, unless we keep it, we shall leave the orator nothing* [p. 430] *in which he can be great.* For my part I do not so relinquish those two former parts as to deny that a solid knowledge of them is singularly useful to the orator and to everyone who is to think more elegant things more happily, and to some altogether necessary: nor do I so hold the third place, concerning manners and life, as to dare to advise any who would think beautifully to insert into their works bare precepts of living well and happily, as they are comprehended by the intellect and demonstrated by reason, unless they wish for Antony’s applause, § 121. Nor yet would I think it my concern that the philosopher should everywhere think of life and manners in such a way that nothing obscure should seem left over, in meditations more exactly directed to the cynosure of the intellect and of reason, for readers who will bring almost only the analogue of reason to the examination of them, § 652.

#### Sectio XXXVIIII. Umbra Aesthetica. — Section XXXVIIII. Aesthetic Shade

§ 654. The foulness of the aesthetic darkness considered up to this point has brought it about that one from whom, even unawares, something of the somewhat obscure should seem to have slipped out while he prepares to think beautifully would scarcely obtain pardon from many; but one *employing a hiding-place of obscurity*, so that e.g. *the same verses may* [p. 431] *be applied, by design, now to one thing, now to another,* would be condemned by the individual votes of almost all the censors. Yet shades neither arise of themselves, nor are they introduced by the artist into certain things, always under evil omens, § 634. A still greater light, § 617, might be poured over certain things lucidly thought by you, § 625, but only in such a way that rounded brevity, S. VIII–XIIII, fitting dignity and magnitude, S. XV–XXVI, lovable truth, S. XXVII–XXXVI, the perspicuity of the whole to be more elegantly conceived, S. XXXVII, the persuasion of good faith, the motion and incitement of minds that are to be hoped for from it, or several of these graces, § 22, together take more harm from it than the beauty of the whole has grown from the given degree of light and brilliance: then you will either bear a deficiency of comparative light of this kind as arising from nature, or you will altogether see to it by the labour of the file, S. VI, by way of an exception not inelegant. § 25.

§ 655. Let us take one notable example from Cicero’s books on divination. Let some recent author charmingly think the same thing for the men of our age, instructed in the religion of Christ and in our manners. Let him also object to himself, as Cicero does, l. I. 35: *I find no cause.* Will he be judged to have beautifully untied the knot if he answers himself: *Perhaps it lies hidden, wrapped in the obscurity of nature?* § 649. Will he not, by most, [p. 432] by that very thing be held to have confessed that it is some occult quality of the schoolmen? Will he prudently expound in many circumlocutions whatever all Etruria raved about in entrails? Will he, if he is wise, be copious on the significations of lightnings and on the most fallacious interpretations of portents? Will he aptly define what the rumblings, bellowings and movements of the earth have foretold, grave and true, to cities? Or will he rather, leaving all the divination of the ancients in the shades of superstition, better follow the light of nature alone? § 654.

§ 656. If there are any among the critics, of whom § 654, with whose authorities one must contend, § 479, let them unroll their own, nay our, Cicero on these matters, Top. 3, where he relates that *Trebatius* was *repelled by the obscurity* of the *books* of Aristotle’s Topics from reading them, and opines that *philosophers ignorant of Aristotle are the less to be pardoned, because they ought to have been attracted not only by the things said and discovered by him, but also by the incredible copiousness, and sweetness too, of his manner of speaking.* There is therefore a certain obscurity which deters even some learned Trebatius from reading, and yet does not take away an incredible sweetness. This assuredly is not darkness, § 614; it will therefore be aesthetic shade, § 634.

§ 657. I. Things which rounded brevity neither allows to be illuminated, [p. 433] nor to be rejected altogether, are to be shaded, § 654. *For I pass over repetitions, and* ταυτολογίας [tautologies] *and* περισσολογίας [redundancies], *which certain writers on the arts have taught are to be avoided in narration; for they are,* if they are truly idle, *faults* not only in narrations but everywhere, *to be shunned not only for the sake of brevity,* but also because *superfluous things are said with tedium,* and hence withdraw the hearer’s attention, dangerously, even from the necessary. Here rather belongs *the art by which we shall bring it about that* some portion of a fine meditation *be not too long, which we shall be able to do by deferring, yet not without mention of what we defer.* E.g. *What causes he had for killing, whom he took as accomplices, how he arranged the ambush, I shall tell in the place of the proof.* Quintilian IIII. 2.

§ 658. II. Things which, compared with the remaining parts of a beautiful meditation, are not of such moment that an even manner of thinking, § 267, could linger in illuminating them, though out of dignity they cannot be omitted along with trifles, § 191, 193, are to be shaded, § 654. E.g. *As the light of a lamp is obscured and drowned by the light of the sun, and as a drop of brine perishes in the magnitude of the Aegean sea, and as the addition of a farthing in the riches of Croesus, and a single step on that road which runs from here to India: so, since the end of goods is that which the Stoics say, it is necessary that all valuation of things situated in the body* [p. 434] *be obscured and overwhelmed by the splendour and magnitude of virtue, and perish.* Cic. de Fin. III. 45. Hence in the picture of heroic virtue, and among the higher gifts of wit, if some mention is also to be thrown in of the form of the body, the charming dignity of the latter can hardly be thought evenly without some shade, § 186, 299.

§ 659. III. Things which aesthetically exact truth forbids to be passed over, and which the same, § 552, especially moral truth, § 433–436, prohibits to be illuminated, are to be shaded, § 654. Let us remain within a kindred example, § 658. *If you say that some things are obscured, and do not appear, because they are very small,* and so seem rather to be omitted than shaded, *we too concede it,* e.g. *what Epicurus says of pleasure, that those pleasures which are least are often obscured and overwhelmed,* to the point that it is beautiful to be altogether silent about them. *But there are not in that kind such great conveniences of the body, so prolonged in time, and so many,* that for them not even lucidly and plainly, § 625, to be mentioned by the heralds of the virtues would be against exact verisimilitude, especially that of aesthetic things, § 211–215. *Accordingly, in the case of those things in which, because of their smallness, obscuration follows, it often happens that we confess it to be of no concern to us whether they exist or not,* nor of concern to the beauty in the whole whether they are thought or not. — — — — *But in those things in which the obscuration* [p. 435] *is not so great, it can nevertheless happen that the very thing which matters is not great;* hence the beauty of verisimilitude may bid us show things of this kind briefly, as through a lattice. *The goods of the body, however, are more like those things which I placed later. For they have an addition worth labouring for,* though not the worthiest. Cic. de Fin. IIII. 29. 30.

§ 660. IIII. Things which are capable of so much light that, shining in full light, it is to be feared lest they impede the perspicuity, necessarily greatest in a beautiful meditation, of the whole to which they belong, until this has overcome their light, are to be shaded, § 654. *Let the lesser be obscured by the greater.* Cic. de Fin. V. 58. Since, however, the whole is greater than any of its parts: the splendour even of the best part, obscuring the perspicuity of the whole, would disfigure a beautiful meditation. E.g. Suppose you have a slender material, or even one of the middling sort, and on its occasion you slip into the commonplaces of the greatest actions, of honour conceived according to the severer formula of nature, of the cognition of celestial things, by which

*The flaming brilliance even of the swift sun is obscured.* Cat.

of the contemplation of things which, hidden and concealed by nature, reason can track down, of the administration of the commonwealth, and the like, § 642. Beware, [p. 436] lest you heap on these so much of a splendour otherwise natural enough, § 622, 623, that in regarding them your good theme, like the three goats of Posthumus, § 222, is lost from sight or vanishes, § 263, 279.

*As the stars yield at their fixed seasons, / As sweet love, banishing Trivia stealthily beneath the Latmian rocks, / Calls her down from her airy round.* Cat.

§ 661. V. If you suspect that there are impediments to that persuasion which you labour to obtain, which would be set forth too clearly, and yet you cannot altogether pass them by, they are to be shaded, § 654. Thus Cicero in part. et 121 judges that *the supports placed for credence must either be dissolved in themselves, or obscured, or buried under digressions;* he wishes *the narration of the accuser* to be *as it were a suspicious explanation, member by member, of the business transacted, with all the arguments scattered through it and the defences obscured, but* he bids *the defender, with the arguments of suspicion either passed over or obscured, to narrate the outcomes and chances of the things themselves.* Hence perhaps the school of Theodorus denied that it is always useful to set forth lucidly, § 170, not as if it were ever permissible to neglect all aesthetic perspicuity, § 618, but because to present all things in an equal comparative light, § 617, is not equally advantageous to each of the two adversaries. By this same [p. 437] rule Quintilian can be judged, to whom, IIII. 2, *it is ridiculous that in certain causes the truth is to be obscured*, interposing the reason that *he who wishes to obscure narrates falsehoods for truths, and in those things which he narrates as falsehoods for truths, must labour that they appear as evident as possible.* Since to shade some truth and to narrate in its place a contrary falsehood, as though it were true, is by no means one and the same thing; indeed, when you hint at things you dare to put forward in the guise of truths, one need not always prudently labour at all of them, that they appear as evident as possible. § 659.

§ 662. VI. Things which it is not permitted to omit altogether, and yet which you do not hope will excite that commotion of mind which you intend, or will excite one altogether contrary, are to be shaded, § 654. Virgil, foreseeing that little or no veneration for his hero would redound from it, if, creeping stealthily with hastened step into a foreign city, unknown to the queen, unknown to the garrison, he approaches the temple in which he may be safe, and behold!

*He enters, covered by a cloud. Wonderful to tell!* Aen. l. 443.

Will all judge it heroic to see one’s own men dragged into court, and yet neither to approach nor to help by a word, but rather to fear, because

*the unknown thing troubles their minds?*

[p. 438] Hence Aeneas and Achates, while they

*Dissemble, and, wrapped in the hollow cloud, look on,* v. 520.

But the women

After *their spirits were raised by these words, both brave Achates / And father Aeneas,* — — — — / *See all is safe, the fleet and their comrades recovered,*

we shall see face to face once more the hero whom we were seeking, and whom some perhaps were missing. Hence

*the cloud poured round them suddenly / Parts, and clears away into the open air. / Aeneas stood forth, and shone in the clear light,* § 619, / *In face and shoulders like a god. For his mother herself / Had breathed upon her son graceful locks, and the purple / Light of youth, and joyful honours upon his eyes,* § 659, / *Such grace as hands add to ivory, or when silver / Or Parian marble is set round with yellow gold.*

§ 663. We have seen the occasions on which, through a charming meditation,

*there is room for more shades too.* § 654–662.

Since, however, we have seen these called, not ordinary manners of thinking but exceptions, § 654, and even *obscurations*, by [p. 439] the writers on the art, even the best of them: in those thoughts too in which there was no need at all of an exception they are badly grasped at by petty wits, who fall not into them but into mere darkness and a plainly rough manner of thinking, § 634. *Some, while they dread the common manner of speaking,* even then, when true beauty would demand that it be kept, *led by the appearance of brilliance, go round everything they wish to say with copious loquacity, and then, joining and mixing that very series with another like it, extend it beyond what any breath could sustain.* § 636.

§ 664. *Others, rivals of brevity, withdraw from their speech even the necessary words, and, as if it were enough that they themselves know what they wish to say, count as nothing how far it concerns others. But I should call that speech idle which the hearer does not understand by his own wit.* But by the hearer is understood one both sufficiently attentive and such, and of such capacity, as the speaker was bound to assume as his principal personal object, § 615, 616. *But that persuasion has indeed already pervaded many, that they think only that to be elegantly and exquisitely said which needs interpreting. But to some hearers too these things are pleasing, and when they have understood them, they are delighted and rejoice in their own acumen, not as if they had heard, but as if they had discovered them.* § 643–645. *Hence this evil is also* [p. 440] *laboured at by certain people. Nor is this a new fault, since I find already in Titus Livius that there was some teacher who bade his pupils obscure what they said, using the Greek word:* Σκότισον [darken it] *Whence, of course, that outstanding praise: So much the better. Even I did not understand.* Quintilian VIII. 2.

§ 665. No oblivion will ever obscure the most wretched *praises* of this arid master, § 255, 256, as long as there survive those who come forth from his school as from the Trojan horse, at whose labourings, whenever they gird themselves to think elegantly, you may chant that saying of Caicus in Virgil, Aen. VIIII. 36:

*What mass, O citizens, rolls on in black darkness?*

Let them be Proserpina’s; they will never be any Venus’s

*torches, fat with much darkness.* Lucr.

Rather may *the first Venus, born of Heaven and Day,* Cic. de nat. deorum III. 60, that celestial, not vulgar one,

*snatch away all the cloud which now, drawn over you as you gaze, / Dulls your mortal sight and casts a damp darkness round you. / Do not fear any commands of your parent, nor refuse to obey her precepts,* § 614, 662. Aen. II. 604.

Of these, however, as none can be thought earlier or more ancient, [p. 441] so none more evident than this, that he who would sacrifice to the Graces should never imitate Cacus, § 633, when he

*From his jaws a huge smoke (wonderful to tell) / Vomits forth, and wraps the house in blind darkness, / Snatching the prospect from the eyes, and rolls up beneath the cave / A smoke-bearing night, with darkness mingled with fire,* Aen. VIII. v. 252.

#### Sectio XXXX. Iusta Lucis et Umbrae Dispensatio. — Section XXXX. The Just Dispensation of Light and Shade

§ 666. He who would think elegantly is most tenacious of sensate perspicuity, § 665. But if at the same time he wishes to commend himself by polish and ornament, he must take singular pains 1) that the brilliance of every meditation, and of the whole to be set forth, be natural, neither less nor greater than nature has permitted, § 622, 623; 2) that, in order to attain this, comparative aesthetic light be so divided proportionately through all the parts of the total beautiful thought that to each so much is assigned, neither less nor greater: 1) to the more important, that it be not obscured by the light of the lesser co-parts but illuminated; 2) to the lesser, that it be not, [p. 442] by the light of the more important co-parts, illuminated but shaded, § 634, 658. Let us call both THE JUST AND CAUTIOUS DISPENSATION OF LIGHT AND SHADE.

§ 667. The just dispenser of light and shade, I. in order to procure for the whole which he means to think its natural brilliance and degree of brilliance, § 666, will well measure, as far as that can be done, 1) the nature of his theme and of the material chosen or prescribed, § 623, of which

*This loves the obscure, that will wish to be seen in the light,* § 624.

Nor would I advise considering the nature of the material universally and only in the abstract, as they say, but with all those circumstances weighed at the same time, as far as one can, in which it will now have to be conceived, e.g. in what place, at what time it is to be thought, over how long an interval of time a sufficiently full meditation of the given material is to be drawn out or cut short. For a longer one will admit more shades, which are to be excluded by a relatively shorter one, § 167.

§ 668. Further, the thing is to be weighed, and its relative magnitude and dignity sought out, § 178, 185. Not only the throne; even cities and forums for the most part know not

*the shade fit for shepherds,* § 368–372.

[p. 443] The same hero, when he is to be described, naturally demands a very unequal light, 1) while he sleeps in his cradle,

*where soft marjoram / Embraces him with flowers and sweet, breathing shade.* Aen. I. 697.

⁠2) while, a man, he frees his fatherland from enemies, the earth from monsters.

*Then a scene of glittering woods,* § 203, / *From above, and a black grove hangs over with bristling shade.* Aen. I. 168.

⁠3) while, grown old, he rejoices in peace that his own virtues too grow green again in the heir of his glory, as

*the Parnassian laurel / Lifts itself, small, beneath the vast shade of its mother.* Virg. Georg. II. 19.

§ 669. This measuring of materials is the more necessary, § 668, because you will find no more fruitful cause of meteors and of βάθους [bathos] than the pursuit of brilliance ending in cosmetics and pigments, § 626, 627, especially in sublime things, S. XXII. As soon as a wood presents itself to the bad geometers among the poets, § 203, large enough for the building of some pyre, it is suddenly

*one than which none is richer in bounteous shade,* [p. 444] *which, reared among the Argolic glades and the Lycaean, / Had lifted its head above the stars.* § 322.

When it is felled, so great is the mischief,

*As when a leader has given captured citadels to greedy victors / To plunder: the signals scarce heard, you would not / Find the city. They haul, they lay low, they drive off and carry away, / Unrestrained. Less is that crash with which wars are waged.* Stat. Theb. VI. 90–117.

§ 670. A truer mathematician of natural brilliance, § 667, is Longinus, when he does not permit, in truly grand things, πανουργεῖν [playing the knave] through illuminating figures, S. XVII, unless that artifice lies hidden in the more august light of sublimity. *For just as the feebler lights vanish when surrounded by the rays of the sun: so* the other *arts of the rhetoricians are overshadowed by sublimity. Nor is that different,* he adds, *which happens in painting. For although shade and light, through colours, lie parallel on the same plane of the panel, yet the light strikes the eyes first, and seems not only more outstanding but also nearer. Wherefore the affects and sublime things too, since they are nearer to souls, by their natural kinship and splendour always excel and stand out before figures* which only paint, § 142, *and shade and cover their art.* Yet a figure which only illuminates is so to be tempered [p. 445] that it be nothing but a certain shade of those which at the same time magnify, § 329, and move, § 666.

§ 671. Further, the just and cautious dispenser of light and shade, having taken the measure of the nature of his material, should attend to its truth and its degree of verisimilitude, § 667. The comparative light of general and of singular truths does not everywhere agree and is not equal. § 440, 561. The aesthetico-dogmatic manner of thinking, although it does not have that obscurity κατ' αἴσθησιν [with respect to sensation], § 631, from which the logico-dogmatic and scientific manner does not shrink, § 652, 566, nonetheless abstains, through a more temperate use of light, from many things with which the aesthetico-historical manner shines, and most of all the poetic, § 566, 561. We have already seen that Cicero, in his writings that are materially philosophical, did not follow the acroamatic manner of thinking, § 629; yet, about to philosophise in the aesthetico-dogmatic way, he foresees *how subtly, how obscurely even against the Stoics* he must sometimes *dispute*. quaest. ac. I. 7.

§ 672. In book II of De finibus, ch. 15, he goes so far as to concede that *it happens in two ways without blame that someone speaks so as not to be understood. If you either do it on purpose, as Heraclitus, who wrote too obscurely on nature, or when [p. 446] the obscurity of the matters, not of the words, brings it about that the discourse is not understood, as it is in Plato’s Timaeus.* Here you have two obscure dogmatists without blame. If by “obscure on purpose” you understand one obscure κατ' αἴσθησιν [with respect to sensation] by deliberate design, lest through scientific and acroamatic meditations he fall into obscurity κατὰ νόησιν [with respect to thought], § 631, 632; if you hold that Heraclitus was such in his writings on physics, then he is indeed without blame, but then he did not speak so as not to be understood, but so as not to be understood only by readers who bring to their reading scarcely more than the analogue of reason and refuse to strain the sinews of reason. § 652.

§ 673. If by “obscure on purpose” you understand one who has it fixed in his mind and resolved to pour darkness over his spectators and sell them smoke, spectators such as he is especially bound to attend to, who are not yawning and who offer the attention that is rightly required; if Heraclitus, at least sometimes, out of black bile and contempt for his fellow citizens, fell into this, § 644, he is not without blame. § 663–665. If by the obscurity of the matters you understand that weakness of most men by which they do not even grasp with the mind a given thing that is by nature rather remote from their senses, although others, exercising the keenness of the mind more diligently, can beautifully [p. 447] understand it, and the one who is to treat the same thing himself perceives it clearly and lucidly; if you have felt that Plato in the Timaeus speaks of matters that are somewhat obscure for this reason, then for this cause he is indeed obscure without blame; but neither did he speak so as not to be understood, but so as not to be understood except by those who take pleasure in sharpening the mind alike by the contemplation of things remote from the senses by nature itself, now a more severe contemplation, now a more relaxed and pleasant one. § 671.

§ 674. If by the obscurity of the matters you understand either that absolute obscurity proper only to chimeras, objective dreams, utopian fictions and their interpretations, or that of those matters which, with regard to the human race, a god so

*presses down in gloomy night,*

that we cannot divine anything of them even by a reasoning probable to aestheticians, so that not even one who was to treat of them ever either understood them or at least lucidly perceived them in his mind; if you grant that Plato in the Timaeus, at least sometimes, set out to depict matters of this kind, or ran into an avoidable obscurity of matters in some other way, § 646, then for this cause he is not obscure without blame, § 638, excused in vain by the obscurity of the matters, § 639, 647–649. For *the worst are* ἀδιανόητα [the unthinkable], [p. 448] i.e. *those things which are open in their words but hidden in their sense.* Quintilian VIII. 2.

§ 675. If, finally, you deny that anyone is understood whom some, even the most ignorant of all matters and arts, indeed even of languages, do not so grasp when he discourses of matters, even difficult ones, that he is judged to have exhausted the whole matter in every particular and to have left nothing undone that anyone might desire to perceive still more clearly, then to speak so as not to be understood in this way is certainly without blame, and not only in these two ways, but in many more:

*and this indulgence we ask and grant in turn.* / *That cook has yet to be born who would know* / *how to season dishes so that they are savoury to all alike.* Horace.

In this sense every dogmatist will find many by whom he is not understood, § 671, people bound to singulars alone, as if to the soil, § 404, or even accustomed to turn over in their minds only heterocosmic things, not to say utopian ones, § 441, 514.

§ 676. Having taken the measure of the nature of his material, the just dispenser of light and shade, § 667, should attend [p. 449] also to its internal ugliness or beauty, § 203, 204, inasmuch as, differing from the beauty of thoughts, it brings it about that beautiful things can be thought uglily and ugly things most beautifully, § 18. For who would doubt that a beautiful image of ugliness must mix shade and light otherwise than not only an ugly disfigurement of beauty but also a beautiful effigy of Venus? § 666. Ovid, presenting a formally beautiful picture of a most hideous little woman, Amores I. 8, among the other strokes of the procuress,

*This woman,* he says, *I suspect flits transformed through the shades of night,* / *and that her aged body is covered in feathers.* / *I suspect it, and it is rumoured. In her eyes, too, a double pupil* / *flashes, and light comes from a twin orb.*

Would he not have kept silent about this light, these flashes, in any matron whom he had set out to praise, even if they had been more conspicuous in eyes redder than this old woman’s were, *eyes tearful with wine?*

§ 677. Finally, prudently weighing the nature of his material, so as to dispense light and shade cautiously, § 667, he should be concerned whether it already seems sufficiently certain of itself, or whether it must hope for all its evidence only from brilliant reasons, whether it is theoretical [p. 450] or practical. § 576. For in theoretical matters and matters of that kind, which seem to be proved in vain, whose reasons seem to be recounted to no purpose, many things will be able to shine which must either be rejected altogether or shown only through shades, where either the greatest contention about the truth of the matter or more vehement commotions of the soul are foreseen to have to be aroused, § 661, 662, or where one is to ward off from his own head the most atrocious charges of an accuser in the forum, or the most imminent danger to his life among robbers,

*who would strew the ground with flowering herbs?* / *or draw green shade over the springs?*

§ 678. The cautious dispenser of light and shade will weigh to the best of his ability 2) his own nature, such as we described it in § 150, by a natural but severe dynamometry, § 457, compared with the nature of the given material considered according to § 667–677. § 666, 622. If there is any thing *which he scarcely recognises when first he stands beside it,*

*such as, in the first days of the month, one* / *either sees, or thinks he has seen, the moon through the clouds,* Aen. VI. 454,

that thing he will never choose for himself, nor will he dare to adorn what he could not smear over except with cosmetics, § 626, § 648. Let him think especially that this is said to him,

*who descends to the fight from the rhetorical shade.* Juv. VII. 173.

[p. 451] and who sometimes trusts so much in the topics, arguments, figures, flowers, chreiai, periods, etc., of his art that, rashly taking up any material whatever, he falls either into the battology noted in § 639, or into shadowy cosmetics, § 109.

§ 679. The just dispenser of light and shade will weigh 3) the nature of those for whose sake above all it is fitting, and his intention, to think charmingly. § 666, 623. Even if the most outstanding wit in aesthetic matters

*believed itself born not for itself, but for the whole world:*

one will hardly be found, however, who, when he wishes to think beautifully, makes the whole race of mortals his personal object, choosing from it no particular and nearer spectators, or at least without taking note of them. Rather, the authors of the first class, among the charming ones, inscribed their books to particular men, from whose condition one could measure what sort and how great readers they had set before their eyes, until this truly beautiful custom degenerated into the trite habit of dedicating, not to say the mercenary one. Whichever spectators the one who is about to think beautifully especially wishes for himself, let him meditate on their nature, as on his own and on that of his material, and on these compared with one another, in circumstances and affections as fully determined as can be, § 667, 678.

§ 680. [p. 452] The student of elegances in thinking who, by an ill fortune, must labour so that the crowd may admire him will not be able to neglect entirely those for whom some torrent or other stands in for all perspicuity, and, whatever it may signify, is nonetheless called clear and limpid when in one unbroken tenor

*a perpetual speech, carried on from the rising brilliance* / *of the dawn to the shade of black night,* / *especially if it is poured out with the loudest clamour.* Lucr. IIII. 540.

While he satisfies these people as far as the richness of his material, S. VIIII, the torrent of his wit, S. XII, and his sides and lungs permit, § 678, he would nonetheless not beautifully forget those among the people who are delighted not so much by trifles, tuneful indeed but poor in substance, as by things showy in their passages and rightly in character, even if they were wholly destitute of other charms, of weight and of art, § 226; better still, he attends at the same time to those among the people who desire something of all of these. § 434.

§ 681. He to whom it is given to be content with few readers, but readers of a more erudite taste too, § 275, should inquire the more carefully into their nature, their capacity, their genius, their habits, their anticipations, so that, since one thing can be exposed from different sides to different lights by someone sufficiently acquainted with it, [p. 453] that face of the thing above all, and that light, may be turned towards them which you expect will be most pleasant to the spectators. Thus certain illustrations will seem to some trite and vulgar, or shadowy and scholastic, which would please others well enough. Certain others, most welcome to these, would transcend the capacity of those and threaten gloom in the place of brilliance, e.g. those to be drawn from antiquities, from particular histories, or from the inner contemplation of nature. § 679, 666.

§ 682. The just dispenser of light and shade will, II, throughout a beautiful meditation pour over each of its parts only so much shade or light as is required so that the principal parts are not obscured by the light of the less principal, but are illuminated by their shade. § 666, 667. This is one of the chief rules of the lucid method, to be expounded more fully when we come to treat of it. § 13. For now we are content with a certain general idea of it, according to which e.g. 1) if the just dispenser whom we seek has flooded some one of the principal parts with a more beautiful light, and it is now to be feared that the spectator’s wearied eye will grow dim if led straight from here to another part more important still and therefore worthy of greater splendour, the imitator of nature should know how, after the former part,

[p. 454] *in the midst of the heat to seek out a shady valley,* / *wherever some great oak of Jupiter with ancient trunk* / *stretches out its huge branches, or wherever a grove* / *dark with thick ilexes lies in sacred shade,* Georg. III. v. 330,

refreshed by which he may himself, with his followers, come forth to new light, § 637. In the Aeneid book V is for this cause a shade interposed between the fourth and sixth books. § 634, 635.

§ 683. 2) The nearer the cautious imitator of nature of whom we speak is to the primary part of his whole meditation, which therefore demands the greatest light, the more he *draws in the shades*, just as midday has the smallest ones; 3) if he has come to those parts in which either a singular dignity is to be expressed magnanimously, or a more evident persuasion more solidly, or a more vehement motion of the soul ardently, he will certainly not waste leisure on things that merely paint,

*strewing the ground with leaves, drawing shade over the springs,*

§ 670, 677; but before he has come to these stronger contests, or where a beautiful order has imposed on their greater voices, as in musical concerts, the silence of some interval, § 682, then he remembers Venus, § 665,

[p. 455] *accustomed always in the shade* / *to indulge herself, and to augment her beauty by cultivating it.* Ov. Met. X. 534.

§ 684. The just dispenser of light and shade 4) will now also, for the sake of perspicuity, be bound to think no thing rashly, as it were most abstract and least determined, which one may not only form more determinately within the mind, but also accompany with a certain shade, § 440. Beautifully, if *Maecenas* is to be set before us, he *brings* certain *shades* with him, as in Horace, serm. II. 8, 21,

*with Servilius Balatro* / *Vibidius.*

Beautifully, if *virtue* is painted, let *glory follow her like a shade, although it has nothing in itself for which it should be sought.* § 212. *If this whole topic is to be despised in ourselves, it is not to be neglected in our own people.* Cic. tusc. quaest. l. 15. Not even the ugly Canidia is, for Horace, without shades,

*a great company attending her.* § 676.

*Why should I recount each thing? how, speaking in turn,* / *the shades with Sagana gave back a sad and shrill sound?* Hor. Serm. l. 8. 40.

§ 685. This just dispensing of light and shade is not [p. 456] one of the first and easiest of artifices, just as it was not in painting, nor is it proved to be so by the evidence of every day still. § 666, 682. The first *painting* was *nothing but what traced in lines only the outlines of the shadow which bodies had cast in the sun,* Quintilian X. 2. *The second, they affirm, was in single colours, and was called monochromaton, after a more laborious one had been invented.* Plin. hist. nat. XXXV. 3. *At length art distinguished itself, and discovered light and shades, the difference of colours setting itself off in alternation. Then splendour was added, something other than light, which, because it lay between this and shade, they called tonos.* ch. 5.

§ 686. The clearest part of an image is called light, the most obscure shade, by pre-eminence; between these, through the cautious dispensation which we have commended up to now, is born that splendour which is called TONE. § 685. Thus *this same thing, which is in the natures of things, can be transferred also to the arts, e.g. of sculpture, of painting. And if this is to be marvelled at in these as it were mute arts, and is yet true, how much more admirable is it in oratory and in language?* Cicero, de or. III. 26. *That highest praise and admiration in speaking has some shade and recess, so that what is illuminated may seem to stand out and be more prominent.*

§ 687. [p. 457] Hence too the artifice of dispensing shade and light well is wont neither to be attained without some instruction, nor to be observed, where it has been applied, except by the skilled and those sufficiently imbued with the discipline that pertains to it. § 685. *How many things painters see in shade and in prominence which we do not see?* Cic. quaest. ac. IIII. 20. Pliny, sending his book in praise of Trajan to Voconius with the very brief letter III. 13, throws in a mention of this rule alone: *As in painting nothing commends the light more than shade, so it is fitting as much to lower a speech as to raise it.* And not with regard to dignity alone, but also *by figuring variously.* § 619. Hence his wish: *Would that at least the order and the transitions and the figures were regarded together* by the reader! § 682. *For even barbarians sometimes find excellently and express magnificently: to arrange aptly, to figure variously, is denied to any but the erudite.*

#### Sectio XXXXI. Colores Aesthetici. — Section XXXXI. Aesthetic Colours

§ 688. Just as in physics *colour* is a modification of light seen, so the modifications of aesthetic light, § 614, are AESTHETIC COLOURS. [p. 458] The colour of which § 545 treats does not more closely belong here, although it can sometimes be among the colours now ours, when in proving it also illustrates. § 142. 143. That tone of which § 685 treats does indeed belong here, yet it does not exhaust all the aesthetic colours, just as no physicist will any longer rashly attempt to derive all colours from some mixture of light and shade, of white and black, of matter and privation. Least of all does the AESTHETIC COSMETIC belong here, an affected, § 110, vividness, § 626, 627. Of the Venus who is now ours, § 665, there is

*a true colour, a body solid and full of sap.* § 619, 622. Terence, Eunuchus II. 3.

§ 689. Let all aesthetic colours have this in common with one another, that the things to be painted with them are thought at least lucidly, § 648, 625.

*For what colour could there be in blind darkness,* / *a colour which changes in the light itself, according as* / *it shines back when struck by a direct or an oblique light?* Lucr. II. 800.

[p. 459] then each kind of colours that differ in this way has in turn its own, so to speak, specific quantity, by the variation of which an ever new and partially new colour is produced, and so you may see, in any whole,

*in any single and pure brilliance,* § 619, / *colours far unlike one another and various.* § 688. Lucr. II. 783.

By which, exhibiting a pleasant variety of parts in the unity of the whole,

*the various colours of things hinder and prevent* / *the whole thing from being able to be of one brilliance.* Lucr.

a thing that would please less through an even monotony. § 688.

§ 690. But just as the monochrome painting, employing *single colours*, notwithstanding its simplicity, both pleased at first and *endures even now*, and is often of greater value than one variegated with colours quite enough and more, whose sole praise is the variety of its colours: so too there is no need in every beautiful meditation to pursue several aesthetic colours differing in quality, or always to hunt after a numerous multitude of them at all. § 685. Let certain things to be thought beautifully, especially shorter ones, have only one colour with respect to quality, but a true and natural one, § 688, differing in quantity from the just dispensation of light and shade, [p. 460] § 666, evenly tempered everywhere to the material, § 267; a manner of thinking can be charming enough that reigns alone through the whole course of the thought, e.g. now rough, now gentle, now subdued, now elevated, now sad, now playful. § 688.

§ 691. Just as *with four colours alone Apelles, Echion, Melanthius and Nicomachus, the most famous painters, made immortal works, when single pictures of theirs sold for the wealth of towns*, but in Pliny’s own age, *with purples migrating onto walls and India contributing the silt of its rivers and the gore of dragons and elephants, there was no noble painting*, Hist. nat. XXXV. 7; so neither, in that beautiful meditation which joins certain colours, even ones differing in quality, is an abundance of these and a prodigy of variation to be sought so much as that those which are employed, fewer perhaps, should nonetheless *set each other off in alternation* and preserve a tone fitted to the natures of the things to be painted, § 685, and should have neither forced *joinings and transitions of colours*, nor an affected ἁρμογή [harmony of blending]. § 688. Plin. hist. nat. XXXV. c. 5.

§ 692. As the classes of physical colours are indefinite, if you attend at once not only to the primitive ones but also to the derivative, not only to the simple but also to the composite [p. 461] and decomposite, especially if you attend also to the degrees of the primitive and like-named ones, e.g. of red, yellow-ochre, green, blue, while in all of them a greater quantity of light, now extensively, now intensively, as to strength and speed, either constitutes the generic and specific difference or at least enters into it, § 689; so too of aesthetic colours, both those differing in quality, § 690, and those diverse in degree of light and tone, there are almost as many orders as there are natures of things, and modes in things, and limits beyond and short of which neither true light can subsist, nor true colour. Yet a certain quantity of aesthetic light also enters into the differences of all these modifications. § 688.

§ 693. Since, therefore, no one should rashly undertake a complete enumeration of the colours, let us stop at the Plinian division, Hist. nat. XXXV. 6: *Colours are either austere or florid.* Let us conceive a scale of tone from the blackest shades that absolute aesthetic light admits, § 617, through the diverse degrees and orders of aesthetic colours up to the most radiant aesthetic brilliance, § 619. On this scale there will be a certain middle tone. The COLOURS that remain below the middle tone are AUSTERE, those that transcend it FLORID.

§ 694. [p. 462] Although, therefore, we reject the plainly rough manner of thinking, § 634, nonetheless the ROUGHISH and austere MANNER OF THINKING, rightly conceived from this, can hold its place in charming meditations themselves, a manner in which an austere colour or austere colours reign, § 690, 693, to be compared with that *rough, uncultivated, retiring and opaque painting which is nonetheless excellent in its kind*, § 624; to it is opposed the FLORID MANNER OF THINKING, in which a florid colour or florid colours reign, i.e. are predominant or alone, to be compared with that *brilliant, cheerful and well-lit painting*, no less *excellent in its kind*, § 624.

§ 695. The roughish manner of thinking does not exclude or shrink from aesthetic light and sensate perspicuity, indeed not even from all brilliance, but only from the greater, § 694, 693. It can be what Antonius says of Crassus’s rhetorical oration, Cic. Or. III. 51, *brilliant on rough matters, full on meagre ones, new on commonplace ones.* Meanwhile the true and natural colour of rough matters is nothing but austere, and a florid thought of rough matters would make a cosmetic, § 688. The oration of Crassus shines, but as much as the aesthetico-dogmatic manner of thinking can shine, which, compared [p. 463] with the aesthetico-historical manners, or with the poetic ones altogether, is always roughish and austere, § 671, 566.

§ 696. You have in Virgil, Aen. IV. 247, an austere description

*of hard Atlas, who props the sky on his crown,* / *of Atlas, whose pine-bearing head, girt without cease* / *by black clouds, is beaten by wind and rain;* / *snow poured over his shoulders covers them, then rivers plunge* / *from the old man’s chin, and his rough beard is stiff with ice.*

But soon a florid one of Aeneas, § 694, v. 261:

*And he had a sword starred with tawny jasper,* / *and a cloak let down from his shoulders blazed* / *with Tyrian purple, gifts which wealthy Dido* / *had made, and had picked out the weave with fine gold.*

How here the colours of images set next to each other set each other off in alternation! In their joinings and transition, how natural is the ἁρμογή [harmony of blending]! § 691.

§ 697. Let us add from Ovid an austere description of Boreas carrying off his bride, Met. VI. 702.

*He shook out his wings, by whose beating all* / [p. 464] *the earth was blown upon, and the broad sea shuddered,* / *and dragging his dusty mantle over the highest peaks* / *he sweeps the ground, and, covered in gloom, the lover* / *embraces with his dusky wings Orithyia, trembling with fear.*

For another florid description of Aeneas see § 662. Tacitus shows both kinds of colour as if from afar, with his usual brevity, when he relates that *Pharasmanes pointed at once to the rough battle-line of his own men and to the columns of the Medes painted with gold, here men, there booty,* Ann. VI. 34.

§ 698. Each kind of aesthetic colours is either painted in natural things, inasmuch as they are distinguished from moral ones, or is found in moral things. The true and natural colour of the latter will give a third species of ἦθος [character], § 195, 495, by which the moral manner of thinking, § 226, may suffice not only for lucidly expressing the morals and characters of persons, nations, etc., in moral terms, but may also know how to assign to each of these that place, that tone, that tempering of light and shade, that colour which is fitting, § 193. Thus to the first appearance of the person of a certain virtue austere colours and the roughish manner of thinking will be suited; the true and natural adornment of every virtue, [p. 465] however, admits in its interior florid colours and the florid manner of thinking. To the first appearance of the mask of certain vices florid colours and a cheerful and well-lit manner of thinking are suited; to the internal ugliness of all vices austere colours and a somewhat obscure manner of thinking, § 676, 694.

§ 699. Let us look at a notable example in Horace, III. ode 24, where 1) describing luxury, as it first appears, in the florid manner of thinking:

*Though richer than the untouched* / *treasures of the Arabs and of wealthy India* / *you occupy with your masonry* / *the whole Tyrrhenian and the Apulian sea:*

at once, somewhat austere, he paints the true inner state of this vice:

*if dire Necessity drives her adamantine* / *nails into the topmost summits,* / *you will not free your soul from fear,* / *nor your head from the snares of death.*

He goes on with the same austerity to describe more roughly the outward habit, as it first appears, of more innocent morals, v. 8–28; still roughish, he returns to setting before our eyes the inner deformity of luxury, v. 29–44.

§ 700. But now, about to urge temperance from the true [p. 466] nature of this virtue, though it is hidden from most, he bids us renounce riches, suddenly florid:

*Either to the Capitol,* / *where the shouting calls us and the crowd of applauders,* / *or into the nearest sea* / *let us send our gems and precious stones, and useless gold,* / *the material of the greatest evil.*

He foresees well how discordant this, whatever counsel it was, must appear to most of the more powerful citizens. Hence, wrapping himself again in his virtue, he proves, austere, that

*if we truly repent of our crimes,* / *the elements of depraved desire* / *must be rooted out, and minds too tender* / *must be strengthened* / *by harsher studies.*

But since he seems to despair of the fruit of his counsels, he rests in a severe and austere image of vices unsound within and under the skin. § 698.

§ 701. Ovid joins the colours of natural and of moral things in recounting the four ages of the world which he feigns, Met. I. Having pursued the moral state of the golden age in the most florid manner of thinking, v. 90–100, § 594, [p. 467] he passes to the natural state, to be sprinkled with the same flowers:

*Of herself the earth gave all things.* / — — — — / *Spring was eternal, and with warm breezes the gentle* / *Zephyrs caressed the flowers born without seed.* / — — — — / *Now rivers of milk, now rivers of nectar flowed,* / *and yellow honey dripped from the green ilex.* v. 112.

§ 702. He now lowers his tone in painting the silver age, v. 112–124, still more in the brief mention of the bronze age, gradually, together with the men of that age

*more prone to horrid arms,* v. 126.

But in describing the iron age, how he bristles all over! § 594.

*Men live on plunder. Guest is not safe from host,* / *Nor father-in-law from son-in-law. Even among brothers goodwill is rare.* / *The husband threatens his wife with destruction, she her husband.* / *Dreadful stepmothers mix lurid aconites.* / *The son inquires before the day into his father’s years.* / *Piety lies vanquished, and the maiden Astraea, last* / *of the heavenly ones, has left the earth dripping with slaughter.* / [p. 468] *And lest the lofty aether be safer than the earth,* / *they say the Giants aspired to the heavenly kingdom,* / *and piled up mountains heaped to the stars.* 153.

§ 703. Few there are, or none, whom it would befit, as

*Every station, and colour, and circumstance befitted Aristippus,* Hor. ep. l. 17, 23.

unless Horace in this too has lied splendidly. Nothing in experience confirms more the principles that deny total similarity and equality than the diversity of characters and persons, of morals and manners of acting. In expressing these, however, in such a way that they can be distinguished, it is of such moment to imitate the proper and own nature of each, that whoever is ignorant of this one thing alone, Horace denies him worthy of the name of poet, when, Art. Poet. v. 86,

*To preserve the described turns,* he says, *and the colours of works,* / *why, if I cannot and know not how, am I hailed as poet?* / *Why do I, perversely modest, prefer not to know rather than to learn?* / *A comic subject will not be set forth in tragic verses.* / *Likewise the feast of Thyestes disdains to be narrated in private verses* / *and ones nearly worthy of the sock,* § 690. / *Let each single thing keep the fitting place allotted to it.*

[p. 469] in natural and mechanical things too, which is easier, but especially in moral things, which is harder, § 666, 691.

#### Sectio XXXXII. Fucus Aestheticus. — Section XXXXII. Aesthetic Cosmetic

§ 704. Of aesthetic cosmetic we have already seen both the definition § 688 and certain kinds, § 626, 627. But the difficulty of distinguishing it from true colours, together with the necessity of doing so, seems to require a separate treatment of it. § 628, 625. Now just as true colours are either austere or florid: § 693, so cosmetic too will be either too austere, not roughish but rough, § 634, or too florid and like Iris,

*Drawing a thousand varied colours against the sun.*

Something roughish already sounds in that line of Virgil,

*Arms and the man I sing.*

More austere is that near the middle of the whole poem:

*Wars, horrid wars,* / *And the Tiber foaming with much blood I behold—*

Meanwhile the true colour expresses according to nature the thing that was at issue, and the differing degree of austerity [p. 470] preserves the described turns and has obtained its fitting place. § 703.

§ 705. With this roughish colour compare the rough cosmetic of Lucan:

*Wars worse than civil across the Emathian plains,* / *and licence given to crime, we sing, and a mighty people* / *turned with victorious right hand against its own entrails,* / *and kindred battle-lines, and, the compact of rule broken,* / *the contest with all the forces of a shaken world* / *for common wickedness, and standards facing hostile standards,* / *matched eagles, and pila threatening pila.* / *What madness?* o! bard! *what licence so great* from the egg?

So let some *cyclic writer begin.* A colour that has not obtained *its place becomingly*, and is more saturated than is just, is cosmetic. § 703, 688.

§ 706. Silius Italicus already begins more austerely than Virgil. § 704.

*I shall begin with the arms by which the glory of the Aeneadae* / *raises itself to heaven, and fierce Carthage* / *suffers Oenotrian laws.*

A beginning at which perhaps Horace, sated, would already cry out: Ho! bombast! Yet you would call its colour fairly [p. 471] natural compared with the opening of Claudian on the Gothic War, on which, at the very threshold, *the gold of a stolen fleece,* that worn-out cosmetic, § 626, has been smeared:

*When the bold Argo broke through the barriers of the untouched strait,* / *the sea armed with clashing rocks,* / *seeking Aeetes and the Colchians, with danger drawing near,* / *while all were thunderstruck, Tiphys alone, after the gods,* / *is said to have kept the ship unharmed with slight loss* / *and to have escaped the twofold ruin of the mountain,* / *and, the clash of the wandering rock eluded, to have led* / *the victorious vessel into the deep.* etc. up to v. 35.

§ 707. A rough cosmetic of this kind, § 704, can not only disfigure singular things, § 708, 706 and the aesthetico-historical manner of thinking, § 566; for the same reason it is to be avoided by aesthetico-dogmatic thinkers, both those exhibiting natural things, insofar as they are opposed to moral ones, and those exhibiting moral things, § 698. The theoretical aesthetico-dogmatic manner of thinking can slip into it, and so can the practical, now the paraenetic, now the elenctic, § 576. One versed in these ought to be all the more careful not to transgress the described turns and the limits of natural colour and of cosmetics, since every dogmatic manner of meditating already seems to have [p. 472] something roughish about it, § 695, and in the colours of moral things the slip is easier, since a double judgement is usually passed on the same things, now viewed from outside and hastily, now from within and more deeply, § 698, 703.

§ 708. The aesthetico-practical cosmetic § 707 that we shall now dissuade from is not that cruder one against which Juvenal inveighs, sat. 2, when

*Beyond the Sauromatae one would like to flee, and the icy* / *Ocean, whenever they dare something about morals,* / *who feign Curii and live Bacchanalia.* § 362.

A subtler cosmetic is here to be blamed in the thoughts even of those who perhaps have neither a bad mind nor a bad heart, though not sufficiently conscious of truer blessedness. Let the question be, e.g., about the true colour of felicity, which the aesthetico-dogmatic thinker is to set forth natural and not painted. They then follow the archetype of their sage and the formulas of thinking or speaking derived from it, which the Stoics, *while they hold them with their teeth and will not let them be torn from their mouth* or mind, *come out rougher, harsher, harder both in speech and in morals,* and in the very manner of thinking. Cic. de fin. IIII, 78. Hence into their pictures of good, evil, virtue, blessedness, felicity a too austere cosmetic is introduced. § 704.

§ 709. [p. 473] Under the same hypothesis § 708 some Epicurean will follow the single pleasure, which if he has formed in his mind, as it is easy and widely spread among the common people, and has not comprehended in his mind the whole system of our heavenly Venus but only some small particle of it, § 646, he will rashly soften into such lightness that he may perhaps imitate quite happily some gentle features of Felicity, but those graver and austere ones at the same time, which to express

*horrid Virtue commands,* / *and a breast armed with Faith, and desire of the Right,* / *with hardship heard,* Sil. XI. 205.

he will be unwilling to weave in with the gayer ones, or at any rate with a slight brush will overlay them through the ill-placed brilliance of a spurious light, and hence will not know how to compose the whole without too florid a cosmetic. § 704. Having finally followed the theory of the Peripatetics in these matters, he will come nearer to the true colour of Felicity. § 688.

§ 710. *Catholic, that is (to say it as we can), universal or perpetual* (Quint. II. 14) things about morals, virtues and vices, sufficiently exact according to the truth of aesthetic colour, § 552, one who is to think beautifully is bound to make familiar to himself, not only if at any time he has a mind to touch upon practical matters [p. 474] dogmatically, § 707, but also because the moral manner of thinking about singulars, whether most strictly true or heterocosmic, uses those catholic and general things, as it were as directing notions, in forming the ideas of singulars § 653, 547. If anyone from the school of the Cynics has sprinkled virtue and morals in general with cosmetics that are in truth too austere, he will also draw the singular virtue of a definite person, whom he will narrate or feign, as one which

*would seem bitterer than Sardinian herbs,* / *rougher than butcher’s-broom, cheaper than cast-up seaweed.* Virg. Ecl. VII.

§ 711. If anyone has slipped into the precepts of Aristippus, and therefore expresses virtue and the whole institution of morals with too florid cosmetics, § 704, from his workshop

*the guardian and rigid attendant of true virtue,*

to be viewed even in the concrete and in a singular individual, § 710, will come forth neat, pretty, dainty, clad in purple, crowned with roses, smelling of pastilles, as he *drinks Hymettian honey diluted with Falernian,*

*among the gleaming dishes and tables,* / *while the eye is dazed by the mad glitter, and when* / *the mind, inclined to false things, refuses the better.* Hor. Serm. II. 2. 4.

§ 712. [p. 475] Just as all learning indeed, both practical and theoretical, the truer it is, contributes much to inducing truer colours upon the things that pertain to it and the singulars which it contains under itself, § 63–67; yet none will more easily let a wit avoid cosmetic, a wit which, moderately happy by nature, will imitate nature alone, than one corrupted by preconceived opinions that tinge everything with false colour: § 707–711. Here especially the aesthetic art, when truer, is a great aid for seeking the true colours in things and shunning cosmetics, § 68, 69; but when false, its precepts do more harm than if you knew nothing at all of such an art, § 73, since while you follow them, as it seems to you, altogether secure, you desert nature with deliberate design, snatching at cosmetics and pigments, § 688, 110, into which perhaps by a merely natural impulse you would not have fallen, § 52.

§ 713. Among the deceptive rules of the aesthetic arts, e.g. of rhetoric and poetics, those that are wholly false, and so false, even aesthetically, that they have not even some pretty semblance of truth to defend themselves with, will not call one away from true colours to cosmetics with so great a danger as those which are both defended by great authorities and in some places conveniently practised, but sin in [p. 476] quantity, as the logicians say, and are thought as catholic, though they are true only particularly. § 73. Hence aestheticians must so greatly distinguish the few *laws* of charms *bound by immutable necessity* from counsels sometimes good, *which, as if it were not lawful otherwise, certain people follow as though commanded,* not seldom preposterously. For *most* precepts *are changed by causes, times, necessity, occasion. None are sanctioned by bills or plebiscites. It is fitting, as we see in pictures and statues, that dress, countenance, posture be varied. Rarely is a kind of catholic precept found that cannot be weakened in some part and undermined.* Quint. II. 14.

§ 714. Those whom rules too lax do not yet sufficiently deflect from the true colour of things into cosmetics, many of these blind imitation twists thither. They perceive that the *orations* of some *Cato,* though *roughish,* are loved and approved. Cic. de Or. 152. And lo! whenever they wish to please, how suddenly they bristle! § 704.

*What? if someone, fierce with grim countenance and bare foot,* / *should counterfeit Cato by the weave of a scanty toga,* / *would he represent the virtue and the morals of Cato?* / *An example imitable in its vices deceives.* Hor. I. ep. 19. 12.

Others perceive that not a few are delighted by the flowers [p. 477] of the poets and of Curtius. Hence they think themselves eloquent only then, when on whatever occasions are given they run riot in flowering. § 254.

*O imitators, servile herd! how often have your tumults* / *moved my bile, how often my laughter!*

§ 715. The charming wits of the Attics were once praised. *Of these there are several kinds; these,* the blind imitators, § 714, *suspected only one, of what sort it was. For they thought that one who speaks in a rough and unpolished manner, provided he does it elegantly and clearly, he alone speaks Attically. They erred in the “alone,”* § 713; *in the “Attically” they were not deceived.* § 694. Cic. or. 28. Meanwhile, however great their error was, it could not but sometimes commend to them cosmetics too rough in place of the true colours of florid things. § 704, 248, 260. Now authors from among the Greeks and Romans are praised as classical by their own right, who have used now austere, now florid colours most fittingly. Since in imitating them it is above all *to be considered that nothing be thought confusedly, nothing distortedly,* Cic. de Inu. l. 29, how could the blind imitators, who strive to have caught in the classical writings some likeness, however slight, to their own, do otherwise than confound colours, as cooks do sauces, and, twisting the most beautiful manners of thinking of some Cicero or even Demosthenes to the most alien things, make a cosmetic of them? § 688.

§ 716. [p. 478] Florid colours *most strongly impel our senses with pleasure, and at first sight move them most keenly.* Often, however, *we are very quickly estranged from them by a certain disgust and satiety.* § 693. *How much more florid in beauty and variety of colours are most things in new pictures than in old ones? Which, however, though they captivated us at first sight, do not delight for long, while we ourselves are held in ancient panels by that very roughness and antiquity. How much softer and more delicate in singing are the flexions and falsetto notes than firm and severe ones? against which, however, not only the austere, but, if they occur too often, the multitude itself cries out.* Cic. de or. III. 98. cf. 99–103, and you will have an excellent antidote against too florid cosmetics. § 704.

§ 717. Meanwhile to more people the too florid cosmetic is suspect, rather than the too austere. For them red lead, cinnabar, indigo and purple are more readily judged cosmetics than those painted even

*with their faces smeared with lees.*

He who prepares an *austere accusation,* even if he should leap over all the bounds of humanity, will seem to himself to be painting the truth that begets hatred, not cosmetic, to be offering an *austere cure,* not the juice of the black cuttlefish and sheer verdigris. That he may escape austerity as too gloomy, [p. 479] Ovid, Trist. l. 1, throws in a mention of florid things even while removing them.

*Nor let bilberries veil you with purple cosmetic,* / *that colour is not suited to mourning.* / *Nor let your title be marked with red lead, nor your paper with cedar,* / *nor bear white horns on a black front.* / *Let these instruments adorn fortunate little books;* / *it befits you to be mindful of my fortune.*

§ 718. Apelles excelled by a like artifice, *which alone no one could imitate. He covered his finished works with a black varnish so thin that this very thing by its reflection called forth the brightness of the colours, and guarded them from dust and dirt, and appeared only to one looking at it close at hand. But also then with great reason, that the brightness of the colours should not offend the sharpness of the eyes, as for those looking from afar through a pane of specular stone, and that the same thing should covertly give austerity to too florid colours.* Plin. hist. nat. XXXV. 10. So great a labour it is for the poet carefully to avoid too austere and gloomy a cosmetic, for the painter one too florid. § 717. I fear, however, lest that witty saying, as they call it, in Ovid Trist. l. III. 10, has something of a cosmetic enticement:

*That the lame verses sink in alternate lines,* / *either the plan of the metre or the long journey does it.* / — — — — / [p. 480] *Do you see the paper pale with a bloodless colour?*

Nay rather, because letters do not blush.

§ 719. The vicious and corrupt manner of thinking, which we have up to this point called cosmetic, § 688, Quintilian says *shines with little flowers that will fall if they are lightly shaken, vanishing and dying,* like the other vicious manners of thinking, *by comparison with better ones, just as wool dyed with a cosmetic pleases apart from purples, and, if you compare it even with a cloak, is overwhelmed by the sight of the better. But if you apply a keener judgement to these corrupt things, as sulphur to the dyed, then that which had deceived sheds its lying colour and grows pale with a certain scarcely describable foulness. So let these shine apart from the sun, as certain tiny animals seem to be little fires in the dark.* XII, 10. If you drag a painted Venus of this kind out of the corners and the shade into the sun and the throng of a larger theatre, *neither does*

*the moist chalk now stay on her, nor the colour* / *painted with crocodile dung.* Hor. ep. od. XII.

§ 720. Upon beautiful thoughts *there ought to sit a certain colour of charm, not smeared on with cosmetic but diffused with blood,* i.e. a modification of aesthetic light determined by the very nature of the matter, of the thinker, and of the principal objects in personal matters. § 688, 622, 623. Cic. de or. [p. 481] III. 199. *The very dignity of form is to be guarded by goodness of colour.* § 188. Cic. off. l. 1. 30. But here *there is a certain colour of truth without any cosmetic.* § 423. Cic. de cl. orator. 162, in whose *lineaments the flower of fresh pigments* should rather *be lacking* than be present in excess. 298. § 691. Yet let not the variation that follows nature be lacking, § 689, since a natural colour drawn beyond the bounds set by nature and continued in one unbroken tenor is in the end nothing but cosmetic. Hence I understand Quintilian, who attributes to *Cassius Severus very much wit, a wonderful acerbity, the highest urbanity,* yet misses in the same man not only *gravity* but also *colour of speech,* since *he gave more to his spleen and his bitter wit than to good counsel.* X. 1.

§ 721. Let us come to some kinds of colours and of the cosmetic opposed to colours, by way of example. I. Cicero misses *in foreign orators a speech coloured with a certain urbanity.* And when asked: *What then is that colour of urbanity? I do not know,* he says, *I only know that there is one. You, Brutus, will understand it when you come into Gaul. In the voices of our orators something rings and resounds more urbanely. Nor does this appear only in orators, but also in the rest.* de cl. orat. 170. Is this colour the same as that ‘I know not what’ which now certain Gauls attribute to their own alone, and deny to foreigners? A cosmetic resembling it is shown by Catullus’s *Suffenus,*

[p. 482] *A charming man, and witty, and urbane,* / *who only just now a buffoon,* / *or if there is anything more polished than that, seemed.*

The same man exhibits a wonderful mixture of cosmetics and open foulness, when in his poems

*that pretty and urbane* / *Suffenus seems a mere goatherd or ditch-digger* / *once more. So much does he differ and change.*

§ 722. II. I shall join together, as it is said in Cicero to have been joined in Rutilius’s manner of speaking, de cl. or. 113, the gloomy and severe colour, the keen and vehement. The first is a colour suited to mourning, not so the rest, except to certain tragic matters. There is however a certain true colour of *severity, not only not invidious, but even popular.* For I read in Cicero Fam. VIIII. ep. 14 *severity,* not ‘truth’, *of chastisement, most welcome both to all good men and to each of the lowest.* There is a certain true colour of a keenness that shines out from modesty, *the whole kind* of which *will excel,* e.g. *in causes in which the mind of the judge can be inflamed by a certain keen and vehement incitement.* Cic. Or. II. 183. Although *there is no doubt that the exordium of a speech should not often be vehement and pugnacious.* 317: Cicero, however, who knew well enough the colours of his works, *confesses that he acts vehemently, denies that he acts angrily.* Philipp. VIII. 16.

§ 723. [p. 483] These colours are counterfeited by the cosmetics e.g. of those *who with grave brow and gloomy frown stubbornly attend to the interests of the treasury, who* Pliny thinks *have never been wanting to princes,* Pan. 41, and by every severity in thinking and judging contrary not only to jests and hilarity but even to humanity. Such bitter severity let alone be loved by

*the severe throng of the Eumenides in the strained court.* Prop. IIII. 11.

True and painted keenness Cicero paints together, forming the morals of one who is to think beautifully, de Or. I. 182, when: *These things,* he says, *help in an orator: gentleness of voice, of countenance, the signifying of modesty, courtesy of words. If you pursue anything more keenly,* (here you have the true colour, § 722) *that you may seem to do it unwillingly and under compulsion. — — — — All those things which belong to the honest, the humble, the not keen, not obstinate, not litigious, not bitter,* (you have the cosmetic of the obstinate and bitter litigant) *greatly conciliate goodwill.* Just as *vehement,* properly and rightly for Macrobius VI. 8, signifies *furnished with excessive impetus and force of mind,* M. § 699: so the *vehement and atrocious manner* of thinking will display a cosmetic rather than a true and praiseworthy colour, unless there be mingled with it *a manner of gentleness and mildness.* Cic. de or. II. 200.

§ 724. Hence *there must be softened in most cases by another colour* [p. 484] *the harshness of speech.* Quint. XI. 1. From which you may gather both that the harsh manner of thinking is a colour, and that manner of gentleness and mildness, § 723, by which some knowing *artificer in handling minds attains that he may seem to do something for the sake of those too against whom* he is armed. Let us join to the colour suited to mourning, § 722, *the colour of solicitude necessary to one in peril,* and that which *penitence alone has, if no excuse falls to* those who had sinned. l. c.

*Behold how many colours the beauteous earth sends up!* Prop.

But beware lest they degenerate into the cosmetic of a manner of thinking either too *savage and violent,* or effeminate, into the foul trepidation of the despairing, or a base and abject execration and palinode of past deeds.

§ 725. III. There is the SUBMISSIVE MANNER OF THINKING, of a mind sparing its powers and impetus out of reverence. So long as this restrains itself from affecting richness, dignity, exact pursuit of truth, brilliance and ornament, more vehement argumentations and contention, only so far as either the reverence due to the thinker, if any is due to him, or that which he himself owes now to the things and persons about which or against which he meditates, now to the principal future spectators of his meditation, κατ’ [p. 485] ἤθη [according to their characters], § 698, shall have demanded: it preserves the true and natural colour, § 622, 623. Nor will this moral, § 226, 227, even-tenored, § 267, and even more strictly temperate manner of thinking, § 270, neglect that rule in Cicero, Off. I. 90: *They seem rightly to teach who admonish that the higher we are, the more submissively we should bear ourselves.*

§ 726. But as soon as the submissive manner of thinking is depressed to the point that one who is to think beautifully either forgets his own dignity, or, exhibiting or creepingly feigning a false and superstitious reverence towards I know not what things or persons, allows himself to be drawn back by them from that copiousness, dignity, profession of truth, ornament, refutation, and finally contention and impetus of mind, which he could have displayed becomingly and elegantly: then this same *humble, submissive, soft, effeminate, broken and abject* manner of thinking, like βάθος [bathos], § 217, is a cosmetic. § 688, 725.

§ 727. Hence Quintilian, XI. 1, counts among the vices *the submissive manner* of thinking *in agitated causes,* i.e. those which, saving the reverence of truer decorum, it is nevertheless not only fitting but [p. 486] even necessary to plead with all impetus of mind and all the sinews of diligence, with all copiousness, dignity, ornament and ardour. In the same place, among other things very pertinent here, he brings the example of *Socrates,* for whom *it would have been profitable towards his acquittal if* with a submissive speech, § 726, *he had conciliated* the minds of the judges to himself, who however, since *that would least have befitted him, so acted as one who was going to value his penalty at the highest honours.* Here he adds: *Since he was little understood by the men of his time, he reserved himself for the judgements of posterity, and by the brief loss of a now extreme old age obtained the age of all centuries.*

§ 728. Quintilian goes on, l. c., to mention Scipio *Africanus,* who *preferred to withdraw from his country rather than contend about his innocence with a Tribune of the plebs, a most lowly man.* So highly did the hero value guarding against the badly submissive manner of thinking, § 726.
