Its sources, according to Quintilian, are *those lowlier vices, submissive flattery, base shame, authority neglected in every business, which generally befall those who wish to be too agreeable.* So grave an adversary of the submissive manner of thinking, when it is a cosmetic, asks nevertheless: *Who indeed does not know that sometimes the speech must be lowered and contracted, lest the judge be unable either to understand or to grasp it?* § 725. The same man praises *Cicero,* who not even *in the cause of Cluentius,* although his *mother openly attacked the life of her son, forgot the reverence which is owed to parents,* and he bids *in a lesser and less* [p. 487] *hostile matter a speech* still *gentler and more submissive.*

§ 729. IIII. There is the ERECT AND BOLD MANNER OF THINKING, of a mind straining its force and impetus for the sake of dignity, in which one thinks and speaks loftily, though not always sublimely, § 287. In this the cosmetic of arrogance is to be guarded against. V. There is the playful and jocose, in which *affected scurrility* is to be avoided, *especially in things not very modest and chaste,* and in general, *that you should not wish to be too ridiculous.* VI. There is the grave, in which care is to be taken that no immature senile authority be detected. VII. There is the sweet, whose *decocted sweetness* Cicero turns away from. But enough of these. § 692.

#### Sectio XXXXIII. Argumenta Illustrantia. — Section XXXXIII. Illustrating Arguments

§ 730. ARGUMENTS whose force (whether sole, or chief, or now especially to be considered) is to pour light upon a given perception are DECLARATIVE (explicating). They will therefore give perspicuity either intellectual, RESOLVING (analytic), such as *definition,* [p. 488] *declaring the proper force of any thing,* Cic. de Or. I. 190, or sensate, § 614, 618, now certainly absolute, § 617, 625, now altogether exhibiting some brilliance, and from their predominant feature they are called ILLUSTRATING (painting). Cicero, Fam. V. ep. 13, judges that *one must more often say, and not only signify,* which can be done more obscurely, *but also declare,* what he wishes Lucceius to see clearly.

§ 731. The most just caution of avoiding cosmetic, S. XXXXII, will persuade one, in painting and illustrating arguments and figures § 730, 26, to pursue especially only those ornaments which, besides illustration, serve at the same time richness, dignity, verisimilitude and the remaining graces of things to be thought, either all of them or at least some, § 142; but those which have only painted and besides seem to contribute nothing to the beauty of the whole, to sprinkle with a sparing hand, or to cut back as ambitious, § 164.

§ 732. Pregnant perceptions, M. § 517, and complex ones, M. § 530, both shine more themselves, other things being equal, than less complex ones, and can become illustrating arguments of the whole meditation into which they enter, § 730. Hence they are beautifully preferred to the less pregnant, the less complex, especially when the [p. 489] adhering perceptions themselves at the same time also prove something, or move, § 731. Hence the great artifice of well-chosen epithets. All beautiful things abound in examples. Let us take Virgil’s first eclogue, the first among his poems. There he does not say: you *sing* Amaryllis, but:

*You teach the woods to resound with beauteous Amaryllis,* v. 5.

he does not say:

*This* city has much *lifted its head among other cities,* but *as much,*

*As the cypresses are wont among the pliant wayfaring-trees.*

He does not say that freedom came to him *as an old man,* but:

*After my beard fell whiter to the barber.*

He does not write: We shall never forget him, but:

*Sooner shall light stags graze in the air,* / *and the seas leave the fish bare on the shore:* / *sooner, the bounds of both wandered over, as an exile* / *shall the Parthian drink the Arar, or Germany the Tigris,* / *than his countenance slip from our breast.*

Finally he does not say: now evening is here, but:

*Now the high rooftops of the farms smoke afar* / *and greater shadows fall from the lofty mountains.*

§ 733. Since HYPOTYPOSIS, the vivid description of something, [p. 490] not only illustrates, § 618, but also proves, § 550, 551, it is deservedly counted among the better of the painting arguments, § 731. DIATYPOSIS is a more ardent hypotyposis. Hence diatyposis belongs among the best of the illustrating arguments, § 731, and its species is CHARACTERISMUS, a diatyposis more concerned with character. § 226. Examples will be given by Lucian, and Theophrastus, and his chief imitator among the French.

§ 734. One of two connected and conjoined, i.e. associated, perceptions can declare the other, indeed even illustrate it, § 730, if at the same time it is vividly attended to together with it, M. § 516, i.e. compared with it. M. § 626. Hence the substitution of the former for the latter in thinking beautifully, or the conjunction of the former with the latter, not without vividness, will yield the illustrating ARGUMENT FROM THINGS COMPARED, which some call the argument FROM MEDITATION, § 730; let us call it the figure, § 26, of COMPARISON and collation IN THE WIDER SENSE, which embraces assimilation, but spreads out also into many other kinds of arguments than those which are sought from the similar. E.g.

*Can there be such anger in celestial minds?* Aen. I. 15. and the illustration of Carthage that immediately follows: 1) from its age 2) from its inhabitants 3) from its site 4) from its riches 5) from its pursuits of war 6) from the favour of Juno, v. 16-22. Hence the hatred against Aeneas, [p. 491] with other causes compared, v. 23-36. Finally the epiphonema of the meditation:

*So great a labour it was to found the Roman race.*

§ 735. Just as among the augmenting arguments we saw augmentation, § 330, and among the proving ones aetiology, § 545, to be the principal figure, so that all the others pertaining to augmenting or proving seem each to be comprehended in its bosom: so let us hold comparison in the wider sense to be the general and principal figure among the illustrating ones, § 734, without which not even hypotyposis can exist, § 733, and to which, as to a fountain, the rest, when at last considered more deeply, run together. For since from one thing that is similar, equal, congruent, or in general notably the same, the other similar, equal, congruent and notably same thing can be cognised, hence also more vividly cognised and illustrated, § 730; M. § 70, 572; comparison in the wider sense will comprehend under itself the illustrating argument 1) from the similar, equal, congruent, notably same, which figure is called, from its chief part, ASSIMILATION, § 734, if similar, equal, congruent, notably same things are conjoined. One of these can also, in thinking, be substituted for the other.

§ 736. The more similar, the less unequal, the more congruent and proportioned the things that an assimilation compares with one another, [p. 492] according to the balance of the analogue of reason, the more beautiful it will be, § 735. The predicates common to the things assimilated, or compared, are called the TERTIUM COMPARATIONIS. In judging the tertium comparationis there is also a certain measuring of quantity, § 314, 315. An illustrating argument from the similar cannot be put forward by a man of sound mind with the intention that one of the compared things be held to be totally the same as the other, M. § 269; hence, when some difference has been detected between them, it is wrongly said that every simile limps. If in thinking of A you wish to illustrate C, and hence take up B, similar τῷ [to] A, in which you can contemplate the same C beautifully and clearly, then A, the SUBJECT OF THE ASSIMILATION, is illustrated through B, the TERM OF THE ASSIMILATION, a simile that does not limp, as regards the tertium comparationis C, even if B could have some predicate D which is not in A. But if that C itself, constituted as the tertium comparationis for whose sake the whole assimilation is carried out, nonetheless stands far otherwise, whether in quality, or in quantity, or in both, according to the very balance of the analogue of reason, in B than in A, then I should not deny that the beauty of the assimilation suffers, § 315.

§ 737. A difference of quantity detected in the tertia comparationis of things assimilated will at least give occasion for an excuse, § 736, such as:

[p. 493] *if it is permitted to compare great things with small,*

and that of Ovid, Trist. I. 5, comparing the piety of his wife with the matronly piety of Livia:

*If it is permitted to liken grand things to small.*

An example of assimilation will be given by Cicero’s Hortensius: *As those who wish to take up the purple dye first steep the wool in certain preparations: so by letters and by such teachings the mind must first be cultivated and be imbued and prepared for receiving wisdom.* The same, for Roscius: *As fire,* he says, *thrown into water is at once quenched and cooled: so a seething false charge, cast upon a most pure and most chaste life, immediately collapses and is extinguished.* Likewise: *As you cannot find crop or tree in every field: so not every crime arises in every life.*

§ 738. The rule which bids us prefer, to arguments that merely illustrate, those which at the same time exhibit some other usefulness, § 731, Quintilian VIII. 3 illustrates thus by assimilation: *Should I think that estate the better cultivated in which someone showed me lilies and violets and pleasant gushing springs, rather than one where there will be a full harvest or vines heavy with fruit? Should I prefer the barren plane-tree and clipped myrtles to the wedded elm and the fruitful olives? Let the rich have those things, by all means; what would they be, if they had nothing else? — — — — And I too shall bring my trees into order and fixed* [p. 494] *intervals. What, I ask, is more handsome than that quincunx? what straighter, in whatever direction you look? But straightaway it is also useful for this, that they draw the sap of the earth equally. The tops of the olive that rise too high I shall check with the knife; it will spread more beautifully into a circle, and will straightaway bear fruit on more branches. The horse whose flanks are tight is more comely, and the same is faster. Handsome to look upon is the athlete whose arms exercise has brought out, and the same is readier for the contest. True beauty is never divided from usefulness.*

§ 739. For assimilation, § 735, *in general this is chiefly to be guarded against, that what we have adopted for the sake of the similitude be neither obscure nor unknown. For what is taken up for the sake of illustrating another thing ought itself to be clearer than that which it illuminates. Wherefore let us by all means allow poets examples of this kind:*

*As when Apollo leaves wintry Lycia and the streams of Xanthus, / or visits his mother’s Delos.*

*The same will not befit the orator, that he should demonstrate the open by the hidden.* Quint. l. c. Let us state the law more generally: The term of every comparison and illustration, hence also of assimilation, should be, for our spectators, to whom we must chiefly attend, better known and more vivid than the subject of the comparison, illustration, and hence also [p. 495] assimilation, § 736. Hence a poet such as Virgil, who could suppose his readers, those chiefly to be attended to, thoroughly versed in the poetic world of Greek mythology, can rightly set up the assimilation that Quintilian cites, as also that of Dido coming into the temple, Aen. I. 502:

*As on the banks of Eurotas, or along the ridges of Cynthus, / Diana leads her dances, whom a thousand Oreads, following, / throng on this side and that. She bears a quiver / on her shoulder, and as she walks towers above all the goddesses; / joys thrill the silent breast of Latona. / Such was Dido, so she bore herself joyfully / through their midst, pressing on the work and the kingdom to come.*

§ 740. The orator, who is bound to take account of a hearer that is above all the people, and neither a mythologist nor a geographer to any great extent, abstains from these assimilations, together with the poet writing for Christians, § 600. In the bucolics the assimilation is beautiful:

*The white privets fall, the dark bilberries are gathered,*

although the term of the assimilation may be somewhat obscure to a reader skilled neither in the countryside nor in botany, since bucolics do not [p. 496] have a reader of that kind as their primary personal object.

§ 741. *That kind of similitude of which I have spoken adorns the oration and makes it sublime,* § 336, *florid,* § 693, *pleasing, admirable. For the further each is fetched, the more novelty it brings and the more unexpected it is.* Quint. l. c. He opposes the *further fetched* to the *common.* Nothing indeed is more discordant than to serve up trite and common illustrations to those expecting beautiful things. For since, as soon as you have begun, they provoke nausea, and a disgust at hearing or watching, in general at attending, how could they heighten the clarity of what is to be illustrated, which rather they extinguish and smother? But to these hackneyed and trite comparisons from Phoebus, from Alexander the Great, from the sun I should oppose new and unusual ones rather than ones fetched from too far. A FAR-FETCHED ARGUMENT is one in which an aesthetically illegitimate leap is committed, one whose elegant combination, namely, with the thing chiefly to be thought is observable with difficulty or not at all by the analogue of reason of the principal spectators to whom you must attend, because of the multitude of intermediate perceptions that are omitted. Arguments, hence figures, illustrations, comparisons and similitudes fetched from too far, because they linger around a wide-open circle, [p. 497] § 138, hostile to roundness, as they open a gap, § 166, against aesthetic unity, § 439, since the term and the subject of the comparison are so distant that no inseparability can be elegantly detected in them, hence distract the attention rather than diffuse it evenly, bid it stick and despair rather than continue right up to the ἀκμὴν [peak], whatever appearance of novelty they may feign, are rather to be guarded against and cut away than sought for the sake of admiration.

#### Sectio XXXXIIII. Comparatio Maioris et Minoris. — Section XXXXIIII. Comparison of the Greater and the Lesser

§ 742. Comparison in the wider sense, the principal figure among the illustrating ones, § 735, comprehends under itself 2) the comparison of a part with the whole, of the whole with its parts, of the superior, of genera and species with the inferior genera, species and individuals, of the inferior, the individual, the species, the genus with the superior species or genus under which it is contained. For who does not know that from the whole the part, from the claw the lion, from genera the species and individuals, from Sinon the Pelasgians, [p. 498] from the Greeks men can be cognised, and even more vividly cognised, hence illustrated? § 730. But since the whole is greater than its parts, and the superior concept is greater and wider, and comprehends more under itself, than the inferior one comprehended under the same together with others, let this comparison of such concepts, as described, be called the COMPARISON OF THE GREATER AND THE LESSER: ASCENDING, if the subject of the comparison is the lesser, a part, or some inferior concept; DESCENDING, if the subject of the comparison is the greater, the whole or some superior concept.

§ 743. An ascending comparison is a) if we render a part and some variation of a certain whole more illustrious from the whole of the same. Virgil, Georg. I, has chosen among the first rules of the husbandman *to learn beforehand*

*the ancestral tillage and the habits of places, / And what each region bears, what each refuses.* 53.

Since he is speaking of the tillage and habit of a single place, which some one *greedy husbandman,* v. 46, may possess, he has as the subject of the comparison a tiny part of the earth; about to illustrate what he understands by the learning beforehand of its ancestral tillage and habits, he takes as the term of the comparison [p. 499] the whole of this field, the entire earth; throughout which everywhere

*Here corn, there grapes come more happily, / Elsewhere the fruits of trees, and unbidden grow green / The grasses,* 56. / — — — — / *From the first nature imposed these laws and eternal covenants / on fixed places, at the time when first / Deucalion cast stones into the empty world,* 62.

§ 744. An ascending comparison is b) if we illustrate some perception presenting something inferior and more determinate through a perception which exhibits what is superior and less determinate to the said inferior. Hence an ascending comparison of this character will be the illustration α) of an individual through its species or some genus under which the given individual is contained. Mercury himself, about to persuade Aeneas that Dido is to be feared, compares with her her sex, as it were the genus:

*A fickle thing,* he says, *and ever changeable / is woman,* Aen. IIII. 569.

β) of a species through some genus of its own. A species of rural economy, agriculture,

*sharpening mortal hearts with cares,* Georg. I. 123.

is illustrated from its genera:

*toil conquers all,* / [p. 500] *Relentless toil, and want that presses in hard circumstances,* 146.

γ) of a genus through its superior genus. The genus of seeds degenerating after some years is illustrated in the same place from its genus, all but the supreme one:

*Thus by fate all things / Rush to the worse and, slipping back, are borne backward?* 199.

§ 745. This ascending comparison into a species or genus they sometimes call illustration from a commonplace, now from a maxim, § 526, now they advise seeking it through the 10 pigeon-holes of the predicaments, and promise it will be found. § 133, 134. For us, if by natural reason any argument of this kind offers itself to those about to think beautifully, we shall make the more of it for this reason, that it illustrates in such a way that 1) it always at the same time proves, § 547-549, which is elegant, § 731; 2) it can be a heuristic means to a more elegant comparison, whenever, when A, an individual, species or some genus, has been thought, there first comes to mind B, under which A is contained, as a species superior τοῦ [to] A, or a subalternating genus. But let this same B then, according to the natural association of ideas or notions, reproduce other perceptions besides A contained under itself, C, D, E, etc., whether of individuals under the same species, or of species sharing the same genus with A, or of genera contained with A under the same superior genus, now [p. 501] one, now several. For then the illustration τοῦ [of] A through B will be an ascending comparison, from which arises an illustration τοῦ [of] A through C, or D, or E, one or several, an assimilation. § 735.

§ 746. A descending comparison, § 742, will be a) if some whole is illustrated α) either from some part of it, β) or by an enumeration of several parts and marks contained in the same whole, which the logicians call PARTITION. Aeneas, about to illustrate the horse,

*Like a mountain, the horse,*

so that from the given magnitude of one part his hearers may measure for themselves the mass of the remaining parts and the extent of the whole, adds, by a descending comparison into one part:

*and they interweave its ribs with sawn fir.* Aen. II, 16.

§ 747. Partition, § 746, either lies more hidden in every definition, description and hypotyposis, with their species, § 733, since every whole that is to be defined or described is illustrated, in the course of defining and describing, by a review of certain of its marks and parts, by which we at the same time obtain a proving argument, § 550, 731; or, more open and more elegantly elaborated, it retains more strictly the name of a peculiar figure, until it is comprehended under the common denomination, [p. 502] soon to be indicated, together with another descending comparison, which will follow. It can be wrapped in praeteritions and in a certain heap of synathroismus, a proof of copiousness, § 148. The ἀπαρίθμησις [enumeration] of sufficiently weighty parts can procure for that which we partition the magnitude and dignity sought, § 298, 348, beyond illustration, § 731. As much as the analogue of reason loses of intensive light and distinctness in the single marks and parts, so much the more does it enjoy extensive clarity through the number of parts or marks, § 730. Whence the primary object of a beautiful meditation, some whole, should not, if you wish to think prettily, be lightly dismissed without some declaration of its parts. Yet I should hardly advise setting this before the eyes complete and explicitly. It is better to carry on a partition once begun for a while, yet not to finish it before you have cast some of its parts so into shade that the spectator seems still to have things which he may deliver to himself, things which lie hidden, and which he will perhaps think better and more numerous than if you had seemed to exhaust the whole by partitioning it. § 654.

§ 748. We have already seen Virgil going through the parts of the shield which Aeneas’s mother was giving him, § 459. Catullus, c. 63, where he partitions

The coverlet, which *varied with the ancient figures of men / Shows forth the virtues of heroes with wondrous art,* 50.

[p. 503] let him follow who will, from v. 52-265, where nonetheless, going on with *Such things,* not *these,* he indicates that he has not yet finished the partition, § 747. Let us excerpt some sub-partition, to speak barbarously with the school:

*But in another part flourishing Iacchus was flitting,* v. 251. / *With his band of Satyrs and the Nysa-born Sileni, / Seeking you, Ariadna, and inflamed with love for you. / Who then, eager, were raging here and there with frenzied mind, / Crying Euoe, tossing their heads with Euoe.* (Behold the whole!) / *Some of them were shaking thyrsi with covered point, / Some were tossing the limbs of a torn-apart bullock, / Some were girding themselves with twisted serpents, / Some were celebrating the dark orgies with hollow caskets, / Orgies which the profane desire in vain to hear. / Others were beating drums with upraised palms, / Or raising thin tinklings from the rounded bronze. / Many were blowing horns with hoarse-sounding booms.* etc.

Compare Ovid’s description of the palace of the Sun, Met. II. 5-18, and that of Corinna, with its:

*Why should I recount each thing?*

begun rather than explicitly finished in all its parts.

§ 749. A descending comparison, § 742, will be b) if [p. 504] something superior, whether a genus or a species, is illustrated through the inferior things which it contains under itself, either one or several. In the former case it will be an example, § 526; in the latter a division, which, if it is aesthetically verisimilar that it has proceeded at least beyond the half-way point towards completion, is AESTHETIC INDUCTION. DIVISION, or the enumeration of perceptions contained under some given one, together with aesthetic partition, whether complete or incomplete, if they are thought more elegantly, as figures, are distinguished by the name of DISTRIBUTION. § 747. A descending comparison of this character, therefore, will illustrate α) some superior genus through an inferior one which it contains under itself, a genus, or species, or individual, now one, now several, β) a lowest genus through its species and individual, and the perception contained under it, either one or several, γ) a species through the individual which it contains under itself, either one or several.

§ 750. Let the greater examples of examples be, first, all fables, § 526. The Aeneid splendidly declares:

*a man distinguished for piety / to have rolled through so many misfortunes, to have undergone so many labours.*

With several examples the Iliad illustrates this:

*When kings go mad, the Achaeans are punished.*

[p. 505] Lucretius declares the empire of Venus with examples at first quite general:

*Since through you every kind of living thing / Is conceived, and, having arisen, looks upon the light of the sun* l. 5. / — — — — / *First the birds of the air signify you, goddess, and your coming, / Their hearts struck through by your power. / Then the wild cattle bound over the glad pastures, / And swim across rapid rivers. So, captured by your charm / And your allurements, all the nature of living things / Follows you eagerly, wherever you go on to lead each one.*

Yet he seems not to have been able to rest before he descended, in illustrating the empire of Venus by examples, right down to a single person, and the suppositum of Mars,

*who often throws himself back into your lap, / Vanquished by the eternal wound of love, / And so, gazing up, with rounded neck thrown back, / Feeds his greedy eyes with love, gaping upon you, goddess.* etc. v. 38.

§ 751. The same, to his general assertions:

*more often of old / Religion has brought forth criminal and impious deeds,* v. 84. / [p. 506] *So much evil could religion persuade,* 103.

does not include innumerable species of superstitions by way of example, but some one atrocious deed,

*In what manner at Aulis the altar of the Trivian virgin / The chosen leaders of the Danaans foully defiled / With the blood of Iphianassa,* etc.

Nor does Homer stop at recounting some species of the ships making for Troy, descending to an enumeration of single ones. Virgil is not content with a certain general distribution of those preparing war against Aeneas throughout Italy:

*Ausonia, unstirred and immovable before, is ablaze. / Some prepare to go on foot over the plains, some rage / On tall horses, high in the dust; all call for arms. / Some polish smooth shields and shining spear-points / With rich fat, and sharpen axes on the whetstone, / And they delight to bear the standards and to hear the sound of trumpets.* VII. 628.

But he proceeds by descending to singulars, v. 630-817.

§ 752. The reason for this, which we have searched out more deeply, § 556-565, and from which we have deduced, § 569, 575, that rule which the manner of thinking, both the simply lucid [p. 507] in aesthetic matters and especially the splendid, demands, § 625, may be commended anew here, in its most proper place, expressed in general and in the terms received in the school: If the logical and scientific manner of thinking more willingly considers its primary objects, not even excepting individuals themselves, where other things are equal, in the abstract: one who is about to think beautifully should most willingly contemplate with the analogue of reason his principal materials not only in the concrete, but also in the most determinate things in which he can, hence in singulars, supposita, persons, deeds, as often as it is granted. M. § 149.

§ 753. Hence an ascending comparison will not lightly thrust itself into the greatest wholes and the highest genera of things, when the subject of the comparison can be, equally well, not only illustrated but also proved by comparing some nearer whole, a species or genus less remote, § 575, 752; since by leaping over these without reason you would produce a far-fetched argument, § 741, and would choose a term of comparison perhaps less known to your primary spectators than its subject is, § 736, 739; since knowledge of the superior genera and of the greatest wholes is for the most part acquired only by the sciences that consider things in the abstract, § 567. Thus in the ascending comparison which § 743 exhibits, [p. 508] Virgil does not appeal to the necessary diversity of all things, M. § 269-272, which even then was not unknown, but stays with the earth and its diversity of fruits.

§ 754. Hence an ascending comparison, having mentioned the whole or the superior, most willingly makes for itself the occasion of descending again from this whole or universal into certain co-parts of the subject of the comparison and other things of the same genus or species, § 745, since in this way the whole and the genus are presented more in the concrete, § 752. To his abstractions, however slight, l. c., Virgil at once subjoins more determinate things, with which, so to speak, they can grow together within the mind into something more vivid:

*Do you not see how Tmolus sends saffron scents, / India ivory, the soft Sabaeans their frankincense, / But the naked Chalybes iron, and Pontus its rank / Castor, Epirus the palms of Elean mares?*

§ 755. Hence, § 752, a descending comparison, to parts that are to be distinguished only in the abstract, e.g. by boundaries established by the surveyor’s method, to marks and characters that are to be conceived by the mind alone through the intellect, to generic or even specific differences, which always leave something indeterminate, will, other things being equal, [p. 509] prefer parts observable in the concrete by the senses themselves, such as in fields with natural boundaries, marks and characters that can be properly grasped at once by sensing and imagining, the differences of the inferior genus to the differences of the superior, those of the species, i.e. specific differences, to generic differences, and to all these it will prefer numerical ones, if it wishes to be thought beautifully, being, to speak barbarously, especially studious of haecceity. M. § 517.

§ 756. Examples, inductions, divisions and distributions, § 749, illustrating a superior genus through an inferior one, are good; through species, better; through individuals, best, § 755. In Ovid you have Metam. III. 206:

*While he hesitates, the dogs saw him.*

Of these there are genera, there are species; but the genera are neglected, and the most determinate species are reviewed, yet only as bound to their proper names:

*and first Melampus / And keen-scented Ichnobates gave the signal with their barking, / Ichnobates of Gnossus, Melampus of Spartan breed.*

After he had named 35 of these, not without certain numerical differences, he nonetheless adds:

*And those whom it would be tedious to recount,* 225. § 747.

§ 757. [p. 510] Would that by so many examples of the ancients, which assuredly did not slip from them inadvertently, as the difficulty of attempting the same will sufficiently teach, beautiful wits could be roused to seek to excel above all in this art, if they are less moved by reasons, § 752. We must hasten on to the other rules of examples and distributions. If examples, divisions, inductions and distributions are rightly required to be better known than the superior thing to be illustrated through them, § 739, we admit the law only as understood relative to the primary personal object of one who is about to think beautifully, § 740. Ovid, well, to his wish, Trist. l. I. 2, 3:

*Nor, I pray, subscribe to the wrath of great Caesar*

first adjoins an ascending comparison, which is at the same time a colour, § 745, 545:

*Often when one god presses, another god brings help.*

Now he illustrates his saying by an induction of examples, which become similar to the wish:

*Mulciber stood against Troy, for Troy stood Apollo. / Venus was fair to the Teucrians, Pallas unfair. / Saturnia, closer to Turnus, hated Aeneas, / Yet he was safe by the divinity of Venus. / Often fierce Neptune assailed wary Ulysses, / Often Minerva snatched him from her uncle. / And for us, though we are distant from them,* § 737, / *What forbids a divinity to stand by against an angry god?* 12.

[p. 511] For to his primary spectators what was better known than the affairs of Troy, and the ministries of the gods through them? § 740.

§ 758. Since new things can generally be supposed better known than old, and nearer places than more remote ones, as examples, M. § 512, then, other things being equal, let the recent be preferred to the ancient, the native to the foreign, and, for the spectator whom you especially desire, the domestic, § 757. The Dialogue on Orators, which some attribute to Tacitus, others to Quintilian, 8: *I more gladly*, it says, *use new and recent examples than remote and obliterated ones.* Things so ancient that they may at the same time seem obsolete, obliterated, or worn and antiquated by long use are beautifully set after new ones in such a way that these, at the same time, have the wherewithal to arouse and feed a just zeal for novelty, § 741. Virgil, about to illustrate the praises of rural life by examples, does not seek Greek ones, nor Asia, nor Egypt, but, writing above all for Romans, consults the antiquities of his fatherland: Georg. l. II. 532.

*This life the old Sabines once cultivated,* / *this, Remus and his brother. Thus brave Etruria grew,* / *and thus, indeed, Rome became the most beautiful of things,* / *and alone encircled her seven citadels with a wall,* / — — — — / *Golden Saturn led this life on earth.*

§ 759. [p. 512] In this law it must be well noted what is added to it: other things being equal, § 758. For in recent examples there can often be many reasons why a prudent author should set them after older ones, e.g. 1) if the recent ones either truly do not have that excellent thing which is to be illustrated through them in as great a light as antiquity had it, or at least, on account of an envy not yet extinct and the customary praises of time past, it would be contrary to verisimilitude, at least that of which § 486, to lay down that ancient things are equalled. Hence in Virgil, in the passage cited, there is a rather deep silence about the Italian agriculture of his own time, but at the same time the counsel that in such a case domestic things, even if done long ago, should nevertheless be preferred, if it can be done, to the ancient and foreign at once. About to bring forward examples of the blessed rural life present in his own time, in a philosophico-poetic ecstasy, v. 475–485, since he cannot wish for what is nearest the court, he nevertheless wishes for what is less remote:

*May the fields and the streams watering the valleys please me;* / *inglorious, let me love the rivers and the woods. O, where are the plains,* / *and Spercheus, and Taygetus, where the Laconian maidens* / *hold their revels! O, who will set me in the cold glens of Haemus* / *and shelter me with a vast shade of branches!* 489.

§ 760. [p. 513] Recent examples are sometimes 2) dangerous, since their truth breeds hatred, and it would rather be called the most false calumny and besmirching of the most illustrious names than an illustration.

*Cervius, angered, threatens laws and the urn;* / *Canidia threatens those to whom she is hostile with Albucius’ poison;* / *Turius, a great evil, if you contend over anything with him as judge.* Hor. Sat. l. I. 49.

Nor is it always permitted here to have recourse to Virgil’s artifice, § 759, and to write against the dead, indeed, yet fellow-citizens and those nearer in place, either sharply, or, more charmingly, to be angry,

*lest some friend of the great* / *strike you with his coldness.*

For this reason, in choosing their examples, satirists sometimes like to flee beyond the Sauromatae, into the poetic world, § 513, in which, however, they set fictions altogether unknown once more, § 518, after analogical ones, § 516.

§ 761. Recent and domestic examples are 3) sometimes rather unknown, even relatively, § 757, namely whenever they either are of a kind that only a long day is wont to place before the eyes of many, e.g. the present counsels of princes, or the foremost of our spectators themselves, [p. 514] most greedy for foreign things, are wont to neglect domestic ones, or to disdain recent things set before their feet, wholly absorbed in digging up whatever is most ancient. For although this custom is not of the best taste, nevertheless he who is going to think beautifully must attend to his personal object as it is, not as it ought to be, and hence as one destitute of anticipations and of the zeal for knowing, if you bring forward domestic things and

*Things unheard by the ancient Cethegi,* § 484.

§ 762. Let examples, divisions, inductions and distributions be quite sufficiently known, and neither worn and hackneyed illustrations, § 741, nor, on the contrary, so vivid that by their stronger light they obscure rather than illustrate their superior perception, against the beautiful dispensation of light and shade, § 682. In the last law one must especially beware lest the difference, and that in the lower concept which is not determined in the higher, receive so much light that the third terms of the comparison, and those things agreeing with the higher which you were labouring to paint more vividly in the concrete of the lower, be overshadowed, and so the example seem to fit less and to disagree with that for whose declaring it was brought forward, § 670. I do not dissuade you from touching the difference, but let it be the shade of the example, § 684, not the light, § 686. Lest examples, seducing under the appearance of erudition, overstep the aesthetic [p. 515] horizon, much less inductions and distributions, § 121, lest by illustrating they procure either βάθος [bathos] or tumidity, § 329, 331, let them rather be, as far as this can be done, such that from them a reasoning example and a persuasive induction can be formed at once, and practical or pragmatic ones, fruitful in porisms to be drawn from them that impel to action, § 731.

#### Sectio XXXXV. Antithesis. — Section XXXXV. Antithesis

§ 763. Comparison in the wider sense, § 735, contains under itself 3) the comparison of opposites, M. § 549, which figure, § 26, let be ANTITHESIS. Among antitheses I number first dissimilitude, which compares what is to be illustrated with something dissimilar, so that the difference of the former from the latter may shine forth more. Cat. 5.

*Suns can set and return;* / *for us, when once our brief light has set,* / *there is one perpetual night to be slept.*

By this collation Horace illustrated his whole ode l. I. 1, and the front of ode 7 of the same book.

[p. 516] *Others will praise bright Rhodes, or Mytilene,* / *or Ephesus, or the walls of Corinth* / *between two seas, or Thebes famed for Bacchus, or Delphi* / *for Apollo, or Thessalian Tempe.* 4–10.

§ 764. Next there belongs here antisagoge, which they call compensation, a species of confession, which places certain predicates of the thing to be illuminated, which seem contrary to the primary one, in the shade in such a way, next to this primary one, that their heterogeneous but weaker perceptions, having gone before, suddenly paint the following heterogeneous but greater and more illustrious one splendidly, and surround it as it were with the light of novelty, § 659, 683, M. § 549. Iarbas in Virgil, Aen. IIII, seems at first to describe the furtive loves of Dido and Aeneas quite honourably, covering with the shade of honesty whatever he judged unworthy and base in them:

*Dido* / *received Aeneas as lord into her kingdom,* 214.

so that not much later he may inveigh more bitterly, with his strength as it were gathered, against the same Aeneas:

*And now that Paris, with his half-man retinue,* / *his chin and dripping hair propped up* / *with a Maeonian turban, enjoys what he has snatched.* 217.

§ 765. If you continue the antisagoge, from it will be born a beautiful [p. 517] CRAMA, which they call praise mixed with blame; it is truly a heap of predicates, at least apparently contrary, of which one class is reckoned bad, the other good, yet so disposed, through the artifice that dispenses light and shade well, S. XXXX, that, according to the intention of those who cunningly mix good with bad, either the latter from the beauty of their opposites gain increases of baseness, or the former from the foulness of their opposites gain increases of beauty, and at the same time a great likeness of veracity, of moral truth, is obtained, § 731, 435. As far as the matter permitted, almost the whole first elegy of Naso’s Trist. II labours in shaping this figure, conceding the author’s guilt so as to solicit the more effectively Caesar’s more illustrious pardon, e.g.

*Why do I now return to the Muses, my crimes, once condemned?* / *Is it too little to have deserved punishment once?* 4. / — — — — / *Take from me my pursuit, and you will take away the crimes of my life too;* / *I set it to the account of my verses that I am guilty.* 10. / Your anger *is indeed just, nor will I deny that I deserved it;* / *shame has not so far fled from my face.* 30. / — — — — / *But yet, as a god is won by the shed blood of a hundred bulls,* / *so is he won by the smallest honour of incense.* / [p. 518] *And I gave pious incense for you, and, one among all,* / *I too aided the public vows with my own.*

§ 766. There is no reason why we should wish to distinguish antithesis and antitheton, or antitheta, whenever antitheta are compared more elegantly, which then usually happens if it is done *without effort*, and seems to have to happen so by a certain natural *necessity*, Cic. or. 166. Otherwise, if, too studiously sought, they smell of the lamp, they will be cosmetic, of those who quibble rather emptily, § 688, M. § 576, against whom Persius truly and gravely:

*Is it not shameful to be unable to ward off dangers from a white head* / *without wishing to hear this charming word: “neatly done”?* / *“You are a thief,” you say to Pedius. What does Pedius do? He weighs the charges* / *in smooth antitheses. He is praised for having set learned figures:* / *“That is pretty!” Pretty, this?* l. 83. § 677.

*The ancients delighted in this kind already before Isocrates, and most of all Gorgias.* Cic. or. 167. They delighted in antitheses, but not badly placed, not anxiously scraped together, not warmed up six hundred times; be a cautious imitator of them, not an ape, § 714.

§ 767. [p. 519] *But if one uses them sparingly and when the matter demands, like some sprinkled seasoning, he will be more pleasing. But he who affects them too much will lose that very grace of variety.* As all illustrating figures, so antitheses *that are secluded and placed outside common use, and therefore more noble, as they excite the ear by their novelty, so they sate it by their abundance, and declare that they did not come in the speaker’s way, but were sought out, and dragged from every hiding-place and heaped together,* Quint. VIIII. 3. Fabius in this very chapter also throws in a mention of antitheton, which he calls *contrapositum*, and says is named by others *contentio*. Of this above all is true his judgement which we have just read, and then most of all when at the same time some figure of diction accompanies it, by the similarity, or the repetition, or the transposition of words, § 26.

§ 768. For here too holds what we already noted in § 145, that the schema of words and their extraordinary arrangement or position, if you use figures in a manly way, is sometimes only accessory, and as it were fortuitous in figures which are not to be despised because they are figures of diction, but because they procure for the thoughts themselves an elegance, and indeed a principal one, they would not have had to be counted among figures of diction. Thus not a few refer antithesis wholly to the figures of diction, [p. 520] as if the opposition of words, and not of significates, were the principal thing. By others at least certain species of antithesis are placed among mere figures of diction, by arbiters of elegances no less unfair, § 13. I shall bring forward examples.

§ 769. Antitheton with some conversion of the perceptions occurring in each of the opposites, or ANTIMETABOLE, surely cannot be excluded from the figures of thought, § 763, on the ground that in the course of expression it can happen that the words by which each member of the opposition is expressed also undergo some conversion. Among figures of diction, however, Quintilian himself has *that figure in which words are repeated in a changed inflection: I do not live in order to eat, but I eat in order to live.* Pretty, assuredly, is not so much the diction as the thought of Epicurus, which you might truly call philosophical: It is better to be wisely unfortunate than to be fortunate unwisely. Here too, as regards the matter, belongs the saying of Simonides which is in Plutarch: Poetry is a speaking picture, and a picture is a silent poetry. Here that saying of Salvian about the Bagaudae: *They prefer to be free under the appearance of captivity, rather than captive under the appearance of liberty.* Here, finally, the saying of Cato and after him of Musonius: *If you have done something right through labour, the labour departs, the right deed remains with you; if you have done something base* [p. 521] *with pleasure, the baseness remains, the pleasure perishes.* Gell. XVI, 1.

§ 770. The collation of beautiful thinking with things different from it, with which it could easily be confused, in order to illustrate its difference, or PARADIASTOLE, and contradistinction, is an antithesis not only of words wrongly held to be synonyms, but also, and indeed most of all, of significates that truly differ, whose diversity, however, is usually not attended to, and hence is sometimes elegantly indicated, if it has moment, § 768. *To contentio* § 767 *is most conveniently subjoined also that species which we have called distinction,* Quint. l. c. Ovid on the edict by which he was ordered to go to Tomi:

*Indeed I am called relegated in it, not exile,* / *and sparing words have been given to my fortune.* Trist. II. 1. 137.

*Nor is it always what is adverse that is set in opposition,* Quint. l. c. For antithesis there is no need that a true opposition be present, such as we logicians call a contradiction truly so called; it suffices that an apparent contradiction, that contrariety or subcontrariety be present, and whatever, at least aesthetically and at first glance, is verisimilarly fighting brow to brow. In the sayings: Man was able not to die, he was able to die, now he is not able not to die naturally, but the time is at hand when he will not be able to die, [p. 522] there is nothing of contradiction, yet, more elegantly adorned, they could give an antithesis, § 763.

§ 771. But by what name shall I now commend the oxymoron? Ennius’: *demented minds*; Terence’s: *to be mad with reason*, and: *that something is nothing*; Cicero’s: *while they are silent, they cry out*; Virgil’s:

*Could the captured be captured?*

Horace’s, but he too jesting here:

*While I, a consultant of raving wisdom,* / *go astray.*

Ovid’s *unjust just* and:

*By these exhortations each, as she is pious, is first impious,* / *and, lest she be wicked, commits a wicked deed,*

which is the most wretched fate of an erring conscience,

*she is pious by impiety.*

that saying of many: *they smell bad who always smell good* — all these, I say, with how great authorities do they cry out, if I am silent, that the figure is pretty? Meanwhile I candidly confess that of all the figures of thought the one that pleases me least is this. The cause I think is the most frigid abuse of it, and the hackneyed examples, and the rather tasteless parodies of the most hackneyed, with which those resound who [p. 523] know no other acumen than one fruitful in paradoxes of this bran.

§ 772. Let it be permitted to fall back to a simpler antithesis, which they are wont to include under dissimilitude, § 763, though in truth we ought rather to call it inequality, when things that agree with one another in some third thing are compared precisely in order that their inequality may be illustrated, or, this being granted, that it may be declared that that is the lesser which seemed to my personal object the greater, or vice versa. Such an antithesis of Romulus and Augustus Ovid has, in order to prefer the latter to the former, Fast. l. II.

*Romulus, yield. This man by guarding makes your walls* / *great; you had given them for Remus to leap over.* / *You, Tatius and little Cures and Caenina felt;* / *under this leader both sides of the sun are Roman.* / *You had some little bit, I know not what, of conquered earth;* / *whatever is beneath high Jupiter, Caesar has.* / *You ravish; this man bids wives be chaste under his leadership;* / *you receive wrong into your grove, he removes it.* / *Violence was welcome to you; under Caesar the laws flourish.* / *You hold the name of master, he that of prince.* / *Remus accuses you; he gave pardon to enemies.* / *Your father made you a god; he made his father one.* 144.

Thus Cicero compares the expert in law and the expert in arms with each other, so as to illustrate the excellence of the latter.

[p. 524]

#### Sectio XXXXVI. Comparatio Strictius Dicta. — Section XXXXVI. Comparison in the Stricter Sense

§ 773. Comparison in the wider sense, § 734, bringing together with what is to be illustrated any associated, conjoined and connected idea whatever, which is neither a similar thing, § 735, nor a part, nor a whole, nor its superior, nor its inferior, S. XXXXIIII, nor its opposite, S. XXXXV, since it has received no special name, let it retain the general one, 4) COMPARISON IN THE STRICTER SENSE. From this it is already clear that the number of illustrations belonging here cannot be reckoned, nor can each one be marked by different denominations. It is better that certain open pens be kept, in which can be enclosed what will promote the business of charming meditation and is not to be admitted into places already shut and circumscribed by their limits. Here, then, refer by way of example the comparison a) of things caused with their causes, which the school calls external, efficient and final. For the comparison of the greater and the lesser will have claimed for itself the internal ones, matter and form, § 742.

§ 774. Hence you have the illustration α) of an effect from its efficient cause.

[p. 525] *Cups, the chased work of divine Alcimedon.* Virg. ecl. 3. 37.

β) of the efficient cause from its effect:

*Conon, and who was the other,* / *who marked out with his rod the whole globe for the nations,* / *what seasons the reaper, what the bent ploughman should keep?* v. 42.

γ) of the means from the end:

*Daphnis’ bow* / *and reeds you broke, which you, perverse Menalcas,* / *both grieved over when you saw them given to the boy,* / *and, had you not done him some harm, you would have died.* v. 15.

δ) of the end from the means:

*Gifts are prepared for my Venus. For I myself have marked* / *the place where the airy wood-pigeons have built their nest.* / M. *What I could, ten golden apples picked from a woodland tree* / *I have sent to the boy; tomorrow I shall send another ten.* 71.

Lo! there are shepherds for you, who

were happily able *to know the causes of things!*

§ 775. Comparison in the stricter sense is b) of the sign and the signified, § 773. Hence the illustration α) of the sign from the signified. Virgil had described the signs portending the death of Caesar in Georg. l. 464–488, [p. 526] and he illustrates them from the signified, according to aesthetic verisimilitude,

*Therefore Philippi saw Roman battle-lines a second time* / *clash among themselves with equal weapons,* / *nor was it unworthy of the gods above that Emathia and the broad plains* / *of Haemus should twice grow fat with our blood,* etc. 492.

The poet at once adds an illustration of this β) signified from a sign now rememorative, or mnemonic, and then future:

*Surely the time will also come when in those lands* / *the farmer, working the earth with his curved plough,* / *will find javelins eaten away with scabrous rust,* / *or with heavy harrows will strike empty helmets* / *and marvel at the great bones in the dug-up graves.* 497.

§ 776. Comparison in the stricter sense, e.g. c) suggests to those who are going to illustrate the affections or changes of souls the associated ideas of the members of the body, which, since it is verisimilar that, given the affections, they are especially affected and more closely touched amid changes of that kind, are considered as it were the seats of these various things in the soul. Thus wit is illustrated by the head and the brain, external sensations by the sense organs, appetites by the heart, the lower ones by the liver, shame by the brow and the face, etc., § 773, 752.

[p. 527] *Those who understand the language of birds,* / *and know more from another’s liver than from their own,* / *I judge are to be heard rather than hearkened to.* Pac. ap. Cic. de div. l. e. c.

*Shame* / *let us squeeze out of the dog’s iron face.* Cat.

*do you see, with what toil of mind and head* / *you would avoid what you believe to be the greatest evils,* / *a small income and a shameful rejection?* Hor. ep. l. 44.

§ 777. Further, comparison in the strict sense d) illustrates an event and a deed, any singular thing whatever, from place and time, through the associated ideas of things perceived in the same place, at the same time, § 773, 752.

*O citizens, citizens! money must be sought first,* / *virtue after cash: this Janus from top to bottom* / *teaches forth; these dictates the young re-chant, and the old,* / *with satchel and tablets hung from the left arm.* 55.

*Sharp winter is loosened by the welcome change of spring and Favonius,* / *and the machines drag down the dry keels.* / *And no longer does the flock delight in the stalls, nor the ploughman in the fire,* / [p. 528] *nor do the meadows whiten with hoary frosts.* / *Now Cytherean Venus leads the dances beneath the hanging moon,* / *and the comely Graces joined with the Nymphs* / *shake the earth with alternate foot, while blazing Vulcan* / *urges on (visits) the heavy workshops of the Cyclopes.* / *Now it befits to bind the gleaming head either with green myrtle,* / *or with the flower which the loosened earth brings forth.* od. l. 4.

§ 778. Comparison in the strict sense e) brings together a thing and a person which is in someone’s power, and him who is its master, leader, commander or possessor. Hence the illustration α) of a thing from its possessor, master, god, in which manner Virgil describes Aeolia

*Here in a vast cave king Aeolus* / *presses the struggling winds and the sounding storms* / *under his rule, and bridles them with chains and prison.* Aen. I. 58.

β) of a person from the superior, leader, prince, king, to whom he is subjected. Virgil had changed the winds into persons; hence they are illustrated through their king:

*Aeolus sits on a lofty citadel* / [p. 529] *holding the sceptre, and soothes their spirits and tempers their wrath.*

given by Jove,

*who by a fixed covenant* / *should know, as bidden, both to tighten and to give the loose reins,* 67.

§ 779. The comparison of possessor and possessed gives the illustration γ) of the possessor from the thing possessed:

*Not to him the rule of the sea and the fierce trident,* / *but to me was it given by lot. He holds the monstrous rocks,* / *your homes, Eurus. In that hall let Aeolus* / *vaunt himself, and reign in the closed prison of the winds.* 145.

δ) of the leader, prince, commander, god from his subjects and the persons set in his power, § 778. Horace to Augustus:

*To you, present among us, we bestow timely honours,* / *and set up altars to swear by your divinity,* / *confessing that nothing like it will arise elsewhere, nothing has arisen.* / *But this people of yours, wise and just in this one thing,* / *in preferring you to our leaders, you to the Greeks,* / *estimates the rest in nowise by a similar reasoning* / *and manner.* ep. II. 1. 20.

[p. 530]

#### Sectio XXXXVII. Tropi. — Section XXXXVII. Tropes

§ 780. By a TROPE I here do not attend only to what, insofar as it *is a change of a word or an expression from its proper signification to another with excellence*, Quint. VIII. 6, much less to the one which necessity and the poverty of a language make necessary, whenever something must be signified for which there is no proper word in the given language, least of all to the one which ignorance of propriety forges in speaking: but to every elegant substitution of one perception for another, whether it be signified by transferred words, which is the best known, or by sounds substituted for one another by the musician, or by colours by the painter, or through any other kind of signs whatever you elegantly express that you have thought one thing in place of another.

§ 781. Let us look at the charming genesis of tropes, and with the throw of a little dust can be settled that *inexplicable* fight, as Fabius calls it in his preface l. c., if not *of the grammarians among themselves*, at least *of the philosophers, about the trope, what its genera are, what its species, what their number, which is subordinate to which?* Whether it is a figure, etc. In a certain place of your beautiful meditation [p. 531] let vividness demand some comparison, § 734, which, however, drawn out, ample and copious, explicitly thinking 1) the subject 2) the term of the comparison, § 736, rounded brevity does not admit in that same place, § 166: you will have occasion for an exception, § 24. The exception by which vividness is thus satisfied, so that rounded brevity suffers no detriment thereby, will be the least of those possible, § 24, surely not inelegant, § 25. But if you now omit the subject of the comparison, and neither think it explicitly nor signify it by a special term, but substitute for it, and in its place explicitly think only, and signify only, the term of the comparison, leaving it to the hearer or spectator silently to unfold for himself from it the subject of the comparison, then you have satisfied the vividness that demanded a comparison, without rounded brevity having suffered any detriment thereby, since you have not increased the series of things explicitly thought by even one sign or little word. Hence is born the not inelegant substitution of the term of a comparison for its subject, a trope, § 780.

§ 782. What, then, are the genera of tropes? what the species? The same as those of comparison in the wider sense, § 734–779. What is their number? innumerable. Which is subordinate to which? The one which is not METAPHOR, a contracted assimilation, [p. 532] § 735–741, nor SYNECDOCHE, a contracted comparison of the greater and the lesser, S. XXXXIIII, nor IRONY, a contracted antithesis, S. XXXXV, will surely be METONYMY, a contracted comparison in the stricter sense, S. XXXXVI, just as the learned man who is called neither theologian, nor jurist, nor physician is by that very fact consigned by the academies to the philosophical order.

§ 783. Are figures tropes, or tropes figures? *Most have thought these to be tropes — — — — The use is the same. For they both add force to things, M. § 515, and afford grace, § 780, 26. Nor are there lacking those who impose the name of figure on tropes, among whom is C. Artorius Proculus. Indeed the likeness is so manifest that it is not easy to discern it — — — — I know how manifold and how scrupulous a dispute these things have. — — — — These things of which we speak, whether they are called tropes or figures, will produce the same effect. — — — — This, however, is to be noted, that trope and figure frequently coincide in the same thoughts. For speech is figured as much by transferred as by proper words. There is, moreover, no slight disagreement among authors both as to what the force of its name is, and how many its genera, and how numerous its species.* Quint. VIIII. 1.

*Grammarians contend, and the case is still before the judge.* Hor.

[p. 533] § 784. To a philosopher it may seem that a knot is being sought in a bulrush, § 781. Not all figures are tropes, § 26, 780. But that good Proculus would be more in the right, if you take the trope according to Quintilian’s notion somewhat extended, § 780, and do not refer to it any necessary transferences whatsoever, much less the tasteless ones of those ignorant of the language they speak. Every trope, as I have defined it, is a FIGURE, but a CRYPTIC one, whose genuine form does not appear at once, since it is a figure contracted through substitution § 782.

§ 785. The logicians of the schoolmen teach the EXPONIBLE PROPOSITION, cryptically composed of an affirmative and a negative, such as the exclusive, the exceptive, the restrictive, etc. Did I not fear being troublesome to Latin ears, I should call tropes exponible figures. For just as the school gave propositions that barbarous denomination because they need exposition, in order that the genuine form of each of the two parts out of which they have coalesced may appear; so the trope, in order that it may appear as a figure even to grammarians, who are not philosophers, needs exposition. If from the term of comparison, which alone is thought explicitly in the trope, the proper signification, you draw out the omitted subject of comparison, the improper signification, [p. 534] and, neglecting the love of brevity, think this explicitly and manifestly alongside the term of comparison, and declare that you are thinking both by terms and signs peculiar to each, THE TROPE IS EXPOUNDED.

§ 786. Expounding a metaphor you will have a manifest assimilation, § 735. Expounding a synecdoche you will see a comparison of greater and lesser. It is therefore either an ASCENDING SYNECDOCHE, or a DESCENDING one, § 742. Expound an irony: you will have an antithesis, § 763. Metonymy, finally, will be resolved into some comparison more strictly so called. § 773, 782. Whatever, therefore, has been said thus far about the figures mentioned, § 730–779, it is not necessary that we repeat it concerning tropes, the cryptic form of those figures.

§ 787. *On the whole metaphor is shorter than simile,* (manifest and explicit assimilation, § 735.) § 781, 782. *in that the latter is compared* (explicitly) *with the thing we wish to express* (the subject of comparison) *whereas the former is said in place of the thing itself. It is a comparison,* (and indeed a manifest assimilation) *that a man did something like a lion. It is a transference, when I say of a man, he is a lion.* (and indeed a metaphor) Quintilian VIII. 6. But metaphor is also bolder than comparison; hence men of learned taste who have ventured [p. 535] to compare a man with a lion do not venture rashly to call the same man a lion, § 313. I do not touch on the species of metaphor, since *we are no longer instructing boys, so that, once the genus has been received, they could not understand the species.* ib. Let these be avoided: 1) those whose *frequent use both obscures and fills with tedium,* § 731. 2) *the low, the sordid, the ugly,* § 736, 762. 3) *those far too great or, what happens more often, too small,* § 737, whence turgidity or βάθος [bathos]. 4) *the dissimilar,* in which the very third term of the comparison differs notably in the term and in the subject of assimilation, § 736. 5) *the harsh, i.e. drawn from a far-fetched likeness,* § 741. *A metaphor,* like every trope, § 781. *if it comes into another’s place, ought to be worth more,* § 26, *than that which it drives out.* Quintilian ib.

§ 788. For practice in the exposition of § 786 and the adjudication of § 787 let these be the examples:

*Man is a god to man, if he knows his duty.* Caecil.

*I myself believed / I was entrusting it to a learned man; I am entrusting it to the greatest of stones.* Plaut.

*Since wrinkles / disfigure you, and the snows upon your head.* Hor.

*Either an Umbrian pig, or a fat Etruscan. / [p. 536] Nor that any swimming timber’s rush / could have outstripped her, whether with oars / she had to fly, or with canvas. / She, afterwards a pinnace, before was / a leafy wood. For on the Cytherian ridge / she often gave forth a whistling with speaking hair.* Cat.

§ 789. Ascending synecdoche, § 786, will be an elegant substitution 1) of the whole for the part, § 780, 743. Plautus in jest calls a slave often flogged with elm rods *an Acheron of elms*.

*He will be a slave for eternity who will not know how to use little.* Hor.

Even eviternity itself, of course, is only a part of eternity. Virgil sometimes puts *elephant* for ivory, the elephant’s tooth. § 753. 2) of the superior for the inferior, § 744. Hither belong the εὐφημισμοί [euphemisms], with which those not gladly mindful of death would circumscribe the things that pertain to it; *if anything graver should befall him, etc.* for the consolation of mourners,

*A little something of consolation / sadder than the tears of Simonides.* Cat.

with which they used to veil the things of Venus.

*I come to you of my own accord to make supplication on account of my folly.* Plaut.

(the violation done to his daughter)

[p. 537] § 790. Descending synecdoche, § 786, is a charming substitution 1) of the part for the whole: § 746.

*Plotius and Varius, and Virgil, at Sinuessa / come to meet us, souls such that earth has borne / none more candid, nor is there another to whom I am more attached.* Hor. I. sat. V.

*Thus perhaps the tender Catullus dared / to send the sparrow to great Maro.* Mart. IIII. ep. 14.

(the poem about the sparrow for the whole book in which it is contained.) 2) of the inferior for the superior perception, § 749.

*You could shut out the fates from no place; when death / has come, in the midst of Tibur there is Sardinia.* Mart. 60.

The former is put for the most healthful place, the latter for a place which was held to be pestilential.

§ 791. Cicero’s ironic mockery is in *C. Verres, urban praetor, a holy and diligent man,* and in that against Clodius: *Your integrity has cleared you, believe me, your modesty has rescued you, your past life has saved you.* Nor is it without mockery, as Fabius rightly notes, the beginning of the speech for Ligarius: *A new crime, C. Caesar, and unheard of before this day.*

[p. 538] *Now graft your pears, Meliboeus, set your vines in rows! / — — — — / No doubt this is a toil for the gods above, this care disturbs / their rest!* Virg.

§ 792. Metonymy will be a charming substitution, § 782. 1) of the efficient cause for the effect:

*Tables rare without a Mentor.* Juv. VIII.

⁠2) of the effect for the cause:

*There dwell pale diseases, and sad old age.* Virg.

*So long as grey hair is far from you in your green years.* Hor. l. 9.

Grey hair is an effect of old age. 3) of the means for the end:

*Send me Phyllis, it is my birthday, Iollas!* Virg.

⁠4) of the end for the means. § 774.

*He slew the due honours upon the altars.* Virg.

§ 793. 5) of the sign for the thing signified:

*While for the boys every father and little mother turns pale,* Hor.

Pallor is a sign of fear. 6) of the thing signified for the sign. § 775.

*Now we have made you of marble for the time being, but then, / [p. 539] when the offspring have filled up the flock, be of gold.* Virg.

⁠7) of phenomena in the body for those that are wont to occur simultaneously with them in the soul, § 776.

*O! you, Bolanus, happy / in your brain! / O! unlucky I, / who purge my bile about the hour of the spring season!* Hor.

⁠8) of the affections of the soul for the state of the body corresponding to them:

*If it did not sit fixed and immovable in my soul / to wish to join myself to no one in the bond of wedlock, / after my first love deceived me, cheated by death, / — — — — / to this one fault perhaps I could have succumbed.* Aen. IV. 19.

§ 794. 9) of the place or the age, the time, the hour, the container, for the thing or person contained in that place or time. § 777.

*Five great cities, indeed, with anvils set up, / forge new weapons, mighty Atina and proud Tibur, / Ardea, and Crustumeri, and tower-bearing Antemnae.* Aen. VII. 630.

*Behold how all things rejoice in the age to come!* Ecl. IIII. 52.

[p. 540] 10) of what is contained in a time or place for the time and place:

*Now even the cattle seek the shades and the cool, / now even the thickets hide the green lizards, / and Thestylis, for the reapers wearied by the fierce heat, / pounds garlic and wild thyme, fragrant herbs.* Ecl. II. 10.

*We sought you in the lesser Campus, / you in the Circus, you in all the little books.* Cat.

(for the bookshops.)

§ 795. 11) of the god, the master, the leader, the ruler for the thing possessed or the person who is in his power: *Without Ceres and Bacchus Venus grows cold.*

*Now next door burns / Ucalegon.* Virg.

*Where is that man? / Do you think he should be devoured now, if you have caught sight of him? / While it is given, while it is hot, it is fitting that it be devoured.* Plaut.

Compare, if you please, § 141, the passage of Catullus cited. 12) of the thing, or person, which belongs to someone, as to a possessor or ruler, for this man, the possessor or ruler. § 778.

*Let arms yield to the toga.* Cic.

*The Rhodopeian heights wept, / and lofty Pangaea, and the Mavortian land of Rhesus,* Georg. IV. 461.

[p. 541] *He, whether the Parthians threatening Latium / he shall have driven subdued in a just triumph,* Hor. I. od. 12.

§ 796. What they call the metalepsis of the antecedent for the consequent, and of the consequent for the antecedent, and which by its own right and desert the metalepsis of concomitants accompanies, when one of simultaneous things is put for the other: this is metonymy, § 782, 773, since it substitutes perceptions mutually associated with one another, of which either both were sensations at a given time, or the one an imagination, the other a sensation, or the one a sensation, the other a foresight, M. § 576. Thus the antecedent is put for the consequent in Catullus’s euphemism about Atalanta’s apple,

*Which loosed the girdle long tied,* 2.

and in Virgil, when he tells of Silenus singing of the most ancient things: Ecl. VI.

*Then he surrounds the daughters of Phaethon with the moss of bitter / bark, and raises them from the ground as tall alders.*

The consequent for the antecedent:

*Swollen figs are cut away, the doctor laughing.* Juv. II. 13.

one of the concomitants for the other:

*You card the wool, and carry back in baskets the finished / fleeces, you twist the spindle pregnant with its slender thread / better than Penelope, more deftly than Arachne.* 55

[p. 542] § 797. ANTONOMASIA, by which for the idea, the concept of singular and individual things, and for the more famous or more usual sign of the same, which in speech is wont to be a name, now usually called proper, there is substituted another concept, or several concepts combined into a periphrasis and circumscription, now reciprocal with the former, now wider than it, or vice versa; is a synecdoche, now ascending, of the species or genus for the individual, § 789, now descending, of the individual for the species or genus, § 790, 786, now, finally, a proper description, one, namely, which does not become wider by describing, is rather a manifest figure, § 550, 733, than a trope. § 780. *The great-souled son of Anchises* in Virgil V. is an ascending antonomasia. For the patronymic, even if there be only a single son or a single daughter, nevertheless extends more widely than these individuals in the realm of possibles, heterocosmically, because there could have been several sons or daughters; indeed even in this world, since it fits not only sons or daughters, but also their descendants

*The children of their children, and those who shall be born from them,*

A descending antonomasia is in Plautus:

*Come this way with me, that you may see at the same time / your Alcumena, the concubine, my Juno.*

*A great and sudden Prometheus is owed to the dish,* Juv. 4.

[p. 543] (for the potter.)

*That none of the gods is deaf, nor a Tiresias.* 14.

§ 798. This descending antonomasia has especially pleased many lovers of charms, § 797, 755. Clodius, burning even for his sisters, Cicero calls *the Roman Jupiter*, § 756, 754. *But what ambushes are here? or what Hannibal sails against us?* Petr.

*She consults about the slow funeral of her jaundiced mother, / but first about you, your Tanaquil.* Juv. 2.

In this way I think the whole fable of the Platonic period ought to be explained, and the passage of Virgil:

*Then there shall be another Tiphys, and another Argo, to carry / chosen heroes; there shall be other wars too, / and again a great Achilles shall be sent to Troy.* ecl. 4.

§ 799. An antonomasia which is a figure rather than a trope you have in the circumscription of Socrates, yet proper to him alone: § 797.

*And ethereal Plato, and he who had fashioned him, / and who, condemned, better condemned his own Athens.* Man.

[p. 544] *believe that this bearded master / says it, whom the dire draught of hemlock carries off.* Pers.

To ascending antonomasia I refer that communication of speech by which one who is to think beautifully attributes favourable things, perhaps fitting himself alone, at the same time to his spectators or hearers, and odious things, perhaps proper to these alone, he nevertheless ascribes to himself at the same time, in the person which they call the first of the plural number; for he puts the species of those present for certain individuals.

§ 800. Εὐφημισμοί [euphemisms], and whatever things are thought prettily κατὰ λιτότητα [by litotes] with some extenuation, § 329, are not so much peculiar tropes as certain cases, at whose occurrence the moral manner of thinking, § 227, will have advised one to take refuge rather in some one of the figures, e.g. periphrasis, or in any trope whatever of those adduced, than to conceive or express less elegantly the thing nakedly and explicitly, such as it may first have come into the thinker’s mind, § 789, 796. Thus the synecdoche of the part for the whole extenuates, unless you wish to speak of things pertaining to yourself, after the fashion of the braggart soldier; it extenuates something praiseworthy, which you would wish to ascribe to yourself or your own, to deny the opposite rather than to affirm that very thing.

*Nor am I so ill-formed; lately I saw myself on the shore.*

*If anything should befall me after the manner of men,* is an ascending synecdoche [p. 545] § 789. *to have lived* is a metalepsis of the antecedent for the consequent, § 796, as is also that formula of speaking by which the things we dismiss, to which we send notice, about to spurn them henceforth, are bidden to live and fare well.

*Let all things become even the midst of the sea. Live, you woods!*

§ 801. A trope, like all things human, admits also of degrees, and of more and less, since it can grow 1) extensively, by continuing a trope of a given kind through several thoughts to be explicitly depicted or signified, and this continuation of any trope whatever will yield ALLEGORY, 2) intensively, when not only the single and immediate subject of comparison is omitted and hidden, the term of comparison being substituted through the trope, but that term of comparison is put from which you must, in expounding the trope, first draw out some definite subject of comparison, which then, as soon as it has been drawn out and found, suddenly passes over into a new and likewise tacit term of relation, which only now, by a repeated exposition, discloses that subject of comparison which was primarily to be thought now, and which outside the trope would have had to be explicitly set down and properly signified. This intensification of tropes, a trope, so to speak, internally doubled, or even multiplied, is METALEPSIS, if we name the metalepsis [p. 546] of which § 796 treats rather a metonymy, as it is.

§ 802. Allegory in the wider sense we attribute not only to every kind of trope, to irony too, § 801, running uniformly on, such as would be a heap of metaphors between whose terms of comparison there is also a certain closer nexus to be observed by the analogue of reason, a heap of metonymies of this kind, etc., but also to a certain blend and mixture of tropes of different kinds succeeding one another in a continuous series, so that allegorical utterances are whatever has a mystical sense underlying it. Now it is clear how great a door stands open here into aesthetic darkness, how great a care is needed, lest the argument seem far-fetched, an argument of obscurity rather than of illustration, § 634, 741. Hence those who prefer shade to night often prefer to PURE ALLEGORIES, in which all the subjects of comparison are omitted, MIXED ones, in which at least now and then the subjects of comparison, explicitly thought as well, are inserted like keys. § 736.

§ 803. A pure allegory of Cicero’s is, for Quinctius: *So it comes about that I, who ought to ward off the weapons and heal the wounds, am compelled to do this at a time when the adversary has not yet thrown any weapon: [p. 547] but to them that time for attacking is granted when the power of avoiding their assault will have been taken from us, and when, if in any matter, as they are prepared to do, they have hurled some poisoned weapon, there will be no room for applying a remedy.* A mixed one he has for Milo: *For my part I always thought that the other storms and tempests, in those billows of the assemblies at least, were to be undergone by Milo.* Hither belong fables, § 526, apologues, § 537, and RIDDLES, a complex of predicates not well agreeing, and scarcely dwelling in one seat, one subject, whose subject, the key, is either passed over in silence or hidden. Virgil, a just dispenser of light and shade, imitating oracles, § 671, did not shrink from the allegory of a descending antonomasia, § 798, 801:

*Neither a Simois, nor a Xanthus, nor a Doric camp / shall be wanting to you. Already another Achilles has been brought forth in Latium, / himself too born of a goddess.*

§ 804. The trope which allegory stretches out, unless you seriously take care, into ugly obscurity, metalepsis intensifies, § 801. But let us look at good ones. In the first example of § 789 you have a doubled synecdoche 1) ascending, of the whole for the part, of elms for rods, and 2) descending, of the inferior for the superior, of elm rods for any wooden ones whatever. In the *sparrow of Catullus* there is, § 790. 1) a metonymy of the thing signified [p. 548] for the sign, or of the content for the container, and then 2) a synecdoche of the part for the whole. In that: *Your integrity has cleared you,* § 791, metaphor and irony are intertwined. In the first example of § 794 there is a metalepsis presenting first a metonymy of the container for the content, and then a synecdoche of the whole for the part, unless you judge all the citizens of those cities to be blacksmiths, like Cyclopes. When for: it is now midday, one says: *Now the cattle seek the cool,* you have a metalepsis of metonymy, by which 1) the content, a rustic phenomenon, is said for the containing time and 2) the contained cool for the containing air or wind, unless you prefer to call the last trope a synecdoche of the part for the whole, to which the trope of the abstract for the concrete belongs. § 794. *With Jupiter cold.*

§ 805. The allegory still lasts, § 801, of the metonymy of the contained rustic phenomena for the containing time, § 794, and yet there is added through metalepsis a descending antonomasia, when *Thestylis* is put for any country woman whatever, § 798.
