When *the man, while he is hot,* is judged *fit to be devoured,* § 795, to the metonymy of the possessor for the thing he has there is added through metalepsis another, by which the phenomena of the body are wont to be substituted for the simultaneous ones in the soul; Jupiter § 141, for the wind is a metalepsis growing together out of a metonymy of the god for the thing over which he presides, and [p. 549] out of a synecdoche of the superior genus for the inferior. For granted that Jupiter, not Juno, now presides over the air, yet the poet wished to indicate not air in general, but air more violently stirred. In the same way *foot* in this passage of Catullus is said through a metalepsis comprising a metaphor and a synecdoche of the part for the whole.

§ 806. To metalepsis may be referred most syllepses, as they call the cases in which one sign seems to sustain two significations, each of them primary. § 801.

*Nereid Galatea, sweeter to me than the thyme of Hybla.* ecl. 7.

Here in: *sweeter* they detect a syllepsis, which, growing together out of metaphor and synecdoche of the species for the genus, is a metalepsis. If we call a harsh and far-fetched trope CATACHRESIS and abuse, am I mistaken, or have we not sufficiently remembered what a trope is? § 780. It is a harsh, it is a far-fetched substitution of perception for perception, of an improper sign for a proper one, of a transferred word in the place of the proper one; but for that very reason, because it is harsh, because it is far-fetched, although it can sometimes be excused by necessity itself, nevertheless it is never a trope, but often a deformation of thoughts opposed to tropes. Of tropes too hold the observations made about figures, § 339, since they are cryptic figures, § 783.

[p. 550] § 807. Hyperbole too, if it lies hidden in the substitution of a lesser perception for a greater, or of a greater for a lesser, can be a trope, § 339, 780. Yet neither is every hyperbole a trope, nor does it constitute a peculiar kind of tropes, being to be referred to synecdoche, the cryptic comparison of greater and lesser, of whole and part, § 742, 782. Much less can I call every emphasis a trope, M. § 517, least of all invent a peculiar class of tropes. Indeed every trope, but also every figure, well placed, beautifully signified, has emphasis as its companion § 26, 783.

#### Sectio XXXXVIII. Thaumaturgia Aesthetica. — Section XXXXVIII. Aesthetic Wonder-Working

§ 808. The light of novelty illustrates perceptions excellently, M. § 549, 550. The intuition of novelty, WONDER, excites curiosity, M. § 688, curiosity attention, M. § 625, 529, attention brings new light to the thing to be vividly depicted to oneself. M. § 628, 531. Hence things to be thought beautifully, when they are to be illustrated, § 730, are well presented when they win wonder by novelty, the desire of knowing more clearly by wonder, [p. 551] and finally attention by the desire of knowing the thing more clearly. The AESTHETIC winning of novelty, through this of wonder, through this of curiosity, through this of attention, let it, for brevity’s sake, be called by us WONDER-WORKING.

§ 809. Crassus in Cicero de or. l. 137, when right at the threshold of his discourse he judges it worthy of an excuse that he will say nothing new, tacitly concedes by that very fact the rule which we have written, § 808. *When you have heard,* he says, *I think you will not so much wonder at these things as judge that, when you desired to hear them, there was no reason why you should desire it. For I shall say nothing recondite, nothing worthy of your expectation, nothing either unheard of by you or new to anyone.* The shepherds themselves gave praise, when

*Pollio too himself makes new songs.* Ecl. 3. 86.

*If novelties bring hope, so that, as in herbs that do not deceive, fruit appears,* § 731. *they are not to be rejected.* Cic. de am. 70. What else does Quintilian do, when he dissuades from *plucking beforehand, not far from the beginning of a speech, all the grace of novelty for the remainder?* IV. 5. Horace agrees l. c. § 294. Let good citizens despise new things, new men, those who are *nothing, unless sons of Cecrops:* we shall rightly love new matter for thinking beautifully [p. 552] and a pleasing novelty in the very elegances of thoughts, § 808.

§ 810. *Those who hear gladly, and attend more, and believe more easily, are for the most part captivated by the delight itself, sometimes carried away by wonder. — — — — Rightly does Cicero write in these very words to Brutus in a certain letter: For I judge the eloquence which has no wonder to be no eloquence at all. Aristotle too thinks that it is this above all which is to be sought.* Quintilian VIII. 3. But what wonder can there be, except that of novelty? § 808. Someone will perhaps object: *that that excellent and divine wisdom is: to wonder at nothing.* Cic. Tusc. III. 30.

*To wonder at nothing is almost the one thing, Numicius, / and the only one that can make and keep a man happy.* Hor. l. ep. 6.

Whence you might gather that the pursuit of wonder is to be banished rather to cosmetics, § 688, and to lies truly not splendid, § 625, than to be reckoned among natural ornament. § 622, 623.

§ 811. But the case is safe. Those for whom it was, whether the custom or the disease of the ancients, even then, when as philosophers they ought by teaching to have instructed reason most distinctly, to hunt after paradoxes with tropes, [p. 553] and with apophthegms often to plunge the free spirit into riddles themselves, § 803, either here too, bidding us *to wonder at nothing,* wish through hyperbole only to call us back from the too vehement, ignorant and stupid wonder of everyday cares, or by a metonymy of the consequent for the antecedent, § 796, forgetful of human affairs and of the blindness unavoidable for mortals, have sung divine wisdom as immune from all ignorance, the mother of wonder; or, finally, have professed a wisdom fallacious rather and painted, than true and natural to man, for whom, if he is wise, eternity will be passed in the most wise wonder at the supreme deity.

§ 812. It is therefore permitted to a wisdom not painted, but sacrificing to the Graces, *to soothe with wonder the minds* not only of *the rude and rustic,* but of men, § 808. Yet lest we be carried away too much by the pursuit of novelty, let these be the cautions. 1) Do not rashly confuse the novelty of the object and matter with the novelty of the things and thoughts, § 18. The latter is sought in the first place, not the former, § 808. I do not deny that it is easier to meditate new things about new things, nor do I deny grace to the novelty of matters, nor light. Meanwhile, not only on the most ancient things, but also on things long known, he can nevertheless turn over in his mind

*songs not before / [p. 554] heard, a priest of the Muses*

*borne through the ether on no common or slender wing,* he can, about the most recent affairs, while they are hot, *unlearned at the crossroads*

*spoil a wretched song on a screeching straw.*

§ 813. 2) Nor should you wish to hunt after matters, or thoughts, absolutely new, never yet thought, by which you would not only be carried away from the most strictly true, § 441, but also, by poetic fictions wholly unknown, § 518, would at last most certainly be stranded in an ugly utopia, § 514, 469, destitute of all verisimilitude, § 483, sheer darkness, § 638. M. 889. Remember Phaethon, whom, eager for absolute novelty and wonder, though already relatively

*fearful at the novelty of things,*

his father admonishes, the common father of charming wits, § 82:

*Your lot is mortal; what you ask is not mortal. / Even more than what it is right should fall to the gods above, / unknowing, you aspire to. / Son, take care, while the matter still allows, correct your wishes,* § 812. / — — — — / *Finally, look around at whatever the rich world holds,* § 513. / [p. 555] *and from so many and so great goods of sky, and earth, and sea,* § 509. / *ask for something. You will suffer no refusal. / This one thing I deprecate, which by its true name is a penalty, / not an honour.* § 810. *A penalty, Phaethon, you ask for instead of a gift,* § 808.

§ 814. 3) In things relatively new for your primary personal object, where that novelty suffices you, § 813, let it not again be enough for you that the material to be chosen in part, and in part the things to be signified concerning it, have hitherto been, at least partially, unknown; let your greatest care here be whether those things are hitherto unknown which, made known by you, will now at last receive through you the light and grace of novelty and will stir wonder, when we now intuit what before was scarcely and dimly perceived, § 808. The discoverer is not a vendor of unknown things, but one who makes known what was formerly unknown. Nor will the man who is much occupied with unknown things, of which he merely betrays his own ignorance, inducing a fresh darkness upon them rather than knowing how to draw them forth into new light, § 627, move the charming desire to know, much less satisfy it, or earn wonder beyond the common herd of the wise. Scarcely anyone, by rattling off unknown things which in the end he does not know how to illuminate, will delude the spectator, like

*the gaping crow,*

[p. 556] with impunity, without its being sung back at the wretched wonder-worker:

*Are you raving, or do you knowingly mock me by singing darkly?* Hor. II. S. 5, 58.

§ 815. 4) Among things relatively new, do not choose to think those which either are truly unworthy of being known, or at least seem to your primary spectators so far from meriting their attention that you, a prudent appraiser of your own gifts, can foresee that it will not be within your power to direct the curiosity of your people towards them, § 808. Should you have spectators, who seem even primary ones, idle lovers of trifles whose ears would be most gladly tickled by the cheap allurement of charming thought, such as are touched upon in § 184, the primary aesthetic law of dignity, which absolute magnitude already demands and involves, § 185, 178, would nevertheless yield shamefully to the inferior rule of observing curiosity, § 808, which is in the end subordinated to the law of aesthetic light, § 614, and not even that necessarily, § 617, 625. It will be greater, and, the rarer it is, by that very fact the more recent, the more curious, the more admirable and worthy of attention, if you can initiate into greater things the spectator who expects anything else and any trifles whatever, and gradually entice, lead and fix his wandering mind in the contemplation of graver and nobler things. Horace attempts the artifice in I. od. 27.

[p. 557] *To fight with cups made for the use of gladness / is a Thracian thing. Away with the barbarous / custom* — — — — / *ah! wretch, / in what a Charybdis you labour, / youth worthy of a better flame!*

§ 816. In things absolutely worthy, even if you have, among your primary spectators too, some who would rather like you, in the treatment of graver matters, to be a less anxious observer of relative dignity, or even that trifles be preferred outright to them, at least comparatively, § 197–201: do not grant too much to the desire to please, since it is only relative, not absolute and universal, *dignity* that is the *admirable* one of § 187, which § 808–810 bid us pursue. Would it happen only in the seats of the pious, what Horace relates in II. od. 13?

*Sappho, complaining on Aeolian strings / of the girls of her own people, / and you, Alcaeus, sounding more fully with golden / plectrum the hardships of the ship, / the cruel ills of flight, the ills of war, / the shades marvel at both as they say things worthy / of sacred silence. But the crowd, / packed shoulder to shoulder, drinks in with its ear rather / battles and tyrants driven out.*

§ 817. [p. 558] But a) some things, most worthy of the attention of mortals, can nevertheless truly be, for certain people who may happen to be your primary spectators, in certain places, times and occasions, less opportune, less timely, for them to turn their mind to now, in proportion to the gravity of the matter; b) they can seem such to others, for whose sake above all you think, through an error, I grant, but an error nonetheless not to be overcome, given your powers and given the time. In either case you would offer your people things perhaps unheard of, perhaps undeservedly unknown, and yet you would move not wonder but tedium. You would intend to give new things, but you would fall short of the grace of novelty, § 815. The graver the things you were preparing to think, the greater the care that will be needed lest you rashly cast them, to be trampled underfoot with contempt, before those who, when grain has been found, prefer, whatever you do, to feed on acorns.

§ 818. 5) Do not seek things unheard of to such a degree that they not only transcend the belief of the wise, § 424, and not only must seem to your chief personal objects to be destitute of the verisimilitude, both logical and aesthetic, which they require, § 431–439, now general, § 440, now the strictest, now heterocosmic, § 441, § 483, but also that you can foresee by aesthetic dynamometry, [p. 559] § 60, that it will not be within your powers, given the time, given the heads, that is, the spectators, to render them even aesthetically verisimilar, so far as is necessary, § 555. Otherwise you will seem indeed to utter new things, but empty nuts, broken glass, interpretations of dreams, Thessalian portents, old wives' tales, absurdities, some new-fangled pedlar of trifles, winning for your people neither curiosity nor wonder, except that which has derision for its companion, and which surely was not what was sought now, § 808–810. Suppose your chief spectators to be such as Juvenal was; suppose that you have neither the power nor the time to set before their eyes the fullest verisimilitude of the Hebrew law, to say nothing now more certain: suppose nevertheless that you expound to them

*whatever Moses handed down in his secret volume.* XIV. 102.

You speak to them new things, many unheard of in Roman laws; but the man *who fears the sabbaths, who worships nothing but the clouds and the divinity of the sky, for whom the flesh of swine and the flesh of men differ not at all, for whom every seventh day is idle, who lays aside the foreskin* — these things they will pursue with neither the curiosity nor the wonder you had hoped for.

§ 819. 6) Do not readily hope to think things more charmingly for the sake of spectators for whom both the whole and the parts of the things to be treated, nay even the parts of the parts [p. 560] perhaps, are so new that you can foresee they can be illuminated only a little, and only by far-fetched means, § 741. For at least the treatment will seem hard to the spectators, § 787, if they do not altogether despair of grasping you, not bringing with them enough extension and at the same time intension in attending, so that all curiosity collapses at once, and with it the wonder that pours light and grace around beautiful meditations, § 808. He who thinks finely neither begins every exhortation from Paradise,

*Nor the Trojan war from the twin egg,*

but, so that he may

*carry the hearer into the midst of things / no otherwise than if they were known,*

he chooses matters of which, when the whole is new, there are nevertheless certain anticipations of the parts among the hearers, and when the parts are created new, there is nevertheless already present in their minds a certain preconceived image of the whole, § 484.

§ 820. MATERIALS ABSOLUTELY EXHAUSTED, about which no mortal could any longer offer anyone anything charmingly new, though they otherwise belong to the aesthetic horizon, § 119, I think there are scarcely any; but RESPECTIVELY exhausted may seem those about which a given wit can scarcely expound anything charmingly new to the primary ones among its given personal objects. Hence I should advise, if the choice of materials is yours, [p. 561] 7) that you do not rashly set yourself a theme which, with respect to you and your spectators, may seem exhausted to you yourself, measuring yourself by your own just foot. For having undertaken it nonetheless, either, despairing, you will throw away the beautiful desire for novelty, against § 808, striving to repeat any commonplaces and things said six hundred times over, or, holding fast to it, you will be in great danger of laying upon your matters a colour that is new indeed, but not natural, § 622, 623, not a colour but a cosmetic, § 688, § 706, 324.

§ 821. 8) Do not heap up so much of novelty and of the unexpected into your meditation that by the very frequency and crowding of new things and thoughts all that pleasant colour of elegantly thought things is taken away, whereby they seem rather to have been poured out suddenly, and to flow with a natural ease, now at last, at the time at which they are set down, than to have been struck out by the wit already beforehand with much sweat, through art and long labours, like Minerva from the head of Jupiter. For then novelty is rendered suspect by excess of novelty, and if one or another acute saying has pleased as new, we believe ten, poured out in one breath, to be old and prepared, § 788. *Most things are more pleasing if they seem to have been found on the sudden, not brought from home, but born from the matter itself in the course of speaking. Hence those not unpleasing figures: it almost escaped me, and: it had slipped my mind, and: you rightly* [p. 562] *remind me.* Quint. IIII. 5. Hence judge the custom of those who speak words of honour face to face, and who not only bring thanksgivings for a present benefit that have nonetheless been prepared at home, but even read them out openly, lest anyone fail to know that they have already given thanks before obtaining their wish.

§ 822. But above all, he who would win for his people a lovable novelty should take care with all his might, 9) not readily to promise that he will bring forth things all new and unheard of, promises of the kind that should for the most part be left to the itinerant eloquence of mountebanks. For since on account of εἰκός [the plausible] it is necessary to mix the known with the unknown, § 819, it is dangerous, not without at least the appearance of arrogance, to reproach one’s primary spectators, not so covertly, with a total ignorance of useful matters, in which they have hitherto been living, and at the same time to raise them, offended, into so many judges and sterner critics of oneself, who first say to themselves:

*What worthy of so great a gape will this promiser bring forth?*

and then, unless you have truly neglected εἰκός [the plausible], can with great verisimilitude reproach him with not having stood by his promises, who has woven into his new things many things known before. And what if there be among them someone perhaps less known to you, who, whether from the truth of the matter or at least in punishment of your seeming arrogance, could, not without verisimilitude, in place of applause, throw back at you [p. 563] outright that he had long since known all these things better than they were brought forth by you?

*How much more rightly he who labours at nothing ineptly,*

Crassus, § 809, who promises nothing unheard of, and by that very fact, since he wishes to seem only about to recall to mind things long known, covertly creeps up upon the desire for novelty, going to obtain that what you perhaps already know you hear gladly, because the things promised were ones you knew less, and that what you had never before conceived in the manner in which they are brought forth, you now perceive the more gladly, because you catch new things offered unexpectedly; and at the same time, in unlocking to you things truly new to you, he modestly dissembles the hearer’s ignorance.

§ 823. Aesthetic wonder-working, but sober, agrees with that man:

*It is not enough to have said: I compose wondrous poems,* § 822,

Hence it does not everywhere labour to cast the hearer into that degree of wonder in which either

*I was stunned, and my hair stood on end, and my voice stuck in my throat,*

or a gentler ecstasy carries the mind out of itself, such as we saw from Catullus, § 87. To procure for one’s people, for whatever novelty, and an intuition proportioned to it, at least a little wonder, and what you might perhaps prefer to call a solicitation to wonder rather than wondering [p. 564] itself, it will help 1) sometimes to recall to mind the most ancient things, which you nevertheless teach to be undeservedly almost lost. Archaisms themselves, well and rightly placed, carry their novelty with them, and things long known, which ought to have recurred to the mind daily, if at some time they are recalled to mind more clearly, as if by the right of postliminy, strike it with their repeated novelty, as it wonders at this very thing, that it could so nearly have forgotten them; 2) if you think to give new light to things not new, as you do not deny, abandoning the novelty of materials and objects, § 812, nay expressly disclaiming it, aim solely at this, that those things which seem obsolete you nevertheless now so deduce, and set before the eyes of your spectators, that this very thing seems to them somewhat wonderful, that a matter and material so often thought by them has nevertheless never hitherto been observed by them in this manner, mode and way, in this series and connection. The more your new manner of thinking about things sufficiently known will then approach nature, the readier, easier and more procurable for anyone it seems, the greater the wonder you can promise yourself.

§ 824. 3) One occupied with materials long known will prudently, right from the beginning of his charming meditation, hint that these same things do indeed deserve new light; perhaps he himself will also wonder how [p. 565] it comes about that in things so well known that manner of declaring such a matter is nevertheless so infrequent, so unusual, which, unless it be the best, surely does not deserve to be neglected and altogether despised, namely the one which he then himself intends to enter upon, § 823. 4) Things which seem less verisimilar, it is often fitting to grant that they do not seem so, even to add reasons for doubting, if there is leisure, and thus gradually to lead the hearer into the hope that perhaps after this very treatment there will be seen by him things which do not now seem so, not only verisimilar but even altogether true, and that his mind is to be newly freed from the doubts already inveterate against the given material, etc.

§ 825. 5) Let your partial perceptions also be known; only compose from them aptly a new whole, and you will not fall short of the praise of novelty. What is better known than colours, than most flowers? yet how many new attires of our Venuses come from them, how many new garlands for girls

*fierce against young men with sharpened nails?*

⁠6) Let the whole be already known; but look around it for yourself, whether there be any part of it, any face or any side less observed by others, yet worth looking at, § 815; everything at last offers itself to the one who surveys it with his eyes. Draw forth this part, face or side of a most well-known thing out of comparative darkness, place this above all in the point of sight for your spectators [p. 566], and so add the rest, as they are more closely connected with it, or more remotely. A city which I have seen six hundred times from afar somewhat from the northern quarter — if you are the first to show it to me to be viewed from the southern quarter, from a vantage point not ineptly placed, you will seem to me, grateful, to have shown a new spectacle, § 823. To recognise then the old towers, palaces and streets which I have long known, but turning another side to the eyes, another arrangement, will increase the pleasure rather than diminish it.

§ 826. 7) It is useful, if it can be done, to show in treating things, but without boasting, that much fallow land remains not yet cultivated, showing more concern about which of so many new things one should choose above all, than about whence one could scrape together something new. Hence you may gather once more how necessary beautiful erudition is for charming wits, § 63, by which they may know the lands known and unknown in the republic of letters, both the boundaries of these among themselves and the broad estates of the unknown ones gradually to be cultivated. 8) Either choose objects truly treated by few, at least do not rashly write an Iliad after Homer, an Aeneid after Virgil, or it will be advisable to declare to those about to hear why those things which seem exhausted are, for now and thus, and in these circumstances in which we now find ourselves, truly not so, § 820, [p. 567] but promise a large and in some measure new gleaning after the harvest, etc.

§ 827. Finally the best counsel for obtaining novelty will be for those who can use it: 9) What a rich vein has once learned about the thing to be thought, now well understood by it, § 639, and has itself meditated upon with itself, as it were provisionally, let it now rather itself think, dispose and express according to the natural rules of beauty, than follow an archetype as a blind imitator, § 714, 715. For then, not inept, such as may become an exemplar and original for some wits of lesser rank, following its own natural genius, proper to itself, bound only by the eternal laws of charm, in other things free, it will not *leap down*

*an imitator into the strait, / whence either shame or the law of the work forbids retreat,*

but rather, with a wit not forced, not affected, as if doing something else, without anxious concern, it will obtain, so far as suffices, the novelty of a nature in some measure different in all subjects and objects, § 823. Not pictures of human life already made by art, not the examples of some former age already expressed by I know not whose brush above all, but the very nature of things, and that of its own age in which it lives,

[p. 568] *I shall bid the learned imitator look to as the exemplar of life and manners, / and draw true utterances from there,* § 613,

which cannot fail at the same time to be new, since the scene of the age changes continually, while

*There is nothing new under the sun.* § 813.

§ 828. After the procuring of novelty, let the second care of wonder-working be the curiosity of the spectators or hearers, § 808, which the arguments moving towards it will obtain, of which below, § 22, 26. These two being posited, wonder will arise, as soon as perhaps ancient things occur in your meditations and yet do not seem obsolete, and new things are not only known or heard symbolically to be such, but your personal objects also intuit them as such, M. § 620. Hence you may gather once more how useful for natural beauty is a deeper knowledge of one’s primary personal object, since the things which are most wonderful to one man cannot always and certainly carry wonder with them for another as well, § 679–681.

[p. 569]

#### Sectio XXXXVIIII. Persuasio Aesthetica. — Section XXXXVIIII. Aesthetic Persuasion

§ 829. Among the primary beauties of thoughts, § 113, 177, 423, 614, we count as the fifth, § 22, sensate certainty, the consciousness and light of truth and verisimilitude to be obtained also by the analogue of reason, PERSUASION, but AESTHETIC persuasion, § 22, M. § 531. For although this sometimes involves at the same time an impulse and incitement to action, it nevertheless has its place in theoretical matters too, and will be fruitfully distinguished, by those who weigh each thing well, from the life of cognition to be considered in what follows, of which persuasion is the antecedent condition, though it is sometimes put for the consequent, § 796.

§ 830. *Plato had so much weight of authority with Dionysius, and so much power in eloquence, that he persuaded him to put an end to the tyranny and to give the Syracusans back their liberty.* Persuasion was present, § 829, although Dionysius, *frightened away from this intention by the counsel of Philistus, began to be somewhat crueller.* Corn. in Dione. Some logicians now call persuasion the erroneous opinion of certainty, [p. 570] that state of mind in which we think ourselves certain through error. From this ambiguity of words there are some who open themselves a window for reckoning every speech fit for persuading among allurements and cosmetic, § 688, rather than among true charms. Nor is it to be denied that those labouring under the error described are not seldom said to be persuaded. So in Cicero, de nat. deorum I. 61, Cotta, *himself a pontiff, who thinks the public ceremonies and religious rites are to be guarded most scrupulously, would wish to be persuaded of this, which is the first point, that there are gods, not by opinion only but even plainly to the truth.* In the same way Virgil:

*And let no prudent authority now persuade you / to stir the earth stiffened by the blowing of Boreas.* Georg. II. 316.

§ 831. *If there shall be any disgraces in his past life, either they will be said to have fallen into that repute falsely, or they will be attributed to the envy of certain persons, or to detraction, or to false opinion, or to imprudence, to necessity, to the persuasion of youth, or to some affection of mind not malicious.* Cic. de Inu. II. 37. Here too who would deny that persuasion has a sinister meaning? § 830. Yet the same Cicero, I. 6, says that *the duty of the oratorical faculty* is *to speak appositely for persuasion, its end, to persuade by diction,* that is, of that [p. 571] oratorical faculty *to which wisdom is at hand as the governess of all things,* 4, surely not bearing another’s error as its aim. Who does not see that here, in the last passage, Cicero intends true persuasion, such as that of which Horace, Serm. I. 6. 8:

*you truly persuade yourself of this, / that before the rule of Tullius and his ignoble reign, / many men often sprung from no ancestors / both lived upright and were honoured with ample distinctions.*

§ 832. Of that which Cicero, in the passage first cited in § 831, had called *the persuasion of youth,* Quintilian gives an example in II. 4, deriving *from extemporaneous garrulity the empty joy of ignorant parents,* but for the youths *contempt of work, a shameless brow, the habit of speaking very badly, the practice of bad things, and, what has often ruined great progress as well, an arrogant persuasion about themselves.* Nor is the persuasion better of which he says in I. 1: *Nothing,* he says, *is worse than those who, having advanced a little beyond the first letters, have put on a false persuasion of their own knowledge.* We have, therefore, an easy way of removing the ambiguity. The distinct consciousness of truth is conviction; the indistinct and sensate, persuasion. This indistinct consciousness of truth is, as all cognition is, either true, true persuasion, § 831, or false, false persuasion.

§ 833. [p. 572] Persuasion in general is a phenomenon of the soul, by which it thinks it apperceives truth, as a traveller the new

*moon either sees, or thinks he has seen, through the clouds.*

Of this Quintilian, I. 2, when he denies *that it is to be dissembled that there are some who, from this almost public custom* of going to public teachers, *dissent by a private persuasion.* For now he posits this persuasion only as something constant by usage and experience, and only afterwards is he going to examine whether it is true or false, § 832. But when through this examination he asserts that *in the seclusion* of those avoiding public schools *the mind either languishes, or contracts a certain mould as if lying in the shade, or on the contrary swells with an empty persuasion,* he is recalling not persuasion simply, but definitely and more accurately an empty, that is, a false persuasion, § 832.

§ 834. To one reading through Quintilian II. 15 it will appear how many controversies we avoid, and blows aimed through the flank of rhetoric at aesthetics as well, as being either a power, or a science, or a practice, or an art, by a slight swerve of the body, when we demand of those who would think beautifully not persuasion in general, § 833, but aesthetic persuasion, § 829, and set this up not as the sole end of rhetoric alone, but among the primary ends of everything to be thought more charmingly, [p. 573] § 5. Neither rhetoric nor its mother, aesthetics, is *a certain depravity of art,* that is, κακοτεχνία [bad art], while each is *a craftsman of persuading,* πειθοῦς δημιουργός [an artificer of persuasion], and, if one may sing with Ennius,

*the marrow of Suada.*

§ 835. Aesthetics, which you would describe less inadequately than *Plato does rhetoric,* and not, like Plato, as *not an art but a knack,* but as both a knack and *a certain art of grace and pleasure,* § 1, does not fabricate persuasion of whatever kind, but such as befits itself, good and truly elegant, and is not what *Athenaeus calls rhetoric, the art of deceiving,* § 830. For it neither is, nor bids rhetoric be, 1) *the power of saying* or thinking *what may be persuasive in any matter,* if under this title *Aristotle seems to have subjected everything to the orator,* S. VIIII. It rather turns away from the empty boasting of certain sophists, who promise, mountebank-fashion, that they will set forth their opinion not only on all matters, but even on each matter on either of two contrary sides with equal persuasion of the hearers, § 818.

§ 836. Neither does it itself persuade, nor does it allow rhetoric, which holds fast to its mother’s counsels, § 835, to persuade 2) as *harlots, flatterers and corrupters persuade, or lead to that which they want,* [p. 574] S. XV. XVI. XXIII. Nor is it only, nor does it allow its own rhetoric to be only, 3) what *Ariston, the disciple of the Peripatetic Critolaus,* affirms it to be, *the science of seeing and acting in civil questions through a speech of popular persuasion,* if you interpret *popular persuasion* rather insultingly *as an art which you think will persuade the learned of nothing.* It rises higher, and draws its rhetoric after it, beyond civil questions into more sublime matters, S. XXI, and into a magnanimity in persuading that transcends the judgement of the popular breeze, § 397, and vulgar assemblies, § 396, S. XXVI.

§ 837. Neither does aesthetics itself pursue, nor does it grant its rhetoric so to pursue, persuasive things that 4) *it prefers credible things to true ones,* S. XXVII. XXXIIII, although it sometimes prefers to certain true things, § 429, 430, others, also true, and at the same time displaying that εἰκός [plausibility] of § 484, whereby they more easily find belief. For neither the aesthetician about to persuade, nor the *orator* who has come out of his academy, as *are the words of Corn. Celsus, seeks only the likeness of truth,* but truth itself, more often even the strictest, § 566, S. XXXV, although, being modest, he prefers to name his findings, from the greater part, verisimilar, § 483, rather than true, for mortals, of whom very few know what truth is and how manifold, though always one, S. XXVIIII, [p. 575] 5) *as slave-dealers, who counterfeit colour with cosmetic and true strength with empty fattening.* S. XXXXI. XXXXII.

§ 838. Rightly, therefore, let the logicians reject false persuasion, and persuasion of the false, or about the false, § 830. Rightly, nevertheless, will a fine wit love true persuasion, § 831, and that of the true or the verisimilar, § 837, the aesthetic, § 834–837, and its habit, suasion and SUADELA, not that vagrant one of § 831–837, but the aesthetic one, § 829. For not her,

*with whom Suadela and Venus adorn the well-moneyed man,* Hor. ep. 16, 36,

but her *who, Eupolis wrote, sat upon the lips of Pericles,* Cic. de cl. or. 59.

*We make you,* Suadela, *a goddess, and place you in heaven.*

§ 839. He who would persuade aesthetically will know how to give his people 1) verisimilitude according to § 423–613, and the light necessary for it, § 624–824. For these being given, the persuasion sought will be given, § 829. Verisimilitude, as much as the absolute desire for truth everywhere demands, § 555, lucid simply, § 625, in at least absolute light, § 617, will exhibit ABSOLUTE PERSUASION in aesthetic matters; but as much verisimilitude as the comparative desire for truth has required in a given meditation, [p. 576] S. XXXV, shining proportionately, § 625, will exhibit COMPARATIVE persuasion. He who accurately furnishes both, as much as the beauty of the whole desires, is AESTHETICALLY SOLID; he who neglects the latter, or is guilty of altogether despising the former, is AESTHETICALLY SUPERFICIAL.

§ 840. There is a certain nation of pygmies, § 359, which thinks itself now philosophical, now mathematical, for which all its own things are solid, but its own alone, like the gate of the underworld in Virgil, VI:

*columns of solid adamant, / so that no force of men, not the heaven-dwellers themselves / can cut them down with iron. An iron tower stands up to the breezes.*

Let them live abounding in their own wit; but for us, who value logical solidity most highly in its own place, now

let *the Paphian myrtles* flower *from solid oak.*

ACCURATE is what follows its laws more exactly, and what proves accurately is SOLID. He, therefore, who expounds the truth which he owed more exactly according to the rules of verisimilitude, and accurately according to the rules of light, as befits an aesthetician — why should he not be solid in his kind, in his sphere and horizon?

[p. 577] § 841. I should not wish the truly charming to hurl their weapons back at those philosophers, or quasi-philosophers, or mathematicians, to whom only their own things seem solid, as that Epicurean in Cicero, de fin. I, 61, who says that he perceives *better* and *more truly* *that no one of the fools is happy, nor any of the wise not happy, whereas the Stoics deny that anything is good save that I know not what shade which they call the honourable, a name not so much solid as splendid.* The same Torquatus, the solidity he had denied the Stoics, denies likewise to the poets, and to the mathematicians, 71: *Would he spend his time in unrolling the poets, as I and Triarius have done at your urging, in whom there is no solid utility, and all the delight is childish? or, like Plato, wear himself out in music, geometry, numbers, the stars? which, setting out from false beginnings, cannot be true, and, if they were true, would contribute nothing whereby we might live more pleasantly, that is, better?*

§ 842. What then? While learned cavillers throw up to one another in turn that nothing solid underlies the things of him whom someone has chosen as his adversary, truth meanwhile suffers, and the spectators of neither party are dragged into a truly troublesome fluctuation about nearly all true things, like a sick man expiring amid the quarrels of his physicians. Philosophers have their solidity, mathematicians have theirs, but there is also one, of another [p. 578] kind indeed, § 829, for historians, orators, poets: just as there is a truth to be set forth, § 423, 424, just as there is a light to be shed around this truth, § 614, 617, so too there is a certainty resulting from these, which, begetting conviction, will demonstrate the logical and intellectual solidity of the author, and producing persuasion, his aesthetic solidity. § 839.

§ 843. Aesthetic persuasion 1) is occupied with those things of which a complete and scientific certainty is given, of which the author perhaps has, and usefully, an intellectual conviction as well; indeed also with arguments demonstrating matters of this kind rigidly and severely, which are, and in so far as they are, at the same time persuasive, § 425, 617, 829, common to the logical and the aesthetic horizon, § 125, in so far as they do not ascend above the latter, § 121, 485. Hence the so often agitated question is best adjudged, whether philosophical matters, and proofs sought from the nature of things, befit the orator, e.g. the sacred orator: it is neither to be roundly denied, nor affirmed, since some things admitting a sufficiently easy persuasion occur in philosophical questions and argumentations alike, while others will hardly be able to be enclosed in reasons fit for persuading, and all of them, if proffered in the same way and manner as they are proffered to scientific conviction, [p. 579] would be chanted in vain to the promiscuous crowds of hearers.

§ 844. Aesthetic persuasion 2), while it aims directly at the sensate certainty of the things to be treated, will sometimes, in persuading about the parts, indirectly set the whole before the beautiful intellect and the sagacity of reason as certain, not without conviction at the same time, § 428, 617. Such a conviction will then sometimes be extensively greater, and may be stronger in the number of its marks, than another that is intensively more distinct and more fit for the sciences, M. § 532. Yet it is not aimed at by the aesthetician primarily in so far as it is conviction, but in so far as we were led to it by persuasions of the parts that were eminently necessary. For Horace *the writer of the Trojan war*

*What is beautiful, what base, what useful, what not,* / *Says more plainly and better than Chrysippus and Crantor.*

We shall not dwell on the comparisons, § 842. It suffices as an example that the objects of moral demonstrations are also the objects of the poet; who may persuade of the same things of which they convince, nay, may at length convince the beautiful intellect and the sagacity of reason of the same things of which they had convinced the pure intellect and the solidity of reason, M. § 637, 645.

[p. 580] § 845. Aesthetic persuasion 3) is occupied with things aesthetically probable, whether they are at the same time logically probable or not, § 485, provided only that the logical improbability, now present also to the minds of your people, is not so great that, destroying all aesthetic probability, it takes away all persuasion at the same time. § 486. Here belong Horace’s persuasive arguments to the ship, I. od. 3:

*So may the goddess who rules Cyprus,* / *So may Helen’s brothers, bright stars,* / *And the father of the winds guide you,* / *All the others bound fast, save Iapyx.*

It is logically improbable both that a ship is moved by good vows and words, and that the ship’s tutelary deity steers the ship, and that those twin fires, which among sailors are as a good omen, are Helen’s brothers, and are bright stars, and that there is a father of the winds. Yet they were not aesthetically improbable to Virgil, a poet so well versed in mythology that he fashioned nymphs even out of ships, upon whom passive and active persuasion assuredly falls, § 836.

§ 846. Aesthetic persuasion most gladly, on account of wonder-working, § 824, is occupied 4) with those things which had been, to your people, even aesthetically doubtful and [p. 581] improbable, if you know your powers suffice to render your people nonetheless aesthetically certain of the same. § 818. Because most declaimers of suasoriae neglect this hypothesis, Juvenal, Sat. I, laughs in his own person at their habit of chasing paradoxes:

*and we* / *Gave counsel to Sulla, that as a private man he should sleep* / *Soundly.*

But now the same Satire persuades more happily of a corruption of morals, not to be curbed even by judgements and penalties, which is aesthetically improbable:

*Since this despoiler of a ward driven to prostitution presses the people* / *With flocks of companions, and this man, condemned by an empty* / *Judgement. For what is infamy, when the money is safe?* / *Marius in exile drinks from the eighth hour, and enjoys the gods* / *In their anger. But you, victorious province, weep.*

#### Sectio L. Evidentia Aesthetica. — Section L. Aesthetic Evidence

§ 847. From verisimilars of this kind, § 843–846, when aesthetic persuasiveness, § 838, sheds about them sensate perspicuity, § 618, there arises thence [p. 582] sensate evidence, M. § 531, which others would call demonstration to the eye, to the senses, palpable demonstration, the analogue of a demonstration that convinces intellectually. This evidence, since very many judge it immediate, intuitive and patent of itself, they not rarely prefer to one that must first be elicited by arguing, even if the latter should at length most fully convince the intellect. The opinion of very many is put forward by Cotta in Cicero, de nat. deor. III. 9: *I neither, in cases at law, if anything is evident* (sensately) *about which there is agreement among all* (for there is not agreement among all about everything that is evident to the intellect, nor are all the things about which there is said to be agreement among all intellectually evident) *am accustomed to argue. For perspicuity* (sensate) *is weakened by argumentation.* (since an intellectual one is rather aimed at) *nor, if I did this in forensic cases, would I do the same in this subtlety of discourse — — — — But because you did not trust that the matter was as perspicuous as you would wish, you therefore wanted to teach by many arguments that the gods exist. For me one thing was enough, that our ancestors had so handed it down to us. But you despise all authorities, you fight with reason. Allow, then, my reason to contend with your reason. You bring forward all these arguments why there should be gods, and a matter in my opinion by no means doubtful you make doubtful by arguing.*

[p. 583] § 848. Lucilius had begun his speech to the same purport, II. 4. *What can be,* he says, *so open, and so perspicuous, when we have looked up at the sky and contemplated the heavenly bodies, as that there is some divinity of a most excellent mind? — — — — He who doubts this, I really do not understand why he could not likewise doubt whether there is a sun or none. For how is this more evident than that?* They agree in this, that the existence of a divinity is evident to men, sensately too; they disagree in this, that Lucilius labours, not badly, to join this evidence with the intellectual, while Cotta, less well, rejects the evidence of arguments. § 841, 847.

§ 849. But just as some seem to themselves to be convinced even mathematically through intellectual evidence, yet sometimes with a false persuasion: so too it is necessary to distinguish true sensate evidence from false. § 832. Aesthetic evidence follows the former, § 835–838, although sometimes, through human error, it can lead into an evidence to which not a little false persuasion is admixed, § 830. Livy, Ἀδεισιδαίμων [free from superstition], VIII. 9, gives a pretty example of this thing, relating *Decius devoting himself for the legions, and, girt in the Gabine girding, hurling himself into the midst of the enemy.* For after he has reported [p. 584] certain things from the false persuasion of the spectators: *Most evident,* he continues, *was this, that wherever he rode in on his horse, there they were terrified, no otherwise than as if struck by a pestilential star: but where he fell, overwhelmed with missiles, from there now, beyond doubt, the dismayed cohorts of the Latins made flight and desolation far and wide.*

§ 850. For this reason Cicero, top. 97, demands *that narrations look to their own ends, i.e. that they be plain, that they be brief,* § 170, *that they be evident, credible, moderate, with dignity. Which, although they ought to be in the whole speech* (in every charming meditation), *are yet more proper to narrating.* Here plain credibility will fulfil many of the requirements of evidence. § 847. The same, de am. 27, when he says *that friendship arises rather from a certain application of the mind, with a certain feeling of loving, than from a reckoning of how much utility it is going to have; which indeed, of what sort it is, can be detected even in certain beasts, yet much more evidently in man:* comprehends under the name of the more evident what is much truer, and clearer, hence also more certain by the very sense.

§ 851. We shall therefore procure for charming meditations that beautiful evidence of which we have hitherto treated, 1) by our truth, § 423–613, by giving it its light, § 614–828, 2) by stripping the cosmetic of truth from the falsehoods and errors opposed to this [p. 585] truth. § 763. *Since two causes stand against perspicuous and evident things, just as many aids are to be procured against them. For the first thing that stands against them is that people fix and direct their mind too little upon the things that are perspicuous, so as to be able to recognise with how great a light they are surrounded. The second is that certain people, hemmed in and deceived by fallacious and captious questionings, when they cannot dissolve them, fall away from the truth. It is therefore needful both to have ready what can be answered on behalf of perspicuity, and to be armed, so that we can meet the questionings and shake off the captious snares.* Cic. quaest. ac. IIII. 46.

§ 852. Perspicuous and evident are often, for Cicero, among synonyms. For thus, hiding under another’s person, 17, ἐνάργειαν [vividness], he says, *of the Greeks let us, if you please, name perspicuity or evidence, and let us fabricate words, if need be.* But since philosophers have no need of synonyms, we call evidence only the perspicuity of the true, in such a way that we grant indeed that all evident things are at the same time perspicuous, *because nothing is clearer than* ἐναργείᾳ [vividness], yet do not concede that all perspicuous things are by this very fact at the same time evident. § 614. ἐνάργεια [vividness] *is called by Cicero illustration and evidence, which seems not so much to say as to show.* [p. 586] Quint. VI. 2. We for our part shall not deny that all evidence illustrates, § 730, since we too *think that no speech can be found more illustrious than evidence itself,* yet we do not grant that every illustration has its evidence within it. § 731. *Evidence, or, as others say,* ἐνάργειαν [vividness], *representation, is more than perspicuity* and illustration. *It is a great virtue to enunciate the things of which we speak clearly, and so that they seem to be seen,* with the certainty customary to experience. Quint. VIII. 3.

§ 853. The clearer, though sensately, § 614, hence the more vividly, § 619, the more numerous and the greater the truths which in persuading are set before the mind’s eyes of your personal objects: the greater will be the persuasion, the greater the evidence that will accompany it, M. § 880. Hence, on account of the greatest persuasion that you may be able to furnish, a new obligation arises for charming wits to choose for themselves, as far as they can, the truest themes, since, other things being equal, the truer a thing is, the more persuasive it will likewise be and the more evidently it can be shown. § 555.

§ 854. Aesthetic persuasion too, like logical, will either be direct and ostensive, by which we evidently declare that what we posit is true, or very verisimilar, attending less to the falsehood opposed to it, [p. 587] or apagogic and proceeding indirectly, leading to what is aesthetically false and like the false, or incongruous, by which we surround what we posit with the light of truth and verisimilitude, once the falsity of what is repugnant to it has been detected, § 851.

#### Sectio LI. Confirmatio. — Section LI. Confirmation

§ 855. Direct and ostensive persuasion is CONFIRMATION, just as the apagogic, persuading of the falsity of the opposite, the analogue of logical refutation, is REFUTATION, which you might also call the weakening of the contrary. Cicero joins this twofold way of persuading thus, de Diu. II. 38: *You indeed,* he says, *do not confirm the fall of the victims by the likeness of the lots, but weaken the lots by comparison with the victims.* Nay, even short of art, nature herself judges both faculties conjoined, since *we are so born that we can confirm what we intend, and refute what is said against it.* de or. I. 90. He relates that the rhetoricians likewise *bid* us, among other things, *to confirm our own points by arguments and reasons, then refute the contrary.* II. 80.

[p. 588] § 856. It belongs no less to poets than to orators to persuade, § 829. Hence Lucretius, about to persuade, though unhappily, both refutes, II. 174, *of the human race*

*all things for the sake* / *When they feign that the gods established, in all things* / *They seem to have strayed greatly from true reason*

and confirms, 177:

*For although I may not know what the first-beginnings of things are,* / *This nonetheless I would dare, from the very workings of heaven,* / *To confirm, and to establish from many other things,* / *That by no means was the nature of the world created for us* / *By divine power, which is endowed with so great a fault.* / *Which things we shall make plain to you later, Memmius.* / — — — — / *Now is the place, as I think, in these matters to confirm* / *This too for you, that no bodily thing can by its own force* / *Be borne upward, and move upward.* / *Nor let the bodies of flames deceive you in this,*

and so he goes on refuting.

§ 857. The first postulate here is, if you intend anything, that you *confirm it without doubt,* even though [p. 589] not always can all uncertainty be removed; although sometimes something may be lacking to full persuasion, or may lie open to the analogue of reason, yet that state in which the reasons on both of the contrary sides seem comparable and equal must be utterly torn out, first from your own mind, then from the minds of your people, unless you want your vain attempt at persuading to be laughed at in you. A certain certainty can coexist with a certain uncertainty, but no certainty, hence no persuasion, no evidence, no confirmation, with the total uncertainty of doubters. Hence the very patrons of catholic doubt, as soon as they prettily want to lead someone into this hazard, are wont to forget their own counsels so far that they seem to doubt of nothing less than of this, that one must doubt of everything.

§ 858. Next, never set about confirming by reasons and arguments things of which your people have already persuaded themselves that they are sufficiently immediately certain. § 847. Persuasively Epicurus *denies that there is need of reason or of disputation as to why pleasure is to be sought, pain to be fled. This he thinks is felt, as that fire is hot, that snow is white, that honey is sweet, none of which it would be needful to confirm by elaborate reasons; it is enough merely to point them out. For there is a difference between an argument and conclusion of reason and a moderate noticing* [p. 590] *and reminding. By the one certain hidden and as it were involved things are opened up, by the other ready and open things are indicated.* § 851. Cic. de fin. I. 30. Just as the demonstrator effects identical indemonstrable propositions from no further arguments, so he who would confirm persuasion, in judgements which seem to the senses of his people or to the analogue of reason sufficiently evident of themselves, careless of further proof, rests content.

§ 859. Further, if the things you wish to confirm must be proven by reasons, by arguments sought a priori, either prefer, if you would persuade elegantly, or at least add, as often as it can be done, reasons a posteriori. Divination can be confirmed a priori; yet, in a way more fit for persuading, *Cratippus is wont to conclude his reasoning from the very examples of true prophecies and dreams in this manner: If without eyes the office and function of eyes cannot exist, but the eyes can sometimes fail to perform their function: he who has even once so used his eyes as to discern true things has the sense of eyes that discern true things. Likewise therefore, if without divination the office and function of divination cannot exist, but someone who has divination can sometimes err and not discern true things, it is enough for confirming divination that something has once been so divined that nothing seems to have fallen out by chance.* [p. 591] *But of this kind there are innumerable instances. It must therefore be confessed that there is divination.* Cic. de diu. I. 71.

§ 860. If in your meditations 1) certain more general and more abstract things, 2) more determinate, individual and singular things should admit some confirmation by arguments as well, yet time and place do not permit you to confirm both: take the former rather, like lemmas from the intellectual disciplines, as if known, and spend your effort on the proof of the latter, rather than the reverse. § 677, 752. Pliny, l. XXXVII, in the proem, taking *gems as the majesty of the nature of things compressed into a narrow space, confirms that in the age of Ismenias emeralds were wont to be engraved, by the edict of Alexander the Great, by which he forbade that he be engraved on a gem by anyone other than Pyrgoteles, without doubt,* § 857, *the most famous in that art.* c. I.

§ 861. An ARGUMENTATION exhibiting complete certainty is NECESSARY; one that furnishes an incomplete certainty, in things somewhat uncertain, is only probable. For that doubtful and much more improbable argumentations carry with them no certainty, no persuasion, no confirmation, we have already seen. § 857, 485. Hence rightly Cicero, de inu. I. 44: *Every argumentation will have to be either probable or necessary.* [p. 592] Unless he errs in describing the necessary: *necessarily,* he continues, *are demonstrated those things which can neither happen nor be proven otherwise than they are said,* he slips at least in the appended example: *If she has given birth, she has lain with a man.*

§ 862. If, confirming elegantly, you should sometimes allow yourself to touch upon some necessary argumentation too, one, namely, which, exacted to the formula of logic, would at the same time approve to the intellect the complete certainty of its form: yet it is your part now to abstain less from charming concealments of this, namely of the legitimate form, since it can suffice you to proffer to the analogue of reason a sufficiently evident verisimilitude of the scientific argument § 483, 829. But for this a complete conviction of the form is not required. Hence Cicero advises *to treat* even *the manner of arguing which turns on necessary* § 861 *demonstration* § 847 *chiefly either by complexion,* the enthymematic-cryptic dilemma, *or by enumeration,* the disjunctive syllogism in the modus tollendo ponens, but again fairly cryptic, *or by simple conclusion,* of which he gives as example the enthymematic hypothetical syllogism in the modus ponens. 45.

§ 863. Cicero indeed adds: *And to this one will have to attend diligently,* [p. 593] *that this kind may not in any way be refuted, so that the confirmation does not merely have in itself the manner of argumentation and a certain likeness of a necessary conclusion, but that the argumentation itself consists of necessary reason.* But a fair interpreter will notice 1) that the diligence due in the aesthetic forum is demanded, not the mathematical accuracy of logicians, 2) that just as only a persuasive confirmation is intended, so too one need not take care that your argumentation cannot be refuted even by logical subtleties and by the most intense force of purer reason, but that it cannot be reprehended, § 855, that it not be ready to hand to detect in it, even to the sense and before the tribunal of the analogue of reason, some aesthetic falsity S. XXVIII, 3) that paralogisms, to be sure, and mere sophisms are rejected, in which there is no force for persuading, but only an empty show of certainty, § 837, yet that it suffices for Cicero that the argumentation consist of necessary reason, and that he does not require that the whole of this sufficient reason in arguing be, as far as possible, most manifestly submitted to the perspicacity of reason itself. § 802, 843.

§ 864. Suppose you, about to meditate elegantly, light upon a judgement of the kind we logicians call demonstrative, and which hence, in the sciences at any rate, needs demonstration, yet the same, as far as [p. 594] you know, is admitted among your people, agreed upon among all; suppose there is also ready to hand some necessary argumentation, of which, however, you foresee that either the whole compass, or some part which you cannot cut away, will be less perspicuous and evident to your people than the conclusion itself already is. In this case gladly omit that whole necessary argumentation of yours and rest content with a moderate noticing of your demonstrative judgement, which is yet at the same time now sufficiently evident of itself, § 858, 847. The same rule will prescribe the measure and limits of how far it is to the purpose of one who would confirm elegantly to ascend in the analysis of arguments through prosyllogisms.

§ 865. Suppose you light, in aesthetic meditations, upon a demonstrative proposition which you well foresee needs the confirmation of arguments, and you have a necessary argumentation ready to hand, but at the same time some other, merely probable one. Meanwhile, in the former, your mind forebodes that your people, your primary personal object, will stick at the very hinge of the proof, as if at some Gordian knot set before them, and that the sensate evidence relative to that necessary argument of yours will be wanting to it, either wholly or in great part, while the intellectual evidence transcends, now at least, the horizon of your people. [p. 595] But this other, the probable proof, you may hope will be plain and perspicuous to your people and thoroughly evident even to the analogue of reason itself. Here prefer the probable argumentation to the necessary. The uncertain to the certain? Credible things to the certainly true? § 837. Assuredly. But not because the uncertain is to be preferred, but because for your people it is more sensately certain than things intellectually certain, which, now at least, they do not understand. Not because the credible is not certainly true, but because for your people this probable thing is nonetheless more credible than those certainly true things, whose evidence, now at least, transcends them. § 839.

§ 866. *Probable,* when Cicero says, 46, *is what is wont to happen,* § 484, offers an occasion to observe the fourth species of ἤθους [character], § 195, 495, 698, by which the moral manner of thinking § 226 1) as if doing something else, tacitly persuades its people ever more and more intimately of the approved morals of the author, § 227, so that they seem to themselves to have evidently detected, by their own exertion at length, the person of the thinker and his moral character, as absolutely lovable, so now simply honourable, now noble, now altogether grand, according as he has wished to paint himself in proportion to his materials, his objects and his natural dynamometry, § 462. § 213

[p. 596] § 867. The persuasive ἦθος [character] § 866 2) mindful of those *who will think nothing right save what agrees with their own morals,* Corn. pr., whom you know to be not the fewest, if any things that it wishes to persuade agree with these, it will triumph by commending them through the custom of the ancestors and sacrosanct institutions and rites, through the very usages of nations, and through the long practice of the more moral; if any things to be persuaded seem to disagree with these, it does not take them up to be treated, except under the hypothesis of § 846; 3) it labours to persuade its people intimately of the truest beauty of the best morals, of the true baseness of vices, chiefly through the natural colours of both, § 574; 4) in morals most strictly true it not only calls a spade a spade, but also confirms it, when the pretty mask can be beautifully drawn off from vices, and the ugly one from virtues, § 580.

§ 868. The persuasive ἦθος [character] 5) interprets morals and deeds, of which one can doubt, now in a good, now in a bad sense, not without probability, at least aesthetic, according to the rules of moral truth, of which § 435, 467; according to the same 6) it will surround morals and deeds feigned historically with such probability that, although we may not know how the matter was done, [p. 597] we should yet scarcely shudder at an oath of credulity, persuaded that it could not have been done otherwise, § 509; 7) in the very poetic fictions of moral matters it will observe the certainty resulting from the analogy with the poetic world § 511, 513, and from their internal verisimilitude, § 589, so that your spectator is persuaded that, though not things which were done, yet things most truly narrated are such as ought to happen, heterocosmic things, § 586; 8) everywhere it will so temper light and shade in painting the naked truth of moral matters, § 666, that this sometimes shines out in full light, § 625, now is only hinted at, many things being left to the spectator to think over, § 654–662, now things most strictly true lie open to the analogue of reason and to historical faith, § 441, now the spectator, vividly feeling that truth rules in the very fables, § 556, is at the same time swept into admiration of splendid lies, § 477, by well-placed wonder-working, § 808.

§ 869. *Probable,* further, is for Cicero *what is placed in opinion,* de inu. I. 46. We have conceded that to be verisimilar, § 484, yet we cannot generally attribute probability to it, not even aesthetic probability always. § 485. Cicero’s examples are: *That for the impious punishments are prepared in the underworld; that those who* [p. 598] *devote themselves to philosophy do not think that there are gods.* The first is still the opinion of very many, yet I should not obtain probability, even aesthetic, among some of my hearers by arguments sought from the underworld, among those, namely, who wrongly opine that it is something great to deny everything that is to come after this life. Nor among others should I now make more progress in confirming by threatening the stone of Sisyphus and the fleeing waters of Tantalus, since they no longer have these punishments of the underworld in their opinions. The second is a most true opinion, if you speak of the gods and of those who philosophise more exactly; but if you impute atheism to philosophers, you will now find many hearers upon whom accusations of this kind no longer impose.

§ 870. Our assent given to something uncertain is OUR OPINION. Someone would in vain thrust his own opinions upon those to be persuaded for the sake of confirming, unless they are at the same time the opinions of those for whom he chiefly thinks. Hence before all it is to be seen, in the use of opinions, whether they are opinions of your primary personal object, or perhaps only of others. We opine either, without having weighed the reasons for assent, by PREJUDICED OPINION, or we assent to an opinion now logically, now at least aesthetically [p. 599] probable, or even one doubtful to us, or one altogether improbable, since we think that this is either permissible or altogether morally necessary, or we posit it as verisimilar for us, until it becomes clearer whether it is probable or not, so to speak, interimistically and provisionally. Since you can seldom and hardly be certain to which class a given opinion within the minds of your people belongs, after this, whether what can be adduced for confirming is placed in their opinion, it will be your greater care whether the opinion of your people is, in the judgement of your own mind, an error or not, so that you can act according to § 574.

§ 871. If, finally, Cicero calls *probable* everything *that has* with respect to customs and opinions *some similarity, contraries, equals, and whatever falls under the same reasoning*, de inu. I. 46, I fear that, if you follow him, you will often enough confuse the verisimilar and the probable, the perspicuous and the evident. § 852. In the very examples chosen by that most eloquent man I am mistaken, or else a rather small power of persuading the analogue of reason will be detected, if it is the mark of the learned to distinguish what is splendid and dazzles the eyes at first glance from what is evident? Meanwhile those pigeon-holes, which will not always give one, will at least sometimes supply a probable confirmation, § 132.

§ 872. [p. 600] Of equal value are the loci of probables that follow, 48, *the sign, the credible, the adjudged, the comparable.* He says that a *sign* is *what signifies something*, etc. Thus far who would deny it? But when he adds of signs in general that *they need testimony and weightier confirmation*, I should concede that about all signs the less, the less 1) all signs are only probable, since from some there is complete persuasion, e.g. from the putrefaction of the whole body the certainty of death, 2) probables too, if what is said be true, always need weightier confirmation, § 861, 865. Wretched sons of the physicians, who in their probable prognosis, having collated all the signs of diseases, nevertheless everywhere need testimony or weightier confirmation!

§ 873. *The credible* is not so much *established without any witness by the opinion of the hearer*, § 869, 870, as it persuades, on account of its internal probability, even then, when we have even the authority of the witnesses less thoroughly examined. *The adjudged* is for Cicero *a matter approved by the assent, or the authority, or the judgement of someone or of some persons*, and that *either religious, or common, or approved. The comparable* the same author divides into *image, collation and example.* § 871.

§ 874. [p. 601] After Cicero has recounted these things, § 861–873, and others like them, 49, he concludes thus: *But the fountain of confirmation has indeed been opened, so far as our ability allowed, and demonstrated no less lucidly than the nature of the matter bore.* § 639. Yet he does not seem to have satisfied himself, when he at once adds: *For the present we have only scattered, confusedly and in a mixed way, the numbers, modes and parts of arguing.* Let us say what the case is. The fountain of any confirmation whatever will be opened not by certain general topics, but by the nature of the theme more deeply known, and by the truth of the things to be confirmed, examined as clearly as can be done. The scientific conviction of the one who is to meditate beautifully, and the habit of demonstrating rigorously, will profit him much, if he knows how to temper the power of reason and to arouse the power of illuminating the arguments splendidly. Nor will any rhetorician, any aesthetician, demonstrate these so various fountains, but rather beautiful erudition, § 63, and experience, which will supply the practice of setting forth, in matters of fact, existence by the most probable signs. To those who are to draw from the fountains already described, certain topics will perhaps sometimes give some handle; otherwise they will be sand for a foundation of aesthetically solid confirmations. § 839.

§ 875. [p. 602] After *the invention of the argument* Cicero prescribes *the completion of the argumentation*, 50, and thinks it comes about *either by induction or by ratiocination*. Now even logicians of one semester know that induction is a species of ratiocination. The same can be proved, if we take Cicero’s own description of *ratiocination*, 57: *It is a discourse eliciting something probable from the matter itself, which, when set out and known in itself, confirms itself by its own force and reasoning.* But it is a long-lived error to separate induction from ratiocination, as though from something opposed and plainly different, since ratiocination and its genus, approaching more closely to ordinary syllogisms, have been confused almost up to these very times. We shall not now say much about the form of beautiful argumentations, since there is a fitter place in aesthetics for expounding it.

§ 876. To the place in aesthetics on the right forming of beautiful ratiocinations let us relegate for examination what Cicero has on inductions, n. 51–56, and the treatment of charming ratiocinations, both simple and concatenated, from which it will appear that the former can be, and usually are, tripartite, yet do not always necessarily consist of even so many parts: that the latter can be of only three parts, yet usually are completed in four [p. 603] or five parts, although they do not altogether shrink from even more members. Whence the controversy of which Cicero treats, l. c. 57–76, can be adjudicated. Now let us only note the judgement of the same author, by which he reckons that *there are abhorrent from oratorical use* (hence much more from poetic, in general from aesthetic use) *many and obscure things* (κατ᾽ αἴσθησιν [according to sensation], § 631) *about which a certain art has been constituted* (the logical one), namely *by what reasonings argumentations are handled in philosophy.*

§ 877. For it furnishes the occasion of now adding to the liberty conceded in § 862, 863 an expressly dissuasive counsel: that in charming confirmations you do not employ ratiocinations whose form, legitimate according to the art of logic, appears at once to those versed in logic, retained without any concealment, without any leap, without any additions or variations, and which the logicians call formal. That formal syllogisms of this kind give to those unskilled in logic some certainty, yet a greater one than others, I have never found by experience, nor am I convinced of it a priori, if they are compared, as is proper, with the truly persuasive ones. Those skilled in logic will either judge it worth the effort to contemplate some one of your ratiocinations also in the greater formal certainty of reason, [p. 604] and then they themselves will be able to clothe your ratiocinations in logical form: or they do not desire certainty of this kind, at least now; hence you will neither profit nor particularly please these. Meanwhile you will be judged to think things transcending your present horizon, § 121, maimed things, § 159, neglecting the aesthetic ἦθος [character], § 193, 194, badly thin things, § 232, dry, meagre, arid, bloodless, jejune things, § 246, preferring a badly placed zeal for truth to your present zeal for aesthetic verisimilitude, § 555, 561, proving but not persuading, § 541, aesthetically obscure things, § 633, 636, and yet not aesthetically solid. § 840, 842.

§ 878. The one who is to confirm, caring rather for the matter of his proofs, according to S. XII, will be able sometimes to uncover a certain hyle and as it were forest of reasons 1) of several coordinate ones, each of which is indeed connected prettily enough with what is to be confirmed, yet seem fairly independent of one another, 2) of ones subordinated among themselves, so that the whole and entire series, drawn out all the way to what is to be confirmed, are not so many, but to each of these many concur as further proofs, and can grow together into a good long concatenation of several proving members. If [p. 605] brevity bids some be left out, the later ones will be cut away more charmingly than those described in the first place. For the number and as it were a certain synathroismus of ever new and new arguments will have more grace, more vividness, than the stretching out of a single argument, perhaps transcending the patience of the analogue of reason. The latter would exhibit an intensive consciousness of the truth, a certainty of reason lovable to sagacity, a greater one, I confess; but the former, seen by the analogue of reason to prove more copiously, promises the greater extensive consciousness of truth now to be sought, sensate evidence, § 847.

§ 879. Yet let the one who is to confirm not only count his arguments, § 878, but also weigh them, § 177, judging how much the truth and verisimilitude of what is to be confirmed can be made manifest from each, M. § 697, so that he can employ those worth relating, placing each according to the just dispensation of light and shade, § 666, with the FRIVOLOUS ones altogether rejected, whose power of proving lucidly is either none or infinitely small. § 839, 191. Nor should you rashly hope that these can accompany the weightier ones, at least as shades. § 661. For what? if the admirable feebleness of even one proof of this kind be dragged out of the shade, and it be evidently set before the eyes [p. 606] how inept it is for persuading? Then indeed it will be able to sprinkle even the best of the others with the sheer darkness of contempt and oblivion. § 665. *It is better to refrain altogether from reproach of a life earlier lived than to assail the accused with trifling, or frivolous, or manifestly false charges, because credit is withdrawn from the rest; and he who objects nothing may be believed to have omitted abuse as superfluous, while he who heaps up empty charges confesses his argument on the earlier matters to be empty, in which he would rather be beaten than keep silent.* Quintilian, Inst. or. L. VII. c. 2, *on conjecture.*

§ 880. If the one who is to confirm has rested content, in choosing arguments, with those alone that illustrate verisimilitude, § 483, § 730; in my judgement at least he has satisfied his present duty, § 839, 855; yet since he is bidden by nature itself, § 104, to illustrate that species or degree of verisimilitude which he had promised, or which the matter, or his personal object especially to be attended to, has rightly demanded, he will either omit altogether, or at least cast into the shades, arguments that would only illustrate a verisimilitude heterogeneous from the one sought, or less in the same degree, reserving the light solely for those which exhibit such and so much verisimilitude as was demanded. S. XXXV. Examples will declare the general [p. 607] law, § 730, more determinate rules.

§ 881. If the question is of the natural possibility of some object, § 432, or of the moral one more broadly so called, § 433, for arguments that would confirm only the absolute one there is either no place, or a place only in the shade. § 431, 880. If the question is of the moral truth of some object, § 435, for arguments that would confirm only its moral possibility more broadly so called, § 433, there is either no place, or a place solely in the shades. § 880. If the hypothetical unity of action, place, time is sought, § 439, for arguments that would confirm the absolute unity of generalities there is no place, unless perhaps in the shades. § 880. If the question is of the singular truth of an object, i.e. its existence, for arguments that would confirm solely the truth of the generalities under which the object is contained there is either no place, or only one in the shades. § 440, 880. If it is asked whether something is true in the strictest sense, for arguments that would declare only any possibility whatever of it, or its heterocosmic truth, there is either no place, or only the shade. § 441, 880.

§ 882.
