If the one who is to confirm fictions true in the strictest sense, § 506, should bring forward for persuasion merely historical ones in the stricter sense, § 509, [p. 608] or altogether poetic ones, § 511, even if the former did not seem to contradict our world, nor the latter the world of the poets, § 513, nor to savour of utopia, § 514, analogical ones, § 576, nor altogether unknown, § 518, nor anomalous figments, § 520, indeed if he has poured the chief light upon fictions of this kind, he will rightly be judged to be covering a lie with a lie, lest the rain come through, § 881.

§ 883. If the question is of the verisimilitude, in the strictest sense, of a given object, § 530, let the one who is to confirm omit fables, § 526, historical ones too, and much more poetic ones, § 527, or at least not dwell much on them, § 881, unless perhaps sometimes in the case of things probable in the stricter sense, § 532, or of narrations of the kind which, mingled with fabulous matter, can at least render aesthetically probable that they contain things true in the strictest sense, § 526, 527, § 533. In the last case the one who is to confirm, appealing to those things which he sufficiently shows to be true in the strictest sense in the narrations that are called fables, § 485, 847, has in general no cause either to pass over these arguments in silence or to shade them, § 880.

§ 884. Since the one who is to confirm aesthetico-dogmatic things satisfies his present duty, although he does not [p. 609] ascend beyond dogmatic verisimilitude, § 577, to be lucidly set before the reader, § 839, there is in general no reason for him to refrain from mentioning, or even from setting in some brilliance, persuasive arguments that are perhaps logically doubtful or improbable, yet aesthetically probable, or that can conveniently be raised by him to this probability, § 486. The aesthetico-historical manner of thinking in the stricter sense, zealous for the true and the solid, will suffer the one who is to confirm its matters neither to halt within the reasons of possibilities of whatever kind, § 881, nor within some poetic verisimilitude to be brilliantly painted, § 880, nor to rise to the rational calculations and discussions of credibility, of the authority of witnesses, of the interpretation of ambiguous passages, of the adjudication of apparent contradictions, of geography, chronology, genealogy, etc., § 580, 583. Hence the so many prodromes, notes, marginalia, appendices, etc. of historians wishing to sacrifice at once to charming solidity, § 839, and to the logical accuracy of the critics, § 584, 847.

§ 885. Let the one who is to confirm have persuasive arguments enough in number: it does not suffice thereupon to reject the frivolous ones, and those not promising the verisimilitude sought: upon each of the things to be thought explicitly there must also be poured enough extensive light, vividness, [p. 610] and clarity, in proportion to its dignity, § 618, so that the very things which are employed for persuading be illuminated, especially in that part, side or face of themselves which contains the NERVE OF THE PROOF, the necessity, these being posited, of admitting at the same time the truth of what is to be confirmed, § 855, 839. Hence if, even of coordinate reasons, § 878, and ones not inept on account of § 879–884, there still remain more in the hyle of the arguments that are to persuade than that each can be set forth in a rounded brevity in a light sufficient for it: it is better to bring forward some of the same, the aesthetically firmer, in all their natural brilliance, § 625, 622, without neglecting even true colours, § 688, or the most florid ones, § 693, up to a not mediocre wonder-working, if it can be done, § 808, than, with all of them too much heaped up, each set out point by point and cut short, or at any rate more briefly than is right, perhaps to overwhelm and to blunt the readers, e.g. the primary ones, and not truly to persuade. § 839, 634.

#### Sectio LII. Reprehensio. — Section LII. Refutation

§ 886. Although refutation can sometimes be attempted only with the intention of confirming a thesis by indirect proof, § 854, [p. 611] we shall nevertheless consider it here in those weightier cases where the contrary of what we are to confirm, opposed to it, and the antithesis of our thesis, could seem to our primary object to contain in itself something of truth, or much. § 855. For to refute or reprehend aesthetically those who dissent is more necessary for those who are to think beautifully than it is for the logical demonstrator to refute those who contradict his rigidly demonstrated conclusions, because 1) it is laid down in the opinion of many, though a prejudiced one, that things against which many objections (of whatever kind, however small) can be raised do not seem certain. § 870.

§ 887. 2) Truly also every probability, as much the logical as the aesthetic, grows and is intensified not only by increasing the reasons fighting for your opinion, but also by diminishing and diluting the reasons that would have made against it. § 485. Hence for logical probabilities too it is more necessary to weaken the reasons for doubting, or to expose to the intellect the falsity of what is said against them, than it is for demonstrations that you would call complete and mathematical. 3) The balance of aesthetic probability is not drawn over to your side by so slight a weight as that of logical probability, nor do we discern so acutely by sense as by intellect [p. 612] which side of the reasons outweighs. Hence aesthetic persuasion is served not only by adding weights to the side that ought to sink, but also by taking them from the side that ought to rise more noticeably, by which the latter will seem more like the false, the former more like what is sensately true. § 483, 489.

§ 888. Columella may truly have said: *To have reprehended the sinner is not enough, if you do not also teach the way of the right*, and the one who is to think prettily may rightly join both, § 854, 855; yet in § 886, 887 we seem to have given reasons from which it can in part be understood how it comes about that in the sphere of sensate cognition you more easily find pedagogues than fathers; those who try whether

*they can catch back from ruin those going to it,*

than those who directly lead those not yet fallen into byways into the love of the best; satirists, finally, more easily than exhorters. The best is what Cicero attributes to virtue, if the pleasure of Aristippus should flatter our senses, Qu. ac. I. 139, if in similar cases, practical as well as theoretical, the persuasion of a good man *calls back, or rather reprehends with the hand. It says those are the motions of cattle. It joins man to god.* § 829, 838.

§ 889. To open the fountains for refutation is the less necessary, if Sallust to Caesar spoke truly: [p. 613] *For reprehending the deeds or sayings of others everyone’s spirit burns. Scarcely does a mouth seem open enough, or a tongue ready enough, to unroll what the breast has meditated.* Yet Cicero had scarcely defined it, de inu. I. 61, as that *by which, in arguing, the confirmation of one’s adversaries*, § 886, 855, *is diluted or weakened or lightened*, § 887, 854, when he assigns to it the same fountain which confirmation uses, 62, on which see § 861–873 and especially § 874, where what was said most truly suits at the same time every fountain of refutations. § 65.

§ 890. Meanwhile let these be counsels for those who are to refute: 1) let the falsity not only of the arguments and premisses, but also of the conclusion that is principally to be impugned, be painted. § 839. What is useful in logical refutations themselves, I judge here more necessary, § 887, since many of us are so constituted that we tacitly settle with ourselves that, even if some conclusion has hitherto been proved either wrongly or from less suitable premisses, it is nevertheless not yet on that account already false; that it can be true enough, and by a reason still unknown to us either already duly proved, or soon to be proved by someone more learned than we. The sap is drawn from this not-so-false opinion of many in a given example of refutation, if we do not halt [p. 614] at undermining the arguments of the antithesis, but also declare this conclusion itself to be aesthetically false, S. XXVIII, in such a way that our primary object despairs that it can be sustained by any props whatever, not only those given, but also perhaps those to be found. § 455.

§ 891. This counsel, § 890, is satisfied partly already by the confirmation itself, partly by the art of joining to our direct proof, and to the subversion of the antithesis’s premisses, or of its form of proving, or of both, things deduced from that same conclusion and antithesis taken as a premiss, lucidly, or even splendidly deduced, which yet at last and as if unexpectedly set before the eyes the great falsity and incongruity of the same for the analogue of reason, § 853, 884. Cicero de Inu. I. 63: *Every argumentation is refuted if either of those things that have been assumed one or more is not conceded* (by denying the premisses), *or, these being conceded, it is denied that the conclusion is made out from them: or if the very kind of argumentation is shown to be faulty* (by denying the form to be either certain or legitimate at all), *or if against a firm argumentation another equally firm, or firmer, is set.*

§ 892. [p. 615] In the same refutation several members of the disjunction set down by Cicero can be attempted one after another, § 891, provided only 2) that against the adversary’s arms contrary arms are always prepared, which seem to pour forth more true light, so that the greater light of ours obscures theirs. S. XXXX. Equally firm ones please only as much as firmer ones, § 891. Hither belong: to uncover the prejudices lurking in what is to be refuted, § 870, to break them, together with other false premisses, by more evident instances, S. L, to elude by comparisons the paralogisms of what is to be refuted, whose evident premisses nevertheless give a plainly false conclusion, S. XXVIII, to set before the eyes illegitimate leaps and the impossibility of supplying what is missing, etc., what the logicians teach, to carry out elegantly and to the measures of the analogue of reason. § 844.

§ 893. Especially 3) what are called arguments ad hominem will be usefully employed, by attacking the man you refute with his own arms, since there is scarcely anyone who can persuade himself to follow a man contradicting himself as an author of truth; hence in this way at least the prejudice of authority is shaken, which could have fought for the patrons of the antithesis, as at least aesthetically solid, [p. 616] § 839. *It is the part of the skilled man to find in the adversary’s pleading things that fight with themselves, or seem to fight.* Quint. inst. V. 13, the whole chapter of which deserves to be read here. 4) In refuting be the more solicitous about a manner of thinking sufficiently dignified, § 223, the less the aesthetician, with the logician, can urge abstinence from everything that moves the affects of the mind, § 22, and the more difficult it sometimes is to act vehemently and yet not angrily, § 722–725.

§ 894. The last counsel of dignity, § 182, I should wish to be considered here too as flowing from the very fountains of thinking elegantly, § 866, and not only insofar as virtue and piety demand the same, § 183. What not a few of the ancients permitted themselves in obscenities, § 184, almost more of them have taken a like licence in refuting, so that to impugn by reasons an old, but bad, custom seems the more necessary, the more examples, otherwise notable enough, today’s imitators of the same can easily shelter it by. How in Homer hero reprehends hero, who does not know? But how much more mannerly already is Virgil’s Aeneid in this? *In this very man* (Demosthenes), *who diligently examines the weight of all his words,* [p. 617] *Aeschines reprehends and harries certain things, and mocking them says they are harsh, odious, intolerable. Nay more, he asks him, since indeed he calls him a beast, whether those are words or monstrosities: so that to Aeschines not even Demosthenes seems to speak Attic.* Cic. Or. 26. At this refutation of the *beast*, whose part is it to blush? Demosthenes’s, or rather Aeschines’s?

§ 895. *How much more rightly this man, who contrives nothing* wrongfully: Cassius against Plancius and his patron, Cicero, to whom nevertheless *weightier and great* things now seem to be at stake, because Cassius *wished now as it were to reprehend and covertly accuse* Cicero’s *departure, which he had often lamented, saying that not aid had failed* Cicero, *but* Cicero *had failed the aid*? Cic. pro Plancio 86. Who does not know that the same departure could have been not only *reprehended* but even *harried* with *harsh, odious* words and such as would have seemed *intolerable* to Cicero, *mocking* both what was done and what was not done, had not the accuser of Plancius prettily, § 893, preferred a subdued manner of speaking against Plancius’s patron? § 725. Would that Cicero himself, against Piso, a man of censorial rank, against Antony himself, had everywhere tempered his vehemence with elegant modesty. § 722–724.

§ 896. Not the iambics of Archilochus, not the hendecasyllables of Catullus, not Naso’s Ibis, or other things, otherwise charming, but, on account of the neglected well-mannered [p. 618] manner of thinking of those who refute shamefully,

*I delay the darts dyed in Lycambes’s blood.* § 893, 186.

If one does not go where one must go, neither must aesthetic gravity go where one does go, § 189. I do not yet dare to prescribe in general to those who are to refute beautifully that they abstain altogether from everything that they call personal, or more barbarously personalities, from everything that most nearly touches that singular man whom you have contradicted, his fatherland, family, wit, character, life, youth, and whatever else there is, § 752–757; yet I confidently bid be far off, if anyone wishes to sacrifice to the graces, even the rummaging in the adversaries themselves for the lowest and most abject objects, § 195, since they cannot at all be set forth, § 224, 225, in a manner of thinking well-mannered in the stricter sense, § 227, or only absolutely such. § 228.

§ 897. Lest I myself commit, by providing them, what I dissuade from, it is not pleasant to bring forward examples abject even at first glance. Let one suffice, Seneca’s apocolocyntosis against Claudius, by which that Stoic branded upon the praise of his virtue a stain less sung of, yet perhaps less to be washed out, than avarice. Who would believe it? A philosopher, as great as Seneca seems, in exile in Sardinia had descended to tears and supplications [p. 619] poured out to Polybius, Claudius’s freedman, in which he flatters the wisdom of Claudius more than is enough; recalled by the same Claudius, not so much a bad as a simple man, and heaped with many benefits, so far as anything could proceed from the same which was done in his name, he praises the same emperor, his own, after his death through the mouth of Nero for his wisdom, so that he moved the hearers to laughter, and at the same time, no political reason pressing, he nevertheless laughs so bitterly at the man killed by poison that he could not restrain himself even from this jest, which I have never read without indignation: *This was his last utterance heard among men, after he had emitted a greater sound from that part with which he spoke more easily: Oh dear! I think I have befouled myself.* It is almost shameful to have written these things after Seneca.

§ 898. Verbal injuries, which wound even simple honour, are indeed most shameful in refutations which ought to guard the majesty of sublime things, § 317; more shameful in the middle manner of thinking, which already demands civility and the manners of intensified honour, § 278; nor yet are they free of shamefulness in the thin manner of thinking itself, § 262. If you have first been assailed with mud of this kind, and ought not to abstain from retorts against the aggressor, you owe it to yourself, you owe it to the spectators, and to aesthetic magnanimity itself, [p. 620] § 189, 275, S. XXIIII–XXVI, to examine on this Lydian stone the argument from envy, the synathroismi of consequences, undeserved or immoderate mockeries, mindful of the nation and the age, § 867, of which what seem bearable to the one will nevertheless be judged by the other already to wound the ἦθος [character] altogether. § 213, 212.

§ 899. You will both more easily obey good morals, and more often spare yourself the expending of vain labour, if you think it said to you 5) that by no means are all things to be refuted by you that either have been said or can be said against your opinions, but only those which you suspect to be verisimilar to your primary personal object unless you refute them, and which you may hope, after your refutation, will be held by the same among aesthetically false things, § 887. The same reckoning holds for answers to the refutations of others directed against us and ours, § 886, 898. Just as *that is most foolishly prescribed, that what cannot be defended should be dissembled by silence, if indeed it is that on which the judge is to pronounce:* so others, *lapsing through diligence, think that answer must be made even to all the words or little sentences* that they suppose can be contradicted, *which is both endless and superfluous*, § 171. *Let there be something you answer.* Beware *the vice of one too anxious, and wrestling with every moment.* Quint. inst. V. XIII. To how many writings could that helmeted prologue [p. 621] have been fitted less elegantly, which Cicero prettily set before l. I. de finibus bonorum et malorum?

#### Sectio LIII. Argumenta Persuasoria. — Section LIII. Persuasive Arguments

§ 900. ARGUMENTS whose power (whether the only one, or the chief one, or the one now most to be considered) is to procure sensate certainty for a given perception are PERSUASIVE, § 829. Therefore all arguments, all figures that prove vividly, S. XXXIII, and that illustrate beautiful verisimilitude, Sect. XXXIII, will be persuasive, § 26. You have a handle for elegantly intensifying the efficacy of your arguments, § 142, if you illustrate especially those that prove, and consecrate those that paint to aesthetic truth, since then the former will not only prove, the latter will not only bring splendour, but both together will persuade. § 143. Thus an apparent pleonasm, whose idly placed element is busy in giving the hypothesis and determination of another, or in general the middle term, will confirm, § 145, 855, e.g. *With these my own eyes I saw it.* Against that:

*Have we seen? or do those who love fashion dreams for themselves?*

§ 901. [p. 622] By a like artifice, things that would seem synonymies or exergasiae can be joined as equipollent, of which, nevertheless, the more acute reader or hearer would see one to be the reason, sufficient in elegant matters, of the other, § 900, 146. The one who is to confirm, § 878–885, who is to satisfy and refute, mindful of § 899, being about to cast certain proving or refuting things into the shades, will sometimes charmingly have recourse to preterition. § 148. Nor am I sorry to have referred hither INTERROGATION, § 332, or the cryptic declaration that we wish someone to state, or to think, his own consciousness about the truth of some matter. For if only you prudently take care not to interrogate where it could be foreseen that your primary personal object would either hesitate or altogether dissent, without further persuasion added: by one apt interrogation you will obtain much, so that the hearer, e.g., himself hands over to himself and recalls to mind afresh whatever reasons he already knew about the matter you assert, and at the same time not rarely is won over to you, when he notices, not unwillingly, that you place no small confidence in his own knowledge, § 840; sometimes he is moved more sensibly, when you invite him to descend into himself to search the innermost senses of his mind, and to find already long since certain for himself those things which are now called good or bad. § 142.

§ 902. [p. 623] Repetition, with its many species, is here also to be repeated, even for this very reason, that it not only persuades, § 333, but has indeed a great power of persuading, not any inept and tautological repetition, but that of the principal aetiologies contracted into some rounded brevity. § 545. For since every aetiology vividly thought is a persuasive argument, § 839, let us call that one of them without which persuasion would seem unable to be completed, with the ancients, the REASON by eminence, and the chief of the aetiologies or reasons, the FIRMAMENT, cf. Cicero de Inu. I. 18, 19. To set the reason and the firmament before the mind for thinking briefly, but repeatedly, either in the same or in slightly inflected modes of thinking, has this efficacy for persuading, that the argument in which you place most confidence sticks more firmly in the mind of your personal object. § 342–345. Not without the allurement of persuasion the *Pervigilium Veneris* so often repeats:

*Now let him love who never loved. And he who has loved, now let him love.*

§ 903. In these arguments let the climax of proofs be commended, and that gradation, § 334, by which the whole seems to have been rendered more certain than its most certain part, examined not by the goldsmith’s balance but by a certain popular scale, § 250, [p. 624] 879. Being on guard against even the shade of an anticlimax in proving, do not be easily persuaded by the counsels of those who urge interposing among stronger arguments a weaker one, which you would not dare to put first. § 879. Is a running-down from the more certain to the less certain ever charming, unless perhaps in Pyrrhonian jests? In the hyperboles of those who confirm, e.g. *it is clearer than the noonday sun*, and of those who refute, e.g. *those who have spoken against the sun*, let cryptic ones be preferred, which so transgress the limits of the certainty and improbability to be assigned that this very thing scarcely, or not even scarcely, appears to have been done. § 339.

§ 904. I do not deny Quintilian’s rule, § 903: *Let the orator* (everyone who is to persuade beautifully) *display confidence, and always speak* (think what is to be signified) *as though he thought best of his case* (thoroughly persuaded concerning the personal objects he is to persuade). Inst. V. 13. I only warn that the ostentatious displayer of excessive certainty is in danger as to his credit. Hence I recognise cases in which DOUBT can prettily be employed for confirming or refuting, the narration or simulation of a state of mind for which, counting up the reasons fighting for a certain opinion and against it, they are reckoned equal, especially if you charmingly pass from this to a pretty parrhesia of evidence. § 349. S. L.
